PAILLIEUX Honorary Board Member D. BOIS Assistant de la Chaire de Culture de la Société nationale d'Acclimatation ! au Muséum d'Histoire naturelle de Paris Troisième édition entièrement retaite 80 FIGURES DANS LE TEXTE PARIS LIBRAIRIE AGRICOLE DE LA MAISON RUSTIQUE 26, RUE JACOR, 26 1899 1 It is with a deep sense of sadness that I see published today, without M. Paillieux being here to receive it. Paillieux to receive the first copy, the third edition of this book, whose preparation my late collaborator had followed with such interest, and which he did not have the joy of seeing published before his death. Despite his advanced age, Mr. Paillieux had retained a verve that made me hope, for many more days, for his active and precious collaboration in our common work, pursued for over twenty years. Indeed, it was in 1876 that Mr. Paillieux did me the honor of associating me with his work. Attracted to each other by a similarity of tastes in studies, it wasn't long before we struck up a friendship that has been one of the greatest charms of my life. I shall never forget the joys I experienced in the company of this good man, of keen intelligence, ardent, gifted to the highest degree with the spirit of research, of remarkable uprightness of character and exquisite kindness. Thanks to him, for almost a quarter of a century I have been able to pursue studies that would have been impossible for me to undertake on my own. I am at a loss for words to express the gratitude I owe to this excellent man, who honored me with his trust and friendship. Given my involvement in Mr. Paillieux's work, it is not for me to pass judgment on his achievements. I can only say that he was a passionate and selfless amateur, practicing horticulture at its most useful for mankind - the search for new food plants - and that the greatest joy of his old age was to see the Crosne he had so ardently propagated enter the mainstream. However, to pay due homage to his memory, I reproduce below an article which appeared in the Revue Horticole of April 16 1898, under the signature of its director, Mr. Bourguignon, an article which I am publishing in full, despite the overly eulogistic sentences devoted to me personally. Paris, January 2nd 1899. D. Bois. NICOLAS-AUGUSTE PAILLIEUX! In its February 16th issue, the Revue Horticole announced the death of Mr. A. Paillieux, who died in Paris at the age of 85. But the short notice it was able to insert in its fortnightly chronicle is inadequate in view of the services rendered by this fine man, not only to French horticulture, but to the country itself, for it is in these terms that we feel we should describe the introduction and popularization of a new vegetable. If we look at the new food plants introduced into our gardens over the last century, we'll see that there are only three that can be considered to be of any interest: Chinese yam, which has not spread much for various reasons; chervil bulheux, of excellent quality, but of very uncertain cultivation; and Crosne. Thanks to Mr. Paillieux's patient research and persevering efforts, Crosne is now widespread not only in Frauce, but in all European countries, and every year consumption of this excellent vegetable grows more considerable. In Paris, Crosne now appears all winter not only in fruit shops, but also in the carriages of four-season merchants; and its price has become accessible to all. This is because the Crosne combines first-rate qualities: absolute rusticity in our climate; extremely easy cultivation; very abundant production; simple and rapid culinary preparation; beautiful appearance, pleasant flavor; and lastly, a great wealth of easily-assimilable substances, making it invaluable for delicate stomachs. If the service rendered by Mr. Paillieux is really as great as we say it is, how is it that not only has his name remained virtually unknown to the public, but that he himself has never received encouragement or rewards of any kind from the Societies that must not have been unaware of his work, or from the powers that be? The reason lies in the truly exceptional modesty of this silent worker. Mr. Paillieux himself abhorred noise, abhorred loud advertising; and unfortunately, all that is necessary to attract attention. He would have liked nothing better than for people to talk about Crosne, to say all the good things it deserved, but only on condition that the vegetable be the focus of attention, without mentioning the name of its popularizer. What we were unable to do during Mr. Paillieux's lifetime, we have (1) Revue horticole, 1898, page 176. the right to try now, and we consider it our duty to pay his memory the tribute it deserves, by saying what Mr. Paillieux was, what he did, how he directed his research, and by trying to shed some light on this life of probity and useful work. Mr. Paillieux, born in Paris on September 10, 1812, was destined for commerce and industry as soon as he had completed his classical studies. We don't need to go into the serious qualities he showed in business: intelligent, hard-working and active, he quickly saw the embroidered tulle and embroidery company he managed prosper. We can only say that it was under the trade name of Paillieux-Salats that the lace-like cotton tulle industry, which has become so important since then, was created in Saint-Pierre-lez-Calais around 1840. Having left the business in 1871, Mr. Paillieux, who had always been involved in gardening in his spare time, devoted himself entirely to horticulture. He would spend summers near Villeneuve-Saint-Georges, in the small village of Crosnes, whose name, hitherto unknown, is now popular, since it was precisely this village that Mr. Paillieux chose to give a common name to Stachys affinis. As soon as he settled in Crosnes, Mr. Paillieux wanted to give his passion for gardening a useful purpose, and resolved to devote himself to the experimental cultivation of all plants, rare or little-known, which could be assumed to have food-giving properties. As soon as his crops were established, Mr. Paillieux entrusted their upkeep to Mr. Véniat, an experienced gardener, whose name must be mentioned here, as he remained in his service for twenty-five years, demonstrating real intelligence in the execution of the work entrusted to him. Mr. Paillieux became a member of the Société nationale d'horticulture de France in 1875, and in 1876 presented to the Society the first plants obtained from his garden at Crosnes, notably the edible Souchet (Cyperus esculentus), the tubers of which are used in Spain to make a drink called Chufa. It was at this time that Mr. Paillieux made the acquaintance of Mr. Bois, then a botanical preparator attached to the Muséum's herbarium, who immediately became, and remained until the end, his assiduous collaborator in the research and study of useful plants. The choice of this collaborator would suffice to show the extent to which Mr. Paillieux knew how to judge the value of men. A knowledgeable botanist with a thorough understanding of plant growth and properties, Mr. Bois also possessed the qualities of order and method essential to the successful conduct of experiments... Let me stop here, because our excellent friend and collaborator, Mr. Bois, is also a modest man, and he would not forgive the Revue for saying here all the good it thinks of him. As early as 1876, Messrs Paillieux and Bois began experiments to see whether it would not be possible to increase the number of our all-too-scarce winter vegetables, by subjecting certain plants to etiolation, in order to obtain products similar to those obtained from wild Chicory, and which are so sought-after under the names of Capuchin Beard and Witloof (Endive). Starting from the principle that the parts we look for in vegetables are generally those which are removed from the influence of light, they cultivated in the dark, for two consecutive winters, a large number of plants which seemed to them to be of such a nature as to be able to be made usable in this way. The results of these experiments were recorded in a small book entitled Nouveaux légumes d'hiver, published in 1879. In this work, the authors recommend the culling of various plants to obtain etiolates, which could constitute a new element in the supply of our tables. Mr. Paillieux wasted no time in broadening his experiments by applying them to the study of food plants used in various parts of the globe, but as yet unknown in France. And so began a campaign of correspondence and representations, which you'd really have to have witnessed to believe. Thousands of unanswered letters and unsuccessful approaches could be counted! Nevertheless, he did so much and so well that he ended up attracting the interest of naturalist travellers, botanists, directors of French and foreign colonial gardens, consuls, missionaries and certain large horticultural establishments, who became his suppliers of seeds and living plants (1). (1) To give an idea of the number and quality of the correspondents from whom Mr. Paillieux received shipments of seeds or plants, we publish below a list of the main ones: Europe France. - Natural History Museum. - Societé nationale d'acclimatation. - Maison Vilmorin-Andrieux et Cie - Dr Sagot, author of the Manuel des cultures tropicales. - M. Todaro. - Horticultural Society of Pallanza (Lake Maggiore.) - Botanical Garden of Turin: Dr Mattirolo Russia. - Société impériale d'acclimatation: Mr. Zolotnitsky. - Jardin botanique de Saint-Péters- bourg: M. Regel and M. Bataline. Asia China. - R. P. Heude, Dr Bretschneider, Mr Ford, Mr Eug. Simon, former consul. Japan. - Dr Hénon, Mr Jarmain, Mr Bernhardt Bing. Persia. - Mr. Pissard. Costa Rica. - Mr. de Lafon. Venezuela. - Dr Ernst and Mr Hahn. Aroentine Republic. - Mr Barbier and Mr Berthault. Chile. - Mr Philippi. Brazil. - Mr Albuquerque. West Indies. - Mr. Bureau. Oceania Australia. - Baron von Mueller. New Zealand. - Dr Kirk. New Caledonia. - M. Perret. Over 250 plants from all over the world were cultivated at Crosnes. There were many failures, but nothing discouraged Mr. Paillieux, who found indomitable energy in his passion to be useful. The results of these trials, both good and bad, were collected in a volume entitled Le Potager d'un curieux, Histoire, Culture et l'sages de cent plantes comestibles peu connues ou inconnues, published in 1885, in collaboration with Mr. D. Bois. This highly original book was a great success, and a second edition, published in 1892, presented the results of new experiments. In studying all the food plants available to them, the authors were not only aiming to provide continental France with new products. Among the species they had in their possession, there were a good number which, although uncultivable in temperate regions, were of the greatest interest to countries in the inter-tropical zone. In this respect, Le Potager d'un curieux is destined to be of great service to all those involved in colonial cultivation. A number of plants have been propagated by Mr. Paillieux. Among the most important is the Soya (Soja hispida), for which he has published a complete monograph. But, as we said earlier, the real service rendered by Mr. Paillieux was the introduction and popularization of Crosne (Stachys affinis), which has taken such a prominent place among our most precious winter vegetables. Stachys affinis, Bunge (S. tuberifera, Naudin), absolutely unknown outside China and Japan, was sent by Dr. Breitschneider, physician to the Russian legation in Peking; and it was the few tubers that arrived in the most mediocre state of preservation that Mr. Paillieux cultivated, and which were the original source of all those used to spread the plant everywhere. Mr. A. Paillieux died in Paris on February 8, at the age of 85, after a short illness, just as he had begun correcting the proofs of the 3rd edition of Potager d'un curieux. He retained the same zeal for work and passion for horticulture to the very end. Mr. Paillieux was an honorary member of the Council of the Société nationale d'acclimatation, and for many years vice-president of the Société's plant section. He expressed himself with remarkable precision and clarity, and charmed by the elegance of his speech as much as by the soundness of his judgment. All those who had the honor of knowing Mr. Paillieux, as well as remembering the prodigious activity of this worker who seemed always on the move, will treasure the memory of his exquisite affability and kindness. (Revue horticole.) L. BOURGUIGNON. For the past ten years, we have been cultivating a large number of little-known or unknown exotic edible plants. We consider as little-known those whose names we read in a few horticultural treatises, but which are rarely or never found in gardens. We'll cite as examples, among twenty others, Burdock, Baselle, Glaciale and Benincasa, which we consider to be little-known plants. We consider unknown plants to be those that appear only in botanical nomenclatures and are not cultivated anywhere. Examples include Tuberous Mustard, Tuberous Stachys, Miôya, Kudzu, Tuberous Asparagus, etc. There are very few food plants that are absolutely unknown. Among the peoples they visited, explorers had only to open their eyes to see what they ate. Savages eat all plants that grow spontaneously and require no care. Peoples with a civilization different from our own, but highly advanced, cultivate everything that pays for work; they uproot from the wasteland and the woods everything that is edible. In Japan and China, the populations are so poor that no edible plant is neglected. As one of our correspondents said of Lilium tigrinum: "In the province I lived in, they didn't grow it for the table; yet the poor eat it; but what vegetable don't the poor Japanese eat? Another correspondent, referring to Momordica Charantia, wrote: "The Chinese eat it too; they eat everything. It would be easier to list the things of vegetable origin that the Chinese don't eat than those they do." From the foregoing we must conclude that all edible plants are known; that, if we can still come across some that have not been reported, this will be a very rare case; finally, that we do not claim to have introduced absolutely new plants, and that, in reading us, you will not be making a voyage of discovery. If we consult M. A. de Candolle's book L'origine des plantes cultivées, we can see that central and northern Europe was, in more or less ancient times, extremely poor in vegetable plants, and that our soil spontaneously produced only a very small number of them. The species we have today, of which we have obtained countless varieties, came to us successively from countries warmer than our own. Only one vegetable plant, an insignificant one at that, the arrowroot, came to us from the North. Reduced to its natural production, France would have virtually no vegetables. Beans, peas, beans (1), onions, salsify, potatoes, melons, pumpkins, tomatoes, large-fruited strawberries and thirty other common vegetable plants would be lacking, which demonstrates two things: firstly, that new vegetable plants can only be introduced into our crops by requesting them from warmer climates than our own; secondly, that these plants, given the care appropriate to their nature, can thrive in our climate. After ten years of more or less fruitful research, we believe the time has come to report on trials whose end we will never see. The task we have undertaken is of such a nature that it cannot be completed. Experience has shown us that a plant which (1) The origin of the common bean is not known, but it is too sensitive to the cold to have been born in our climate. shows itself to be resistant to cultivation in our climate sometimes has early varieties which, not requiring as long a vegetation period as their type, can take their place in our gardens. These varieties need to be discovered. And it's not just a few leagues from Paris, in the garden we have at our disposal, that we need to continue experimenting with exotic vegetable plants. As we have said, all those whose introduction can be attempted belong to climates much warmer than the one around Paris. Crops that fail in our garden will often succeed between the Loire and the Mediterranean. What we can only obtain using market gardening methods can, in the Midi, be obtained in the open air and become the object of a lucrative trade. Railroads facilitate a vegetable trade that distances once made impracticable. We hope that experimentation on the plants to which we draw attention will take place at various points in the region, under conditions more favorable than those imposed on us. Meritorious efforts are already being made in our Mediterranean departments. We wish them every success. We present the results, good or bad, that each trial has given us. Future experimenters will therefore be able to avoid useless experiments, or at least choose the plants that seem to offer the best chances of success. Educated by the synonyms we have carefully indicated, he will not be exposed to receiving under an unknown name, and consequently as a new plant, a species he may have had for a long time, and which, sometimes, he may even have rejected from his crops. Our aim is to extend the field of vegetable horticulture; if we happen to have helped propagate useful plants, however few in number, we shall consider ourselves very fortunate. In the thirteen years since the Société nationale d'Acclimatation began publishing Le Potager d'un Curieux, we have not interrupted our research for a single moment, and have been able to obtain and experiment with over a hundred plants not included in the first edition. Today, after twenty-three years of research, the field seems more or less exhausted, and we doubt whether it will be possible to find one or two species each year that have so far eluded us. Asia, Africa and America have given us what they have, but Oceania, Australia and New Zealand have provided us with nothing, because they really have no indigenous food plants, spontaneous or cultivated, other than Yam, Potato and Taro. It's not, however, that edible plants are in short supply on the inhabited surface of the globe. An American botanist, Mr. Lewis Sturtevant, of South Framingham (Massachusetts), has taken the trouble to count the world's edible plants and has found 4,233 of them, divided into 1,353 species and 170 genera. 211 species, according to the author we quote, are cultivated for cooking, as vegetables; this small number of species would make up only a quarter of the total number of known edible species. Future researchers will undoubtedly be encouraged by these figures to dig even deeper into the immense field in which we no longer know how to discover anything. We wish them well. But, if it is today, if not impossible, or at least very difficult, to find plants in exotic food flora that can be usefully introduced into our vegetable gardens, it would be relatively easy to increase the resources of this kind in our colonies, by giving each of them what it lacks and what the others possess. In the course of our research, we have come to recognize that vegetables commonly used in some of our far-flung possessions are often absolutely unknown in their French sisters. Without neglecting what can be borrowed from abroad, from Japan, China, India, South America and even Africa, we feel that the best way to achieve our goal is through exchange between our colonies, through mutual acclimatization. Success is not in doubt. In several of our possessions, gardens have been established and maintained at public expense, in which European vegetables are grown, and some of them vegetate fairly well, provided the seeds are frequently renewed. In all their establishments or stations, our missionaries also maintain gardens in which they grow these same vegetables, for their own nourishment, and also to teach and propagate their cultivation and use. Just imagine how abundant and healthy the vegetable supply of our colonies would be if, in addition to these European vegetables, which are painstakingly obtained, we were to add those which refuse to grow in our climate, but which would thrive in French Guiana as well as in Gabon, the West Indies and Cochinchina, etc., and which would spread everywhere where our compatriots still suffer cruel hardship today due to the extreme scarcity of food plants. Let's assume for a moment that we're dealing exclusively with the Congo. It has a colonial garden in Libreville, which is in excellent hands, and missionaries in Haut-Congo who do not neglect to grow French vegetables. From Japan and China: Daïkon, Pé-tsaï, Sinapis napiformis, Trapa bicornis, Nélombo; From Cochinchina and Tonkin: Baria beans, Pachyrrhizus angulatus, Ipomxa reptans, numerous varieties of Sojas, the best varieties of Dolics; From Pondicherry and English India : Baselle with heart-shaped leaves, Benincasa cerifera, twenty varieties of Phaseolus Mungo, Cajan or Ambrevade (Cajanus indicus), the square pea (Psophocarpus tetragonolobus), a perennial legume whose pod, raised by four very prominent wings, is excellent for eating green; From New Caledonia and Tahiti : excellent Taros and thirty varieties of Yam; From the West Indies: Gombo, Maranta arundinacea, Cucumis Anguria, Pachyrrhizus tuberosus, Chayote (Sechium edu e), Solanum betaceum or Tree Tomato, a shrub that produces an abundance of sweet, tangy fruit that can be used to make sauces and marmalades; From Ecuador: Solanum muricatum, Solanum quitoense; From South America : Canna edulis and discolor, Arracacia, Dioscorea triloba, as floury and tasty as potatoes, large varieties of Oxalis crenata, Pachyrrhizus tuberosus de la Paz, white Quinoa, Claytone perfoliée, Physalis peruviana, the seven-year old pea or Ilaricot de Lima (Phaseolus lunatus), A perennial bean whose seed is of excellent quality, Dolic Asperge or long pod (Dolichos sesquipedalis), which could be of great service in our colonies due to its abundant production of pods which, picked before full maturity, can be used like green beans; From Java : Coleus tuberosus; Madagascar: Plectranthus ternatus; Madagascar or Cape Bean (Phaseolus capensis); Spain or Asia: Siam Squash. If the Congo received all these plants, which are easy to grow and common in hot countries, would it not be as well provided for as we are in our civilized countries, and would this wide variety of vegetables not be a guarantee of health for white people? At a time when our colonial expansion is occupying everyone's minds and provoking so much effort, the work we are presenting here appeals to all men of good will, directors of botanical gardens, colonial gardeners, missionaries and, in particular, our Société nationale d'Acclimatation. This new edition of Potager d'un Curieux is accompanied by previously unpublished figures and others whose photographs have been kindly made available to us by the management of the Revue horticole, by Vilmorin-Andrieux et Ce, the Société nationale d'Acclimatation and the journal Le Naturaliste. Our warmest thanks to all. ACCOUB DE SYRIE GUNDELIA TOURNEFORTH L. Fam. Compositae. Milky plant like Scolyma. Stem quite strong, twining, reaching Om, 30 to Om, 40 in height. Radical leaves long, deeply incised, with lobes bordered by spiny teeth. The main vein is thick, white and covered with down; stem leaves are sessile, semi-amplexicaul. In June-August, purplish flowers in oval flowerheads resembling those of certain Panicauts (Eryngium). Involucre uniseriate, monophyllous, shorter than the flowers, with scales ending in a spiny point. Corolla regular, glabrous, with five lobes longer than the tube. Stamens 5, not protruding. Stigma bifid. The four florets around the circumference are male or sterile; the central one is hermaphroditic. Fruit (achaine) fairly large, oval, lacking aigrette. Perennial plant, native to Asia Minor, where it grows in uncultivated areas. Tournefort observed it on his voyage to the Levant, and named it Gundelia, after his friend Gundelsheimer, who accompanied him on the trip. For a long time, it was cultivated at the Jardin des Plautes in Paris, using seeds from this trip; it was then lost, but was soon found again. Today, there are a number of strong Gundelia plants here, but they produce very few fer8 4 Fig. 1. - Syrian Accoub (Gundelia Tournefortii). tiles; moreover, as with many plants with taproots or lactes, clump division is hardly practicable. Gundelia is therefore difficult to propagate. Its cultivation also presents some difficulties. Originating in a country where the wet vegetative season is short-lived, followed by a period of absolute drought, it happens that, when cultivated here, the plant has completed its vegetation by midsummer; from this moment onwards, it begins the period of rest, which would correspond precisely, in its country of origin, to the dry season. Our autumns are usually rainy, so the plant must be protected from humidity by covering it with a bell or a frame, otherwise it will soon rot. We have been kindly provided with a letter from Mr. Blanche, French consul in Tripoli, Syria. It reveals a use for the plant that we had not suspected: "As far as plants that live in all environments are concerned, I'm sending you one to which I'd like you to pay special attention: it's the Gundelia Tournefortü L., with large roots and thorny leaves. The Arabs call it Accoub, from which Vaillant had of course formed the genus Hacub. "For many years now, I've wanted to acclimatize it here; I must have sent you some in the past; I sent some to Decaisne and Palermo, etc., etc., and I didn't succeed; I'm starting my naturalization trials again this year. It is especially as a vegetable plant that it deserves attention. The young shoots, stripped of their thorns, and especially the buds in the nascent bud stage, make up a vegetable that is highly esteemed by the Arabs, and which, in my opinion, rivals the artichoke. In Syria, according to Boissier (Flora orientalis), the young stems of this plant are eaten after cooking. It is known as Accoub. Olivier, in his Voyage en Orient, remarks that the roots of this plant are tastier, far less bland than those of Salsify and Scorsonera. There is no doubt," he says, "that this plant succeeds very well throughout southern France, and may become one of our best vegetable plants. (Lamarck, Encyclopédie, suppl.). It can be seen from the above that the roots, stems and flower heads of Accoub are equally edible, in short that the entire plant makes an excellent vegetable. Before Mr. Blanche's letter, it had not been mentioned that the budding flower heads were particularly sought-after, and it is precisely these that we have been able to sample. Up to now, we haven't had enough plants to be able to sacrifice a few of our subjects to eat the roots or ribs, but once the experiment was over, one of us was able to write to friends in June 1886: "Excellent vegetable! Fierce plant! My gardener approached it carefully, pruning shears in hand. He detached about thirty round, flowering buds the size of a large pineapple strawberry. "I cut off the thorns on them and threw them into the fresh water as I went along. "On contact with iron, the section turns black like that of an artichoke. "The buds were cooked in water and accommodated in butter like flageolet beans, without parsley. "The result was extremely satisfying, and this is not only the opinion of the enthusiast writing to you, but also that of his whole family. "The taste of the vegetable is also close to that of Asparagus and Artichoke. "Accoub would be called by gourmets to the highest destinies, if it were not the most formidable of thistles, if cooks could accept it. One of our correspondents, Doctor G..., wrote to us from Dijon in October 1890: "This year, I harvested a crop of Accoub that was found to be excellent by all. The seeds sprout with difficulty, some after a month, others after three months. The flower aborts. The plant then needs at least four years to reach maximum production. We would nevertheless like to draw the attention of enthusiasts to the Gundelia; if it cannot be sold commercially, it deserves a place of honor in their gardens and on their tables. The difficulties its cultivation may present in the vicinity of Paris would not exist in the Var, Alpes-Maritimes, etc., where the distribution of humidity and drought is much the same as in Syria. It is highly desirable that serious trials be carried out, and that a happy outcome be the prize of our consul's persevering zeal. ALLIUM ODORUM L., Mant. 62 (1). Family Liliaceae. Bulb tunnelled, brown. Spike naked. Leaves linear, flat, canaliculate. Multiflora umbel. Flowers white, erect, fairly large, borne on 10 to 15 mm pedicels; bracts hyaline. Spathe bifid, membranous, three or four times shorter (1) This plant should not be confused with the Fragrant Garlic, Allium fragrans Veut. or Nothoscordum fragrans Kunth. than the umbel. Anthers tawny lilac. Ovary three-located, green, surmounted by a short style. Fig. 2 - Garlic with edible flowers (Allium odorum L.). Scented garlic is a vegetable from Japan, where it is called Nira, and from China and Cochinchina. We have received seeds from Yokohama and Saigon. It is pretty enough to be considered ornamental. Allium odorum can be found growing wild in the mountains around Peking. It is also cultivated in all gardens, under the name of Kieu. The Chinese eat the whole plant raw. It flowers in August, and at this time of year, cut flowers are sold at the market, which they also eat (1). The above information suggested the idea of a successful trial. We decorated a Chicory salad with Allium odorum flowers, as we do with Nasturtium flowers. Mixed with the salad, these flowers gave it a special fragrance, not unlike that which connoisseurs seek when they introduce what is commonly known as capon into Chicory salad. Our friends, after tasting, have given their approval to our attempt, and there may be a use for it that should not be scorned. Garlic flowers can be picked for two months, from August 1st to October 1st, and perhaps even longer. The plant produces an abundance of seeds, which are sown in April; in this way, flowers only appear in the second year, but when bulbs are planted, they appear as early as August 1st. Allium odorum is a hardy perennial. According to M. de Heldreich, Allium roseum L. and the related A. neapolitanum Cyrillo produce edible bulbs. Both plants are native to (1) Docteur E. Bretschneider. MINISTÈRE DE L'AGRICULTURE BENTRE DE RECHCRCRTS) MQNUCS OE PRNVERC of the Mediterranean region. The latter is grown extensively in the south of France, and its pure white flowers are shipped to florists in major cities for winter bouquets. Another species, A. leptophyllum Wallich, from the Himalayas, produces bulbs more strongly prickly than those of ordinary onions. Captain Pogson considers them to be sudorific. Its leaves are said to make a good condiment. We have received Allium nipponicum Franch. el Sav. from Japan, courtesy of Mr. M. Vilmorin. According to Messrs. Batchelor and Miyabe (Ainu Economic plants) (Transactions of the Asiatic Society of Japan, vol. XXI, 1893. p. 231), this Allium is known as Membiru, Nobiru among the Aïnos, who generally eat the bulbs fresh, in salads, and who sometimes also use them "to give a delicate flavor to food". Another Allium species, A. japonicum Regel, from Japan, which we found mentioned in Mr. Edward Kinch's List of food plants used in Japan (Transactions of the Asiatic Society of Japan, tome XI (1883), was also supplied to us by Mr. M. Vilmorin. The plant we received under the latter name was none other than A. vleraceum, a common plant in France of very mediocre interest. 3 ALSTRŒMERIA LIGTU L.; A. Ligta Ruiz et Pav, Bot. Mag. tab. 125; Red, Litiac. tab. 40; Delaun, Herb. de l'amateur, vol. 2. Family Amaryllidae. Stems, some sterile, 20 to 25-centimeter haules, terminated by a rosette of leaves; others, 3-6 florets, 30 to 40 centimeters high. Leaves small, lanceolate-linear; those of spatulate rosettes, oblong. Flowers large, spindly, as if bilabiate, arranged in a simple umbel. Peduncles longer than flower leaves. Sepals white, speckled with red. Petals scarlet, immaculate. Roots fasciculate, rounded, thick, covered with brown, slightly gnarled bark. This plant grows on the banks of streams in Peru and Chile. In Chile, it is called Liuto. "Alstrœmère Liuto appears to be the plant that yields the verilable Chuno, a starch extracted from tubers in the same way as potato starch. In Chile, it is frequently used, especially for the sick and those with delicate stomachs. It grows especially in the provinces of Cauquenes and Concepcion". (Claudio Gay, Flora Chilena, vol. VI, p. 85.) The interest of this note lies in the information we have gathered on Liuto's precious starch. We won't change a syllable in the answers to our questions. - Fig. 3 - Alstrœmère Liuto (Alstrœmeria Ligtu L.). Liuto grows only in sandy, damp soil with a temperate temperature similar to that of the Seine department. Only one species is known in the country (Concepcion). The A. pelegrina mentioned by Cl. Gay is unknown. The plant's Chilean name is Liuto, the only one known in the country of production. Chuño is sold for local consumption. It is widely traded in Chile. Little is exported. Chuño is not made in factories or in families. Poor people pull up the roots. The women do all the manufacturing, which takes place in March and April, and take the product of their labor to market as they go along. You could ask for it in Chile and receive a certain quantity. It sells by the measure: 10 to 12 francs per decaliter. It keeps perfectly well. Dr. Thévenot's opinion on the food value of Chuño de Liuto: Dr. Thévenot practiced medicine in Chile for many years, where he was highly regarded. He now lives in Paris, and we were introduced to him by one of our friends, Mr. A..., a large landowner in Chile. Here's a summary of the interview we had with the eminent practitioner on the subject of Chuno: "Chuño is an excellent food, not as rich as some other starches, but light, easily digested and very useful for children and convalescents. It is used to make highly prized pastries. "Mr. A... was kind enough to bring us some Liuto roots and a certain quantity of Chuño from Chile, which, when tasted, lived up to the opinion we had formed of it, and enabled Dr. Mène to present a report to the fifth section of the Société d'Acclimatation on May 12 1885, from which we extract the following: "The small quantity of Chuño that I received as a gift from Mr. Paillieux to the Société d'Acclimatation did not allow me to carry out many experiments, and I cannot give complete conclusions on the value of this product; but the results I obtained are very good. "As a foodstuff, Chuño is excellent in soups, either with fat or milk: a tablespoonful for a bowl of broth or milk; dilute cold and pour while boiling, leave to boil for four to five minutes. Chuno doesn't thicken much during cooking, and forms a soup without lumps, revealing a few small transparent grains like tapioca and giving only the broth or milk a slightly thicker consistency. "Very pleasant to the taste, it makes a really delicate soup, very easy to digest. "I've given it to convalescents, who digested it without any difficulty and asked me for more as it was pleasant to the taste and sustained them well. "I gave it to children aged six to ten months in milk; the parents were very satisfied and asked me if it was possible to obtain more, as the children took this soup with pleasure. "In two patients suffering from gastralgia and with very poor digestion, Chuno was well tolerated, without causing the stomach upsets and swellings to which they were prone after ingesting almost all foods. "Administered externally as a poultice, in a case of incipient phlegmonous inflammation of the dorsum of the foot, and in a stye of the upper eyelid, it produced marked relief. "Used in lotions with lukewarm water (one mouthful for two-thirds of a bowl of water), followed by an application of Chuño powder, it brought rapid relief in a case of erythema of the leg. "It can also be used in place of rice powder to alleviate razor burn when the beard has been shaved." We don't need to add anything to the above to appreciate the services that Liuto would render if it could be cultivated. The climate would no doubt not be an insurmountable obstacle. We know that several Alstrœmères are hardy in the Paris climate, and for several years we have had a clump of A. pelegrina L. that has withstood the winters without shelter. A. versicolor Ruiz et Pav. could be cultivated as an ornamental plant for its curious flowers of the most brilliant and varied colors, but it tolerates the open air perfectly in the Botanical School of the Muséum. AMARANTUS OLERACEUS L. ; Euxolus oleraceus Moq; Albersia oleracea Kunth. Family Amarantaceae. Annual plant with erect or ascending stems, Om.30 to Om.50 high, lined with pale-green, stalked, oval, very obtuse, notched, slightly rough leaves. In July-August, greenish flowers, arranged at the top of the plant and in the axils of the upper leaves, in spikelets shorter than the leaf petioles and forming erect, obtuse, dense spikes; calyx once as long as the bracts. Fruit utricular, oval, smooth, acute. "This useful and edible plant is very common in the West Indies. It is found everywhere. Inhabitants eat its leaves, seasoned like those of French spinach, to which it bears a striking resemblance. It is used in the famous Creole stew called Calalou, with Giraumon buds, Purslane, Gombo, Morelle laman, poultry, a piece of ham, crabs, crayfish and, above all, lots of Pimento cheese. (Descourtilz, Flore des Antilles.) In Brazil, this species and A. flavus L. are eaten like spinach. They are both confused under the vulgar name of Caruru. "Amaranth and its varieties are cultivated on a large scale by the natives throughout India. It is used during the rains. The part of the plant that is eaten is the stem, which is tender and succulent. It is cut into small pieces and prepared like beans. To my taste, it's a very tasteless vegetable, barely acceptable when you can't get another fresh vegetable. "Dr. Roxburgh says there are several varieties cultivated as vegetables, of which the following should be distinguished: "2. viridis. - Green, ordinary, widely cultivated. "3. ruber. - Beautiful variety with bright, light-red stems. Branches, petioles, veins and the leaves themselves are more rust-colored. "y. albus. - All the parts that are red in the previous variety are bright white in this one. Widely cultivated in Bengal. "5̀. giganteus. - 5 to 8 feet tall, with a stem as thick as the wrist. The tender, succulent tops of its stems, as well as its branches, are sometimes served on our tables as a substitute for Asparagus (1)." Amarantus gangetieus L. (Lal Sag (2)), tristis L., (Mekanada in Sancr.; Sirru Kirai, in Tam.) are still cultivated in India. R. P. Camboué sent us seeds of A. spinosus L. from Madagascar, which the Madecasses eat (1) Firminger, A manual of gardening for Bengal. (2) According to the Special Catalogue of exhibits, etc., Colonial and Indian Exhibition. Colonial and Indian Exhibition, 1886, Amarantus gungeticus is perhaps the most important Sag (green vegetable used like Spinach) cultivated in India as a vegetable herb under the name Anampatsa. This species is used for the same purpose in many regions, notably in India, where it is called Kantanatya. We have received from Mr. Pierre, agent for colonial cultivation in Gabon, the seeds of a plant labelled Spinach of Gabon, which we have recognized as Euxolus caudatus Moq; this species is frequently used as a vegetable in our West African possessions. AMARANTUS PALMERI S. Wats. On his return from a trip to Baja California, M. Diguet, a traveler and naturalist, gave us a bag of Amarantus Palmeri S. Wats. seeds, known in Baja California as Quelite. According to a note given to us by M. Diguet, this plant grows in clay or sandy-clay soils. It is only found after the summer rains, when it develops rapidly. It does not grow during winter, even after the rains. Its size varies according to the circumstances in which it grows: between 30 centimetres and 2 metres. The regions that this plant is particularly fond of forming real fields are the Sierra plateaus, and especially certain parts of these plateaus known as Lagunas, depressions where rainwater accumulates for several days. The great plains of the Pacific slope are even more propitious; as the soil here is highly impregnated with nitrates, Quelite reaches its maximum size, i.e. two meters. Quelite is used in Baja California as fodder, and Californians eat it young as a salad and spinach. M. Diguet says it's an excellent vegetable, which he prefers to spinach. As fodder, Quélite can be consumed by livestock, either fresh or dried. Fresh Quélite should be used as soon as it is cut, as during the withering period it undergoes a kind of fermentation that makes it toxic. As a yield per hectare, Quélite would give, in dry hay, a weight in excess of three tons. A single plant requires a surface area of 200 to 250 square centimetres for normal development. The plants we obtained in 1895 were very few in number and developed poorly. However, we were able to collect seeds which enabled us to sow them again in 1896. This time, the plants grew vigorously and reached over 1 meter in height. The leaves, prepared like Spinach, did not seem to us to have any marked superiority over those of other Amaranths. Our supply of seeds will easily enable us to cultivate the plant a little more extensively, with a view to verifying M. Diguet's assertions that Quélite could be useful as a fodder plant, due to its considerable yield. Trials could perhaps be carried out in Algeria. To this end, we have sent some seeds to Dr Trabut, government botanist in Algiers. All in all, vegetable amaranths are used by many populations, and it is for this reason that they are included here. We do not recommend their cultivation. ANGOURIE DES ANTILLES West Indian cucumber. - Brown cucumber. - Spiny cucumber. Wild cucumber. - American spiny cucumber. CUCUMIS ANGURIA L. Cucurbitaceae family. Annual plant. Creeping, angular, hispid stems, 2 m to 2*.50 m long. Leaves petiolate, palmate, deeply sinuous and rough to the touch. Axillary yellow flowers, small like those of Bryone. Female flowers are followed by ovoid, whitish-green fruits, spiked all over with small spinular spikes. Very long peduncle. The fruit's small size and soft spines give it the appearance of a Horse Chestnut. This cucumber has long appeared in catalogs under the name of Arada cucumber, which does not belong to it. The Arada Cucumber, described by Descourtilz, takes its name from a particular conformation which makes it resemble, in a certain respect, the women of the Aradas tribe of negroes. We have so far been unable to obtain a copy. This is Linné's Cucumis compressus. The Angourie cucumber grows naturally everywhere in the West Indies, mainly in dry savannahs and near rivers with rich vegetation along their banks. It is found in New Grenada, Brazil, near Bahia, and throughout South America, mainly in its eastern part, where it is frequently cultivated in vegetable gardens. The cultivation of Angourie presents no difficulties. Five months elapse between sowing and harvesting. Sow in a greenhouse in March; pot in April; plant under a bell from May 15 to 25; harvest from August 10. Fruiting is extraordinarily abundant, with around a hundred fruits per plant, but if the plants are rained on for several days, the crop is completely destroyed. However, it is impossible to grow them under glass, as they quickly fill the trunk, suffocate there and can no longer produce anything. Angourie doesn't require new or old soil; simply plant it in bunches 2 meters apart, topped with a little consumed manure. Although it does not tolerate rain, it should be liberally watered. Fruits forming far from the plant are not affected by water. The water should be poured into the center of a small basin around the plant. The bells are not removed, but raised on three racks. On August 10, 1876, we presented to the Société nationale d'Horticulture de France, Angouries admirably well grown, sown on March 16 and loaded with a multitude of fruits ready for harvest. In the Paris climate, this is an amateur plant that we took great pleasure in cultivating. In the Midi, its cultivation would certainly be profitable, as we can see from its uses. Angourie fruit is eaten as a salad. In Basse-Terre (Guadeloupe), our naval infantrymen gather them on their walks around town and add them to their regular diet. This pretty little cucumber is prepared in a variety of ways: in sauces, calalou, and vinegar preserves, particularly in those in the colonies known as A charts. According to Descourtilz (Flore des Antilles), to prepare it, you cut it down the middle and remove the infinite number of seeds it contains, then cook it on its own, or with ham, crabs, tomatoes or cod. To preserve it in vinegar, strip it of its seeds and add stems, vine shoots and green chilli fruit. Dr. Sagot, in the Bulletin de la Sociéte nationale d'Acclimatation, 2e série. volume VI, 1872, page 550, tells us that the young cooked fruit of Cucumis Anguria is tender and very pleasant. In good soil, the plant bears a lot of fruit. It is the Pepinhodo mato of the Portuguese colonies. M. Naudin, in the Annales des sciences naturelles, has published an instructive note on C. Anguria, to which we refer the reader. According to him, the plant is indeed of American origin, which he had initially doubted. It is considered a wild plant and cultivated as such in a large part of America. In this respect, it seems to have been put to some use in Italy in the last century, as we learn from Gilii and Xuares, in a now very rare opuscule (Osservatione fitologice, etc.), which was part of Mr. Delessert's library and that of the Institut. We experimented with Angourie as a cooked vegetable. Picked at half-girth, its fruits were peeled, cooked whole and served like fresh flageolet beans. It's a pleasant dish that will please most, but not everyone. We have preserved the fruits of our small Cucumber in vinegar, prepared with Elder flowers, Pepper, Tarragon, Catawissa Onions, etc., without removing their seeds. We consider this precaution unnecessary. This preserve is good and pretty. The fruits should be picked before they are fully developed; their skin hardens quite quickly. In conclusion, we strongly recommend Angourie cultivation to amateurs in the Paris region and to horticulturists and market gardeners in the south of France. Sales of its fruit seem assured. ANYS-WORTEL ANNESORHIZA MONTANA Eckl. et Zeyh. et A. MACROCARPA Eckl. et Zeyh. Umbelliferae family. Under the name of Anys-Wortel, Mr. Dr. Mac Owan, government botanist at Cape-town (Cape of Good Hope), sent us, in May 1895, the seeds of two species of Annesorhiza, plants of the Umbelliferae family, which grow wild on the Cape mountains, and are eaten by the boërs and natives. The edible part is the root. You'd think," Mr Mac Owan told us, "you were looking at parsnips as wide as a finger: their flavor is such that you'd suppose they'd been cooked with Anise." The seeds so kindly sent to us by our kind correspondent germinated very well: they belonged to two species: Annesorhiza montana Eckl. et Zeyh., with flat, bi-tri-pinnate leaves; A. macrocarpa Eckl. et Zeyh., with very indented leaves. The plants obtained were planted outdoors, in well loosened soil, and we covered the plantations with glass cases to shelter them from the cold during the winter. At present, both species continue to vegetate; they flower and have even given us a few seeds. The leaves have a strong aniseed scent. Unfortunately, the root has not yet developed to the point where it can be used. GLYCINE APIOS L. ; Apios tuberosa Mœnch. 23 Leguminosae family. Perennial, herbaceous, glabrous; stems to 2 meters, climbing, arising from a tuberous root. Imperipinnate leaves; in August-September, dark purple flowers, variegated with pink-chair, fragrant, in axillary clusters. Around 1845, the agricultural world was in the grip of a veritable panic. The potato was suffering from a disease that was threatening its complete disappearance. People wondered how they could do without the precious Solanée, how they could replace it. Entire populations, if not in France, at least in other countries, were threatened by the horrors of famine. The terror was great, as was the eagerness to experiment with the cultivation of plants proposed as substitutes for the diseased tuber. The potato has not disappeared, and neither has the disease, but it only causes the grower accidental losses. We can therefore say, without too much sadness, that the meritorious efforts that have been made to replace the potato have been absolutely in vain. Arracacia cannot be grown in France. The Picotianene produces nothing; the Olluco, no more; finally, the Apios, which we are dealing with at the moment, is barely edible and its yield is practically nil. We'd like to pass it over in silence, but its name made some noise in the years following the invasion of the terrible disease, and we owe it at least a mention. Under the title: Culture de l'Apios luberosa comparée à celle de la Pomme de terre, G. Moretti, professor at the University of Pavia, published an extensive note which the curious can read in Revue Horticole, 4e série, volume I, 1852, page 84. The following is as brief a summary as possible: "Jacques Cornut, a Parisian physician, was the first to make Apios tuberosa known, in 1635. The plant was originally from Canada; it quickly spread to all the botanical gardens of Europe, and became naturalized in Italy, Austria and Bohemia. "In the Comptes rendus de l'Académie des sciences, February 1849, page 194, M. Payen reported on the chemical analysis of Apios tubers: Nitrogenous matter...................... Fatty substances..................... Starchy starch, dextrin, sugary matter 4.50 0.80 analog, pectic acid, pectin, etc. 23.55 Cellulose, including epidermis.......... 1.30 Mineral matter.................... 2.25 Water................................. 57.60 100.00 "At the same time, M. A. Richard sent the Institut de France a note on the nutritional qualities of Apios and even proposed a cultivation method which the professor, whose note we summarize, considers impracticable. "In the first days of March 1848, Messrs Barbieri and Moretti had planted 2 kilograms of Apios tubers; they harvested 3 kilograms on the following November 8. "A second trial, planting 1 kilogram of tubers, yielded 1/,185. A comparative cultivation of Apios and Potato demonstrated that the latter provided six times as much nutritive substance as the former. "A notice by Mérat on several tubers proposed as substitutes for potatoes (Revue horticole, vol. IV, p. 13-14) presents conclusions absolutely unfavorable to Apios. Le Bon Jardinier says that, when we eat a certain quantity of these tubers, they leave a sort of singular and unpleasant catch on the palate and the back of the mouth, which is due to the presence of a milky juice very similar to rubber. Apios can be grown as a curiosity. Every year, it produces underground sprays that swell from distance to distance and present a string of tuberosities of a highly original appearance. It is also an ornamental climber suitable for covering trellises and arbors. APONOGETON DISTACHYUM THUNB. Family Naiadaceae. Beautiful aquatic plant, swimming, perennial, native to the Cape of Good Hope. Stump formed of ovoid, agglomerated, brownish tubers. Leaves glabrous, stalked, the longer the deeper the water in which the plant grows; elliptical, lanceolate, cheerful-green blade. Peduncle more or less long, swollen at the tip, Fig. 5 - Aponogeton with two spikes (Aponogeton distachyum Thunb). bearing two opposite spikes of white flowers exhaling a most suave odor. Flowers lack both calyx and corolla, and are formed of oval, whole, distichous bracts, fused with the axis, accrescent, in the axil of each of which are about twenty stamens and 5 or 6 pistils. The fruit is pear-shaped, unilocular and contains 1 to 3 seeds. "This curious aquatic plant could be naturalized in our ditches, marshes and lakes (Australia), in consideration of its edible tubers. The part of the plant that bears the fragrant flowers provides a Spinach." (Mueller, Select extratropical plants.) Aponogeton distachyum is natur alized in Hérault and Finistère, and could be in most of France. Protected by 1 or 2 meters of water, it doesn't suffer from the Fig. 6 - Rhizome of Aponogeton distachyum. cold and even flowers in the middle of winter in the départements just mentioned. Delile, director of the Jardin des Plantes de Montpellier, introduced it to the region a long time ago, but despite its merit, and with the exception of amateur gardens, it has not spread in the region and has remained confined to the Lez, the main river in the area. It has lived there for over fifty years, and the late. M. Planchon collected it there in 1844, making it the subject of his first botanical work (1). In the Lez, Aponogeton is very rarely found (1) Annales des sciences naturelles, IIle série, t. I, p. 109 et seq. downstream from its original locality; it does, however, bear fruit, but its seeds, if not carried to the sea, fall and germinate on the spot. "The plant has long been naturalized around Brest. Thirty years ago, it was much more common here than it is today. It had multiplied so much in certain streams that it prevented the water from flowing, which led to its destruction in several places, and most notably in the Kérolan stream, from which it has completely disappeared. It was one of the curiosities of Brest, beginning to bloom around Christmas and continuing until July. "It has been said, but we repeat it with reservations, that the Aponogeton distachyum was brought from the Cape, around 1840, by Rear-Admiral des Rotours, owner of the Kérolan stream; but it is also claimed that it came from Toulon to the Brest Botanical Garden, around 1857, brought by workmen who put it in the Kérolan stream. Mr. Blanchard, head of the Botanical Garden at the Brest Maritime Hospital, to whom we owe the above information, goes on to say in his letter to us about the Aponogeton: "In any case, the Kérolan stream is the first place where it was acclimatized. From there, it was carried to a pond at Maison-Blanche, where it is very abundant and from which the tubers I am sending you have sprouted. It was also taken to Poulaz-Ferenten, where it is also quite common; these three localities are in the commune of Lambézellec; I know of no other for the moment. "One of my workers had planted it in the Gouesnon river, near a mill; it must have been uprooted, as I have passed through this locality several times and have noticed no trace of it. "I haven't eaten any of its tubers, and I don't know of anyone who has taken a fancy to them; I've seen it in some damp meadows around the Kérolan stream, and I don't think cattle touch its leaves, but I'm not sure. "I must also tell you that it doesn't grow everywhere, because I've planted it in streams other than those I've mentioned, and I've never noticed a single foot of it; at the Garden, it doesn't even grow very well in our ponds. It's not the cold that bothers it, it's the water, which probably doesn't suit it. "One of my Bretons remarked that it prevented other aquatic plants from growing in its vicinity; thus, Potamogeton, Callitriche and Ranunculus would generally be suffocated by its presence; but I wouldn't certify the fact, as I've never noticed it. In any case, it's a beautiful plant in countries where it can live in the open air, because it flowers almost all year round, and it's especially in winter that it has merit, because it blooms abundantly and spreads a delicious scent in the places where it's grown." Wishing to experiment with various edible aquatic plants, we had a hole dug and filled with water, which is large enough to hold those that accept our climate. The Aponogeton blooms there from April to July with extraordinary abundance and perfumes the air around it. We're convinced that, if we don't put a stop to it, it will invade all the space we have and make life impossible for any other plant. How is it not yet introduced into all lakes, ponds or basins deep enough for frost not to reach it, in all streams whose current is not too fast? This can only be explained by routine and carelessness on the part of the owners. We do not propose to prepare its flowering stems as spinach, although, according to Baron F. von Mueller, this can be done; we do not even believe that its rhizomes can feature on our tables. Their fine, white substance is too compact, making them difficult to deglaze, and they borrow an unpleasant, muddy flavor from the soil in which they grow. They should nevertheless be considered edible and could undoubtedly be used in starch factories, as Nelumbium and Sagittaria roots are in China. In any case, we recommend the plant for the beauty, abundance, duration and fragrance of its flowers. In the Revue horticole, 1876, page 330, M. Carrière suggested growing this plant terrestrally, in pots and greenhouses, during the winter. The abundance and fragrance of its flowers would be of great merit, especially for the bouquet trade. M. Carrière has obtained excellent results. We don't think his example has been followed. The editor of the Revue Horticole goes on to say: "Another quality lies in the tubers of Aponegeton, which contain very large quantities of a brilliantly white starch that makes them edible and means that, cooked and cut into pieces and reduced to a paste, poultry and even pigs are very fond of them." Whatever the merits of Aponogeton distachyum, it would be desirable to experiment with the cultivation of several species belonging to the same genus; but we lack information on these species, so we will speak of them very briefly; we will even limit ourselves, for some of them, to giving their names and their homeland: Aponogeton monostachyum L. f. (Roxb. Flor. ind., vol. Il, p. 210), India. Nama is the name of the plant and its root is called Nama dumpa. It grows naturally in fresh, stagnant, shallow water: it appears and flowers during the rainy season. The natives are passionate about its roots, which are almost as good as potatoes. A. echinatum (Roxb., loc. cit., p. 210) Found, with the previous species, in shallow, fresh water. A. undulatum (Roxb., loc. cit., p. 211). Native to Bengal, with tuberous, edible roots. A. microphyllum (Roxb., loc. cit., p. 211). Tuberous, edible roots. A. crispum Thunb. (Mueller, Select extratropical plants, p. 30). From India and New South Wales. The roots of this aquatic plant are starchy and very tasty. A. angustifolium Ait. Cape of Good Hope. A. crinifolium Lehm, Cape of Good Hope. A. desertorum Zeyh, Cape of Good Hope. A. junceum Lehm, Cape of Good Hope. A. zosterxfolium Schrard, Cape of Good Hope. A plant related to Aponogeton, OUviranpra reNESTRALIS Poiret, which grows in mountain streams in Madagascar, also produces edible tubers. R. P. Camboué sent us a shipment of these under the name Ovirandrana (Ovy, tuber, Randrana, branch, rope-like braid). Ouvirandra is very difficult to grow, even in a hot greenhouse. This plant has extremely curious, windowed leaves; as the veins are not filled with parenchyma, they resemble lace. De Flacourt, in his Histoire de la grande isle de Madagascar, says: Ouvirandre, small roots as big as octopus, very good to eat. Found in ponds. ARACHIDE Ground pistachio. ARACHIS HYPOGÆA L. ; Trew, Plant. rar. tab. 3, fig. 3; Rumph, Amb. 5, tab. 156; Turpin, Dict. des se. nat. 254, 256. Leguminosae family. Annual plant with fibrous roots, usually giving several stems bristling with soft hairs; leaves alternate, petiolate, with two pairs of obovate leaflets, entire, obtuse; stipules adnate to the petiole; flowers small, yellow, axillary, sessile, usually geminate. After fertilization, the young fruit curls towards the ground, where it sinks to mature. The fruit is an oblong, cylindrical, strangulated pod containing 2-4 seeds, criss-crossed in all directions by veins arranged in a network. The plant is said to be native to equatorial America; it is generally grown in India, Senegal and the southern United States, as well as in certain parts of South America. The largest production areas are on the west coast of Africa and in India. The flavor of the fresh peanut seed is reminiscent of that of the green bean; it is an excellent food containing around 30 0/0 of nitrogenous matter; roasted, it has the flavor of hazelnuts. The seed contains around 45 to 50 0/0 of oil, which can be extracted Fig. 7 - Groundnut or Pistachio (Arachis hypogæa). by simple expression with or without the intervention of heat. Peanut oil, when extracted cold, is edible, almost colorless, with a pleasant taste and odor; it is said to be commonly substituted for olive oil in the trade. When this oil is obtained by the intervention of heat, it takes on a dark color and an unpleasant taste and smell; it is then 3 unfit for consumption and is used either for lighting or for making soaps, after having been treated with alkaline solutions of potash and soda which saponify it. The oilcake is used as animal feed and is an excellent fertilizer. As the oil in shelled peanuts goes rancid fairly quickly, European manufacturers usually ship the fruit in the shell, which keeps longer without losing its quality. In the past, Sénégambie alone produced almost all the groundnuts sold in the market, and thus made substantial profits, as the selling price remained relatively high. Indian growers in turn began to cultivate the precious legume, and as labor was plentiful and cheap in India, competition developed to such an extent that our African colony gradually lost its monopoly on this crop. Pondicherry used to be the port of export for peanuts from India, but the Pondicherry trade is now threatened by the expansion of peanut cultivation in English possessions. In 1876, Marseilles received only 11,000 tons of groundnuts from India, compared with nearly 44,000 from Africa. Today, imports of this legume into France are estimated at over 150 million kilograms, of which 70 million kilograms come from Senegambia. The main import ports in France are Marseille, Bordeaux and Dunkirk. Peanuts must be grown in light, loose, sandy soil, into which the young fruit can easily penetrate. In Senegal and India, seedlings are sown in July-August. Harvesting takes place 5 to 7 months later. Production is 2,000 kilograms per hectare on fertile soils. There are two main varieties of groundnut in Senegal: A. de Galam, which is small, has a pleasant flavor and yields superior quality oil; and A. Cayor, which is larger, but yields less and is of inferior quality. We don't need to dwell on this subject any further, but we do recommend the cultivation of the Ground Pistachio to enthusiasts in our southern departments, as it will provide them with a pleasant-tasting kernel and the most singular spectacle of fruiting. We have grown the plant in the Landes department. We knew that trials had been carried out there during the First Empire. We only sowed a garden bed made of fine sand, blackened by heather and fern detritus. Vegetation grew steadily, but without much development. We did, however, have the pleasure of harvesting a small crop. ARRACACIA ARRACACIA XANTHORRHIZA Bauer (I). Arracacha esculenta DC; Conium Arracacha, Exot. fl., tab. 152; Bot. Mag. tab. 3092. Family Ombeltiferes. Very fleshy roots; stem 60 to 90 cm long, herbaceous, sparsely branched, ridged, glaucous, lined with (1) In Central America, Arracacia are famous for their edible roots, mainly those of A. xanthorrhiza, which are eaten in Colombia, boiled, like our Potatoes, and some leaves smaller than the radicals. Radical leaves long-stalked, from Om, 40 to Om, 50 long, irregular, biternate, segmented Fig. 8 - Arracacia (Arracacia xanthorrhiza Bauer). oval, irregularly three-lobed, acuminate, coarsely incised-toothed, glabrous and dark green; in July-October, dark purple or yellowish flowers, used to extract an analeptic starch, compared to Arrowroot, and to make a fermented liquor said to be stomachic. A, moschata is used for the same purpose in Mexico (H. Baillon, Histoire des Plantes, vol. VII, p. 193). arranged in few, slightly concave umbels. We don't think we should overlook this precious plant, which has so far rebelled against any attempt at cultivation in our climate. We were no happier than our predecessors in the trial we carried out. A crate of Arracacia tubers was brought to us from Venezuela by a kind friend. Dr. Ernst, Professor of Natural History in Caracas, had presided over the selection and packaging of the roots. We shared the contents of this crate, which was in the best possible condition, with the Museum. We planted our tubers in the first fortnight of June, on a bed and under a ventilated frame. Vegetation took off admirably, and the stems grew rapidly. By August, they were full of flower buds, but these failed to open and the stems broke at half-height. When we lifted the plants in October, their primitive tubers were rotting away, and no new ones had formed. Our failure was complete. We won't dwell on the undisputed merits of a plant that has thwarted every attempt at cultivation. We were unable to obtain seeds, which we requested from the countries of origin without obtaining any. Would sowing have given good results? It's doubtful. In any case, the trials that have so far failed will undoubtedly be repeated, and this forecast commits us to indicating to our successors the sources from which they can draw all the desirable information. We wish them every success in their future endeavours. Publications to consult: Bulletin de la Société centrale d'Horticulture de France, vol. V, p. 251, 1829. ASPERGE TUBÉREUSE ASPaRaGuUs LuciDus Lindl, Bot. reg. 1844, misc. no. 36; Asa Gray, Bot. jap. 403; Miq, Prol. 315. In Japanese: Tomo Roki; Ten mado, according to Miquel; Te mon dó, according to X Fam. des Liliacées. Climbing plant, sarmentose, about 1*, 50 high; branches with small, whitish, scale-like leaves, from the axils of which arise fertile branches or green ramuscules (cladodes), usually mistaken for leaves; these ramuscules are solitary, linear, falcate, shiny green; peduncles uniflorous. "This plant is close to A. falcatus, from which it differs in having smaller solitary leaves and flowers that do not form clusters. (Lindl., loc. cit.) " In this Asparagus, it is the roots and not the shoots that are food." (Miquel, loc. cit.) A few years ago, tuberous asparagus was brought to our attention by Mr. Eugène Simon, former French consul, who had eaten its tubers and sent a plant to the Muséum. After a five-year wait, we finally received it from Japan. Left unprotected, it survived the winter of 1882-1883 well. We would have been wiser, however, to cover her with a bit of bedding. So far, it has only given us very small tubers, the shape and volume of Olive. When tasted, these tubers are neither floury nor free of bitterness. At the same time as the previous species, we received from Japan a curious form of Asparagus trichophyllus Bunge. This plant is very dwarfed, barely reaching Om, 15 in height; it grows in compact clumps and, like A. lucidu, produces fleshy claws. These asparagus may be considered as food plants in China and Japan, but they will probably have little success here. Another species of asparagus, Asparagus ascendens Hamilt. native to India, produces tuberous roots from which we make a kind of salep called White Musli. Mixed with water, this Musli makes a pleasant, starch-free mucilage much appreciated by the natives. BAMBOO COMESTIBLES In a book entitled Étude pratique du commerce d'exportation de la Chine, published in 1848, Mr. Natalis Rondot says, in the article Bamboo: "The uses of Bamboo are so numerous in China, the services it renders are so great, that it justly deserves the name of national tree". After listing the uses of the various parts of the plant, he adds: "Its young shoots, tender and delicate, are a vegetable that can be eaten in a variety of ways, and are said to be as good as Asparagus. Boiled, seasoned and candied, they produce excellent preserves, so sought-after that they form a fairly important branch of the domestic trade and are shipped in large quantities to various parts of the empire, especially to the capital, where they feature at the banquets of the great. Buddha's priests, who take a vow of abstinence and adhere to a low-nutrition, plant-based diet, have found in this delicacy a resource equal to that offered by fish to our clergy". All travelers who have visited China and Japan cite the many applications of this precious plant. R. Fortune concludes the chapter he devoted to Bamboo in his Voyage agricole et horticole en Chine with these words: "Whatever incredulity this long enumeration may find in the reader, I will add that I am far from having cited all the uses of Bamboo in China, and, in truth, it would be almost as difficult to say what it is not good for as to indicate all the uses it is given; it is in demand everywhere and for everything; it is useful to the Chinese throughout their lives and does not even abandon them at their death, since it is used to shade their graves. " According to Dr. Mène (1), the species of bamboo most commonly used in Japan for food are Mo-só, Madake, Ofetchiku, Hatchiku and Metake. "The Japanese call young bamboo shoots Take-noko. They eat them cooked; at other times, they preserve them in brine or vinegar, some whole, others cut in their longitudinal half; they resemble medium-sized asparagus, remarkable for their knots spaced 1 to 2 centimetres apart. The Chinese also subject them to desiccation, forming bundles which are shipped in large quantities to Manchuria and Mongolia; they simply need to be immersed in hot water to return to their normal size, without losing any of their taste. "In India, bamboo shoots are eaten in salads, purées and fried. Dr. H..., to whose kindness we have had recourse on so many occasions, provided us with the following information on the use of edible Bamboos in Japan: "After planting, it takes at least three years before you have shoots worth eating. We never cut off all the stems, but we thin out the old stems in winter, always removing the weakest ones, and we take half or three-quarters of the young shoots as vegetables, sparing the larger ones to fortify the plant, which is smoked abundantly every year on the surface, preferably with horse manure. "In autumn, we cover the plants with a certain thickness of dry leaves or litter. "I don't think there are many Europeans who have tried bamboo shoots as much as I have. I ate them two or three times a week for four months every year, for three years. I'll tell you how they're used in Japan. "In April, we eat Mô-so-také shoots. This is the earliest and produces the largest shoots; they are often 12 or 15 centimetres in diameter and often weigh 2 kilograms. In May, when Mô-só shoots become too hard, Ha-tsikou and Go-santsikou are eaten. These much smaller shoots are also very good. In June and July, we eat Ma-také, which is the latest variety; its shoots have the great disadvantage of being quite bitter. They can only be eaten after blanching in two or three waters. Apart from this, preparation is the same for all of them: they are stripped of their tough, spathiform sheaths, and cut circularly at the point of insertion. If the shoot is advanced, the top is removed, which is unpalatable; the rest has a consistency similar to artichoke stock or cardoon root. Cut into thin slices, blanch and serve with a sauce made from Cho-you and grated Katsoud́ (dried meat from a fish of the same name, which is close to tuna). "This is how I ate them, and I enjoyed them; but, for the taste of Europeans who are not used to doing without fat in their sauces, it would be better to accommodate them, like Cardoon or Celery, with butter and meat juice. "In Japan, it is said that Bamboos grain once every fifty years, and that there is a shortage of Rice that year. It's likely that they grain only in very hot, very dry years. "Unfortunately, the fourth, which is the most beautiful and the best species as a food plant, Mo-sô-také, does not grow here; it is not warm enough during the summer; in other words, it would grow even less in the vicinity of Paris, or at least no better. "The only one whose botanical name I know: Phyllostachys aurea, is the one called Go-san tsikou (Bamboo from the land of Go). It is also called Hotei tsikou, Zin-men tsikou (Man-faced bamboo). It is grown in China as far north as the Great Wall, where the thermometer falls to -20° every winter. It was highly recommended to me in Japan for its resistance to the cold. In fact, it's the hardiest of the big bamboos and the least difficult to work with in terms of terrain. It's very good to eat and produces excellent quality wood. Its faults are that it has a stiff habit and doesn't grow very big. "Ha tsikou has a very attractive half-full growth habit. It is a little less hardy to the cold than its predecessor and more demanding in terms of terrain. It is very good to eat. It was imported from China to Japan. "Ma-daké is the only large bamboo found in the wild in the mountains of Japan. It is very robust, reaching 25 or 30 meters in height and 10 or 12 centimeters in diameter. It is sometimes eaten in Japan, but is bitter and inferior to other vegetables. The bamboo commonly known as Ma-daké is Phyllostachys mitis (Bambusa mitis, B. edulis); as for Mo-só, it is probably B. macroculmis A. Rivière. As Dr. H. Rivière so aptly put it, the latter species is not cultivable in our climate, but it does very well in Algeria, and is the one that produces the largest clumps and strongest stems, rapidly reaching 15 to 25 meters in height and 15 to 20 centimeters in diameter. Messrs Auguste and Charles Rivière, in an extensive work published in 1878 (1), claim to have tried eating young bamboo shoots prepared in various ways. They claim to have obtained only mediocre results, but add that they had not subjected them to any preliminary preparation (2), which, according to Dr. H..., is essential. In the Paris climate, Phyllostachys aurea and mitis withstand the cold perfectly, but their shoots are too spindly, and we have to confess that, to date, we haven't found it possible to use them. For further information, read, in addition to the works cited above: Verdier de Latour, Études sur les Bambous, Algiers, 1854. Dr. J. Vidal, Notes sur les usages des Bambous (Bulletin de la Société nationale d'Acclimatation, 1874, p. 743). Munro, Transanctions of the Linnean Society, XXVI. (1) Les Bambous (Bulletin de la Société nationale d'Acclimatation). (2) Page 606. Edible burdock. - Gobc. LAPPA MAJOR Gærtn. var. EDULis L.; L. edulis Sieb. 45 Fam. Compositae. Biennial plant, 2 to 2.50 m high. Involucre glabrous, with scales scarcely serrated at the base, the rest perfectly smooth; the inner ones mute, radiating; flower heads arranged in corymbs. In July-August, purple flowers. Fusiform roots, gray on the outside, white on the inside, 40 to 45 centimeters long after four months of vegetation. Gobô du Japon is considered, not as a species, but as a simple variety of the great burdock, Lappa major, a common plant throughout France, where, according to Duchesne, it is called Bouillon noir, Gloutteron, Grippe, etc., depending on the place. The same author says that in Scotland, its roots, leaves and young shoots are eaten. It is likely that only the indigent use it. Here's what Poiret has to say about it in his Flore médicale: "Whenever I taste burdock root, I'm astonished not to find it more often in kitchens than in pharmacies. It can be prepared in the same way as Scorsonera, while the young shoots, picked in spring, can be eaten like artichokes, cardoons and asparagus. In any case, the Japanese have a variety that is superior to the common burdock and quite worthy of our attention. Gobô is generally cultivated in Japan, and its seeds were included in all the shipments we received from that country. In the last collection we received were three packets of these seeds, one labelled: Gobó, tout court; another: Hockate Gobó; a third: Umeda Gobô. The latter was said to be taller or larger than the Fig. 9 - Japanese burdock (Lappa major Gærtn., var. edulis). other two, and its cultivation did not justify this indication. We have to say that, except for the height of the stems, we recognized no difference between the three varieties; the stems of Umeda Gobô rose less high than those of the others. We presented roots of Japanese burdock to the Société nationale d'Horticulture de France on August 26, 1880. These roots had been sown the previous May 10, and were ready for consumption; but we don't recommend imitating us and sowing in spring. Summer drought hardens the roots, and besides, this kind of vegetable plant is less useful in the season when fresh vegetables abound. We therefore suggest sowing on July 15, in rows 20 centimetres apart, and thinning out the sowing so that the plants enjoy this 20-centimetre space in all directions. It's a good idea to sow in soil that has been broken up to two spade-irons deep, so that the roots can penetrate easily and don't branch out too much. Numerous bifurcations or ramifications of the roots are, in fact, a serious disadvantage for market sales. Sometimes, a real bundle of roots descends from the collar of the plant, too small to be sold. This damage must be avoided as far as possible, and the most effective means are to break up the soil, sow late and, in dry weather, water to stimulate vegetation. After one hundred to one hundred and twenty days, the roots are as big and longer than the best-sown salsify. As winter approaches, vegetation comes to a halt; the leaves, which are still healthy, are fed to the rabbits, who are greedy for them; the harvest begins and continues despite the frosts, if the plantalion is covered with litter. The preparation of Gobô for the table is the same as for Salsify or Scorzonera. It would be a good idea, however, to cook the roots of the Japanese plant in two waters, to remove the slight wild flavour that some people reproach them with; but how can we get cooks to start cooking in the first water and finish in the second? We doubt we'll succeed. Two notes on Japanese burdock, published by Dr. Sacc and Mr. J. Dybowski (1), are of interest. The latter presents Gobô only from the point of view of vegetable cultivation, which is our own. Dr. Sacc proposes it to farmers as a forage plant. We do believe that it could be useful as a forage plant, but it's not our job to deal with that question. As a vegetable, Gobô seems to us inferior to Salsify and Scorsonera; it cannot, to any degree, stand comparison with Spanish Scolyma; but its rapid vegetation, the length of its roots which enables it to feed without exhausting the soil, its resistance to cold and drought, the date at which it is sown which makes it, within a fortnight, a catch crop like Turnip, do not allow it to be neglected. bablement utilisé. Dybowski (Journal de la Soc. nat. d'Horticulture de France, 3e série, t. Ill, p. 770). BASELLE BLANCHE Chinese spinach.
