BASELla ALBA L., Spec. p. 390, no. 2; Wight, Icon. tab. 896; Gandola alba Rumph, Herb. Amb. 5, p. 417; Plukn, Phyt. t. 63, fig. 1. family Salsolaceae. A climbing plant with very succulent stems and leaves used as a vegetable in the manner of Spinach, cultivated by the natives and lining their homes in all parts of India, but scarcely accepted as a vegetable by Europeans (Thomas A.-C. Firminger). Its Hindostani name is Poi. White Baselle may be considered a variety of Red B.. Its leaves and stems are consistently greenish, rather than purple-red like those of B. rubra. In terms of cultivation and use, it differs in no way from the latter. BASELLE ROUGE Malabar spinach, Brède d'Angola, Brède Gandole. BASELLA RUBRA L., Spec. plant. p. 390, no. 1; Gandola rubra Rumph, Herb. Amb. 5, p. 417, t. 154, fig. 2; Basel 'a rubra Lam. III, t. 215, fig. 1. Annual or biennial plant. Stems spindly, fleshy, twiggy, crimson-red, rising to about 4 ft. high, rolling around the plants around them. These stems have alternate, oval, entire, slightly acuminate, thick, fleshy leaves, purple-red like the stems and borne on a short, thick petiole. The flowers are small, reddish or light purple and arranged in axillary spikes on peduncles longer than the leaves. This plant is native to the East Indies. In the 8 Fig. 10 - Red Basil (Basella rubra L.). Indies and America, it is cultivated in gardens and the leaves are eaten cooked (Brèdes), seasoned in various ways, and especially spiced and spiced, which is then called Calalou-Baselle. Each plant can provide three good dishes in the course of a summer. All Baselles, except the white one, are more or less red, and their berries, like those of Phytolacca, provide a very beautiful purple juice, which we have not yet been able to determine (Descourtilz, Flore des Antilles). We have cultivated Baselle rouge. We sowed it on a warm bed under a frame, and we grew it in a greenhouse.

planted in the open ground towards the end of May. As the walls of the kitchen garden are to be used exclusively for vines and fruit trees, Baselle cannot be used there. We trained it on a coarse, very wide-mesh trellis, raised on stakes with a southern exposure, and, under these conditions, it developed well and ripened its seeds well. The need to trellis Baselle does not allow market gardeners to grow it, but it can be grown in the kitchen garden. It's quite a good vegetable. Its thick leaves can be picked all summer long, once the spinach it is intended to replace has all but disappeared from gardens. CORE LEAF BASELLE BASella CORDIFOLIA Lamk, Dict. 1, p. 382, no. 3, B. crassifolia Wight. This species differs from B. rubra and B. alba in the shape of its leaves, which are large, almost rounded and heart-shaped at the base. It is grown in gardens in the Malabar region. It is fleshy and succulent, with a flavor comparable to that of Poirée, but a little inferior; it is laxative and not very nourishing. Its leaves are eaten cooked and mixed with Brède (prickly pigweed), in much the same way as we eat our spinach (Lamk., loc. cit.). Messrs Vilmorin-Andrieux and Cle, in their work Les Plantes potagères, p. 32, refer to this plant as Baselle de Chine à très larges feuilles, and say that it was imported from China in 1839 by Captain Geoffroy. According to the same authors, "this plant would certainly be preferable to other species of Baselles because of the breadth of its leaves and the abundance of its product. However, cultivation does not seem to have spread, probably because of the difficulty of germinating it in France. MM. Vilmorin-Andrieux et Cie, in the List of Novelties they put on the market in January 1897, add that "this Baselle is invaluable as a substitute for Spinach in places where production of this excellent vegetable is almost impossible in summer due to drought. Sown in April on a bed and transplanted in May, preferably at the foot of a wall facing south, just a few plants are enough to obtain, with a little support and a few waterings, an ample supply of leaves that will renew themselves throughout the summer". Dr. Trabut, government botanist in Algiers, has cultivated Basella cordifolia in our colony, and the results he has obtained show that apart from intertropical regions, where the plant has its marked place, it can be of great interest in warm temperate regions. In Algeria, the plant is very vigorous, bearing fruit at the end of summer; "its broad, succulent leaves cook to a pulp that is less dry than that of spinach. Combined with a little sorrel, Baselle is a vegetable that pleases everyone. In the note he published on this plant in the Journal de la Société nationale d'Horticulture de France, 1896, p. 145, Dr. Trabut adds that Baselle should be raked. This makes harvesting easier. In the Algiers Hospital garden, over 350 kilograms of leaves were harvested from a 50-meter square in the summer of 1895. Baselle thrives in oasis gardens. In Biskra, it has given very good results. Dr Trabut recommends successive sowing from April onwards. By sowing fortnightly, so as to keep the plants young, and giving them sufficient water, you'll obtain very vigorous plants that will produce truly extraordinary results. BENINCASA CERIFERA Savi; Cucurbita cerifera Fischer. Cucurbitaceae family. Annual plant, native to India and China. Stems about 2 meters long; leaves cordate, with five acute, crenate lobes; simple tendrils; in May-July, yellow flowers, large, pedunculated, axillary, solitary; fruit green, hanging. India, 1827. We have grown Bénincasa often and have not experienced any failures; but we have grown it under a frame and only exposed it to the air at the end of summer, when the fruit was already large. We think it's wise to do so. The Bénincasa cérifère is a delicate, light food which, prepared like the Cucumber, is, in our opinion, much preferable to it. The authors who wrote about it before us express the same opinion. M. Naudin, in Annales des sciences naturelles, 4° série, tome XII, cahier n° 2, page 9, tells us that B. cerifera is one of the classic and most esteemed vegetables of Austro-East Asia, and particularly China. He cites Loureiro, who, of all the Fig. 11 - Benincasa cerifera Savi). squashes, knows of none more wholesome or suitable as food. Its introduction into Europe, says Mr. Naudin, dates back nearly half a century, yet it is scarcely known outside a few botanical gardens. The volume of its fruit, which is that of a small pumpkin, sometimes much larger, its easy preservation for several months and sometimes even for a whole year, the excellence of its flesh and, finally, the ease with which it can be cultivated, should long ago have led to its inclusion in vegetable gardens. It's hard to understand why it has been forgotten. This fruit is remarkable for the abundant waxy, powdery exudation it covers when ripe, and which continues long after it has been picked. So far," says Mr. Naudin, "I know of only two varieties of Bénincasa, and they differ very little from one another. The first, found in our botanical gardens, is recognizable by its cylindrical fruit, 0.25 to 0.40 m long. The finest sample I have seen was sent from Algiers to the 1855 Universal Exhibition; it measured almost Om.60 long by Om.20 to Om.25 thick. The second variety, recently arrived from China, differs in that its fruits are simply ovoid, shorter and at the same time larger. The secretion may also be less abundant. MM. J. Monnier et Cie, in their book entitled: Les Plantes potagères, say that the Bénincasa remained forgotten for a long time, but that reintroduced a few years ago by the Société d'Acclimatation, it seems to have regained, if not some vogue, at least the place it deserves among vegetable plants... It has many similarities with the cucumber and is preferred to the latter by many enthusiasts because of its lighter flesh and less pronounced flavor. According to Thomas A.-C. Firminger, the Benincasa is a very large and beautiful ovoid squash (1), which appears to be covered with a pale greenish-white waxy flower. He doesn't know if Europeans eat much of it, but the natives eat a lot. It has a very pleasant effect on thatched cottages, where it can often be seen in the most exposed and least protected position. When I wanted to know (1) This is undoubtedly the Chinese variety, he says, whether its fruit was not in danger of being frequently stolen, I was assured that other fruits in the same situation would be quite likely to be stolen, but that the Benincasa enjoyed a special respect due to certain religious considerations which made it perfectly safe. In Bengal, its seeds are sown during the rains and the vegetable is eaten in the cold season. The Bénincasa is, in our opinion, worthy of the attention of connoisseurs. On October 26 1882, speaking to the Société nationale d'Horticulture, we said (1): Bénincasa fruits, knotted under glass, are always very beautiful, whereas those that receive rain and are subject to atmospheric influences are always stained and deformed. There can be no doubt, therefore, that the plant is best cultivated in the Melon region. Besides, it could only be an amateur plant here. Only three fruits can be obtained under a panel, and their market value cannot match that of Cantaloupes, which would have occupied the same space. Benincasa should therefore be cultivated beyond the Loire. Its fruit keeps well throughout the winter, travels well and could be sold in Paris at a moderate and remunerative price. In 1888, Mr. Charles Naudin sent us a charming little variety from India, roughly spherical in shape; its drawback is that it yields very little edible flesh. (1) Journal de la Société nationale d'Horticulture de France, 3e série, 1882, t. IV, p. 720. LEOPOLDIA HOLZMANNI, Heldr. 57 Family Liliaceae. M. de Heldreich, in a work entitled Les Plantes utiles de la Grèce, shows the importance of spontaneous edible plants in the diet of the Greek people. "The numerous and rigorous fasts of the Greek Orthodox Church, during which neither meat nor anything animal, such as milk, butter, cheese, eggs, etc., nor fish on certain days, nor even oil, are allowed to be eaten, force the Greek people to often resort to a purely vegetable diet, which among them, perhaps more than among any other people, plays the principal role in the household. "In summer and autumn, fruits of all kinds, especially those of the Cucurbitaceae; in winter and spring, vegetables and wild herbs are the dominant foods. However, Olives, Onions and Garlic are, in fact, the mainstay of the frugal meals of the working class and thrifty country folk in all seasons. As it is these two classes who observe fasts most rigorously, and as vegetable gardening is found only in the gardens around the big cities and is almost entirely unknown in the interior of the country, spontaneous plants above all serve as food. "In winter and spring, especially on Sundays and feast days, it's a general occupation for women and children to gather and pluck these plants from wheat and fallow land, when they are still young and tender. Cooked in water, or more often simply scalded and prepared with lemon juice, salt and oil, as in the old days, and without the latter on days of great fasting, they form a real foodstuff on the frugal peasant's table, not just a simple meal. "Usually, the dish consists of a mixture of several kinds of these herbs, or just one kind, depending on the circumstances. "In addition, many other herbs are eaten raw, in salads or even unseasoned. Leopoldia Holzmanni is said to be highly prized by the Greek people. This plant can hardly be distinguished botanically from Muscari comosum, the weed of our fields; it has only been cultivated by our gardener out of obedience and with great repugnance. Last winter, M. de Heldreich gave us the following information on this unusual plant: "You have seen, in my little monograph on the genus Leopoldia (Bellevalia), that in Greece we have a fairly large number of species, all of which have more or less affinity with the Bellevalia comosa (Hyacinthus comosus L), which is very common in Europe and with which you are very familiar. The most common species in the Greek flora is L. Holzmanni HELDR. but the bulbs of all the species are very similar and the people do not distinguish between them; they collectively call them Bolboi or Borboi (plural of Bolbos), and this is surely the Bolbos edódimos, gnórimos pasin, ou esdiomen, eustomachus enkoileos purros, etc., of Dioscorides (Mat, med., Leopoldia is nowhere cultivated, but the various species, and especially L. Holzmanni, are so abundant on dry hills, in seedbeds and elsewhere, that large quantities of bulbs are easily collected and taken to market to be sold (in Athens at 20 centimes per ogat, weighing around 1,250 grams, or 26 centimes per kilogram). "It's a highly esteemed food, considered (as Dioscorides already said) to be very healthy and excellent for the stomach. "They are eaten in spring (February-March), especially during the Great Lent before Easter. As for preparation, it's quite simple. Once the bulbs have been cleaned of their outer scales, they are boiled in water. After a good cooking, the water is drained off and the bulbs are placed in vinegar with a certain amount of salt. This preserves them for quite a long time, but they are ready to eat after just 24 hours. Once you've removed as much of the vinegar as you want to eat, you can season them with a little olive oil and lemon juice (ad libitum). They are eaten with bread during Lent, or as a sort of hors d'oeuvre with meat. The taste is always a little bitter, but quite pleasant. "As for cultivation, I've said that they're not grown here. They're found wild in large quantities." Keenly interested in such precise information, we asked Messrs. de Vilmorin to bring us a fairly large quantity of L. Holzmanni bulbs, and these gentlemen requested them from Dr. Gennadius, director of the Botanical Garden of Athens, whose kindness was well known to them. He hastened to send them, with this note: "Leopoldia Holzmanni is considered a very stomachic food. After cleaning the bulbs, they are soaked in water for a few hours, then boiled, changing the water once or twice to remove their bitterness, and eaten as a salad." Given a good supply of bulbs, we planted most of them. They didn't give any sign of life the year they were planted, because their vegetation had begun in Greece. In 1890, the plant grew well, but our gardener, in his horror of weeds, removed many of them. Tasting Leopoldia Holzmanni has not been kind to him either. It's too bitter. Perhaps we didn't prepare it with due care. It is certain that the bulbs of this plant are of great service to the Greek people. We could try using its congeners, Muscari comosum, so abundant in our harvests. It would be nice to get rid of an enemy by eating it. BOUSSINGAULTIA BASELLOIDES Kunth. Family Salsolaceae. Subterranean apparatus highly developed. Roots few in number, fibrous and fleshy. Tubers abundant, agglomerated and branching, elongated, irregular, with a number of more or less elongated underground buds throughout, giving them an irregular shape, and bristling with large spikes. Externally, they are an earthy gray-black, while internally they are homogeneous, with a very white tissue, abundant in thick, stringy mucilage. Stems are long and stalky, reaching up to 10 meters in length; they are loose, spindly, leafy and supported only by a support; lying on the ground, they root easily; leaves are abundant and fleshy. The plant flowers but does not bear fruit, however well exposed it may be. Boussingaultie is native to Mexico and Chile. This plant may be of some use in ornamental gardens, but we don't need to concern ourselves with it in that respect. We have tasted its leaves, prepared like spinach; they are barely edible. The tubers are no better. According to Mérat, their slimy nature and unpleasant flavor make them unsuitable for use as food (1). According to Philippar, they have a very bland taste and are so abundant in mucilage, even after cooking, that it is impossible to eat them (2). According to Louis Tellière, when Boussingaultia baselloides was introduced into our country, it was thought that its leaves could be eaten like spinach and its tubers like potatoes; but it was soon recognized that neither (1) Notice sur les tubercules proposés pour remplacer la Pomme de terre. Librairie agricole de la Maison rustique, rue Jacob, 26. (2) Notice descriptive, culturale et économique sur deux plantes tuberculeuses Bull. de la Soc. d'Agriculture, 2e série, t. IV, p. 425, 1848). others could not serve as food, the leaves being very safe and fat, and the tubers being soft and slimy. I have eaten," he says, "both leaves and tubers, and I confess it is very bad (1). These testimonies are sufficient proof that Boussingaultie has no right to any place in the vegetable garden, and for this very reason, it provides us with a subject for meditation. How is it that, to replace the potato, plants of no value whatsoever have been proposed? How could anyone believe for a moment that Dahlia, Apios, Boussingaultie, Picotiane, Olluco, Poire de terre Cochet, could replace the Potato? Some of these plants refuse to be grown at all, while others are inedible. Are researchers of new plants invincibly prone to all kinds of illusions? Following their example, we promise ourselves not to give in to the same temptations as our predecessors. Can we? CAMASSIE COMESTIBLE CAMAssia ESCUlEnTa Lindl; (Anthericum esculentum Spr.; Phalangium esculentum Nutt.; Phalangium Quamash Pursh; Scilla esculenta Sims.). - Greenhouse flora, 3, 275; Botanical Register, t. 1486; Bot. Mag. t. 1574 and 2774. Family Liliaceae. This plant takes its generic name from the American name Quamash or Camash, which the type species bears in its native country. (1) Boussingaultia baselloides (Revue horticole, 1869, p. 419). Bulb medium, ovoid, smooth, brown; leaves linear, gutter-folded, attractive green; spike 3 to 5 decimeters long, erect, bearing numerous flowers in elongated clusters, accompanied by a scarlet bract; flowers purplish-blue in one variety, white in another. Perianth with six lanceolate linear divisions; ovary rounded, triquetrate, surmounted by a style longer than the stamens, terminated by a trifid stigma. Fig. 12 - Camassia (Camassia esculenta Lindl.). This beautiful plant is worthy of a place in our gardens as an ornamental; its habit is somewhat reminiscent of the Saint-Bruno lily. It is native to north-western North America, in rocky valleys, along the Colombian coast, etc. It flowers in May-June, in the summer, in the fall and in the fall. It flowers in May-June. "The Camassie, whose bulb is good to eat, can be grown in the open ground in the Paris climate. (Jacques, Hérincq et Duchartre.) " Wild people eat its bulbs cooked; they harvest them for winter and eat a lot of them." (Répertoire des plantes utiles, Duchesne.) " The grass disappears under the Quamash flowers in the meadows around Oregon-City. The natives make an enormous consumption of its bulbs." (Information given by a former resident of Oregon-City.) " This plant is not cultivated in Oregon, at least in the part I've lived in. The Indians gather it from the somewhat damp prairies, where it grows in fairly great abundance." (Excerpt from a letter we received from a former Oregon resident). "Kamass root or Wild Hyacinth (Camassia esculenta). This root has the shape of an onion and the volume of a walnut (Carya alba). It produces a pretty blue flower and grows on rocky hills. It is harvested in June and July. "Eaten raw, it has a pleasant, mucilaginous taste. Boiled, it resembles the common potato. "To preserve it, the Indians dig a pit and line it with stones on which a fire is lit. When these are sufficiently heated, they are cleaned and the roots are piled on top. Grass or twigs are first spread over the pile, and finally it is covered with earth. "After a few days, the pit is uncovered and the white Camassie roots have converted into a perfectly cooked, dark-brown homogeneous mass, about the consistency of a soft strong glue and as sweet as molasses. "With the roots cooked by this process, we often make, by stirring and pressing, large cakes which, slightly dried in the sun, become flexible and supple and resemble the black tobacco carrots of the navy. "This preparation is not of an appetizing color, but its flavor is sweet, mucilaginous and as pleasant as that of the fresh root, except for a slight smoky taste it contracts as it cooks. "In this compressed form, Camassie keeps softer than raw or simply cooked, and can be kept for a year or more. "The roots, boiled in water, produce very good molasses, which is highly esteemed and used by various tribes on days of great rejoicing. The Indians of Cape Flattery, the Nez-Percés of Idaho and those of Pitt-River, in California, are the greatest consumers of this food, under the name of Camass root" (1). "Eight days ago, while camped at Deer-Park, on Lower Arrow Lake (Columbia River development), I had the opportunity to witness how the Indians cook Camassia esculenta. This species of Camassia is very abundant along the Columbia River, between the Upper and Lower Arrow lakes, flowering during the first weeks of May. "The Indians collect the bulbs before the seed is completely ripe; they consider this the best time. The individuals I'm talking about had at least twenty to twenty-five bushels. "For two or three days, before cooking began, the women were busy cutting and bringing to camp branches of Alder and Maple (Alnus rubra and Acer glabrum). Several bunches of the broad leaves of Lysichiton kamchatcense (Skunk cab(1) Extract from the Report of the Commissioner of Agriculture for 1870; Washington, Government printing office, 1871. bage) and two or three of Alectoria jubata, the black-haired lichen that grows in abundance on Larix occidentalis, were brought in at the same time. "With everything ready, the men cut down a large Pine tree, with the sole apparent purpose of procuring its smaller branches, for they made no use of any other part; then, near the edge of the lake, in a sandbank, they dug a hole about 10 feet square and 2 feet deep, which they filled with broken Pine branches ; on top of this, they piled several cords of dried Cedar and Pine, which they covered with small pebbles; then fire was set in the pile in several places, and it was left to stand for several hours. When the Indians returned, the burning stones lay in a mass of ash. They removed the few pieces of unburnt wood that remained near the edges, and the women, with wooden spades, leveled the sides of the heap with sand, throwing enough over the stones to fill all the little crevices, through which a tongue of flame could shoot out from the coals still burning under the stones. The whole was then covered a foot or more thick with branches of Maple and Alder, after they had been well trampled. Broad leaves of Skunk cabbage were placed on top, until all the cracks were sealed. Slabs of Tamarac bark were spread over the smoking mass, and the bulbs placed on top. About half of them were in closed bark baskets, each containing about a bushel and a half. They were carried to the center of the las or pyre. The Lichen I mentioned was then placed on the unoccupied bark, after being thoroughly washed, and the remaining bulbs were spread over it. The whole was then covered again with branches and leaves, and a roof of bark slabs was made, over which two or three inches of sand were thrown. On top of this we piled the materials for another fire, more substantial than the first, which we lit just as the sun was setting, and continued to burn all night. "The next day, our camp was far away, and I was unable to see the result of the day's work. An Indian who could speak a little English told me, however, that the oven would have a day to cool down and that, when it was opened, the bulbs in the baskets would be converted into flour from which bread could be made, while those mixed with the Lichen would have united with it and formed a solid substance, resembling, in consistency and color, a tobacco-black carrot (plug) that could be broken up and kept well for a very long time (1). Cultivation. This plant is perfectly hardy, even in the climate of Paris; in the more northerly departments, however, it is wiser to keep a few bulbs in a frame, or to protect those left outside from sudden temperature variations and excessive cold, by means of a layer of leaves or straw. The bulbs are planted in light soil in August or September. Flowering takes place outdoors from May to June, and as early as (1) Sur la cuisson du Quamash, by Mr. James M. Macoun (Garden and Forest, vol. III, 1890, p. 343). May for bulbs kept under glass. Camassia is grown mainly in Holland, as an ornamental plant, where it has so far only been propagated by cloves. By cultivating Camassie as it is done in Holland, we have been able to observe: 1° That it presents no difficulties and that the plant is quite hardy in the Paris climate; 2° That the harvest obtained every two years is quite abundant and would be remunerative as soon as the excellent material that it makes available to cooks was intelligently used. Uses. In November 1886, we received 1,200 Camassie bulbs. We planted 750 of them, and used the remainder for experiments in cooking, pastry-making and confectionery. Pies made from these bulbs gave us mediocre satisfaction, but a sweet pastry we presented to the Société d'Acclimatation was really excellent. This paste was absolutely pure. It was nothing other than the cooked bulb, crushed and topped up with sugar, with no perfume, no mixture whatsoever. It was a good cough remedy, as pectoral and soothing as marshmallow paste or Arabian Nafé (okra) paste. In fact, it was much more pleasant. Here's the formula: Peel the bulb with a silver or ivory knife; leave nothing to color the paste; steam between two cloths; grind with a wooden roller on granite. At about this time, place sugar on the stove and cook to the same point as for fondant. The quantity of sugar should be equal to that of the paste. When you're ready to work with the fondant, add the ground paste and proceed in the same way as for all fondants. Keep in reserve and use as required. Camassie is more fatty than floury, but its flavor is so clean and pure, and its whiteness so appetizing, that we believe it lends itself to many culinary uses. We regret not having succeeded in making Camassie a resource for the table. We don't have at our disposal the skilful man who would be willing to apply his art to creating the sauces, jellies and dishes that the bulbs would provide. Enthusiasts will cultivate the plant; if they don't use its bulbs to make pectoral paste, or lartelettes, or sweet entremets, they will at least have one more pretty flower in their gardens in the spring. In the Manuel de l'Acclimateur, Messrs Naudin and Mueller list Camassia Fraseri Torr. (Scilla Fraseri A. Gray) as also producing edible bulbs. This species is said to be very prolific. To date, we have only been able to obtain a few bulbs of this type from Quamash, and it is impossible for us to comment on their value. We have also had in our possession a few bulbs of another Liliacea, Calochortus luteus (Cyclobothra lutea Lindl.), which the Indians of Utah and Arizona eat, and which the Americans call Wild Sago. This species, as well as several others belonging to this genus, is a very pretty plant; but it has not given us any results from the special point of view with which we are concerned. Finally, Dr. Philippi sent us from Chile a number of onions of Conanthera bifolia Ruiz et Pavon, and Cumingia tenella Don, other Liniaceae with edible bulbs of little interest. CANNA COMESTIBLE Starchy balisier, Capacho, Achira. CANNA EDULIS Ker., Roscoe, Monandrian plants of the order Scitamineæ, pl. 5; Bot. Mag. tab. 2498. Scitamineae family. Rhizomes tuberous, voluminous and starchy; stems 3 to 4 meters long, rounded and reddish; leaves large, ovate-lanceolate, with very pronounced veins; spathe lanceolate, acute, green, with purple margins; corolla with light orange-red outer divisions, the inner ones darker. "This species is native to Peru, where it is cultivated as a food plant. It is often called Toloman. A starch is extracted from its rhizome, known as Chouchoute starch, Toloman starch or Tolomane starch. In Trinidad, C. edulis is known as Toulema or Tulema (every month). The starch extracted is called Tulema starch (Heuzé, plantes alimentaires, vol. Il, p. 57). "An article sold in stores under the name Tous les mois bears a very close resemblance, in quality and appearance, to manufactured Arrow-root, to which it is considered superior for children's food, because it is less constipating. Dr. Lindley assumes that it is the product of Canna edulis. If this is the case, as the plant thrives perfectly in India, it would be extremely easy to manufacture, using the same procedure as for Arrow-root. The same would be true if, as Mr. J.-M. Jones says (1), there is a giant species of Arrow-root (Canna coccinea), the product of which is known as Tous les mois." (Firminger, Manual of gardening for Bengal). "A kind of Arrow-root called Tous les mois was imported into England from St. Kitts about the year 1836, and, as it was recognized as forming an excellent food product, national markets were opened to it. Until now, it has been imported into England exclusively from St. Kitts; but the plant is now grown over large areas in Australia" Nicholls and Raoul, Petit traité d'Agriculture tropicale, page 359. "I have found no indication in herbarium labels of the food use of Canna roots, and I have no clear idea of the distinction of species (1) The naturalist in Bermuda. in this very difficult genus: they all look alike, there are some in all parts of the world, and horticulture ignores their certain provenance and has created hybrids. "I believe that many Cannas, perhaps all, have the edible cooked tuber, at least the new part of the tuber, terminal bud and lateral buds. However, there must be some species where the tuber is slightly larger and more tender. Cultivation and climate must influence the edible quality of the tuber. In very smoky soil, it should be better and bigger. I assume that the northern climate is also favorable to its quality. I don't know if the marshy quality of the soil has any influence. "The remarkable thing about all Cannas is that, although they come from hot countries, they grow easily in summer in our gardens, flowering in the open ground (except for a few large species, 3 meters high, which can only flower in the South or in greenhouses) and, for the most part (except for L. liliiflora and iridiflora), form a tuber that is easy to store in winter in an orangery, cellar or cold greenhouse. "Small Cannas have an elevation of 80 centimetres or 1 metre, large ones 3 metres in warm countries. The same country often has several species at the same time. I've seen two in Guyana, and in my herbarium, I have a third from that country. Simmonds cites, among the Cannas whose grated tuber yields starch: C. Achiras Gillies. C. glauca Lin. C. flaccida Dill. C. edulis Ker. C. edulis has been taken to Australia and has spread from the Botanical Garden to the estates. "It seems to me that new tubers in formation should be cooked separately; not yet stemmed or rooted, they are much more tender and delicate. Older tubers sometimes have a few fibers. Canna edulis has no distinct taste of its own. It is mealy, tender and a little watery. The new tuber buds make up at least a third of the stump, perhaps half. They can be used either for human or animal consumption. I think it's better to use them separately. (Extract from our correspondence.) In the spring of 1879, a friend of ours brought us rhizomes of Capacho (Canna edulis) from Caracas, which we planted on an old bed. The plants vegetated vigorously and reached a height of 2.30 m; they did not flower. We detached the new buds from the stumps and cooked them in salted water; we removed the fibrous envelope and obtained a good vegetable, tender, starchy, somewhat resembling artichoke stock in flavor and consistency. For four years, we have been growing and tasting Capacho, and we believe that this Canna can be a useful addition to our vegetable gardens. The Société d'Acclimalalion has generously rewarded our experiments, and Messrs Vilmorin-Andrieux and Cte have included the plant in their catalog. Capacho culls in the same way as all Cannas, whether in the spring it is put into vegetation under a frame, or left in place during the winter, under the protection of a little litter. It does not flower in the Paris climate, which is why it is edible; if it did flower, if it did ripen its seeds, it would certainly become too fibrous to be used. Our horticulturists will be able to take advantage of this vegetable, which is both highly productive and easy to store. List of food Cannas. C. edulis Ker, Roscoe, Scit. 5; Bot. Mag. tab. 2498. Peru. C. discolor Lindl, Bot. reg. tab. 1231. Trinidad. C. indica Lindl., Roscoe, Scit. 2; Bot, reg. tab. 776. West Indies. C. paniculata Ruiz et Pav. Peru. G. gigantea Desf., Roscoe, Scit. 4; Bot. Mag. tab. 2316; Bot. reg. lab. 206. Brazil. C. coccinea Ait., Roscoe, Scit. 11; Amer. mérid. C. glauca Lin., Roscoe, Scit. 7. southern Carolina. C. flaccida Dill, Roscoe, Scit. 6; Redouté. Lil., 107. Caroline. All provide a valuable starch, and many can be prepared as a vegetable in a variety of ways. For the latter use, we believe they should be harvested before flowering. CANNA DISCOLOR Two-colored balisier. CANNA DISCOLOR Lindl. bot. reg. tab. 1231. Rhizomes tuberous; stems 3 to 4 meters long, reddish; leaves oblong-oval, colored reddish-angled below, lower ones washed with purple, upper ones colored only on the veins. Erect bunches, with red rachis, emerging from a coiled spathe, covered with abundant glaucous dust; flower very bright red outside and pale yellow inside. "This species is native to the island of Trinité, where it is widely cultivated. It supplies the starch known as Canna-root". (Ileuzé). It is less productive than Capacho (Canna edulis). In the Paris climate, this species rarely flowers. A comparative tasting, carried out with the most scrupulous attention by several people whom Messrs Vilmorin-Andrieux et Cie had called together for this purpose, demonstrated that Canna discolor was of superior quality to Canna edulis. Several varieties of ornamental Cannas, tasted on this occasion, were declared inedible. We believe that, in amateur gardens, Canna discolor should be grown in preference to Capacho. We know of several food Cannas that we have not grown. In Ruiz and Pavon's Flore du Pérou, all Cannas are named Achira. C. paniculata is Achira Cimarrona. C. indica is simply Achira. C. iridiflora is Sumac Achira. Its rhizome is not tuberous. According to these authors, the roots of C. paniculata are edible, but tasteless. Peruvians, they say, eat C. indica roots prepared in a variety of ways. "Canna indica. - It is widely cultivated in Colombia to extract the starch from its roots, which is given to the sick, like Arrow-root or Maranta starch....." (Note by M. Posada Arango, Bull. Soc. Bot. de France, t, XVIII, 1871, p. 372). "Four species of Cannas supply Tolomane starch. These are C. edulis, coccinea, discolor and glauca. This starch, also known as "of all months", comes to us from the West Indies and was imported into England in 1836. It is remarkable for its satiny sheen and is composed of flattened grains of considerable volume". (Pennetier, Matières organiques premières, p. 150.) CAPUCINE TUBÉREUSE TROPÆOLUM TUBEROSUM Ruiz et Pav, Fl. per. 314; Bot. Mag. tab. 3714; Fl. des serres, 5,432; Belg. hort. 2, 36. Tropéolées family. Roots tuberous; stems numerous, branched, glabrous, from Om, 50 to Om, 60 in height. Leaves cordate at the base, with 5 or 7 entire lobes, indented, sometimes bilobed; petioles twice as long as the blade, twisted into a tendril; peduncle much longer than the leaf; calyx crimson red, shorter than the spur; petals orange-yellow, toothed, streaked with black on the tab, about as long as the sepals. Blooms in October. Tuber pear-shaped, almost spherical, attenuated at the base, varying in size from that of a Chestnut to that of a medium-sized Pear. Its color is pale yellow, variegated with blood streaks. Its surface is covered with slightly protruding nipples. "The tubers of this plant are said to be edible in South America. Here, we haven't yet been able to make use of them. I have tried pickling them in vinegar like gherkins, but have not been satisfied with the result. A subscriber to the Revue horticole Fig. 13 - Nasturtium tuberosum (Tropxolum tuberosum Ruiz et Pav.). had the same idea, and enjoyed the product in a different way... What does this mean? It seems that we shouldn't discuss tastes, or that my soil wasn't suitable for the plant. Our subscriber left his tubers to marinate for three months, added no seasoning, and found that, "in this state, they offered a species of Pickles much more agreeable to the taste than the real thing, besides which the vinegar acquired a perfume suitable for use in sauces and sulades." "Ordinary potato cultivation is suitable for this plant, which is lightly ridged. The tubers only begin to form in September, and should not be harvested until the last possible moment to avoid the effects of frost." (Capucine tubéreuse, by Neumann, Rev. hort., 1845-46, p.17.) " The tuberous nasturtium, Tropœolum tuberosum, or Ysaño (pron. Ysagno), is regarded by everyone in Europe as such a detestable vegetable that there is absolutely no use for it. In fact, when Ysaño tubers are pulled from the ground, they have a most unpleasant pungency, accompanied by the odor peculiar to all species of nasturtium. "Well, in Bolivia, we've found a way to get rid of these defects, and have succeeded in making the Ysano, if not an everyday vegetable, at least a very edible one. "Coction is not enough to produce the desired result; freezing is added. "Tropœolum tubers should therefore be eaten cooked and frozen, and they should be eaten before they thaw, i.e. crunchy. In this state, I can affirm, as I've tried them many times, that they are quite a pleasant dish. "There's hardly a day that goes by that you don't see one or two rows of market women in La Paz selling nothing but these frozen Ysaños, or Taiachas, as they're called, which they protect from the sun by wrapping in wool or straw. The women of La Paz are all extremely fond of them, and are in the habit of taking them as refreshment during the heat of the day, by soaking them in molasses." (Note by M. Weddell, Rev. hort., 1852, p. 148.) That nasturtium tubers, frozen and soaked in molasses, are a pleasant refreshment during the summer heat, we have no intention of denying, but we don't expect our Frenchwomen to ever be served it at Tortoni's. So we'd still be in the presence of a new idea: the use of nasturtium tubers as a refreshment. We'd still be dealing with a beautiful tuber, easily obtained but useless, if the subscriber to the Revue horticole, quoted above by Neumann, hadn't taught us how to use it. A few years ago, we candied Nasturtium tubers in vinegar, and the result was entirely satisfactory. They were tasted by our family and friends, and our preparation was unanimously approved. We were far from believing then that we had been beaten to the punch, and that thirty years earlier, the subscriber had candied these tubers in vinegar, without the addition of any condiment, and had publicized the success of his attempt. Between Neumann's unfavorable opinion and that of the subscriber, we can therefore make a decision, and we do so without hesitation: the subscriber is right, a hundred times right. We have to say that we have prepared Capucine tubers like all our vinegar preserves, by adding Tarragon, Elderflower and Chilli. They turn the vinegar pink, but this coloring doesn't last long. Tuberous nasturtium, preserved in vinegar, retains its distinctive flavor, albeit slightly attenuated, an advantage not possessed by the vegetables usually associated with gherkins. We have proposed (1) a new composition of Pickles which rightly includes the Tuberous Nasturtium: Chinese Hibiscus or Catawissa Onion; Angourie Cucumber, from the West Indies; Miôga, from Japan; Tuberous Stachys (Crosne), from China; Tuberous Nasturtium. We believe we can add to this list the very bitter young fruits of Momordica Charantia, M. Balsamina and M. muricata; the former is eaten in China and Cochinchina, and the latter two are also edible. In Réunion, M. muricata is prepared in a variety of ways for the table, and is used in the composition of Acharts. Edible merchants sell Capucine tubéreuse for use as an hors-d'oeuvre. In dinners ordered from them, they present it in ramekins, cut into extremely thin slices and seasoned, like celery, with salt, oil, vinegar and mustard. It's a good idea to prepare this appetizer a few hours in advance. Tuberous nasturtium is very easy to grow. Its tubers are planted at the beginning of May, in the ground, 80 centimetres apart in all directions; the plantation is hoeed until the stems, spreading over the soil, cover it completely. Before planting, we put the tubers to vegetate in cups which we place under frames, as we do for (1) Nouvelle composition de Pickles (Bull. de la Soc. nat. d'Accl., 1883, p. 235). Cannas and Dahlias. This practice gives us excellent results. The leaves freeze before the tubers are fully formed; we use the frames, which are no longer in use at that time, to cover the plants from the beginning of October. Harvesting should only take place in November. Tropwolum polyphyllum Cavan and edule Paxt. tubers from the Chilean Andes are also said to be edible. Finally, according to F. Mueller (Select extratropical plants), those of T. sessilifolium Pœpp. et Endl., another Chilean species, are the largest and best. In Brazil and Paraguay, the leaves and fleshy stems of T. pentaphyllum Lamk are eaten as a vegetable herb. GAIRDNER CARVI CARUM GAIRDNERH Benth. et Hook. Umbelliferae family. This plant is abundant in Washington, Idaho, southern California and Utah. Asa Gray says its roots are an important food item for the Indians. It is biennial. "Geyer probably had this plant in mind when he spoke of an Umbellifer as being among the delicacies of the Indians in Oregon. This root, truly delicious, bursts when boiled and shows a floury substance, white as snow, with a sweet flavor like cream and possessing somewhat the fragrance of Parsley." (Lindley). Our American correspondent, Dr. Havard, sent us seeds and roots of this plant, and for several years we carried out cultivation trials with a view to ascertaining its food value. Unfortunately, the results did not meet our expectations: the plant, which is very hardy in the Paris climate, developed perfectly; it even flowered and fruited, and many seedlings survived the winter of 1890-1891 without suffering. From a practical point of view, however, Gairdner's Caraway is of no interest; the roots we obtained after two and even three years of vegetation were so small that we felt they could not be used. CHAYOTE, CHRISTOPHINE, CHOUCHOUTE Vegetable pear (English Colonies). - Chuchu (Brazil). - Chocho (Jamaica). - Pipinella, Chayota, Chahiota (Madeira). SECHIUM EDULE Swartz - CHAYOTA EDULIS Jacq. GriZgures: Jacquin, Amer., Il, p, 155. - Descourtilz, Flore des Antilles, tab. 328. - Flora Brasiliensis, II, 4, p. IIl, tab. XXXV. - Gardeners' Chronicle, 1865, p. 51 - Le Naturaliste, 1891, p. 170. Cucurbitaceae family. Plant native to Mexico, with tuberous stump, perennial, half-linear stems, climbing, bearing five-lobed leaves. Greenish or yellow flowers, the "96 CHAYOTE Fig. 14 - Chayote (Sechium edule Swartz). 4, Ramcau. - B, Female flower. - C, Male flower. - D, Fruit. 83 some male, others female, on the same plant. Pear-shaped fruit, from Om, 10 to Om, 15 long, containing a single seed, with a surface covered with stiff hairs, and greenish or creamy-white in color. There are two distinct varieties: one with pale green flowers and fruit, the other with creamy-white flowers and fruit. The fruit of the latter variety is also larger. According to Macfadyen Flora of Jamaica, vol. Il, p. 141), the white variety is the most delicate; added lemon juice and sugar, this fruit is used to make excellent tarts; the root, properly prepared, is very healthy and very pleasant to the taste. Lunan (Hortus jamaicensis, vol. I, p. 182) says that Chayote fruit is a healthy and pleasant vegetable, but that it is much improved by Lemon juice. Prepared with lemon juice and sugar, it is an excellent substitute for apple marmalade. The large roots, boiled or roasted, are floury and healthy. Lowe (Flora of Madeira, vol. Il, p. 292) writes of Sechium edule: "It is a favorite and highly esteemed vegetable. It resembles a young Squash rather than a Cucumber; but, when ripe, it is firmer and meatier in consistency, with a nutty flavor. The large-fruited variety, cream or white in color, looks better, but is considered to be of poorer quality than the green variety." We have grown this plant vigorously without fruit, but if Chayote cultivation is impractical in central France, it gives good results in the Mediterranean region. Cultivated in good southern exposure, the plant develops well and produces abundant fruit. For a number of years now, some edible merchants, notably Mr. Hédiard, have been selling Chayotes during the winter; but the price is still very high, and this excellent fruit will only become popular the day intelligent growers begin to cultivate it on a large scale in our Algerian colony. Chayote keeps for a very long time and can travel far; it would be an excellent trade item that would find easy outlets. Prepared in white sauce or like cardoons, it is an excellent dish. Sold at an affordable price, it would quickly take an important place among our all-too-rare winter vegetables. The plant is propagated by planting the whole fruit. The stems can be climbed on trellises, but can also be left to lie on the ground, like those of ordinary squash. CHERVIS Berle des potagers, Chirouis, Girole. SIUM SISARUM Lin. Umbelliferae family. Perennial plant with tuberous, fusiform, fasciculated roots, about Om, 20 long, fleshy, russet outside, white inside. Stems cylindrical, 0°, 30 high, reminiscent of parsnips; leaves pennate, those at the apex with 3 oblong-acute, toothed segments; involucre with 5 reflexed leaflets; flowers small, white, with a calyx with a denticulate or almost null blade. Flowers in July-August. Jacques and Hérincq date the introduction of this plant to Europe from 1548. It is thought to have come from China. However, it has been said that Chervis was one of the food plants of the ancients. Édouard Martens is far from affirming this (1). Poiret does not doubt it: "Pliny," he says, "tells us that the Emperor Tiberius, during his stay in Germany, found Chervis roots so delicious that he demanded a certain quantity each year as tribute. Dupuis, after quoting Poiret, adds: "It seems that the Chervis of the ancients is none other than parsnip. In his work on the origin of cultivated plants (2), M. Alph. de Candolle makes an in-depth study of the question, but without resolving it. We refer the reader to it. We have tried, on several occasions, to grow Chervis, and we must confess that we have not obtained the tender, delicate roots that were the delight of our fathers. We are inclined to believe that we have been unskilful and have not known how to cultivate Sium Sisarum. In our pamphlet entitled: Nouveaux légumes d'hiver (3), we said: Chervis is now known only by name in the Paris region and in the (1) Les plantes alimentaires des anciens, by Edouard Martens (Rev. de l'instruction publique en Belgique, nouvelle série, t. I). (2) Origine des plantes cultivées, by Alph. de Candolle; Paris, Germer, Baillière et Cle, 1883. (3) A Paillieux et D. Bois. Nouveaux légumes d'hiver, expéiences d'étiolement pratiquées en chambre obscure, Paris, 1879. It was once highly esteemed and widely cultivated. Nowadays, it is no longer cultivated, but garden lovers may well find a place for it in the kitchen garden, once they learn that its roots lend themselves to two very different uses. Once again, it will be a dual-purpose crop. In fact, its etiolated shoots produce a 6 X 8 Chervis salad (Sium Sisarum Lin). Fig. 15 - Plant habit. Fig. 16. - Roots. aromatic, with a pleasant flavor, which can be eaten with or without mixing. An interesting acquisition. We would also return to the preparation of Chervis roots, as practiced by our forefathers, and we would like to reproduce here a recipe from 1656. The author of Délices de la campagne said: "This root is so delicate that it almost only wants to enter hot water to remove its skin; then it is fried, having been powdered with flour and dipped in batter like Scorsonère. Orange juice is its real sauce. If, when cooked and peeled, you eat it in butter, in a touring or German sauce, or in oil, in a salad with Spanish chervil, as soon as it starts to sprout its leaf, it's a delicate and delicious meal." Carrots, parsnips and celery have produced many varieties, but not so the Chervis. It has remained unchanged since its introduction, some three hundred and forty years ago. Perhaps it has degenerated. It used to be widely cultivated; its roots were found on the best tables, and Linnaeus reports that, in his day, it was grown in almost every garden. Greatness and decline! What can be the cause of the oblivion into which it has fallen? It would be wrong of us to teach the cultivation of the Chervis after admitting that we have undoubtedly done it badly. We will describe it in the words of Mme Aglaé Adanson: "Chervis requires open, soft, deep soil. It is sown light on March 1st, in newly spaded soil, in rows of five rays Om.04 deep, which are covered with a rake. When the plant is strong enough, it is thinned out to Om,10, weeded, crimped and watered from time to time. It is good to eat from November until it bolts. Even though it's perennial, it's a good idea to renew it every year; it'll be much better. According to Mme Adanson, the germination period of its seed is four years, and according to M. Vilmorin, only three years. We recommend growing Chervis; it's a vegetable with an excellent flavor, which, it seems, can be grown abundantly and tenderly by careful cultivation. Publications to consult: Note sur le Chervis, by M. le docteur Sacc (Bull. de la Soc. d 'Acclimatation, vol. Il, 1855, p. 561). Le Chervis, by M. Huzard (Bull. de la Soc, nationale d'Hort. de France, 2e série, 1856, vol. II, p. 147). Chervis. Note, by M. A. Dupuis (Rev. horticole, 2e série, 1856, vol. V, p. 302). Another umbellifer, Tinguarra sicula Parlatore (Athamantha sicula L., non Ucria), native to Sicily, produces a fleshy root, reminiscent in shape of Salsify and with an aromatic flavor quite comparable to that of Parsnip. We grew the plant from seeds kindly sent to us by Mr. Todaro, Director of the Palermo Botanical Garden. Growing Tinguarra presents no difficulty, but it's not until the second year after sowing that we have roots large enough to use. We have collected many seeds, but we don't think there is any point in propagating this plant in gardens. CHUCKLUSA PEUCEDANUM CANBYI Coulter et Rose. Fam. Umbelliferae. In 1894, Mr. Meehan of Philadelphia sent us tubers of this plant. It is the Chucklusa of the Spokane Indians. The stem reaches 10 to 25 centimeters in height; the tubers are globular and measure 6 to 12 lines in diameter. According to Coulter and Rose, Revision of north American Umbelliferæ, p. 56, Chucklusa is, after Quamash (Camassia esculenta), the most important food plant in the region where it grows wild. Mr. Meehan included two other species of Peucedanum in his shipment: 1° P. farinosum Geyer, or Tuhuha of the Spokane Indians; 2° P. ambiguum Nutt. We have grown these plants in the open ground, sheltering them with glass boxes during the winter; they flowered in February, in 1895 and 1896, and even fructified; but we have recognized, as for Bunium incrassatum, from Algeria, that these are plants that can only be used where it is possible to harvest them in the wild and in sufficient quantity. Once cultivated, it takes too long to obtain small tubers of no particular merit by sowing, the only possible method of propagation. Another member of the Peucedanum genus is P. Cous Watson, whose starchy tubers are sought after by the Indians under the name of Cous (Coulter and Rose, loc. cit., p. 58). CIBOULE CATAWISSA Catawissa onion. ALLIUM FISTULOSUM L., var. Family Liliaceae. Very large, perennial, bulbiferous Ciboule, i.e. producing small bulbils instead of flowers, like the Rocambole Onion. Planted in spring or autumn, as the plant is perfectly hardy in the Paris climate, these bulbils give rise, in the first year, to two or three stems topped with bulbils which, as soon as they are formed, develop new stems crowned with new bulbils, which very frequently give rise to a third tier of shoots, the whole rising from Om, 75 to om, 80. After a year or two, the vegetation changes. The clumps become very vigorous, consisting of twenty bulbils, but developing secondary stems much less frequently. The taste of bulbs and shoots is more or less the same as when eaten, although the first envelope, which is very hard, is removed (Vilmorin-Andrieux et Cie). The Catawissa Onion has hitherto been considered to be of American origin, but recently, while perusing Dr. E. Bretschneider's book entitled: Early european researches into the Flora of China, Fig. 17 - Ciboule Catawissa (Allium fistulosum). We had the satisfaction of discovering its true homeland. A Frenchman named Louis Le Comte, a Jesuit missionary to China in 1687, published a two-volume work in Paris in 1696 entitled Nouveaux mémoires sur l'état de la Chine. The author, born in 1655, died in Bordeaux in 1729. Le Comte speaks (I, 178) of a particular Chinese onion in the following terms: "I have seen a species of onion which does not come from seed like those of Europe, but, at the end of the season, small filaments emerge from the tip or stem of the leaves, CIBOULE CATAWISSA Ciboule Catawissa. Fig. 18 - Whole plant. Fig. 19 - Inflorescence. in the middle of which forms an Onion similar to that which germinates in the earth. In time, this little Onion grows leaves like those that support it, in such a way, however, that their size and height diminish as they move away from the earth." This description would probably not be sufficiently convincing, if Dr. Bretschneider did not add the following: "This onion appears to be the one described as Lou tz'tsung (Onion growing in tiers) in the Kiu huang pen ts'ao, published at the end of the 14th century. It also features a good figure. The description states that four to five small onions grow at the top of the leaves, and that other onions grow on top of these, forming three to four tiers. These onions are seedless...". The Catawissa Onion was imported from America by M. A. de Lentilhac aîné, and offered for sale by M. Gagnaire fils aîné, horticulturist in Bergerac. We cullivated it as soon as it was introduced, and we'll say what we think of it later. M. Gagnaire wrote in Revue horticole, 1875, page 57: "No one is unaware that the onion which is eaten green in spring, in Paris as well as in the provinces, is, on the one hand, the result of sowing carried out by gardeners during the month of August, while, on the other hand, and particularly in our region, Green onions are obtained by planting, in September and October, bulbs unfit for consumption, which, in spring, produce three or four green stems, sometimes more, which are detached from the stump according to the needs of the house or the sale. "Whatever the means employed, the fact remains that the onion to be consumed green in spring must be sown, transplanted and planted annually in autumn; and if, on the other hand, the aim is to obtain small onions in the garden for preserving, I don't have to tell you how much care this work requires, not to mention the fact that it's not always easy to achieve satisfactory results. However, with the Catawissa Onion, these inconveniences disappear, since it has the faculty to give each spring, and for three or four years, green Onions in abundance, in summer bulbils in quantity for candying, and that it requires no other culture than that which I am going to indicate. "The Catawissa Onion is a vegetable plant with a perennial stump, producing from twenty to thirty stalks at its base in spring, as big as Leeks, long, tender and excellent for eating green; earlier by a fortnight or even a month than Onions planted in autumn. It is grown in multiples of bulblets, which are planted from October to February, and treated as follows: "The land destined for the Catawissa Onion having been worked and thoroughly amended beforehand with a heavy layer of manure or fertilizer, several furrows spaced at Om,40 to 0m,50 each are drawn with a chalk line, in which the bulblets are placed, equally spaced Om,40 to Om,50 apart. This distance, which can be easily taken advantage of in the first year when growing Chicory, Lettuce, Carrot, etc. between the rows, is essential in the second year of planting, because of the development that strains are bound to take on. Bulbils planted from October to February will grow vigorously in spring, but in the first year they will only produce a single stem, which can be supported with a small stake. During the summer, this stem will produce one or two stages of bulblets at the top, which can be used for planting, or taken advantage of by pickling in vinegar, like gherkins. "The second year is the first harvest. From the end of February to the end of April, sometimes even into May, in place of the bulbils planted the previous year, we find a clump of green onions, as big as Leeks, containing twenty to thirty stems of a flavor and quality that are second to none to the best cultivated onions; and since a hundred clumps of Catawissa Onions will not be enough for an ordinary household to consume the green stems they provide in spring, those that remain on the vines develop, reaching a height of Om.80 to 1 meter, and are crowned at the top, in summer, with one or two tiers of bulbils that can be used as I indicated above. "From then on, Calawissa Onion clumps will produce abundant stems every spring, and bulbils in quantity every summer, for two, three or even four years, without any care other than a few hoeings applied during the vegetation period and a good ploughing in spring, a little before the stems appear. "The Catawissa onion is extremely hardy, withstanding 20 to 30 degrees below zero without deterioration. M. Gagnaire's note is followed by a few observations from M. Carrière, who acknowledges that the Catawissa Onion is absolutely distinct from the Rocambole Onion, which was disputed. We will add nothing to the foregoing regarding the cultivation of the Catawissa Onion, other than to endorse it. As for its uses, there are some whose importance we can neither acknowledge nor deny. We know that green onions are eaten in large quantities, but we've never eaten them, and we've never seen anyone around us eat them. We haven't even thought of using Catawissa in this state. We therefore don't know whether its stems have the flavor of the common onion, but we can affirm with M. Gagnaire that its strains are extremely fertile. We shall confine ourselves to assessing the merit and usefulness of its bulblets. Chinese Allium has its own distinctive flavor, which is precisely not that of Onion, Chive or Shallot, which is why we recommend its cultivation. In fact, Catawissa bulbils, preserved in vinegar, are excellent and different from any similar preparation. The plant is also very curious. According to Baron von Mueller (Select extratropical plants), Allium canadense Kalm (Wild Garlic), a species native to North America, could be cultivated or naturalized in wet meadows because of its aerial bulbils, which are much sought-after for the preparation of superiorly fragrant pickles. CLAYTONE PERFOLIÉE CLAYTONIA PERFOLIATA Willd. ; C. cubensis Bonpl. Bot. Mag. t. 1336; Belg. Hort. 1.58 Family Portulaceae. Annual plant. Leaves veinless, those at the top opposite, rounded, fused at the base to form a cone, radicals petiolate, oval rhombiform; in March-June, white flowers. small, pedicellate, arranged in clusters, lower ones fasciculate; petals whole or slightly indented. We have grown the Claytone to be able to express a personal opinion about it. The experience has not been unfavorable and, however, Fig. 20 - Claytone perfoliée. we have not renewed it, to save time and space. Claytone is a good substitute for Spinach. Like most spinach substitutes, it has neither vices nor virtues. In fact, it belongs to the innocent Purslane family. The underlined word belongs to the note we're about to read (Revue horticole, 1829-31, vol. I, p. 357): "Claytone, an annual plant, 12 or 15 inches high, originally from Cuba (1) was brought back by M. de Humboldt in 1804 and given to the Jardin des Plantes in Paris, where it has almost become acclimatized, since it grows there by itself in several places. Until now, it had been considered a useless plant, or rather, it had been neglected, even though its succulence and tenderness should have led to the suspicion that it was good to eat; all the more so as it belongs to the innocent Purslane family. Nevertheless, in the twenty-six years since botanists have seen it reappear each spring, they have not been tempted to subject it to any experiments. It's true that the usefulness of plants hardly occupies them; their science is limited to counting and measuring their parts, nothing more. "Fortunately, there are also a few men who examine plants from other points of view, who seek to apply them to our needs, without however neglecting the somewhat arid science of the botanists. One such man is Mr. Madiot, director of the Rhône department's naturalization nursery in Lyon. He has found that perfoliated Claytone leaves are good to eat raw in salads, and cooked, like sorrel or spinach, under a fricandeau. This is what he tells us in one of his letters, dated December 4. He tells us, moreover, that the seed of this plant is greedily eaten by small birds. "Claytone is easy to grow. It is sown in spring, very lightly, because it branches a lot from its base, on a well loosened, well top-dressed bed with good exposure. It is watered and weeded as needed; it rises and grows (1) C. perfotiata is native to North America; it was introduced from Vancouver by Menzies in 1796. rapidly, branching so much from the base, if sown light, that M. Madiot has counted up to 63 branches on a single plant, capable of growing to 15 and 18 inches in length; but these are cut off 2 or 3 inches from the ground before flowering is complete, and others grow back which can be cut off two or three more times. It's enough to leave a few feet for seeds. We make use of the stems, which are tender and succulent, and the leaves, which are shell-shaped or ear-shaped, placed at great distances on the stems." Duchesne says of Claytone: "This watery, refreshing vegetable is eaten like Purslane in South America. M. Sahut, from Montpellier, considers it excellent, providing a dish with a finer and more delicate flavor than Spinach. In our opinion, Claytonia can be grown like any other summer spinach. We have grown Claytonia virginica L., a perennial species native to North America, whose tuber is said to be edible. C. virginica is a fairly ornamental plant, with small but very abundant pink flowers that follow one another uninterruptedly for quite a long time. From a nutritional point of view, it is of absolutely no interest to us. The plants we have obtained from seed have only given us a strain that cannot be pulped, even after several years of cultivation. In our climate, C. virginica also requires light winter shelter. Kou or Kiao, Kiao paï tsaï, Kiao sun. HYDROPYRUM LATIFOLIUM Griseb. 101 Grass family. This plant was discovered more than fifty years ago by Turczaninow in Trans-Balkan Russia, where it grows wild. This Russian botanist originally named it Zizania palustris. It is grown throughout China, in water, like Rice, for its stems, whose lower part (of the young plant), near the roots, is eaten as a vegetable. In Beijing, the plant reaches a height of 2 meters. This plant is mentioned by: Franchet et Savatier, Enumeratio plantarum in japonia crescentium, II, p. 156; Dr Mène, Productions végétales du Japon, p. 230; Siebold, Synopsis plantarum œconomicarum japoniæ, 1827, 368 (Graminea makomo frumentum pro farina in provinciis septentrionalibus cultum). Dr. Bretschneider, to whose kindness we have had recourse so many times when it came to obtaining information on plant production in China, wrote us the following: "In Chinese botanical books, this plant is described as Kou or Kiao. See Pentsaokang mou (Bretschneider, Botanicon sinicum, p. 54, chap. XIX, p. 26); Tchi wou ming shi t'ou K'ao (Bot. sinicum, p. 72, chap. XVIII, p. 13). "In the Kin huang pen ts'ao (Bot. sinicum, p. 49), an old 14th-century book in my possession, I find an article on Kiao cultivation, accompanied by a figure (leaves and roots). "The plant is also illustrated and described in the Japanese botanical Phonzo zoufou (Franchet et Savatier, loc. cit., I, p. 15, chap. XXXIII, p. 21 and 22). The figure is uncharacteristic. "The cultivation of Hydropyrum in China is very ancient. It is mentioned in classical books. According to Chinese books, this plant is also cultivated for its seeds. This is not the case in Beijing, where it is grown only for its young edible stems. Its common name in Beijing is Kiao pai tsai (Kiao cabbage). This vegetable is also known as Kiao sun (young Kiao shoots)". Under the title On a Chinese culinary vegetable, Mr. H.-F. Hance published the following interesting note in the Journal of Botany (1872, p. 146): "Among the vegetables esteemed by the natives, there is one called Kan sun by them, and known to Europeans who do not reject it, as some do, simply because it is Chinese, under the name Cane-Shoots. "It is brought to market in white, cylindrical pieces, 2 and a half to 3 and a half inches long and 1 to 1 and a half inches in diameter. It is attenuated towards the top into a conical point, surrounded and surmounted by leaves and thatch, from which it is easily detached. "The taste of raw Shoot is reminiscent of a half-ripe walnut, but it is never eaten unless cooked. The Chinese prepare it with meat; foreigners cut it lengthwise, in two or three pieces, boil it well and serve it with melted butter. Prepared in this way, it is, to my taste, one of the most pleasant and delicate vegetables I know. It's hard to say exactly what flavor it has. It is perhaps that of Green Corn which comes closest, boiled as the Americans eat it under the name of Green Corn, although it possesses a particular flavor of a delicacy to which I know nothing comparable in any other vegetable. "A very slight examination shows that these Cane-Shoots, as they are called, consist of the solid base of the stem of a grass; but, although I had long desired to determine the species, it was only the other day that I succeeded in obtaining live samples, in flower. On examining them, I was greatly surprised to find that they related to Hydropyrum latifolium Griseb. a plant discovered, some forty years ago, by Turczaninow in lakes at the confluence of the Schilka and Argun rivers, in Amuria, and since found on the Ussuri ripper, in Lake Kengka, in the vicinity of Peking (from where I had specimens collected by M. Swinhœ) and in Japan. It is not spontaneous in southern China, but only cultivated (in slack waters) for the table; and I find that it is also grown as a vegetable in Kiu-Kiang, on the Yang-Tze. I assume, although I have no definite data on the development of its cultivation, that it is very widespread in this country. "As a species, it is very close to H. esculentum Link. the Indian rice or Water oats, Zizania aquatica L., from North America, with which Trinius, with hesitation, combines it; and even Grisebach, having no specimen of the latter herb, was not convinced of their difference. I'm of the opposite opinion, however. Turczaninow notes that his species also differs in the presence of an elongated rhizome, which he asserts is absent in H. esculentum. "Endlicher combines the genera Hydropyrum and Hydrochloa, and Dr. Chapman, while admitting the latter genus, refers Hydropyrum to Zizania, as does Professor Asa Gray. Meissner recognizes both Hydropyrum and Zizania as valid genera, dubiously combining Hydrochloa with the former. Admittedly, these genera are extremely confused and poorly distinguished. "It is the extreme connection of the herb submitted for examination with the American species which, although it furnishes an excellent grain, somewhat resembling Rice, which was in general use among the Indians, especially among the tribes living in the vicinity of the great lakes, and has never, I suppose, been employed otherwise, leads me to think that the identificalion of the Chinese vegetable may be of interest and importance to American gardeners; for I cannot doubt that Hydropyrum esculentum would yield a product identical in volume and flavor to that of the plant it so closely resembles. "I call the attention of skilled horticulturists in the United States to this herb, in the belief that it will pay for the trouble of its experimental cultivation. "Our American cousins are, as a rule, more fond of and connoisseur of vegetables than we English, and like a greater variety of them at their meals. "If the preceding article had the effect of introducing a new, and, as I think with many others, exceptionally delicate and select vegetable, to their tables, I should feel only too glad to have been useful in calling their attention to it. I also hope to succeed in sending to my country live specimens of the Asiatic species, which, according to the temperature of the localities where it grows, would undoubtedly be immediately naturalized in the lakes and marshes of northern and central Europe and would provide, without requiring any particular care and without encroaching on the land occupied by other crops, a healthy and pleasant food. Hydropyrum esculentum has been acclimatized in England and Scotland". Our attention was drawn to this plant by Mr. Eugène Simon, former French consul in China, who had sent a plant to the Ministry of Agriculture. This plant having been entrusted to the good care of M. Hardy, we asked for information from the eminent director of the Ecole nationale d'Horticulture de Versailles, who replied: "On November 20 1857," he told us, "I received a certain quantity of an edible reed from Chang-Haï. "This reed bore the Mandarin name of Kao-paé and that of Co-ba in the vernacular. "Co-ba, it seems, doesn't produce seeds, so I only received plants that were in very poor condition. I was, however, able to keep one which vegetated quite miserably for two years and eventually perished, despite the assiduous care with which it had been treated. "The plant could not be determined scientifically. "Here is some information I had at the time about Co-ba. I think you'll find it interesting. "There are two kinds (1) of Co-ba: the Co-ba of FouTcheou, and the Co-ba of Chang-Haï. These are aquatic plants that grow in all running or stagnant waters in northern China. "The first type produces two harvests a year. Its roots do not trace. Care must be taken at each harvest to separate the young shoots from the large stems and replant them in the mud. "The second yields only one crop a year, unless there is a long, hot autumn; but the roots trace and spread in all directions; it grows spontaneously once planted. "The base of the stalk is eaten to a length of Om, 25 to Om, 30, either raw in salads, or cooked in a variety of dishes. "Co-ba is good for harvesting only when it is completely white. Its delicate taste is reminiscent of saw palmetto and bamboo. It can be stored for several months and is easy to transport. "As for cultivation, it is said to be as follows: "The Chinese pull up Co-ba plants, which they dry (?) in the sun for a few days; then, from November 15th to December 15th, they put the roots in the ground, or rather in mud, in running or stagnant water. Harvesting takes place between September 15 and October 15 of the following year. This cultivation applies to ordinary Co-ba, which produces only once a year and has a tracer root. "(1) It would be interesting to know whether one of these two types is not Hydropyrum esculentum (Zizania aquatica), which does not seem to differ much from Hydropyrum latifolium. subject to regular cultivation. It also requires flooded soil and yields two harvests a year. It is replanted each time and smoked. "The material used for manuring is generally the residue of the oil pea known in Chinese as Téoupinn, which is spread in the proportion of 60 kilos per half-hectare. Mr. Eugène Simon, former French consul in China, gave us the following information on attempts to introduce Co-ba in France: "Co-ba was sent by Mr. de Montigny and myself. I did not make any shipments in 1858. "In 1861, I sent a shipment to the Ministry of Agriculture. The Co-ba plants were planted in damp soil and in a greenhouse at the Ward. I don't know the non-tracking variety, which gives only one crop. "I shipped the cultivated species that yields two harvests a year. My consignment was given to Mr Hardy, who, in 1864, showed me some plants that were still alive, but in poor condition. Since then, no further attempts have been made to introduce the plant. We finally succeeded in obtaining the plant, thanks to the kindness of Vilmorin, and entrusted it to a serious amateur, Mr. Latour-Marliac, from Temple-sur-Lot, so that he could experiment with its cultivation in the south of France. In a letter we have received, our correspondent tells us that the plant withstood the 16° cold of the winter of 1891-1892; that it grows vigorously, but that he has not yet been able to taste its stems; with its beautiful upright leaves, it would certainly be of some interest from an ornamental point of view. MANDERI CUCUMBER CUCUMIS SACLEUXII Paill. et Bois (1). Family Cucurbitaceae Stems flat-lying, furrowed, covered, like all parts of the plant, with stiff, whitish hairs. Fig. 21 - Mandera cucumber (Cucumis Sacleuxii). Petioles grey, sometimes up to 1 decimeter long. Rounded kidney-shaped leaves, obscurely lobed, irregularly toothed, scabrous on both sides, grayish-green, especially on the inner side(1) Experimental cultivations in 1889. Revue des Sciences naturelles appliquées, No. 8, April 1890. férieure. Simple tendrils. Flowers monoecious, solitary: male flowers borne on filiform peduncles 2 to 2.5 centimeters long; calyx campanulate, 6 to 7 millimeters long, hispid, with subulate teeth; corolla about twice the length of the calyx, hairy on the outside. Female flowers with peduncles about 7 cm long; ovary hispid. Fruit ovoid, 8 to 10 centimeters long and 4 to 5 centimeters in diameter in the middle, a little scabrous, dark green with pale-green longitudinal stripes. Seeds brownish, small, compressed, 1/2 centimeter long and 3 millimeters wide in the middle, attenuated at both ends. The seeds of the Mandera Cucumber were sent to the Museum by R. P. Sacleux. M. Maxime Cornu was kind enough to give us a few. We sowed them in April, under glass, and transplanted the seedlings into pots in the open air, as is usually done for cucumbers. The plant vegetated vigorously; its stems intertwined so tightly that, together with the leaves, they formed a thick fabric that could be lifted in one piece like a coat, but not without pricking your hands, as the plant was covered with numerous stinging hairs. The flowers didn't bloom on top, but underneath, they withered away for lack of air, and fell without setting fruit. We remedied the problem by giving the plants a little air, using poles slid horizontally under them, but it was too late. We obtained abundant, well-developed fruits, but they remained green and did not ripen their seeds. It seems to us that the stems of the Mandera cucumber should be tied to lanterns, or trained on a wide-meshed trellis, as is done for the Row Melon. We cultivated the plant again in 1890, with no further success. But one of our correspondents. M. Latour-Marliac, from Temple-sur-Lot, to whom we had entrusted some seeds, has made an abundant harvest of perfectly ripe fruits, albeit with the color indicated in our description; they are divided lengthwise by narrow white stripes which contrast nicely with the dark green of their rind. We presented fruit preserved in vinegar to the Société nationale d'Acclimatation. When tasted fresh, we recognized a strong cucumber flavor. They could be used for preserves by harvesting them young and as large as olives. The cultivation of Mandera cucumbers is only practicable in the south of France. SIKKIM CUCUMBER CUCUMIS SATIVUS L., var. sikkimensis J. D. Hooker. Cucurbitaceae family. "The Sikkim Cucumber is the most remarkable variety of cucumber for its size and the volume of its fruit. The leaves are almost as large as those of Pumpkins, often with 5 or even 9 lobes. The fruits, about the size and shape of fine Cavaillon melons, are ovoid, elongated, very regular, with a rounded outline, and usually contain five placentas instead of three; the flesh is white and very thick, and the skin finely mottled with yellowish-white and russet. I know this variety only from a colored drawing in the possession of Mr. J. Dalton Hooker and from a few dried fragments in the Kew Herbarium. According to this learned traveller, the Sikkim Cucumber is cultivated throughout English India, but particularly in the province whose name it bears here. It is an excellent vegetable, which the natives eat indifferently cooked or raw. It would be desirable to introduce it into the vegetable gardens of Europe". (Naudin, Cucurbitacées). We were fortunate enough to realize this wish, but eighteen years had passed since it was first made. In March 1877, Dr Hooker, whom we can't thank enough for his courtesy, sent us seeds of Cucumis sikkimensis, which only existed in the Royal Gardens at Kew, of which he is Director. Sown on a bed and under a frame, these seeds gave us vigorous plants and abundant fruit of excellent quality. Their volume is that of the largest white cucumbers, and their color, which is truly curious when ripe, is clearly reticulated. They seem to have a small-meshed net stretched across their brown background. That's the least of their merits. They're equally good served in the ravier as hors-d'oeuvres, or dressed like ordinary cucumbers. In cultivating Cucumis sikkimensis for the first time, we took every precaution to ensure success; but, later, we left several plants in the open air and were able to observe its hardiness.
