On July 12, 1877, we presented to the Société centrale d'Horticulture de France (Henri Véniat, gardener) fruits of C. sikkimensis, on which a report was submitted on the 26th of the same month by Messrs Arnould-Baltard and Hédiard, who declared that, charged by the committee for vegetable cultivation to assess their eating merit, they had recognized them as excellent, with very fine flesh and much more delicate than that of the ordinary Cucumber. We also presented some at the exhibition opened by the same Society in October 1877. More recently, we have distributed seeds of the Sikkim Cucumber to members of the plant section of the Société d'Acclimatation, and we shall continue this work of propagation of a plant that we consider a happy acquisition. We conclude with an extract from an article in the Botanical Magazine, January 1876, pl. 7206, (Journ. de la Soc. nat. d'Hort., 1876, p. 365): "This singular variety of Cucumber is commonly cultivated in the eastern part of the Himalayan chain, i.e. in Sikkim and Nepaul, where its fruit is obtained and consumed in immense quantities, and where cultivation of this plant extends over large areas of land up to 1,500 meters in altitude.... Its fruit reaches Om, 38 in length and as much in circumference... Mr. D. Hooker says that in 1840 he saw all the inhabitants of Sikkim, men, women and children eating these Cucumbers almost from morning to night." We hope that the cultivation of C. sikkimensis will become widespread in our country. MUSKED CUCUMBER Calabaza de China. Curuba or Curua (in Brazil). SICANA ODORIFERA Ndn, Ann. des se. nat. ser. 4, vol. 18, p. 181 tab. 8; Cogniaux, Flora Brasil, fasc. 78, p. 22, tab. 3, and Monographiæ Phanerogam, p. 522; Fd. André, Rer. horticole, 1890, p. 515 (with colored figure). Fam. Cucurbitaceae. Perennial plant, native to Brazil, cultivated in tropical parts of South and Central America. In 1888, M. de Lafon gave us a packet of seeds of this curious plant, which he had observed on his trip to Costa Rica. He enclosed a note from Julio Rossignon's Manual del Jardinero y Arbolista, with the translation we reproduce below: "In Central America, there is a variety of cucumber improperly called Melocoton in Guatemala and in all the Central American provinces, and Calabasa from China to Panama, which is remarkable for its size, its beautiful foliage, its thick, light-yellow flowers, and above all for its fruit, which, when ripe, turns a purplish-red DR Fig. 22. 22 - Musk cucumber (Sicana odorifera.) A. Portion of twig with tendril, leaf and female flower. 1. Male flower bud, not blooming. 2. Open male flower, showing reflected lobes of calyx and corolla. 3. Male flower, longitudinal section, showing stamens. 4. Female flower, showing pistil and ovary cut longitudinally. and give off a very strong, slightly musky scent. "In Central America, the ripe fruits of this Cucurbitaceae, to which we have given the name Cucumis Abelmoschus elatior, are placed in chapels during Holy Week and at the Nativity, where they give off a sweet fragrance. They are sometimes served half or three-quarters cooked in syrup, and seasoned in the same way as potatoes. "As vegetables are generally in short supply in hot climates, we thought it would be useful for those of our readers who are unfamiliar with the properties of this Cucurbitaceae to go into these details. What's more, Calabaza de China can be grown as an ornamental plant to line walls, form trellises or climb to the top of some tall tree; nothing is as pleasing to the eye as a trellis (cradle, arbor) laden with dark purple or reddish fruits, interspersed with golden-yellow flowers and green leaves." These seeds did not germinate in the Crosne garden. M. Naudin, director of the Villa Thuret in Antibes, was happier than we were. He obtained the plant and was able to harvest two fruits, which were the subject of the following note, published in the Revue horticole, 1889, page 35: "Sicana odorifera is a remarkable Cucurbitaceae from Brazil, whose climbing stems, 8 to 10 meters or more long, cling to the smoothest walls with the help of their tendrils, the ends of which spat out into very adherent suckers, which is not peculiar to this species. It is monoecious, with yellow flowers, and its fertilized female flowers give rise to cylindrical fruits, the size of a very large Cucumber or a small Butternut squash (Cucurbita moschata), which turn red or orange as they ripen. They are edible, but not to everyone's taste, due to their aromatic odor. They are used as a means of keeping insects away from apartments, as the smell puts them to flight. "Sicana requires more heat than our ordinary Cucurbitaceae, even more than the large varieties of butternut squash that rarely ripen in Paris. It will therefore be a southern plant, at least until it produces less demanding and earlier varieties. It would have succeeded perfectly in Antibes if this year's summer had been normal; but it was exceptionally cold, and everything was delayed in the gardens by more than a month. "We did, however, obtain two fruits after artificial fertilization, and one of them, which seems to me to have reached its full size, will perhaps ripen in the heated apartment where we keep it in reserve. If this happens, we'll have seeds with which to repeat our experiment, and probably with more complete success, for years, like days, follow one another and are not alike. "I have just mentioned the possibility of obtaining from Sicana, sooner or later, varieties better suited to our climates than the plants first introduced. Whether we regard this result as a fact of acclimatization or judge it otherwise, the fact is no less certain for the Cucurbitaceae of exotic origin that we have long been cultivating in our gardens. Whenever I have received seeds of these same species from tropical countries, the plants have been more difficult to raise and later than their French counterparts, which have been introduced long ago and somehow softened by long culling. We can therefore hope that the same will be true of Sicana and probably of several other species. The fruits harvested by Mr. Naudin ripened perfectly, enabling Mr. Ed. André (loc. cit.) to publish a good colored figure. We saw these fruits at the home of the editor of the Revue Horticole and we fully agree with him when he says they exude a penetrating smell of Pomme de Reinette. CORCHORUS POTAGÈRE Vegetable mallow, Jewish mallow, Melonkie. CORCHORUS OLITORIUS L., var. edulis (1). Tiliaceae family. Annual plant, native to India and tropical Africa. Waste is used to make paper pulp. These fibers are actively traded. In 1877, 200,000 barrels of jute were imported into England, and 25,000 into France. (capsule) siliquiform, long, hairless; seeds angular, greenish. According to Poiret (Voyage en Barbarie, vol. II, p. 179), the plant is cultivated both in the Levant and in Barbary. We devoted only one panel to it, and our harvest did not allow us to make a dish from it for our table; we therefore confess that we did not enjoy its leaves, either as a salad or as spinach. It seems certain to us that Corchorus olitorius cannot be usefully cultivated in the Paris climate: moreover, the number of salads and spinach substitutes is so great that this plant seems to us to be absolutely devoid of interest. Bicorne jaune, Martynie jaune. MARTYNIA LUTEA Lindl. Pedaline family. 119 Annual plant, height Om, 50, pubescent glandular; leaves cordate-orbicular, a little toothed; in August, flowers yellow, ample, infundibuliform; fruit with beak much longer than the body. Native to Brazil. Sown at the end of April on a bed and under a frame, transplanted into cups and kept under glass, like Melons, until the end of May; then placed on an old bed and generously watered, it acquires enormous proportions. Its branches are as thick as spade handles, and its yellow flowers form abundant conical clusters, followed by large, horny fruits. As curious as the Martynie is, we wouldn't bother with it if its fruits, picked as soon as they form, didn't go into vinegar like gherkins. It can therefore be classified as a vegetable. Here's what William Darlington says about a Martynia, in his Agricultural Botany (Philadelphia, 1847): "This plant, native to Mississippi and the plains of Mexico, has been much cultivated for some time for its singular fruits, which, in their youth, before becoming hard and woody, are used to make the condiment which bears the name of "Martynia".

Pichles." Clémenceau, in the Revue horticole (1867, p. 109), gives the same information: "... Fruits picked very young, when only a few days old, are preserved in vinegar and eaten like our little green gherkins. In this state, the fruit is firm, crisp and of excellent quality. Even though Martynia is not used for food, enthusiasts grow it as an ornamental plant, producing extremely cuCornaret (Martynia lutea). Fig. 23 - Shoot with fruit. They leave an infectious, abominable, stercoral odor on the fingers. Duchesne says that the inhabitants of Cartagena (Colombia) eat the roots of M. Craniolaria Swartz with beef, or candy them for dessert; but Mr. Triana assures us that only the indigent use them. Duchesne adds that, in North America, these roots are used to make a bitter, refreshing beer. CUCURBITA MELANOSPERMA A. Braun. 121 Cucurbitaceae family. Perennial, cullivated as an annual in the Paris climate. Stems spindly, long-creeping; leaves 5-lobed, sinuate; fruit ovoid-rounded, mottled white, full and covered with a subligneous rind. Flesh soft, white, fibrous; seeds blackish or even very black. We borrow from Mr. Ch. Naudin's historical account of the genus in a memoir entitled Nouvelles recherches sur les caractères spécifiques et les variétés du genre Cucurbita (Annales des sciences naturelles, 4° série, t. IV, 1856): "Cucurbita melanosperma was announced in the year 1824, precisely under the name melanosperma, by Al. Braun, in the plant catalog of the Carlsruhe garden; then, in 1837, by M. P.-C. Bouché, of Berlin, under the name C. ficifolia. It is finally described in detail under its first name by Mr. Al. Braun, in the Appendix specierum novarum, etc., of the Berlin Botanical Garden, in 1853, and, according to the same author, in Volume I, page 362 of the 4th series of the Annales des Sciences naturelles, in 1854. "This species has only been known in Europe since the beginning of this century. It is not known precisely from where or by whom it was introduced, but its common name of Siamese gourd leads us to believe that it originated in southern Asia. "It has not yet given rise to any variety in Europe, and is the same today as it was the first day it appeared there. Cucurbita melanosperma has sometimes been called Malabar squash, but we still call it Siam squash (Cucurbita melanosperma), as it appears in the Manuel de l'Amateur des jardins. It is an annual in the north of France, where it freezes in winter, and a perennial in the south, in Hyères, for example, where its stems can survive the winter unaffected by the cold, harden, become somewhat woody and last for several years. Under these conditions, they can grow to an inordinate size, for example 30 to 40 meters long (1). (1) Manuet de l'Amateur des jardins, t. IV, p. 255. The Siam squash is beautiful. It's as big as the Watermelon and looks a bit like it. It is green, speckled with white, with white ribbon-like lines running from the umbilicus and narrowing towards the stalk. Seeds are large and black, sometimes brown. They are very easy to grow, and in no way differ from the pumpkins and gourds usually grown in France. Its yield is considerable. In 1877, 20 vines planted by us on upturned turf produced 130 fruits, weighing a total of 670 kilograms. In 1878, 4 plants gave us 70 fruits, weighing a total of 370 kilograms. The Siam squash runs a long way and requires a lot of space; as it spontaneously pickles, only winter can stop its development. When ripe, its fruits are wrapped in a woody rind that preserves their contents from rot for a long time, to the point where they can be kept intact for a year, two years and perhaps even longer, like Coloquintes. This is a fact to which edible merchants and restaurateurs can testify, who, without using them for any other purpose, place them as ornaments in their displays. In France, the Siamese gourd has hitherto been a mere object of curiosity, but in China, it is used to feed livestock. We know that the herd of yaks sent to France by M. de Montigny, our Consul General in China, in 1854, was accompanied by a supply of melanosperm squash, destined to feed them. On this subject, we quote M. Naudin: "When the yaks were sent from China to France by M. de Montigny in 1854, a large quantity of melanosperm squash was taken on board at Shang-Haï to serve as food for the animals during the journey. When they arrived at the Museum, where we know the yaks stayed for almost a year, there were still several barrels of these perfectly preserved fruits. It would seem, from this fact, that the plant is cultivated on a large scale in China. Its semirusticity below the latitude of Paris, its ease of cultivation and the long life of its fruit may one day make it an economic plant of some importance in Europe, for feeding livestock, especially during the winter". The foregoing prompted us to introduce the Siam pumpkin to the cows of several farmers in our village. Its fruits, divided into three or four pieces, were eagerly eaten by them. The experiment involved 200 or 300 kilograms. The Siam squash forms fruit until the frosts, which means that the fruit is not ripe and cannot be preserved. They should therefore be given to the livestock first. Ripe fruit can be kept indefinitely. Picked while still young, Siamese gourds can be prepared and eaten like cucumbers, which they do not taste like. We've used them in this way, creating a pleasant, delicate dish. In the Canary Islands, the Siamese gourd is grown as a vegetable under the name of Pantana (1). In Spain, Cuba and South America, it is used to make highly prized jams known as Cabellos de Angel or Angel's Hair, the recipe for which is given below. The pulp of the Black Seed Squash contains filaments that are not destroyed by cooking, so they are found when tasted, and (1) De la végétation aux îles Canaries, by Dr. V. Pérez and Dr. Paul Sagot. the impression one gets is singular. Angel Hair is no less a good jam. We said earlier that the Siamese gourd was still only an object of curiosity in France, and that it was only used to garnish or decorate a few storefronts of caterers or edible merchants; we set out to demonstrate that this gourd can be used on any table; that its long, easy shelf-life makes it a food available at all times, and particularly useful in winter; and that its cultivation should therefore be encouraged and propagated. The whole point of this note lies in this demonstration. Mr. J. Mamoz has provided us with the movens. This skilful practitioner, always driven by a desire to be useful, has researched and found several ways of preparing Siam squash, which tend to prove that it should take its place among our usual vegetables. First, we give the recipe for jams, a recipe long practiced in Spanish countries, but absolutely unknown in France: Confitures de Courge de Siam, dites Cheveux d'ange (Cabellos de Angel). A large basin should be used, to ensure easy rotation. When the water is boiling, throw in the squash, broken into pieces, rind and pulp, and even the seeds. After half an hour, three quarters of an hour at the most, the pulp detaches from the rind without too much difficulty, and you can be sure that it is cooked to perfection. Remove from the heat and throw the pieces of squash into cold water. They are successively washed in two or three waters, then, with the handle of a tinned spoon or a silver spoon, the pulp is detached from the rind, and this pulp is again plunged into cold water. The filaments are broken down to isolate each one. Wash again. Seeds and anything else that might alter the jam's whiteness are removed. Press by hand, in a cloth or with a press, to squeeze out all the water. Boil sugar at 32 degrees, or at the lowest setting, and throw in the pulp filaments. Boil slowly, stirring the bottom with a spatula, for a quarter of an hour. When the syrup has reduced to a trickle, remove from the heat. Add to the jam the essence of your choice: lemon, orange, vanilla, etc., and fill into jars. The amount of sugar used should be sufficient to allow the pulp to bathe easily and soak completely in the syrup. We now turn to the new recipes proposed by Mr. Mamoz. Whatever way you want to prepare Siam squash, you should cook it as follows: break or cut a squash into quarters, either twelve or fifteen pieces, and put them in a basin of boiling water. Boil for one hour. You can tell when the squash is cooked enough when the pulp detaches easily from the crust. Then remove from the heat; throw the pieces into cold water; detach the pulp from the rind with a spoon; break up the pulp, which divides into large white threads. The pulp is then pressed to squeeze out the retained water. In this state, it can be used in a variety of ways: 1° If you want to make a fatty soup, cut the pulp into small pieces and use as much as a pigeon's egg for as many soups as you have to serve. It goes without saying that the fatty broth must be salted and seasoned as for any other soup; 2° For milk soup, same proportions. The threads are cut shorter, and this soup is very similar to vermicelli with milk, with the difference that the threads are less melting in the mouth. In our opinion, this soup is preferable to fatty soup; 3° Seasoned with chicken sauce, Siamese squash makes a very good vegetable dish, comparable in taste to pearled bacon or cardoons; 4° Squash can be prepared with tomato sauce. It's a pleasant dish, but one we wouldn't put first; 5° Prepared like macaroni, i.e. with alternating layers of grated cheese and pulp, with butter, breadcrumbs, etc., our squash makes an excellent dish; 6° Siamese squash fritters are perhaps the best of all. Well-pressed and well-divided filaments are placed in a prepared batter and amalgamated. A large handful of these filaments is then thrown into the boiling frying pan. The resulting doughnut is golden, appetizing and very pleasant to eat. To conclude, we transcribe a letter addressed to Mr. Mamoz, who shared it with us: "Colonies agricoles et industrielles de Moisselles et de Fouilleuse, administration, boulevard de Clichy, n° 8. "My dear Mr. Mamoz, You ask me if I'll have more space this year to plant Siam squash. "I am quite willing, in view of the results obtained in the first year, to plant a larger quantity of these gourds, and I would be grateful if you would provide me with the necessary seeds. "I was very satisfied with last year's harvest, although for the first time we didn't do what we should have done in terms of planting time and care. "As far as the use of the product is concerned, of the recipes you gave me, three in particular were successful: gratins, fritters and jams. There is another, much more general use: soup. I intend to put these various methods to good use with the next harvest, and hope to make a very good food from this type of squash. "Signed: L.-P. Bochet, founding director". We lack the space to cultivate the plant from the point of view of livestock feed; but we are convinced that, cultivated for this purpose, it would render serious services. According to information given to us by Mr. J.-R. Villavicencio, the Siam pumpkin is widespread in the hot, humid valleys of Colombia and Ecuador. It is known there under the name of Sambo. It is perennial and yields several harvests. Indians in the poorest provinces use it as a staple food. In the Cauca valley (Colombia), the raw fruit is used as cattle feed. 129 CRAMBE TATARIA Jacq, Miscell, II, p. 274. - Jacq, Icones rariores, tab. 129. - C. tatarica Willd, Species, vol. III, p. 419.- C. laciniata Lamk, Dict. vol. Il, p. 163. Cruciferae family. We reproduce below a notice by M. Alexandre Sébéok de Szent-Nillôs, a Hungarian nobleman, published in 1779 by Jacquin, in his Miscellanea austriaca (1). (1) Jacquin, Miscellanea austriaca ad botanicam chemiam et histoiam na turalem spectantia, cum figuris, vol. Il, p. 274. We owe the translation of this interesting note to Dr. Ed. Bamberger, assistant librarian at the Muséum. We are pleased to be able to express our gratitude to him here. In fact, Dr. Bamberger made the following remarks about the way in which he handled certain parts of his translation: "While tightening the text as much as possible," says M. Bamberger, "it has been necessary to rectify some of the errors. Bamberger, "I had to resort to a few periphrases, and sometimes sacrifice rigorous versioning and stylistic correctness, in order to convey the author's thoughts. I was faced with a triple obstacle: 1° a Latin text full of low Latin expressions; 2° the transition from an ancient language to French, which is always perilous in scientific matters; 3° a work of criticism dating from a scientific era more than a century before ours. "I have therefore taken care: 1° to quote the Latin text whenever it was obscure, so as to leave competent people free to criticize the chosen version; 2° to always put the Latin name next to the French name, and very often to stick to the former in cases where I ran the risk of attributing to one plant a name used today by another; 3° to put the Latin geographical names next to their translation, and sometimes even to limit myself to the former, as a modern region does not strictly agree with an ancient one. The Crambe Tataria. "Dissertalion inaugurale médicale soumise à la discussion publique, by Alexandre Sébéok de Szent-Niklôs, Hungarian nobleman. Vienna, June 5, 1799 (1)." Crambe tataria foliis multifidis. Tataria ungarica Clus. Hist. p. CXCI. Tataria ungarice edulis, panacis heraclei folio, semine libanotidis cachryophoræ. Bauh. Hist. vol. III, part. 2, p. 163. Panaci Heracleo similis ungarica. Bauh. pin. 153. Cachrys ungarica, panacis folio. Tourn. Inst., 325. The first, most famous and tireless of all plant researchers in Pannonia and Austria (1), Charles Clusius, offered the learned world a Notice on this plant; it is not inappropriate to reproduce his entire description here. He wrote "This plant, whose root is very thick and very long, is not commonly found; in fact, I remember having received some the size of an arm and the length of a cubit, and some larger, from the illustrious Lord Balthasar of Bathyan, who, in my favor, ordered me to bring some from Transdanubian Hungary (2) so that I could import them into the garden in Vienna. It produced leaves that differed little from those of Rave (3) in their incisions and cut-outs, but were shorter, more like the leaves of Panax Heracleum, covered with a rough, bristly down, contrasting with the greenery in their paleness (4); others, divided by slightly shallower, equally rough cut-outs, from the middle of which emerge (1) Stirpium pannonicarum austriarumque. (2) Ungaria transdanubiana. (3) Rapi. (4) Ex viridi pallentia. ge a stem a cubit or more high, an inch thick, ridged, concave and knotty, rough like the leaves; it is embraced by means of a wide pedicel by small, multifid leaves, equally rough thanks to the down that covers them. The stem ends in an umbel, similar to the umbels of Panax Heracleum, in which the flowers are similar in shape and color (1). Faded flowers were followed by rare (because some flowers are not fertilized), very thick fruit, rather similar to the large, ridged fruit of Libanotis cachryfera. "I cultivated these roots for two years, before a single one produced a stem, flowers and seed; then they rotted and gave off such a foul smell that they were thrown out of the garden. "The Hungarians neighboring Erlau (2), as well as those living immediately beyond the borders of Dacia, feed on them in years of famine and misery, instead of bread; this was assured to me not only by this illustrious man (3), but by other distinguished men who lived in this province. The result was that I was left in doubt as to whether it was identical with the Baltracan born in Tartary (4), as recalled by Josaphat Barbarus, a Venetian pratrician, in a letter to Peter Barocci, bishop of Padua, added at the end of the last chapter of his narrative published on his departure for Persia. "(1) Summus caulis in umbellam desinebat, Panacis Heraclei umbellis similem, in quibus flores et forma pares et colore. (2) Agriæ vicini. (3) Heros ille illustris ? (4) An eadem esset cum Baltracan in Tartaria nascente. name of Tartaria; indeed, like the Germans, they commonly name Tatars those we call Tartars. "Thus concludes Clusius; Jean Bauhin copied him in his Histoire des Plantes; but it is to be believed that neither he, nor any other author of those he cited, except Clusius, saw Hungarian Tartaria (1). "All of them, imitating Clusius, wrongly classified it among the Umbelliferae. It must be admitted that, in the first month of stem growth, as long as the plant still bears its curled, folded leaves (2) and its flowers closed and clustered on the ramuscules, it resembles, in a way, a Heracleum, in such a way as to be able to impose it on a botanist; but it differs greatly from it after its blossoming, so much so that I cannot wonder enough that Clusius, the most exact man besides, could have confused a cruciform plant (3) with an Umbellifera. "Indeed, we find no mention of it in his Histoire des Plantes de Pannonie et d'Autriche, published in 1583, and, before his return, he died in Vienna in 1587. It may well follow that this description was less well elaborated by him; perhaps it was from memory that he entrusted it to paper after the plant's death; what seems likely to corroborate this conjecture is that he did not depict such a rare plant and that, contrary to his habit, Clusius said nothing of its (1) Sed nec hunc, nec præter Clusium auctorem alium ex citatis ullum Tatariam ungaricam vidisse, credibile est. (2) Dum planta folia adhucdum sua rugose complicata... gerit. (3) This was Tournefort's name for the plants that made up the fifth class of his system. "For a long time now, Jacquin's illustrious professor had been possessed by the desire to see and examine the plant he knew from Clusius; an opportunity soon presented itself: The famous imperial astronomer Maximilien Hell was leaving for Agria (the Erlau of the Germans, the Eger of the Hungarians), a town in the county of Hevesch (1), in Hungary; Jacquin asked him, if he came across the plant called Tataria by the locals, to be so kind as to bring it back to him on his return, or to send it to him. "This distinguished man took care to transport to Vienna two roots which he had had dug up; he brought back dried samples of the flowering plant himself, and, on the way back, took care of the roots which, after a six-week journey in summer, arrived putrefied and fetid. One of them was four feet long and, though truncated, almost as thick as a leg. The dried samples indicated, against all expectations, a cruciform plant. "The very learned and skilful surgeon of the Caramellia regiment, H. Engelsdorfer, also carried out this mission, and more happily, in 1777; he sent to the Botanical Garden, at the request of the illustrious professor, several roots that he had been able to collect that were shorter and thinner; they recovered very well and produced ripe flowers and fruit every year. "He answered several questions (although he was unable to answer the others because of his sudden departure for the Bohemian army): 1° that the plant was now called Tataria by the locals; 2° that it (2) Comitatus Hevessiensis in Hungaria... grew in the fields, in the middle of the harvest, in a soil composed of fertile, black earth, one foot deep, under which lay a three-foot layer of yellowish clay, then, finally, and below, sand; that the root of the adult plant penetrated to this depth; that because of this, it was difficult to remove it whole, because the hole, as soon as you reached the sand, filled abundantly with gushing water: 3° that every year, the roots were torn up and cut by the plough, and that, even when thus wounded, they still grew back; 4° that nowhere around Erlau did he discover that they were consumed, except by children who ate the cooked root because of its sweet taste (1); 5° that some were eaten by hares, which are very numerous in this area. "It grows not only around Erlau, but also in other parts of Hungary. The servant of a very learned professor, who was of Hungarian nationality, having seen some brought in, immediately recognized it, referred to it by the Hungarian word: Tatar-Kenyér, or Tartarian Bread, and declared that he had eaten some in Debrezin. On his suggestion, we made a salad from the fresh, raw root, peeling it, cutting it crosswise into thin slices and seasoning it with oil, vinegar and salt; it pleased everyone. "A pleasant taste is also obtained by cooking the still tender stem, before the flowers have developed, and preparing it in the same way as cauliflower (2). Its use is not, however, very convenient, unless you take care to remove the bark fibers, which are numerous, tough and difficult to chew. (1) Ob dulcedinem. (2) Brassicæ caulifloræ adinstar... "At the same time, the Reverend Norbert Boccius, superior of the Order of St. John of God in Feldsperg and a tireless researcher of Moravian plants, also made known this plant discovered by him and a learned professor in that country. He found it growing abundantly in vines, in clay soil, near the village of Hurtau, near Aussitz; it is known there, he says, as Hieronymus-Würzel (1), or St. Jerome's root, and is carried to Bohemia, where farmers give it to cows to increase their milk. ." Clusius' conjecture that our plant is identical with the Batracan of the Tartars is destroyed by this: Josaphat Barbarus split the bark of the Batracan at the moment of ripening, separated it from the woody part (hence the conclusion that the stem appears to be perennial and frutescente) and added that the seed was of a strong odor, but nevertheless of a pleasant flavor; none of this relates to our plant. "I think it far more worthy of attention that Clusius says, according to others it's true, that in certain parts of Hungary, the inhabitants feed on this root in times of famine and misery. In any case, the root's long lifespan, its volume in the adult state, its resistance even to deep lesions, its resistance to the cold of our winters, etc., all encourage its cultivation, or at least its preservation, (1) Racine de Saint-Jérôme (St. Jerome's Root) seems to me to be the correct translation; the word saint is often neglected by Germans, who give the fruit of the Carob tree, commonly known as Pain de Saint-Jean (St. John's Bread), the name Johannisbrod. (Translator's note.) (2) The conditions in which this plant grows naturally, as indicated above, suggest that it prefers open areas. "Its Hungarian name of Tataria, which Clusius believed to be derived from the Tartars, so concerned our illustrious professor that he sent a drawing of the plant to the famous Pallas in Petersburg, asking him if he had encountered it in his travels. "Here is the scholar's reply: "The Crambé, whose drawing you submitted to me, corresponds in every way to the one that grows here in this vast southern plain stretching from the Dnieper (1) to the Jaik, the Rymnus of the ancients. Nowhere does it extend beyond the 51st degree of north latitude. It likes dry soils, as well as loamy (provided it's not salty), sandy and humus-rich soils. However, it prefers the depressions found in arid plains, varying in size but never reaching, in dry terrain, the size of the drawing transmitted to me. In succulent soils near rivers, where it also spreads, it produces the largest, most pungent-tasting roots. In dry soils, it acquires the taste of turnip (2); the Cossacks who inhabit the deserts of the Don (3) eat it avidly, raw or cooked. Its vulgar name in this country is Katràn, with the epithet bjeloi or white, to distinguish it from a kind of Statice growing in the same regions, multi-stemmed, very branchy, with thick roots, which is harvested in abundance, in the absence of Oak bark, for the preparation of leather, because it is very suitable for this use; it is called Krasnoi Katràn because of its red flowers. Near Irtisch (4), I found very rare individuals of Crambé à (1)... In Borzsthene (2)... Napi... (3)... Tanaensibus in desertis. (4) Ad Irtin... with less laciniated leaves and sometimes in bloom, so that we can judge their difference. Moreover, on our Crambé, which blooms near the Volga, there is an abundance of a particular Meloë, which is completely related to Linné's Meloë de Syrie (1), but always smaller than the Meloë vésicant (2), and with a black head instead of the blue of the rest of the body." "Here is a description of the plant, based on live specimens in the Garden, and others dried from the forests (3), as well as on sketches: "Perennial root, long fusiform, as thick as the arm and thicker in the adult state, two to four feet long, cylindrical, simple or divided inferiorly into limb-like forms (4); it blackens in the air; fresh, it is even black; washed and dried, it is rather grayish; it emits few fibrils throughout its extent. Inside, it is clearly fleshy, whitish with a slight dirty-yellow tinge, with irregular radiating lines; the flavor is mild, with no real pungency. Kept in the air, it gradually shrinks, becomes rough and covered with protrusions, hardens, ceases to be like itself (5) and becomes useless. "The radical leaves that first appear are often small and entire; then they themselves, as well as the stem leaves and those of the lower branches (6) are variously and irregularly multifid, sometimes very ample, pinnatifid, of decomposed form or (1) Meloe syriaca. (2) Meloe vesicatoria. (3) Instead of forests, we should always understand, no doubt, the state of nature. (4) ... Aut inferne incrura divisa. (5) Fitque sui dissimilis... (6) Et caulina atque ramea inferiora. overdecomposed (1), sometimes divided into oblong, toothed and acute strips, of a slightly glaucous green, glabrous on both sides; the upper leaves of the stem and branches are simple and oblong. The angular petioles are glabrous in the larger leaves, but the petioles of the smaller ones, like the ribs, are very thick and prominent, and the veins are bristling with white hairs. However, this hispidity and the shape of the leaves not only vary in the different plants, but also in the different parts of each plant, so that a description that would suit each and every one would be in vain applicable here. "The flowers, which are very numerous, spread out in terminal, stalked corymbs, exude a strong, pleasant honeyed odor, so much so that it is as if you were approaching the nostrils of a bee's comb; The calyx is greenish-yellow, the petals milky-white, the anthers greenish, the ovaries green, the stigma yellow, the fruit pea-sized, consisting of a shiny green fleshy envelope, but dry, rough and poorly colored when ripe, and a seed with a blackish envelope and a pale yellow embryo (2), with a slightly acrid and unpleasant taste. It begins to flower in mid-April. The fruit ripens in June, but only a few reach the desired stage of development; most remain small and unripe (3). Considerations relating to fruiting (4) must be accepted as such. (1)... Decomposite vel supra decomposite pinnatifida (2)... Nucleo... (3) Steriles perstant. (4) In fructificatione character observatur nune subjungendus. "Calyx tetraphyllous, leaflets oblong, obtuse, concave, canaliculate, spreading, deciduous. "Corolla with four petals, oval-faced, very obtuse, flat, almost twice the length of the calyx, inserted on a short, erect tab. "Stamens. Six thick, erect threads, two of which are opposite, the same length as the calyx and simple; the other four are longer than the first and bifurcated at the apex, one branch of this bifurcation directed towards the ovary and sterile, the other slightly inclined outwards and antheriferous. Incumbent anthers. "A green glandula alternates with the threads belonging to the large stamens. "Ovary ovate, style absent, stigma capitate. "Fruit globose, unilocular, indehiscent. "Single, subrounded seed. "On the plate (which has not been included with the Mélanges, due to its size, but which can be studied in the fascicule of drawings) (1) are represented: one of the least large roots, whole, but much reduced; then the upper part of the same, of natural size; the top offers several sprays deprived of leaves as they are in winter; at one of the sprays, contrary to the reality of this season, are painted the bases of three perfectly recognizable leaves. At the top of the drawing, where the caulescent part begins, there are no radical leaves (2). Then there is a flowering branch, the first of its kind in the world. (1) The fascicle of drawings referred to here is none other than Jacquin's remarkable work entitled: Icones plantarum rariorum. The plate devoted to our plant is the one hundred and twenty-ninth. Part of the drawing is colored. (2) Caput autem caulescens caret plerumque foliis radicalibus. which arises from the stem. I have seen it emerge in such abundance from the stem of a cultivated plant, that it occupied a space four feet in diameter with its tightly-packed flowers (1). "There is also half of a large leaf, a whole, toothed radical leaf of recent growth, a fruiting shoot and the embryo of a seed. "Against all expectations, analysis of the root, treated by distillation, produced results quite different from those usually obtained with cruciform plants. It yielded an aqueous mucus, an acidic spirit (2), an empyreumatic oil; having obtained the above, it was treated in a new vessel and at a higher temperature; a small quantity of a volatile alzaline spirit was obtained, just as with other plants an acidic spirit is abundantly obtained. By incineration, the residue yields fixed vegetable alkaline salt in great abundance. C. Tataria seeds are difficult to obtain. We asked in vain for them in Pesth and Vienna; they finally came to us from Saint Petersburg, thanks to the benevolent enterprise of MM. Vilmorin, who obtained them for us from M. Regel. But the learned director of the Bolanic Garden in St. Petersburg was unable to provide us with the information we wanted on the plant's cultivation and uses. To the two questions put to him on our behalf by Dr. Bretschneider, with the kindness he always shows us, Mr. Regel replied that Crambe Tataria grows wild in southern Russia; that it is not cultivated(1).... Ut hæc aream occuparet diametri quatuor pedum densissimam. (2) -. Spiritum... vait nulle part; qu'il n'avait lui-même fait aucune expérience concernant cette espèce et sa culture; qu'à Saint-Pétersbourg on ne pouvait pas cultiver du lout. The seeds we received gave us five plants in the spring of 1881; they did not flower. In 1882, the plant developed somewhat, but has not yet flowered. Finally, in 1883, it flowered and gave us an extraordinary quantity of seeds, which, when sown immediately, i.e. around July-August, did not germinate. As you can see, we have made very little progress in the experimental cultivation of C. Tataria. We doubt very much whether it will ever be a vegetable to be introduced into our kitchen gardens, but it does interest us a great deal, and we'll tell you why. In France, little use is made of these light, easily digested starches, particularly suitable for feeding children and convalescents, of which considerable quantities are imported from England under the name of Arrow-root (see the chapters on Maranta arundinacea and Canna). The name Arrow-root is wrongly given to the starch obtained from various Canna, Tacca pinnatifida and, according to Simmonds, a few other plants. The real Arrowroot comes from M. arundinacea, but we can't say whether this is superior to the similar starches often substituted. Liuto's Chuno is another type of Arrowroot (see the chapter on Alstrœmère Ligtu). Leaving aside Sago, Salep and Tapioca (Manioc), this brings the number of plants from which beneficial starch is extracted to four main species: Maranta arundinacea; Canna; Tacca pinnatifida; Liuto (Alstrœmeria Ligtue). However, we are inclined to believe that the roots of C. Tataria are also suitable for the produclior of an Arrow-root. C. Tataria is classified as a food plant by the ancients. "Crambe Tataria Jacq., a plant from Hungary, appears to be the Chara whose roots fed Caesar's soldiers in Albania (Caes., B. C., 3, 48); Hungarians still make bread from it in times of famine (1)." Crambe cordifolia Steven, from Persia and the Caucasus to Thibet and the Himalayas, at altitudes of up to 14,000 feet, is a beautiful perennial, very hardy in our climate and extremely easy to grow. Its roots and leaves are edible. Harvested as they begin to develop, the leaves are tender and can replace cabbage, but their flavor is milder. PARA SPILANTHES OLERACEA L. Compositae family. Annual plant, native to South America, which, along with its fusca variety (S. fusca Desf.), is cultivated in our gardens as an odontalgic. According to P. Joigneaux (2), the leaves of Para cress have a very penetrating flavor that provokes salivation and hence digestion. Its flowers (1) Les plantes alimentaires des anciens, by Édouard Martens. (2) Le jardin potager, by P. Joigneaux, page 307. pressed between the jaws are reputed to cure even the most unbearable toothache. It is a very energetic antiscorbutic, and highly esteemed in tropical regions, where it advantageously replaces Cochlearia, which would not grow there. It is an ingredient in many toothpastes. Rousseau, Histoire naturelle et médicale du Cresson de Para, 1825, has highlighted the merits of this plant, which is extremely easy to grow, even in our climate. The hot, peppery flavor of Para watercress makes it highly sought-after as a condiment in hot countries. It is used in small quantities and well chopped, in combination with salads. R. P. Camboué sent us some seeds from Madagascar, with the following note: "Anamalahobé, a very tasty vegetable in Tananarive; grows well in the vicinity of the capital, where it is cultivated in fairly large quantities. We cannot recommend Cresson de Para as a vegetable in Europe, where we have Cresson de fontaine, which has the same properties and a flavor better suited to our palate. The same cannot be said for tropical regions, where the lack of fresh vegetables is almost absolute, and where antiscorbutics are necessary. This is why certain plants are sought after under the name of cress, which if cultivated in Europe would seem useless to us: Lepidium chilense Desf., Chili. - Piscidium Forst (L. oleraceum Ait.), Oceania. - virginicum L., Sept. Amer. Senebiera pinnatifida DC, Temperate subtropics. Europe, America, Oceania. Nasturtium indicum DC, India. Gynandropsis pentaphylla DC. tropical Asia, Africa and America. Fig. 26 - Gynandropsis pentaphylla DC. This plant, which belongs to the Capparidae family, was sent to us in 1897 by Baron d'Yvoire, under the name Momendu Ajoouo, which he had received from a missionary living in the Lake Nyassa region (Africa), with the note: "Cruciferous plant whose young shoots are prepared and eaten like spinach". In the French West Indies, this plant is used to make the famous Calalou dish, known as Mougambi. It is an annual and can be grown without difficulty in our climate. Kan-lu, Tsao che tsan; Tignou tzé. China. Tsyo rogi, Choro-gi, Daima giku, Daima gik. Japan. STACHYS AFFINIS Bnge, Enum. pl. chin. n° 289; S. Sieboldi Miq, Prol. Fl. Jap. p. 44; S. tuberifera Ndn. Bull. Soc. d'Accl., 1887, p. 394. Fam. des Labiées Synonyms: English: Chinese Artichoke; German! Knollenziest; Italian: Tuberina (1). DESCRIPTION. - MORPHOLOGY Perennial plant. Stump emitting numerous underground, tuberous rhizomes. Tubers formed by a succession of nodules, rather resembling the swollen root collars of rosary oats (Ar(1) G.-S. Bullo, Raccoglitore Anno, XIII, series III. 10 LE POTAGER D'UN CURIEUX Fig. 27 - Crosne (Stachys affinis), Iubercules. V CROSNE Fig. 28 - Crosne (Stachys "ffinis), flowering branch. 147 rhenatherum elatius, var. bulbosum Gaud.; Avena precatoria Thuill.) Stem simple or stalked, erect or flattened at base, quadrangular, 25 to 40 cm high, hispid at corners. Leaves opposite, petiolate, rough, hispid, cordate at base, acuminate, crenate-toothed; lower ones ovate-oblong, gradually smaller as they grow higher. Flowers sessile, grouped 6-4 in distinct false whorls. The flower consists of a subcampanulate or infundibuliform calyx, obconical, with equal, acute teeth. Corolla 10 to 24 millimeters long, purpurine, with exserted tube, before a ring of hairs inside. "Tubers have the same morphological value as those of the potato. They are swollen twigs, tuberculated under the influence of the underground station. As they swell, each internode of the modified branch loses, so to speak, in length what it gains in diameter. "On a cross-section of one of these swollen, perfectly round internodes, we find, as in the aerial branch, four libero-ligneous bundles occupying the place of the rounded angles. The bark is not very thick, while the medullary tissues develop considerably, forming almost the entire mass of the tuber. Lateral roots, arising directly from the node, form on either side of the angular libero-ligneous bundle, so that there are eight roots at the same level, a little above the emergence of the leaves. Other lateral roots may form above these, always in the same way. When the tuber is planted, the buds that develop into twigs and stems are those closest to the upper end (1). Habitat. - Denominations. "In a memoir entitled Fragmenta floræ orientalis (Bulletin de la Société impériate des naturalistes de Moscou), vol. 54, no. 4, M. de Maximowicz, a learned Russian botanist and himself a well-known explorer of eastern Asia, has given brief but precise information on the Labiatae in question, concerning its history, homeland, synonymy and, I might add, dietary characteristics. In the above-mentioned memoir, it is stated in substance: Stachys affinis Bunge, Enum. pl. ch. bor. n° 289. S. Sieboldi Miq, Prolusio, p. 44 (description follows). Hab. - Northern China to Kantaï (Bunge); Peking (Tatarinow), where the plant is cultivated under the name Kan-lu, because of its fleshy root, edible according to Skatschkow and Dr. Bretschneider, who found it on Mount Po-hua-shan. In Japan, the plant has been observed in Yedo, where it is rarely cultivated under the name Daima Gik (Siebold). The Japanese botanist Tchonowski observed it in the principality of Nambu (M. de Maximowicz does not say whether it is cultivated or spontaneous)... Stachys affinis Bunge is known to be cultivated in several Chinese provinces. It is discussed at some length in volume IV (vegetable plants) of the great Chinese encyclopedia entitled Tchi wou ming chi thou Kuo, where the plant is (1) P. Maury, Association française pour l'avancement des sciences; Oran, 1888. skilfully illustrated as Tsao che tsan (silkworm stone plant). I am indebted to the kindness of a priest of the Missions étrangères, the R. P. Mutel, a learned sinologist, for this information. Mutel, a learned sinologist, the translation of the article concerning the Stachys affinis, and I give it here, deleting the passages unnecessary to the subject: "The Tsao che tsan was revealed and mentioned for the first time in the great Flora called Pen-tsao-hou-eipien: it is the Kan-lu (sweet dew); its stem and flowers resemble those of the aquatic thyme (Lophantus?); the root has unions (united pearls). In northern lands, it is grown "extensively as a vegetable. Tou-peu-Tsucun (a poet of some sort) says this in his poem "du Ta-houah-tsai" (vegetable from Jede's ring): the plant "called Kan-lu, what does it produce? At its decline, it bears "Jade rings, bound together and yet mobile, ind" hanging from each other. These rings are precisely the "vegetable" we're talking about. The above passage from the Chinese encyclopaedia describes the Kan-lu in an entirely oriental manner, i.e. by comparison with objects we can actually see. Nevertheless, it shows that it is cultivated mainly in the northern provinces, and that the describer's imagination was particularly struck by the pearly, jade-like appearance of its rhizomes. Elsewhere, it is said that Kan-lu holds a place among the food plants listed in the Kiou-Hung-Pentza (Flora of the Salvation from Dearth), a work often cited in Chinese books. In this respect, Kan-lu deserves attention, and the multiplicity of its rhizomes, combined with their nutritional qualities, indicates that its cultivation can, to a certain extent, offer a resource not to be scorned. Very recently, Mr. Delavay, a missionary in Yunnan, sent the Museum Herbarium some fine specimens of Stachys affinis in flower and fruit; they were accompanied by the following label: "N° 3079. Red flowers. Cultivated for its edible root; small tubers in rosaries. Chinese name: Tignou-tze (earth button). Harvested at Tcheou-choui, near Tapin-tze, September 23, 1887." Another label reads: "The woods at Talong-tan, near Tapin-tze." This shows that the plant is not only cullivated in northern China, but also in the Austro-Western provinces of the empire, where the name given to it is not that of Kan-lu, no doubt due to a particular dialect, but which nonetheless offers a meaning analogous to that of: united pearls, ring of jade, which, as I said above, serves elsewhere as a term of comparison for the rhizomes of the plant. Siebold received Stachys affinis from the Decima Garden in Japan, but he seems to have been unaware that it was a food plant and gave it no name. For his part, M. de Maximowicz saw it in Yédo, in the gardens, where, he says, it is rarely cultivated and where Dr. Savatier collected a single specimen. Miquel, Prolusio, p. 44, had given Siebold's plant the name Stachys Sieboldi; but, with the help of the extensive material he obtained, Maximowicz demonstrated that it did not differ from Stachys affinis. Finally, more recently, M. Faurie, a missionary in Japan, sent us a specimen of this species, with this note: "Aomori (prov. sept. de Nippon); the plant is cultivated there for its root. It is also shown under the name Tsyo rogi, Chorogi, in CROSNE, volume XI, plate 13, of the Japanese collection entitled Somokou dusets (1)". INTRODUCTION IN FRANCE - CULTIVATION AND YIELD In the spring of 1882, we received from the Société nationale d'Acclimatation a box containing rhizomes of Stachys affinis, a plant that had long been on our desiderata lists. The rhizomes had been sent by Dr. Bretschneider, physician to the Russian Legation in Peking, who was kind enough to respond to a request for useful plants from China addressed to our Minister in Peking, Mr. Bourée, by the Société nationale d'Acclimatation. All but five or six of these rhizomes had rotted during the trip, but the plant's power to multiply is such that the loss of most of them left us with no regrets. In the first year, every tuber planted on an old bed gave us a satisfactory harvest, and by the end of the second year, the clumps left in place gave us two to three hundred to one. On January 4 1888, M. Paillieux presented the following note to the members of the plant section of the Société nationale d'Acclimatation: "Gentlemen, if you will allow me, I will speak to you again today about Stachys. It will probably be the last time. "You know that, for three years, I have offered and given to everyone, in and outside Paris, seedlings of our new vegetable. Last spring, however, I predicted that it would not be offered on the market again this winter, and that it would only be grown in a few amateur (1) A. Franchet, Le Jardin, 1889. gardens. It was impossible to predict when the plant would be adopted by growers and enter general consumption. "Now, I'm loaded with years and in a hurry to act before age forbids any work. "I knew that bulbous chervil, introduced into Western Europe in 1726, had only been known in France for fifty years, and was still not widely grown today. "If I consulted my memories, they would tell me that, in the space of a hundred years, only two new vegetables had been introduced to France: the Chinese yam, which has almost been abandoned, and the bulbous chervil, which I mentioned earlier. "I wanted to spare Stachys the long incubation period undergone by previously introduced plants. I wanted to try to achieve in three months the propagation of the excellent vegetable that our Society has introduced, and which, in my opinion, is destined to occupy a large place in general consumption. "I thought I could only achieve my goal by immediately becoming a producer and seller. The foregoing considerations led me to this decision. "I rented some land around my garden and planted Stachys, thus securing a harvest which is not yet complete and which will give me around 3,000 kilograms of tubers. "Convinced that the words Stachys affinis could not be pronounced by our cooks, I named the tubers Crosnes, which is the name of my village. "I had three thousand prospectus cards printed to publicize the vegetable, which have been a great help to me. I distributed some of them to the members present at the January 4 meeting, and I'm making some available to you again today. "From the last days of November, I've been making room, looking for buyers, rejected by most, welcomed by a few. "At the moment, I have ten buyers in Paris whose sales are increasing daily. I ship our vegetable to Lille, Roubaix, Amiens, Reims and even Brussels. I'm expecting requests from a few other towns. "I have the satisfaction of seeing that purchases are constantly being renewed. Our success is not a flash in the pan; it's a fire that won't go out. "I had my tubers tasted by Brébant, the friendly and renowned restaurateur you all know. He recognized their merit, put them on his daily menu, and included them in the Japanese salad, the fashionable dish whose recipe is pleasantly given in Francillon, and the author is now familiar with Stachys. "MM. X..., from Paris, have used 100 kilograms of Stachys to make their Pickles. They are sending some to a house in the provinces, and wrote to me on January 30th: "Yesterday, Saturday, the courier gave us a case of Stachys on your behalf. Please send us more: "30 kilograms next Tuesday; "30 kilograms next Saturday. "In case your crop runs out, please reserve about 100 kilograms for us. These Stachys are all shipped to the provinces and eaten as fresh vegetables. Success seems assured, and we believe that next year you can produce them without fear. "L'Art culinaire of January 30 contains a small note on Crosnes, with a very exact figure of the tuber: "Under the name Crosnes du Japon, the Société nationale d'Acclimatation has just introduced and wants to propagate this new vegetable. We can only applaud this attempt. "Appearing only in winter, when our excellent French products are in very short supply, this vegetable can be of real use in culinary service. Its shape is original and graceful; its taste is reminiscent of the artichoke. It is very easy and quick to cook, and can be used in a variety of ways. "To spread the use of our vegetable more and more, I have just made an agreement with an active and intelligent young man who will continue the work begun. "Unfortunately, it is doubtful whether my harvest will meet demand during the month of February. I couldn't possibly undertake a more extensive cultivation. "As soon as Stachys appears at Les Halles, do I need to tell you that your colleague will disappear as a salesman and return purely and simply to being the researcher of new plants to whom you have always given your sympathy and encouragement? Success was thus assured from the start of 1887. It grew steadily. Mr. Paillieux was overwhelmed with requests, which he met to the best of his ability. Hundreds of publications, in France and abroad, special journals, bulletins of horticultural societies, scientific bulletins of major newspapers, celebrated the new plant. As early as 1891, commission agents at Les Halles received and sold large quantities of tubers. Our vegetable could be seen in almost every fruit store in the capital; it even made an appearance in the small cars of four-season merchants. It's now a household name. The price has dropped considerably. Consumption will be widespread. It's a given, a definitive conquest. In Le Potager d'un curieux, 1st edition (1885), p. 88, we were the first to mention the cultivation of Stachys, twelve years ago. We publish hereafter all the information we have gathered since then, indicating, where appropriate, the sources from which we drew it. The plant is hardy. Since 1882-83, it has withstood our winters without suffering. It will certainly withstand the coldest weather. We've been growing Crosne for fifteen years, which allows us to indicate a very simple cultivation procedure. The tubers should be planted in February, after which they would begin to vegetate. They are very willing to come out of their rest. If a plantation is covered with manure so that frost or snow do not interrupt the uprooting, the rhizomes germinate immediately. We can't say which soil Crosne prefers. All soils are suitable, but it seems to us that in a compact, clay soil, digging would be very laborious and too often interrupted by frost, snow or rain. We therefore recommend choosing soil that is a little sandy, or in any case well loosened. We plant in holes 20 centimetres deep, spaced 40 centimetres apart in all directions, with three tubers per hole. We repeat the hoeing until October 1, to keep the soil perfectly clean. When we do the last hoeing, we think it's a good idea to lightly butter the clumps. We don't hoe after October 1, because the tool would cut the rhizomes that extend horizontally. In the Paris climate, we believe it's wise not to start uprooting before December 1st. Tubers are not perfect until then. They begin to vegetate again on March 1st. In home gardens, we pull as the table requires. There's no need to worry about frost. In the field, large quantities are harvested at a time for sale. Tubers can be stored in sand, in a dry place. They do not fear the cold, but when exposed to the air, they wither and are no longer presentable after twelve or fifteen days. Yield, as always, is more or less considerable, depending on the fertilization and the care given to the crop. We dare not say what it might be in a garden, but we have seen that it always exceeds expectations. In large-scale cultivation, we think that we could count on 12,000 kilograms per hectare; but the plant is above all, and can be exclusively, destined for small-scale cultivation, because of the difficulties of uprooting and preserving the produce. No matter how much care is taken in uprooting, a host of offshoots appear in May. This plant can be transplanted and produce a harvest equal to that produced by the tubers; but if left in place, it is unpleasantly surprised, when winter comes, to have almost no harvest at all, as the plant quickly exhausts the soil. Exposure to too much heat is not without danger. It's true that we've only once lost a plantation to sunburn, and we've always feared that this accident would happen again. In the south of France, Stachys must be grown in the shade, and requires frequent, moderate watering. Dr. Louis Planchon, who has experimented with growing this plant in Montpellier, tells us that he has obtained good results only thanks to these conditions; he adds, moreover, that nowhere in the region has Stachys flowered. We know of no other enemy to Crosne than the white grub. It takes around 600 tubers to make 1 kilogram. INTRODUCTION IN OTHER PARTS OF EUROPE Crosne has spread from France to England, where it is known as Chinese Artichoke. Numerous cultivation trials have been carried out, as evidenced by articles published in horticultural journals such as The Gardeners' Chronicle, The Garden, etc.; however, we do not know whether the plant is widely cultivated in this country, or whether it has definitively entered the consumer market. In Belgium, it was cultivated soon after its introduction, and success was at least as complete as in France. In Germany, where the plant is known as Knollenziest, Crosne is cultivated only by a few horticultural enthusiasts, but efforts are being made to spread it. According to Villkomm, attempts made in Austria suggest that Crosne can thrive in open fields in all parts of the empire, and that it would be worthwhile extending its cultivation. In Italy, as early as 1887, Messrs Ingegnoli frères of Milan recommended its cultivation; the plant, which they had been experimenting with for some time, had given very good results, and they wrote that it "deserved one of the best places in vegetable gardens". The Italian newspaper Raccoglitore had written about it in 1886; in 1887, various cultivation experiments were carried out in the province of Padua and in the Royal potager agraire, thanks to the active president of the Comice agraire de Padoue, Professor Keller. Mr. Bullo, in agreement with Professor Saccardo, proposed the name Tuberina for the plant, which has the merit of being easy and brief. He published in the Raccoglitore, vol. XIII, ser. III, a long note on the history, uses and cultivation of this new vegetable. We have extracted some interesting information on the introduction of Crosne in Italy. "In Lombardy, several individuals grow Stachys affinis; however, it has not yet appeared on the Milan market. On the contrary, in Monza, near Milan, it is very much in vogue: we started growing it in the Royal Park, and, having pleased the court, all the city's families wanted to grow it. It is sometimes seen on the Monza market and, in memory of the protection given to this vegetable, it is called Pomme de terre de la Reine (Pomo di terra della Regina). I would have really liked to keep this name," he says, "but thinking that it is too long to become popular quickly, and that there are already other plants with almost similar names, I decided to propose the name Tuberina, which is shorter and easier, and which cannot be confused with others. "I was supported in this idea by competent people who encouraged me to make the proposal. "In Piedmont, Stachys is known by name only; we hope soon to see it cultivated there too, at least on a trial basis. "In the southern provinces, it is still unknown; the Société d'Acclimatation in Palermo is currently trying to make it known in Sicily, and the Dammann house in Saint-Giovianni di Teduccio has been experimenting, but, to tell the truth, with unsatisfactory results, obviously due to the too-high summer temperature. "Italo Mazzon, the excellent poultry farmer from Padua and lover of all that is new and useful, cultivated it as early as 1887, and this is what he wrote to me on the subject: "Stachys affinis is a plant that deserves to be cultivated on a large scale, especially in sandy soils. The tests I have carried out have convinced me that, in clay soils, although quite productive, it would not be to be recommended, because the rather fragile roots would tend to lengthen and the nodes to enlarge. A tuber of any size, planted in suitable soil, yields about 1.5 kilograms of product, and many other tubers about the size of a finger. "I was already convinced that Stachys must give a large product, but experiments made at the Royal potager agraire have never given the product noted by Mazzon, and this must depend absolutely on the quality of the soil where the experiment was made. "Mazzon sent Stachys tubers to the Treviso exhibition in 1888 and won a medal. CHEMICAL COMPOSITION OF TUBERCULES (1) "Of particular interest are the tubers known in the trade as "Crosne", which are formed by the swollen internodes of the underground branches of a Labiatae (Stachys affinis)(1) A. de Planta. This work was carried out at the agricultural chemistry laboratory of the Zurich Polytechnicum, directed by Professor E. Schultze Revue générale de botanique, February 15 1889, p. 85. These tubers, which are being used more and more every day in France, England and Switzerland, are indeed a first-rate foodstuff, and the nature of the substances they contain was known only very inaccurately. "I shall not insist here on the details of the procedures I used for the chemical analysis of tubers (1), and I shall leave aside their morphological development. "Here is the result of the analysis of slow-growing tubers harvested in February in Paris: Water............................ Protein substances........... Amides......................... Fat (extracted by ether)...... Carbohydrates, mainly galactan...... Cellulose...................... Ash........................ TUBERCULES Fresh. Dry. 78.33 1.50 1.67 18 6.68 7.71 82 16.57 76.71 73 3.38 1.02 4.70 100.00 100.00 "The tubers therefore contained 21. 67 p. 100 dry substance, the centesimal composition of which is shown in the preceding table, right-hand column. "It can be seen that nitrogenous substances are found in significant proportion in this food, and that carbohydrates, in very high quantities, are represented above all by galactan, a substance discovered in 1886 (1) See the article I published in Nobbe's Versuchestationen, 1888. 11 by Messrs. E. Schultze and Steiger, in the seeds of Lupin (Lupinus luteus). "Galactan, this new substance, is intermediate between starch and sugar; it is a material analogous to dextrin, but is very clearly characterized. The proportion of galactan in tubers is around 75%. 100% of galactan in Stachys affinis tubers is quite remarkable, and no plant is known to contain a similar proportion of this substance at any stage of its development. Sugars are found only in trace amounts in these tubers, and even then their presence is not certain, as glucose is formed after the interversion of galactan by extended hydrochloric acid. "I would add that, among the nitrogenous substances, I was also able to isolate glutamine and tyrosine. "The richness in non-nitrogenous substances, and especially in galactan, which is a very easily assimilated substance, shows that crosnes are a precious food in many cases for the sick and for all those who suffer from a delicate stomach. "The only analysis of these tubers that I could find in my bibliographical research was published by M. Carrière, in the Revue horticole, in 1885. "The chemist who carried out this analysis found 68.96 O/0 starch, and the tubers I analyzed in February contained no trace of it (1). "It is true, as M. Dufour (from Lausanne) has noted, that traces can sometimes be found (1) The analysis published by M. Carrière gives only 17.88 e/, of starch and non-nitrogenous substances. Revue horticole, 1885, p. 236 - P. et B. d'amidon dans les tubercules de Stachys affinis récoltés en automne, mais ce n'est là que de l'amidon transitoire qui, selon toute apparence, se transforme en galactane pendant l'hiver. This interesting fact shows that a chemical study of these tubers at various stages of development could yield remarkable results. "In any case, it is not this transitory starch (existing only in trace amounts) that may have been found in the analysis I have just quoted. It is, without doubt, galactan that has been counted as starch, and the difference between these two bodies is important enough from the physiological point of view, as well as from the dietary point of view, to deserve to be pointed out." Messrs. Planta and Schulze discovered a new substance in Crosne, which they named Stachyose and described in two memoirs summarized below: STACHYOSE. - Extracted from the juice obtained by pressing Stachus affinis using a series of treatments with lead acetate, hydrogen sulfide and alcohol. Slowly crystallizes by careful addition of alcohol to its aqueous solutions in the form of shiny crystals adhering to the walls of the vases in which they are deposited. Weakly sweet, highly soluble in water, strongly dextrorotatory; reduces Fehling's liquor only after boiling with acids, like cane sugar; gives by oxidation with azotic acid about the same quantity of mucic acid as milk sugar. Its formula is CISH32 OIe - 3 Ha O. Stachyose resembles tactosine, which Meyer extracted from the Caryophylae, but differs in its rotatory power, which is lower than that of lactosine (1). When boiled with extended acids, Stachyose gives a (1) See Planta and Schulze, Berichte der deutschen chemischen Gesellschaft, t. 23, p. 1692. mixture of reducing sugars containing half its weight in galactose, the other half being a mixture of glucose and levulose. In this respect, it can be compared with raffinose from beet or cotton cake, which gives the same reducing sugars in the same circumstances, but in equal proportions. It is therefore possible that the formula for Stachyose should be doubled and written as C38 Het O3* (1). In the two memoirs we have just summarized, the authors do not indicate the quantity of Stachyose supplied by tubers. Just as starch and inulin give glucose and levulose when heated with acidulated water, so Stachyose treated in the same way gives galactose, but at the same time as glucose and levulose: CE H32018 - 2H2O stachyose CHROS Ce He 9e glucose T levulose C HOU galactose FOOD USES Crosnes are all the better for being freshly plucked. They don't need to be peeled; simply wash them carefully so that no earth or sand remains in the internodes. They cook in twelve to fifteen minutes; if left too long on the fire, the water will penetrate them and they will become mushy and bland. Cooks have given the newcomer a warm welcome; indeed, there's no other vegetable that's so quick and easy to prepare. This one (1) See Planta and Schukze, same collection, t. 24, p. 2705. therefore met with no resistance and immediately became a regular, a friend of the house. Its flavor is pleasant, but weak. It has to be cooked in saltier water than usual. We don't know how the Chinese prepare it for the table, but here it accepts all the preparations with which we are familiar, and that's enough. It is likely that the Japanese prepare it with their excellent and inevitable sauce, "Shoyu"; but we really have no information on its use in Japan other than this: Choro-gi has tuberous, caterpillar-like roots, which are preserved in plum vinegar for eating (1). In France, crosnes are usually served as fresh flageolet beans, with or without chopped parsley. They are delicious as a garnish for veal stew, and in this state, the farmers in our village are crazy about them. They are also excellent fried. One enthusiast recommends cooking them the day before and frying them, cold and firm, the next day. They can also be eaten au gratin. They are perfect in salads, simple or mixed. Potel & Chabot sometimes cook them in stock beforehand. Lastly, they are very good in vinegar preserves, combined with gherkins, Catawissa onions and so on. As you can see, all our preparations (1) Le Japon à l'Exposition universelle de 1878, Paris. A la Commission impériale du Japon, 15, avenue de Matignon, 1878. are applicable to them, and the hostess is spoilt for choice. In recent years, marsh marigold (Stachys palustris) has been advocated as being able to provide the same services as Crosne. Some authors have even argued that these two Stachys are identical, and that there's no reason to grow a Chinese plant when we have its analogue in France. We have grown both species side by side for several years. It never occurred to us that they could be confused. In Stachys palustris, the leaves are sessile, lanceolate, whereas in S. affinis they are petiolate, oval, attenuated at the apex and cordate at the base. The former species flowers abundantly every year, while the latter only very exceptionally (1). Finally, and most importantly, the rhizomes of marsh milkweed are very elongated; they can reach 15 to 20 centimetres in length; they are acrid and bitter, almost inedible. To consider using them, we'd have to start by creating an improved breed, as suggested by the ancient authors who wrote about their edibility. Mr. Chappellier, of the Société nationale d'Acclimatation, is undertaking trials to this end, by hybridizing the two species. We hope his efforts will be crowned with success. (1) Stachys affinis has a rare peculiarity, but there are other examples. The abundance of rhizome production is such that the plant doesn't need to form seeds to reproduce. Flower buds appear, but remain small and do not bloom. The first flowers observed in France with any certainty were seen by one of us, in Mr. Le Tourneur's garden, at Roche-Gauthier, near Granville (Manche) (See Revue Horticole, 1891, page 463). On March 24 1892, M. Chappellier presented to the Société Nationale d'Horticulture de France (see Journal de la Société Nationale d'Horticulture, 1892, p. 137), tubers of Stachys floridana Shultlew, (native to Florida), which had been sent to him from the USA under the name Florida Artichoke. The presenter was unable to give any information on the use to which these tubers might be put, but he thought that the vulgar name under which he had received them suggested that they were edible. M. Chappellier has cultivated Stachys floridana, and we were able to see a few plants in flower in his garden at Boissy-Saint-Léger (Seine-et-Oise) (see Revue Horticole, 1893, page 17). The plant resembles Stachys affinis, but is less developed and less vigorous. The tubers are more elongated, reminiscent in shape and volume of the rhizomes of Stachys palustris; they have a pronounced unpleasant flavor that makes them inedible. Mr. Chappellier continues to cultivate Stachys floridana in the hope of one day obtaining a hybrid between this species and Stachys affinis. In the meantime, there is no doubt that, through selection, we can increase the volume of Crosne tubers, if we judge that it is in our interest to have them larger. CYCLANTHERA PEDATA Schrad. Cucurbitaceae family. Annual plant, with climbing, glabrous stems; bifid tendrils; alternate, petiolate leaves; in July-September, small, axillary green flowers, the males in long-stalked corymbs, the females solitary, sessile at the base of the male flowers. Pediaria is native to Mexico. It is a trailing or climbing plant of extraordinary fecundity. We won't dwell on its cultivation, which is that of the Gourdes. We left the stems lying around, and for several months they produced flowers and fruit in abundance. Supported by a trellis, these stems reach up to 4 meters in height. The leaves resemble those of the Virginia creeper and are very filling. Fruit should be picked daily. If they are to be used, they must not be larger than olives, as they soon become soft and spongy. Harvested at the right time, they can be candied like gherkins. Their defect is that they are not crunchy. They have a strong Cucumber smell, which they give up to take on the taste of the vinegar in which they are immersed. The same is true of almost all the vegetables we associate with Gherkins. Cyclanthers are no better or worse than others. They're pretty little vinegar sponges. The seed is black and curious. The plant is hardier than the Cornichon de Paris. COMESTIBLE CYCLANTHERA CYCLANTHERA EDULIS Ndn. Pepino de comer of the South American Spaniards, who eat the fruit in a variety of ways. The plant differs from C. pedata only in its larger size and in the size of the fruit, which is six to seven times the volume of C. pedata and retains its shape, but is smoother. Strong, climbing annual plant with broad, pale-green leaves; female flowers usually solitary and subsessile in the leaf axils; spongy flesh before ripening, with a strong cucumber odor. Rapid growth. Very rambaceous, very fertile plant (Catalogue of Ch. Huber et Cle, horticulturists, Hyères). Cyclanthera edulis appears to be native to Colombia and, if memory serves, Mr. Triana tells us that its fruits are usually eaten halved and stuffed like cucumbers and eggplants. The fruits prepared in this way are therefore, to all appearances, much larger than those of C. pedata, and this is in fact what the Catalogue of Messrs Huber et Cie, quoted above, says; but it is a fact that we have not been able to verify. We received the seeds listed in their catalog from these gentlemen, and gave half of them to a skilled gardener in our neighborhood. We sowed them in trays and on beds and obtained very strong plants, similar to C. pedata, with flowers, but no fruit. Our neighbor obtained flowers and fruits, but these were hardly larger than those of C. pedata. This is what Mr. Naudin reports about C. edulis: "We received from New Grenada, under the name of Pepino de comer, through the intermediary of Mr. de Geoffroy, French consul in Bogota, the seeds of a Cyclanthera which, if it is not identical with C. pedata, as I am tempted to believe, is extremely close to it. We cultivated it at the Muséum in 1858; but, owing to various accidents, the three or four living individuals we had were so retarded that they only flowered in the late season and did not set a single fruit. The ovaries were perfectly smooth, and this is about the only difference I found between them and C. pedata, where they are somewhat mature. "According to what M. de Geoffroy told us, the fruits are edible before they are ripe, and can be eaten cooked. This species is probably the Momordica pedata pomis striatis of P. Feuillée..." Journal des observations physiques, mathématiques et botaniques, faites par ordre du roi, sur les côtes orientales de l'Amérique méridionale et aux Indes orientales, par le R. P. Louis Feuillée; Paris, 1725, vol. Il, p. 754: Momordica fructu striato: "All Peruvians, among whom this plant is found, eat the fruit in their soups; it is extremely refreshing and therefore very necessary in Peru, where the heat is excessive. The failure of Mr. Naudin to grow C. edulis at the Muséum, the failure we experienced ourselves, and the advanced age at which our neighbor obtained some fruit, sufficiently demonstrate that the edible Cyclanthère differs from C. pédiaire, the latter being of much better composition, both very early and very productive; but neither is it proven to us that the plant catalogued by MM. Huber et Cie is the one mentioned by Mr. Triana. There is a desideratum here that has not been satisfied. We have also cultivated the Cyclanthera explodens Schrader, which can be found in botanical gardens and requires no more care than the C. pedata. Its fruits are shorter and smaller than those of C. pedata; too soft to be used as pickles, they have the peculiarity of exploding when ripe, spitting their seeds violently in the face of any indiscreet person who touches them. CYPHIA TORTILIS N.-E. Brown. Family Campanulaceae, tribe Cyphiaceae. In February 1894, Professor Mac Owan, government botanist at the Cape of Good Hope, sent us tubers of a plant which, he told us, was related to the Bosch Barroe (Cyphia sylvatica Eckl.), but nevertheless distinct, tubers which are eaten after being roasted in the ashes of the fire made by outspanned? travelers, but which could not be recommended as having first-rate eating qualities. We planted these tubers under a frame, in the open ground, and, in the course of the year, we observed the flowering of most of the plants grown. This same species had also been sent to Kew Gardens, and in the October 1894 issue, p. 356, Mr. N.-E. Brown described it as new.
