In the autumn, we harvested these tubers, which had not multiplied and had not grown appreciably. We cooked some of them and offered the others to the Museum, the tasting having made us recognize the futility of continuing to cultivate them as a food plant. Japanese radish. 173 RAPHANUS SATIVUS L., var. Cruciferae family. Although Daikons have been described by Henri Rivière as belonging to a particular species, we do not believe we should adopt this view. These plants, like the varieties of Radish we grow in France, vary infinitely, both in the shape of their leaves and their roots, and should be attached to Linné's Raphanus sativus. We had a large collection of Daikons presented to the Société nationale d'Horticulture by gardener Henri Véniat. If we're not mistaken, this was the first time that several varieties of this little-known plant had been presented to the Society. Until then, we'd confined ourselves to showing off a specimen of some kind and saying: here's the Daikon, without considering that this vegetable, like so many others, has properties, qualities and even defects, which vary infinitely, depending on the variety we've grown. Our Radishes are of Chinese origin, but we've obtained countless varieties that appear in the catalogs of grain merchants. Round and short, long and half-long, early and late, white, pink, red, violet, yellow, grey, black, small or huge; it's enough to make you get lost. The same applies to daikons. Some are sown in spring, others, more numerous, are sown in August and used throughout the winter. One is very fast-growing and must be used after a few weeks' sowing, otherwise it will be eaten hollow. Some are inordinately long and require ploughing with two spades; others are short or half-long and require only ordinary ploughing. All the varieties we have are white. Some are said to be red inside and out, and would be the best. We asked in vain for them in Japan; all we received was a degenerate pink winter radish from China. In June 1878, we received three varieties highly esteemed in their country of origin: Sakurashima, Tsuri and Nerima Daïkon. We sowed them immediately; these plants went to seed so quickly that they did not form usable roots, but we knew from then on that we had to sow late. In 1881, we received new seeds, which we sowed on August 1. The result was very satisfactory, and the horticulturists of the Société d'Horticulture de Montreuil can recall a presentation of very fine roots made to them by Henri Véniat. We give here the names of the varieties we have. We mark two of them with a question mark because they are doubtful for us; we consider the others to be correct: Shirimain nedzumi (Fig. 29); Hosone; Mia shige; Arkie (?); Nedzumi; Maru nerima (Fig. 30); Nerima; Kedissune (Fig. 31); Ku nichi; Songetty (?) Ninengu. We lost the Sakurashima and the Tsuri. We did not receive the San Guwatsu, which is cited first by the Japanese author of "Japon à l'Exposition uniFig. 29. - Shirimain nedzumi (au quart de grandeur naturelle). verselle de 1878", and whose name means Daikon of the third month (April). We'll say a few words about each of the varieties we grew in 1881 and 1882. Shirimain nedzumi (fig. 29). - This Daikon is large and short. Its roots do not grow out of the ground. It does not require any deeper ploughing than is usual in gardens: a merit that should make it sought-after. Hosone. - Its roots rise to Om, 20 above ground. It nevertheless requires deep plowing. It grows quickly. It becomes hollow from the first days of October. Mia shige. - Its roots protrude about 1.10 m above ground; it requires deep ploughing. According to the above-mentioned Japanese author, Mia shige Daïkon is a renowned product of Owari province. Arkie (?). - This Daïkon requires deep ploughing. Its roots are only Om.05 to Om.10 high above ground. Nedzumi. - Requires deep plowing. Its leaves are unlike those of any other daikon. Maru nerima (fig. 30). - 15 centimetres above ground. Deep ploughing. Nerima. - 20 centimetres above ground. Deep ploughing. Kedissune (fig. 31). - Very divided and finely cut leaves, almost ornamental. Ku nichi. - 10 centimetres above ground. Deep ploughing. Its name means: nine-day daikon. Songetty (?). - Deep plowing. Ninengu (1). - 20 centimetres above ground. Deep ploughing. All these varieties are sown on August 1 and used in winter. However, we have one which, sown (1) MM. Vilmorin-Andrieux et Cie (Les Plantes potagères) say that, to reach its full potential, Ninengo (or Ninengu) should be sown as early as April. It requires deeply worked and abundantly smoked soil. Some Daikons, according to authorities who seem to be reliable, reach the fabulous weight of 15 to 20 kilograms. in spring, did not go to seed until late summer, so we feared we might not harvest any Fig. 30. - Maru nerima (quarter natural size). Fig. 31 - Kedissune (quarter natural size). and we had to wait a long time for the siliques to dry. This variety, to which 12 the Japanese author quoted above gives the name Natsu Daïkon, i.e. Summer Daïkon, was part of a collection of forty-two seed packets collected by the intrepid traveller Mr. Colteau, a collection which had come to us through the kindness of Dr. Paul Sagot. It contained several other varieties of Daikon, which, sown in spring, went to seed without producing anything useful. It is therefore well established that, with the exception of Natsu Daïkon, sowing should take place around August 1st, and even later in the Midi, on ordinary ploughing for short or half-long Daïkon, and on ploughing with two spades for long-rooted varieties. Sow in rows and, if you want to obtain well-developed plants, thin to at least 15 cm. As for fertilization, we'll quote what Count de Castillon said in Volume IV, page 519, 3rd series of the Bulletin de la Société d'Acclimatation, to challenge certain points: "Struck by the negative results obtained in France in growing Daikon, I wanted to find the cause. To do this, I thought I could do no better than to consult the Japanese work entitled: Só moku so date gusa; here is what it says about Daïkon, which would be more than enough to show the reasons for the lack of success that is generally complained of: in spring" and summer, the ground is dug up extensively; a mixture of ash and pou" drette is "carefully incorporated, and sown from the first to the tenth day of" autumn (late September), in rows and on ridges. There are also daikons that are entirely red, not only on the outside but also on the inside; they are very tender and have a delicate taste. "Japanese cultivation can be summed up in the following three points: 1° deep cultivation; 2° highly active, rapidly assimilable powdered fertilizers; 3° (and this is a crucial point) autumn sowing. It's obvious that the Japanese don't sow Daikon in spring, because they've recognized that seedlings sown at this time go to seed very easily, without producing large roots. Something similar happens with certain plants, such as turnips. "It should also be noted that the variety of Daikon "most esteemed in Japan for its edible qualities" is red Daikon. It is therefore on this variety that "the next trials should preferably focus". We have no doubt that a very good result can be obtained by digging deep into the soil, amending it with ashes, and smoking it with poudrette; but we do not consider these practices necessary. Daikons are extremely hardy and thrive in good garden soil. They sprout spontaneously, and the seeds that spread from side to side give rise to superb plants. We confess to being at a loss to understand the difficulties the first experimenters seem to have encountered. We would like to draw your attention to the comment made by M. Rivière in January 1876, and recorded in the Bulletin de la Société d'Acclimatation, 3e série, volume III, page 39: "M. Rivière points out the advantages he feels would be gained by cultivating Daikon or Japanese Radish (Rapha" nus acanthiformis). He had received a few seeds of this radish from a consignment sent by Mr. Kreutzer, attached to the Japanese legation, and had them tested on an estate belonging to Mr. Talabot, near Limoges, obtaining a sufficient quantity of seed to propagate the plant, which he believes has a great future ahead of it. Its "nutritional qualities, invaluable for dairy cows, which are very fond of it, seem to place it "between the green-necked carrot and beet. "Daikon is also likely to play a major role as a catch crop. Sown in the second half of July, the plant can be harvested in October, so it takes up very little land and is doubly valuable. The way we use Daikon is completely different from that of the Japanese. They eat all varieties, cooked or salted; they also use it as a condiment, grating it when fresh, or drying it. They also eat the leaves prepared in the same way as the roots. Here, however, it's a different matter. We gauge the roots in November and take them fresh as and when we need them. They are tender, full of vegetative water, and less pungent than black radish and even Chinese pink winter radish. They are infinitely pleasing to the people in our families and to the edible merchants to whom we let them taste. Cooked, we find them inferior to turnips. We have yet to experiment with Daikon as a fodder plant, in catch crop. It's not up to us to resolve this important question. Satzouma daikon This variety deserves a special mention. To highlight it, we reproduce a note by Mr. Louis Sisley, published in the Revue horticole, year 1874, page 444: "We extract from the letters of Dr. Hénon, from Ikouno (Japan), a few passages relating to the Japanese radish known as Daïkon: "Daïkons, large white radishes, have almost everywhere replaced wheat, hemp, peas and broad beans, where there are no rice fields. In parts of the country where no water has been brought in, and where rice cannot be grown, Raves or, more generally, a huge white radish or horseradish is grown in addition to the cereals, and is consumed either fresh or raw, preserved in salt. It exists everywhere in Japan and China; "but the seeds I'm sending you belong to "a particular variety, the famous Daikon, the Radish of Satzouma, which differs notably from the other "varieties widespread in the country, since it reaches up to a foot in diameter, which we never see here, in Osaka, Kioto or Yedo. I don't think it's been imported to France, as our compatriots who have been to Satzouma are not numerous. "In good soil, this radish often reaches 90 centimetres in length. It is sown in July-August and harvested in autumn and winter. In "fabulous" times, Sacourasima is said to have had a daikon "that was a league around. These are only "degenerate children", but they are still of a nice "size". Cattle are very fond of daikons, and are "given a large quantity"; but it's men "especially who eat them in great quantities. "They are eaten raw, in salads, cut into very thin slices, cooked in the Japanese sauce called shoyu, and especially raw, after having been preserved in salt for one or more months. The Japanese, incidentally, find that "cheese smells even worse, and are astonished that "we can swallow something so vile. "Daikon candied in salt is the usual seasoning for boiled rice, among the poor as well as the rich. DOLIC BULBEUX Bangkoang (Javanese), Daun Sabran (Malay), Short podded Yam-bean (English Colonies), Dau (Annamite Mandarin), San rucang (Annamite vulgar). PACHYRRHIZUS ANGULATUS Rich, DC, Prod; Dolichos bulbosus L., Spec, p. 1020; Roxbg, Flor. Ind. 111, p. 309; Stizolobium bulbosum Spreng, Syst. 111, 252. GrapiZgne:iustrtes : Flora brasifiensis, vol. XV, I, pl. 53; Plumier, Plantes d'Amér. pl. 222; Bull. of miscell. informat. (Royal gardens Kew), 1889, p. 121. Leguminosae family. Perennial, voluble, giving rise to several rounded or napiform tubers. Stems hairy when young, becoming almost hairless with age. Leaves composed of three broad, hairless leaflets, the terminal one larger, angular, toothed; the lateral ones unequilateral. Reddish flowers in short, pauciflorous clusters, followed by oblong or cylindrical, pointed, glabrescent and slightly gnarled pods. According to Rumphius, this plant is native to the Philippines. It is grown in several parts of India and in the Moluccas. Roxburgh (loc. cit.) assumes that it was introduced from America to the Philippine Islands. "The sample in our collections was collected in the Galam country, near Bakel, by M. Leprieur, who does not tell us whether it is cultivated or wild in this country. "Its importance as a food plant led us to bring it from Java to Bourbon, Cayenne and the Natural History Museum in Paris. It was cultivated in 1824 in the Richard-Tol garden, but we don't know whether it had been brought there from Bakel or Eastern India". (Guillemin, Perrotlet et Richard, Floræ senegambiæ tentamen, vol. I. p. 221). "The root tubers of P. angulatus have the shape and volume of our Rave; sometimes, they become much larger. They are rarely eaten other than cooked, and can be made into a very tasty dish with butter, sugar and spices. "Roots left in the ground until the fruit is ripe lose all their flavor and can no longer be used as food. (Spach, Végét, phanérog., vol., I, p. 334). De l'usage que font les Javanais et les habitants des îles Philippines du Dolic bulbeux, appelé vulgairement dans le pays Ignamas, par M. Perrottet, botaniste-cultivateur (1). "The Dolic bulbous belongs to the Diadelphia decandria and is a member of the Leguminosae family. It is closely related to the bean genus and, like it, grows sarmentose, creeping stems; it produces a taproot, similar in shape and size to the European sweet turnip (Brassica napus maxima), but more nutritious and softer than it. The Malays and the inhabitants of the Philippine Islands delight in it, eating it raw or boiled like potatoes. It can be found on the best tables, prepared in a variety of ways, making it both a healthy and tasty dish. The rural landowner, when he gathers a lot of it, feeds it to his domestic animals; I've noticed that they eat it greedily and that it fattens them up in no time, especially the pigs. "It seems to me of the utmost importance to recommend the cultivation and multiplication of this bulbous plant to the inhabitants of the French colonies, especially as it can, at any time, provide them, without any preparation, with healthy and abundant food. "The growth of this plant is very rapid; three to four months are enough for the root to acquire all its perfection. "(1) Bibliothèque phyysico-économique; Paris, t. X, year 1821, p. 311. however, it is more voluminous and more pleasing to the taste when grown in good, substantial, slightly moist soil. It is easily propagated by means of its seeds, which ripen very well; but, to obtain them, it is advisable to leave a certain number of plants in place, because, for its root to be delicate and succulent, we must not wait for the seeds to ripen; on the contrary, we must pull out the bulbs long before flowering, because then the root acquires a consistency as it ages.

I'm convinced that this plant can be naturalized very well in France, especially in our southern departments. "I'm convinced that this plant can be very well naturalized in France, especially in our southern departments; it's an annual, so it only needs three to four months of heat to grow to its normal size. It would be all the more valuable for us as it would fill the gap often left between the potatoes of the last harvest and the new ones. Bulbous Dolichos has the advantage of growing faster than potatoes, the earliest of which don't usually appear until August, i.e. eight months after planting, whereas Dolichos, sown in April, can be eaten as early as July. "I have scattered its seeds in the French colonies of Africa and America which I have just visited, and where I have had the good fortune to introduce several other useful plants about which I reserve the right to speak to you later. I won't end this note without announcing that Cayenne is producing a rather remarkable crop this year, and that everything leads me to believe that Dolichos will spread rapidly there. "I have been told in Java and Manila that the seeds of this plant are poisonous; this property, which it shares with the potato, although from a very different family, cannot be a reason to keep a plant that is useful in so many other respects away from our crops. We have received seeds and tubers of Pachyrrhizus angulatus from Mr. Martin, head government gardener in Saigon, accompanied by a note which we transcribe: "Cù Sáng (Annamite). This climbing plant produces a very large tuber. It should be planted on a ridge, and heavily shod in due course. When the plant is 30 to 40 centimetres tall, pinch off the tip and false buds to force the sap back into the tuber. "Put two or three seeds in each hole and, when they're well established, leave just one and transplant the others. This plant belongs to the legume family; its tuber is very good cooked or even raw, but its fruit is considered poisonous. The tubers I am sending you can only be used to provide seeds for sowing, as each seed produces only one tuber (1). Sow in early April; harvest in September". We sowed under a sowing frame in April, keeping only single plants; we stubbed out (2), pinched back the main secondary stems and watered frequently. (1) The plant usually produces several tubers. - P. B. (2) As the tubers form quite deep in the soil, far from the plant's collar, the ridging recommended by M. Martin does not seem useful to us. - P. B. The plants vegetated well, and, if they hadn't been pinched, would have risen to 2 or 3 meters in height, like those the tubers gave us. Around November 15, we harvested one, two or three tubers from each plant, slightly smaller than a turnip, which we found tender, sweet and pleasant when eaten raw. The tubers, large and slightly angular, which we received from Saigon in perfect condition and planted under a frame, vegetated with great vigor, but showed no inclination to flower until the beginning of October. In the end, we obtained neither flowers nor fruit. So we don't see how we can obtain seeds for our seedlings in the Paris climate. We have tried propagation by bedding, but without success. So we'll still have to get our seeds from the country of origin, which presents no difficulty whatsoever. It's almost certain that we'll be able to obtain seeds in the south of France, and even more so in Algeria. We would even point out that our seedlings have shown inflorescences which have not blossomed, but which give the assurance of fruiting in a warmer climate than Paris. Mr. Perrottet is undoubtedly mistaken when he says that Dolic bulbeux is an annual; but it seems that, in hot countries, it is cultivated as a biennial, the first year to supply its edible lubercules and the second year, by means of a few tubers preserved ad hoc, the seeds necessary for sowing, the latter, like so many other roots, then losing their edible properties. Pachyrrhizus angulatus is a very interesting plant that amateurs can grow in the Paris area, and that growers in the south of France should not neglect. They should not forget that the sweet root of bulbous chervil is sold in Paris at a high price, and that production often remains below demand! In 1892 (1), Mr. Pierre, director of the Jardin Colonial in Libreville, French Congo, wrote to us about this plant: "Since 1887, I have tried on several occasions to grow Pachyrrhizus angulatus in Libreville. In 1887, I took with me five seeds entrusted to me by Professor Maxime Cornu. I cultivated the plant and the results I obtained are marvellous. In 1887, 1889-1890, I showed this plant and roots, one of which weighed 300 grams, at what is known as the agricultural competition in Libreville. "I harvested 7,000 kilograms per hectare in poor soil. The harvest could have risen to 10,000 kilos if I'd been able to grow an excellent crop and obtain fertilizer, and that in three months. "Three or even four harvests could be made each year, bringing the possible harvest to 40,000 kilograms. In these countries, this plant could be of great importance, not only as a source of food for Europeans and natives, but also for animals. "I recently collected 2,000 seeds and sent them (1) M. Pierre died shortly afterwards; he was succeeded by M. Chalot. to all the posts in the French Congo, as far as the Oubanghi, to the various people who love useful plants, missionaries, planters, etc., and to the possessions north of the French Congo. "Less sweet than the Potato, this precious and precocious vegetable can be considered a substitute for the Potato, which we are obliged to buy on the coast from liners who sell it to us at a very high price: 0 fr. 60, 0 fr. 75 and even 1 franc per kilogram." LOLIC TUBEROUS Ahipa (Peru) Yam-Bean (British Colonies). PACHYRREZUS TUBEROSUS Spreng; Dolichos tuberosus Lamk. Grigures: Plumier, Plantes d'Amer. pl. 220; Descourtilz, Flore des Antilles, VIII, p. 127, pl. 554; Bull. of misc. informat. (Royal gardens Kew). 1889, p. 662. Fam. Leguminosae. This plant is undoubtedly native to Martinique, but has long been known in Peru, where it is cultivated as a food plant (see Notice sur l'Ahipa et l'Aricoma, plantes alimentaires du haut Pérou, by A. Weddell, published in this volume, under the article Poire de terre). The above-mentioned learned botanist observed the plant in La Paz; having been unable to collect more than incomplete samples, he was unable to determine it with any certainty. For many years afterwards, no further samples were obtained, and no one knew to which botanical species Ahipa could be attached. In 1886, Dr Ernst from Caracas sent Kew samples of a plant cultivated in Merida, which he said corresponded exactly to Weddell's description of Ahipa, and which he believed to be Dolichos tuberosus Lamk. There were several specimens of Dolichos tuberosus in the Kew herbarium, including those collected at Tarapota by Spruce. Professor Oliver found this plant distinct from Pachyrrhizus angulatus. Mr. Bentham considered Spruce's samples to belong to the latter species. Finally, Dr Morris thought it might well be a variety produced by cultivation, but marked enough to require a distinct name for cultivation. He published a series of notes on both plants in the Bulletin of miscellaneous information of the Royal Garden of Kew, with a view to making their merits known, accompanied by two good figures enabling the distinctive characters of these species to be easily seen. According to him, Pachyrrhizus tuberosus differs from P. angulatus in its leaves, which are rounded rather than toothed, its flowers, which are white rather than violet, its pods, which are much wider, and perhaps also its seeds, which are kidney-shaped, white, black or variegated. Like the Bulbous Dolic, the Tuberous Dolic is a most valuable food plant for hot countries; but it is curious to note that its seeds, as well as those of several other Phaseolae, possess toxic properties. According to Dr. Ernst, these seeds are used in Merida (Venezuela), either as a decoction or in powder form, to destroy vermin. One of his students wrote to him from the state of Tachira that he had seen a case of poisoning with a type of bean called Tapiramo (a species of Dolichos); cooks therefore usually throw away the first water in which Tapiramo is cooked. For the uses of this plant, we believe we can do no better than reproduce what Macfadyen says about it in Flora of Jamaica, page 285: "Its root is formed of a number of ramifications several feet long, running under the surface of the ground, emitting in their course a succession of tubers. Its seeds are poisonous, but the root yields abundant and very healthy food. The product of three plants is generally enough to fill a bushel. "The tubers, after being boiled, can be eaten like yams and other common roots; they would also produce an excellent substitute for Arrow-root. The starch is pure white and equal in all respects to the latter product. It is very pleasant to the taste and easy to digest. It is used to make custards and puddings. The waste obtained after extraction of the starch, which is unusable in Arrow-root, can be dried to produce a good, healthy flour. A delicious flour can also be obtained by cutting the tubers, drying them in the sun and then reducing them to powder. The plant certainly deserves to be much more widely cultivated than it has been up to now. It should, with great advantage, replace Arrow-root in crops. "It can be planted at any season of the year, and the roots are ready to be pulled up after four to five months. Its production is much greater than that of Arrow-root, and the proportion of starch is also more considerable, so that its low price could put it on the market, even for use by cloth printers, in place of starch, which is usually used. Yam-Bean has been cultivated for many years in Jamaica. It is said to have been introduced from Martinique. SWEET FENNEL FOENICULUM DULCE C. Bauh; Anethum dulce DC. Umbelliferae family. Stems a little compressed at the base, 1.30 m high. Leaves radical, almost distichous, with elongated, capillary segments; umbels with 7-8 rays. Italian sweet fennel is an annual plant. It is said to be native to Italy. Miller, in his Dictionnaire des jardiniers, makes no such claims. It is believed, he says, to have been imported from the Azores. He gives it the name Fœniculum azoricum. Abbé Rozier, in his Cours complet d'Agriculture, confines himself to saying that the sweet fennel of Italy, as reported by travelers, is much less sweet than that of the Azores. He cites the coasts of Attica as one of the most renowned countries for this plant. Sweet fennel is sown in February and March, in nurseries, under frames; later, in the open ground, until the end of July. In Italy, it is still sown in August. As soon as the plant is ready, it is planted at Om, 35 in all directions. This spacing facilitates ridging, which is carried out once or twice. Since the part of the plant you want to obtain is white and tender, and hardly Fig. 32. - Sweet fennel. more than Om, 10 high, it is rarely necessary to ridge more than twice. Fennel needs a light, loamy soil. For several years now, we've been culling it on an old layer, and we get superb plants. It's important to hoe in good time and water abundantly. Thanks to this care, the petioles agglomerated above the plant's collar form a thick, flattened apple, the only one used for the table, to the exclusion of stems and leaves (1). Fennel goes to seed too quickly in summer, and rot often sets in. Keep an eye on it and pull it out in good time. It keeps for quite a long time (1) Les Plantes potagères. Vilmorin-Andrieux et Cre. 13 out of the ground. Besides, it's better to have less beautiful apples than to risk losing everything. Sowing at the end of July is the most important. It's the one that will provide Fennel for the table until January. The plant is placed in three rows in September, so that it can be covered with a frame when winter approaches. All that's needed for the plant to continue to vegetate and develop is for the temperature not to fall below zero. So, as is customary in such cases, straw or leaves are placed around the boxes and straw mats on the frames, and air is let in whenever the weather permits. When the fennel has formed its head, we keep it under the frame, or pull it out and put it in the vegetable greenhouse. It's easy to reach the month of January, and we think it's easy to go beyond that. Fennel seed degenerates quickly, and all horticultural treatises agree on this point. It has to be brought in from Italy every year. We prepare sweet fennel in three equally satisfying ways. We prepare it with cream, juice or parmesan. This last recipe was supplied to us by Le Bon Jardinier, 1873, from which we reproduce the following information: "Fennel is consumed in large quantities in Italy. It is eaten raw, like artichokes à la poivrade, generally without seasoning. It is very good as a garnish for poultry or large meat stews; in white sauce, au jus, au gratin or macaroni. For the last three, it is cooked in water beforehand. To prepare 'au gratin' or macaroni, take a saucepan, put some butter in it, then a bed of already cooked and drained Fennel, cut into quarters; salt and pepper lightly; sprinkle with grated Parma cheese and small pieces of butter, and continue until the saucepan is full. Cook over a moderate heat, covering the pan with its lid, which should be topped with coals. During a trip to Italy, Mr. Audot gathered new information that we are now presenting as a complement to the above. In Naples, especially in the Roman Elats, and even further afield in Venice, Fennel is so widely used that you can't take a step into a town or cross a village without encountering it. There is not a table where it is not served, from January to June. To our heaviness, we'll quote M. Audot: "Like Celery, Fennel is served in blond sauce and salad, or in soup, like Cabbage; but the most general custom is to serve it for dessert with fruit; it then decorates the lable, planted in the water of a glass bowl and raising its fine, elegant foliage like plumes; it is also eaten unseasoned. At first, I laughed at this custom, which reminded me of the English practice of serving Radishes for dessert, and even of carrying some in one's pocket to eat when out and about; but it wasn't long before I got used to Fennel, whose mild flavor and pleasant taste appealed to me; only, I preferred it as an appetizer, like Radishes and baby Artichokes (1)". 11) Extract from Notes sur les jardins du sud de l'Italie, recueillies pendant un voyage en 1839-1840 et lucs à la Société royale d'Horti faveur than in Italy, and it is in vain that most horticultural treatises present Fennel to amateurs as an excellent vegetable to introduce into their gardens. We believe that it has always been grown in France, but has never been widely used. The obstacle is undoubtedly its slight anise flavor. However, we accept this flavor in anisette, gingerbread, croquets and so on. We accept sweet fennel seeds converted into small sugared almonds. Fennel apples, candied like Angelica, are excellent. A tin of candied Fennel was part of the lot exhibited by gardener Henri Véniat (Société d'Horticulture) in October 1877. This preparation was very favorably appreciated by all who tasted it. Our fathers sought out Spanish chervil, which they blanched. It had a pronounced anise flavor. The Germans make great use of Cumin, whose taste is very close to that of Anise; they melt it into bread. The English and Arabs use it as a condiment. The Russians make a liqueur called kümmel. The Dutch are said to use it in their cheeses. The Italians can't do without their fennel. Fennel and bread! they say. In the province of Venice, it's used in bread, just as Cumin is used in Germany. Italian gourmets use the tubular stalk of the Fennel plant as a blowtorch to sniff the culture of Paris, by M. Audot, editor of Le Bon Jardinier (Revue horticole, vol. V (1844-1844), p. 16). and the liqueur acquires an exquisite aroma in the process. In Spain, on the African coast and in the Azores, Fennel is not sought after. It seems to us that the flavor of Anise, which is common to Cumin and Fennel, is considered excellent throughout Europe. In France, it is not yet universally accepted in this vegetable, which is easy to grow and available in all seasons: it's a question of habit and taste education. Abbé Rozier, in his Cours complet d'Agriculture (1786), tells us that sweet fennel is grown in gardens in the north of the kingdom. What was done in the past will undoubtedly be done again. In spite of its slight anise flavor, we say, because of this flavor, say the Italians, sweet fennel is, in our opinion, an excellent vegetable. We recommend it to connoisseurs. FUKI PETASITES JAPONICUS F. Schmidt. Family Compositae. This plant is native to Japan, where it bears the name Fuki; it is the Korokoni of the Ainos, who use it extensively for their food. According to Messrs Balchelor and Miyabe, Ainu Economicplants (Transactions of the Asiatic Society of Japon, p. 222), the Ainos eat mainly the petioles, but also the flower, called Makayo. The petioles are often roasted over the fire, then peeled and eaten; more generally, they are boiled (stewed); they are also made into Pickles or preserves, by salting them after boiling, and mixing them with Ikokuttara leaves (Polygonum sacchalinense), which give them a reddish tint; sometimes no salt is added. The Catalogue of Japanese products at the 1889 Exhibition says of Fuki: "To use the flowers of this plant, collect those that grow on the ground before the leaves; their taste is a little bitter; the pleasant smell is similar to that of Jamasó (Hops); they are used when their length is about thirty centimetres". We received a few Fuki plants from Japan, which we planted in the Crosne garden in 1896, and sheltered with glass boxes during the winter. In March 1897, inflorescences developed, preceding the leaves as happens in the various species of the Petasites genus. The leaves then developed, very large and numerous, reaching a height of seventy-five centimetres. The plant's rhizomes had passed under the glass trunk and invaded all the surrounding parts of the flowerbed. We picked a few leaves and peeled and cooked the long, fleshy petioles. The tasting was not favorable. Fuki may be acceptable as a pickle, but we found it to have such a pronounced aromatic and bitter flavor that we cannot recommend it in any capacity. GLACIAL Ice herb, Crystalline Ficoid. MESEMBRYANTHEMUM CRYSTALLINUM L. De Candolle, Plantes grasses, pl. 128. Family Mesembryanthemata. Annual plant, native to the Cape, Canaries, etc., imported, according to Miller, from the Cape of Good Hope; long cultivated for ornamental purposes. Flat-lying, fleshy herb, covered on all sides with transparent vesicles full of water, giving the impression of being covered in ice. In July-October, white, insignificant flowers. According to Duchesne (Répertoire des plantes utiles), Glaciale leaves are often eaten as a vegetable in Bourbon. In a letter to Messrs Vilmorin-Andrieux and Cle (1), Mr Picard of Saint-Quentin recommends using this Ficoïde as a spinach. Cultivation: Sow in March-April in a well-exposed spot, or preferably on a bed. Transplant to a well-soil bed as soon as the plant has a few leaves, at 0.60 m in all directions; do not neglect watering. The plant (1) Revue Horticole, 1859, p. 72. soon takes on extraordinary growth and can be picked throughout the summer. The Glaciale has its rightful place in the vegetable gardens of the curious. It is both beautiful and useful. There's no one who hasn't admired, on a fine winter's morning, the marvellous effect of sunlight on frost-covered trees. This is the spectacle offered by the Glaciale in midsummer. Unfortunately, it's spread out on the ground, so you have to bend down to admire it properly. It's not exactly an ornamental plant, and that's not why it belongs in this book. We consider it a highly esteemed vegetable, similar to Tetragona in cultivation and use. We pick its tender leaves and the tips of its stems. We prepare them in the same way as spinach, and everyone we've tried them on has agreed that Glaciale is a good vegetable with a distinctive flavor, a sui generis dish that's highly recommendable. Mme Henriette Davidis, whom we like to quote when talking about vegetables that are unusual in France, has this to say about this Ficoïde: "Glaciale is an admirable ornamental plant for gardens, and at the same time provides a delicate vegetable in summer. Its leaves and stems, as long as they are tender, should not be chopped, but only cooked, with breadcrumbs slightly browned in fresh butter, seasoned with salt, nutmeg or nutmeg flower and served with potatoes. Cooking time: barely a quarter of an hour". We give this recipe for what it may be worth; but, in recommending Glaciale as a vegetable, we also take our cue from a note by M. Siroy to be found in the Journal de la Société d'Horticulture de France, 2e série, tome XI, page 525. We believe that the Glaciale should be cultivated, admired and eaten. Our attention was drawn to it by a note from M. H. Lecoq, Revue Horticole, 1869, p. 207: Ficoïde à feuille en cœur (Mesembryanthemum cordifolium L.). Perennial plant, native to the Cape of Good Hope, from where it was brought by M. Bruyère. Although vigorous, it is not nearly as productive as its predecessor. Its flavor is also less pleasant. However, it deserves to be classed as a substitute for Spinach, or better still, Chicory, with which it is slightly bitter. Multiplication by cuttings taken in autumn, overwintered in a greenhouse and planted in spring, or by seedlings as described for Glaciale. The plant is very leafy. It spreads widely, so you need to give it about 2 meters of space. Five or six plants, planted in good soil in May and liberally watered, are enough for table service. Mesembryanthemum angulatum Thunb. from southern Africa, a species closely related to M. crystallinum, can also be used as a spinach substitute. Mr. Mac Owan, government botanist at the Cape of Good Hope, writes that he finds this vegetable comparable to New Zealand Tetragon, but a little more glutinous. GOMBO, KETMIE COMESTIBLE HIBISCUS ESCULENTUS L. Fam. Malvaceae. Annual plant. Stem 6-7 decimeters, simple; leaves cordate, 5-lobed, obtuse, toothed; petioles Fig. 33. - Long-fruited okra. longer than flowers; in June-July, yellowish-sulfur flowers, with purple centers, solitary, axillary; capsule pyramidal or conical; calicle of ten deciduous leaflets; calyx tearing longitudinally. South America. We wouldn't go too far if we said that okra is one of the most precious vegetables known to us. It is used daily in Egypt, Syria, Greece, Turkey, India, Louisiana, the West Indies and throughout South America. Its various culinary applications, as well as its properties as a substitute for coffee and cocoa, are described below. Its cultivation is scarcely known in the Paris region, where the climate is unfavorable: sow the seeds on a bed in February, prick out the young plants also on a bed, and wait until the last days of May to place them permanently on a new bed, under a high frame or on a well-sheltered hillside, in light, well-smoked soil. When the summer is hot, you can succeed by planting on June 1 on an old bed. Harvesting is then later. It is necessary to water abundantly during hot spells. In our climate, okra will never be more than an amateur plant, not very productive, and market gardeners will not grow it. Its capsules travel well without spoiling, and if they were in demand in Paris, Provence, which already sends us some, could produce and sell them, at low prices, in quantities equal to demand. We know of French people living in the colonies who declare Gombo to be excellent; however, there is nothing to suggest that it will soon be a success. Foreigners living in Paris are so far the only ones to buy it when it goes on sale. We do, however, see it occupying an honorable place in a good house, very Parisian and very distinguished. It is frequently served on the table, and the cordon bleu chef who prepares it has kindly shared his recipe with us. Gombo seems to be only an accessory to the dishes of which it is a part, but it is not; it is essential and, without it, these dishes would not exist. Interesting information on okra can be found in the following publications: Flore des Antilles, by Descourtilz, vol. IV, p. 169; Note by Dr Sagot (Journal de la Société nationale d'Horticulture de France, 1872, p. 545); Manual of Gardening for Bengal and upper India. by Thomas A. C. Firminger; Agricultural botany, by William Darlington; Note by Léon Rattier (Revue horticole, 1854, p. 171). From this last, very extensive and instructive note, we feel we must extract a few passages: "During my travels in the East, I almost always heard it called by the Greeks Grekika kerata (Greek horns), and by the Turks Bamieh. The latter name seems to have been adopted in Egypt, according to M. Bové (Observations sur les cultures de l'Egypte, extractes des Annales de l'Institut horticole de Fromont, t. VI, avril 1834)... Wherever the plant is cultivated, it provides an important resource for food and medicine. In the West Indies, the natives eat it boiled in salted water or raw in salads. It forms the basis of Calalou, highly esteemed by the natives and popularly used. Nor do we disdain serving its various preparations on the most sumptuous tables; but then, almost always, it is combined with foods or seasonings of high taste, to spice up its somewhat bland flavor; it becomes a very pleasant dish, but no longer possesses the qualities that make it sought after for medicinal use...". "M. Bové says that in Egypt, Ketmie is one of the country's most common vegetables; that it is highly esteemed in the various regions where it is grown, and that Europeans consume it extensively for seven or eight months of the year. "Nafé paste and syrup, prepared in Paris with the mucilage of edible Ketmie, have given good therapeutic results. "Finally, the infusion of roasted Ketmie seeds has been recommended as a substitute for coffee, and M. Vilmorin, in the latest edition of Le Bon Jardinier, mentions this preparation as one of the least bad of those that have been substituted for mocha. "I doubt that M. Vilmorin has experimented with it himself, for I myself have drunk the infusion of Ketmie seeds in the East, often prepared with more than carelessness, and it has always seemed to me a very pleasant drink, offering a marked superiority over the inferior qualities of coffee and sometimes almost equaling mocha. But to obtain this result, it is necessary to use well-chosen seeds, perfectly ripe and roasted with great care. The process I've found most successful is to place the seeds in a wedge-burner and heat for as long as the crackling sound lasts. As soon as the crackling stops, place them on a marble table, or on any other object that will cool them quickly. Stack and filter. "I have tried to roast only to a degree sufficient to color the inside of the seed a light hazelnut; then the starch it contains remains soluble. After reducing it to a fine powder and passing it through a sieve, we mix it with milk or sugar water; if we then proceed as when we want to obliterate a flour porridge, we obtain a product quite similar to chocolate, very pleasant to the taste, and retaining much of the special aroma that distinguishes the seed...". CALALOU PREPARATION (1) Here are the recipes we have obtained. We give them as they are, without changing a syllable. Louisiana recipe. Calalou prepared with dried okra. - Take 500 grams of beef brisket and 500 grams of ham, and brown them in hot lard. When the beef and ham are well browned, moisten with the following: Shrimp heads crushed in a mortar, mixed with water and passed through a fine sieve. Add the shrimp, lobster or crab tails, chilli, salt and pepper to the pan. Put the okra in the boiling water and sauté (1) In his Flore phanérogamique des Antilles françuises, p. 48t, R. P. Duss, professor at the Collège de la Basse-Terre, describes Calatou as follows: "Calalou is an exquisite dish prepared with Xanthosoma hastifolium leaves, small thorny cucumbers (Cucumis Anguria), Mouzambi leaves (Gynandropsis pentaphylla), small silver-leaved Valerian underneath, (Boerhaavia paniculata), nightshade (Solanum nodiflorum), purslane, French sorrel, tender potato leaves, young okra fruit, Guinea sorrel (Hibiscus sabdariffa), tomatoes and chili pepper. To make the dish even spicier, add a little white wine, cloves, nutmeg and grated cinnamon bark. Cook with bacon, ham bone, salt beef or crabs (vulgo ciriques). Calalou is always served with Ru en grains. Add it at the same time as you moisten (Note by M. H.). We forgot to mention the quantity of Gombo in proportion to the 1,000 grams of meat, but I think 125 grams of dried Gombo should suffice. Recipes from Martinique. 1° Take 250 grams of ham and cook. When cooked, add 250 grams of chopped okra to the stock. Stir and cook for fifteen minutes. Add 250 grams of spinach; stir well and cook for a further fifteen minutes. Add a little butter if the preparation is not fatty enough, salt and a chili pepper. Cook for a further fifteen minutes. With this dish, you can eat rice cooked in water and salted like a rice cake. 2° Take spinach and cook it. When almost cooked, add sliced okra, chopped ham, chopped crabs or shrimps. Add a little butter, salt and pepper. Mix and grind everything together and cook over a low heat. You can also eat rice cooked in butter with this dish, which is very tasty. This recipe was tested at the 1867 Exposition. Recipe from Guadeloupe. (Given by an inhabitant of Pointe-à-Pître). Chopped okra and Seguine leaves (1) are cooked together. Bacon or crab is added; then, when everything is thoroughly cooked, it is mixed using an instrument similar to a chocolate frother. On the other hand, you can have rice cooked in water, and eat one with the other. Take a pound of rumpsteak, cut into small squares, brown in a saucepan with a little butter; add a little flour moistened with broth or water. Cook gently for four hours; add two fruits of Pimento and a chicken, cook with all and 250 grams of sausage. The okra should be cooked for two hours with all this. Put it in at the beginning of the third hour and serve. To eat with this soup, for some people's tastes, a pound of rice, not overcooked, is served on a separate plate.
