Add the water in which the lobster was cooked and the water from the oysters as a stock; then, while everything is still on the heat and boiling gently, add a tablespoonful, for six people, of spun gumbo. Stir, as with tapioca, until it's just right. Chalcam, between Tashkent and Samarkand 209 Gongoulou is a turnip of a very distinct variety, recommended for its beautiful red color, its pretty shape, which is that of a medium-sized spinning top, its strong flavor and its consistency. It doesn't melt when cooked, and is particularly suitable for garnishes and stews. We won't describe it here as a common turnip. We received the seeds through the kindness of Mr. Ermens, former director of cultivation for the Maharadja of Kashmir, who had requested them for us from Mr. Boulay, his successor in Srinagar. Gongoulou is grown in the same way as turnips, and requires no special recommendations. It is an interesting variety to add to the many we already have. Its area of cultivation is very extensive. Mr. G. Capus, the energetic travel companion of Messrs. Bonvalot and Pépin, wrote to us on April 2 1889: "I have encountered this toupiform Turnip in Bockara, Khiva, Turkestan and Kashmir. My notes mention the abundance of this Turnip (red spotted with white (1), on October 4th, in the bazaar of Oura-Tépé, a small fortress-city between Tachkent and Samarcande. The natives cut it into branches or small (1) The root seems to be spotted with white because it bleaches and partially discolors with water and rubbing. 14 pieces, like the Caroltes, and eat it raw or cooked with Palao (rice, mutton, spices), as we do in pot-au-feu. They call it Chalgam. Shall we recount what was reported to us by a merchant? A passer-by came into his house. Give me 2 kilos of Gongoulou," he said. I ate some at the foot of the Himalayas; I want my friends to taste it." Over there, it's eaten as a salad, sliced thinly and seasoned with salt and pepper, oil, vinegar and mustard. We didn't learn the name of this passer-by. GROSSE ANGUINE SOLANUM MACROCARPUM L. ; DC, Prodr. vol. XIII, 1, page 353; Dunal, Solanées, p. 244 Fam. des Solanées Maurice, Madagascar, Comores. Perennial, glabrous, with ample, non-spiny leaves; rather large, blue flowers; fleshy, globose, greenish-yellow berry, reticulated with green, the size of a small apple. This Solanum is grown in France in botanical gardens. We received its seeds from Mr. Daruty de Grandpré, President of the Société d'Acclimatation de Maurice. In his letter of March 15 1886, our kind colleague informed us of the plant's uses: "We prepare its green fruits like Aubergine, or boil them to eat them in salads. There's nothing remarkable about this vegetable, especially for European palates; but the plant brings in a lot and could be useful in the south of France, in Algeria and in the West Indies". Fig. 34 - Large Anguine (Solanum macrocarpum). We have grown large Anguine under cold conditions. It proved ornamental with its beautiful leaves and abundant flowers, but the plants grown from seeds sent from Mauritius did not bear fruit. On the other hand, in 1891, we obtained satisfactory results from seeds that Mr. Cornu, professor of culture at the Muséum, had been kind enough to give us, and which he had received as belonging to an indeterminate species of Solanum, with edible fruit, from Mr. Pierre, director of the Colonial Garden at Libreville (Gabon). This plant, in which we recognized the Solanum macrocarpum, gave us some beautiful fruits under frame, and we were able to harvest a few others, naturally less beautiful, from plants grown in the open air on old beds. Finally, Mr. J. Daveau, director of the Lisbon Botanical Garden, and Mr. Latour-Marliac, from Temple-sur-Lot, to whom we had entrusted a few seeds, sent us some superb fruits which they harvested. We prepared these fruits in the same way as the Aubergine, cutting them in half and sprinkling them with salt to make them drain. With the seeds removed, we filled the central part with minced meat and cooked. The resulting dish is very pleasing in appearance, as the skin of the fruit retains some of its pretty color; as for flavor, we have to declare that it is far inferior to Aubergine and always has a little pungency. In the letter quoted above, Mr. Daruty told us that he would explore Madagascar, whose flora is, according to him, extremely rich, as soon as the treaty with the Hovas was ratified. Here is what R. P. Camboué, from Tananarive, wrote to us on October 5 1886: "Your kind letter of April 23 last came to find me in the capital of the great African island, where I have finally arrived after a long and interesting journey. "Next spring, I shall send you various edible seeds. Despite the change of names, which are a little different from those given by S. de Flacourt, I was able to find some of the plants mentioned in your desiderata. "M. Daruty, from Maurice, one of your correspondents, is entering my home just as I am writing these lines. He has come to make a short excursion in our vicinity. He asks me to tell you that, if he finds anything for you, he will send it to you. "Pending the pleasure of sending you some of our seeds and some of our edible tubers, please accept, etc." This letter gave us great pleasure. To have two correspondents in Tananarive at the same time, who were able to discuss the goal that all four of us are pursuing, and to receive from them the promise of interesting shipments, wasn't that both a very singular encounter and a precious pledge for the future? HARICOT DE BARIA Tonkin bean. PHASEOLUS TUNKINENSIS Loureiro, Flora Cochinch, vol. Il, p. 529; DC, Prodr. vol. Il. p. 293. Leguminosae family. Perennial plant, about 2 or 3 meters high; stems voluble, twining; leaves composed of three leaflets, thick, small, wedge-shaped, flat, glabrous on both sides; flowers very small, greenish-white, becoming yellowish before withering, arranged in short, terminal, axillary clusters; rolled standard; short, compressed, smooth, hanging, somewhat crescent-shaped pods, containing three seeds, small, ovî̈des, dull white, reminiscent in shape and color of the common Hungarian Dwarf Bean. Baria Bean differs from Phaseolus lunatus only in its adult, hairless leaves with firmer tissue, its shorter and more numerous flower clusters, and its ovoid and not strongly compressed seed. "This plant, native to Tonkin, is cultivated in Cochinchina. Its seeds are good to eat. (Loureiro, loc. cit.) The Baria bean is cited by various authors as a very useful food plant and is generally cultivated in Cochinchina. Loureiro describes it as an annual; but we received seeds of two varieties, one annual, the other perennial, from M. Martin of Saigon. We only dealt with the latter. We sowed the seeds in April, in pots and terrines. At the end of May, we sowed in the open ground. On the same date, we placed the potted plants in the open air, with good exposure, and transplanted the terrine plants into the open air. The three cultivation methods we used gave roughly similar results; however, the advantage remained with the potted plants. We harvested few ripe pods, but enough fresh ones to eat twice as a family. Eaten like Flageolet, Baria beans are tender, very sweet and of good quality. However, in the Paris climate, it is only of interest to us if it is perennial, which we very much doubt, as our plants did not complete their full course of vegetation during the exceptionally warm season that has just ended, and were still showing flowers when the cold came. We only kept a small number of stumps, which we covered with cork debris; but these stumps did not produce shoots the following June. We have given up cultivating the Baria bean, which is not to say that we consider it impossible to grow in the Midi or in Algeria, but it seems to us that it requires six months of continuous high temperature, and we are far from being able to grow it here under these conditions. PHASEOLUS RICCIARDIANUS Tenore. Leguminosae family. We cultivated this plant from seeds given to us by M. Decaisne, professor of culture at the Muséum. It was not new to us. M. Durieu de Maisonneuve had already grown it in Bordeaux, praised it and distributed the seeds. Two notes on this plant were published in the Journal de la Société notionale d'Horticulture de France, 1868, p. 64 and 731. The first of these gives it as originating in China. M. le Dr Sagot has given us seeds of this plant, collected in Java by M. Colteau. The stems are voluble and pubescent, as are the leaves. The flowers are yellow, fairly large, and appear in great numbers throughout the summer. The pods are linear, cylindrical, very spindly and hairless; the seeds are small, 6 to 7 millimeters long and about 3 millimeters wide; they are cylindrical, slightly depressed laterally and rounded at both ends, reddish-brown in color; the hilum is linear, white and about two-thirds as long as the seed. The plant is extremely productive, and if it doesn't find enough warmth in the Paris climate to produce a regular, abundant harvest every year, it would certainly thrive in the Midi. We tasted its fruits in salads. The flavor is nil. The plant's extreme fecundity is its only merit. EARTH HORN Hog-nut. AMPHICARPÆA MONOICA Ell. et Nutt. Glycine monoica L., G. comosa L., G. bracteata L., G. monoica Michx, Cryntolobus americanus Spreng, C. sarmentosus Spreng. Leguminosae family. Grows in wooded and bushy areas of North America: Canada, Florida, Louisiana. Annual, climbing plant, about 1.25 m high, with a slender root that plunges deep into the ground, a slender, much-branched stem, pubescent or covered with brownish hairs, bearing trifoliolate leaves with oblong oval segments. The flowers appear in August-September; they are of two kinds: airy and perfect, and imperfect and petalless, borne on loose, whitish, stoloniform underground shoots that grow from the collar of the plant. These flowers are followed by fruits, which are absolutely different in shape in both cases. Those produced in the soil look like small tubers and, as they perpetuate the plant without the need for resowing, the species was long thought to be perennial (1). The perfect flowers are gathered in numerous, short, hanging clusters, attached to the branches by thread-like peduncles. They are composed of a tubular calyx with four or five short, somewhat triangular teeth, and a purplish or almost white corolla with a carina and wings of similar shape, a little shorter and much narrower than the standard, borne on long tabs. The stamens are diadelphous; the ovary is quadri-ovulate. These flowers are often sterile. The pod is small, linear, oblong and compressed, containing three or four very small, kidney-shaped, yellow seeds mottled with brown. In the imperfect flowers, the calyx differs little from that of the aerial flowers, but the corolla is missing altogether, with the exception sometimes of a rudiment of the stamen (1) There is a native Vetch, Vicia amphicarpa, as curious as the Earth Bean, which also gives aerial and underground seeds. The stamens, which are either absent or 5 to 10 in number, are distinct; 3 or 4 of them have perfect anthers, while the others have only rudimentary ones. Ovary 1-3 ovulate. These flowers are usually fertile; they give rise to an obovate or pyriform fruit, blackish purple when ripe, enclosing one or two large seeds with considerably thickened cotyledons, enveloped by a whitish epidermis, variegated with purple. The fruit usually ripens below the surface of the soil. Amphicarpxa sarmentosa is a more recumbent form, bearing only a small number of petal-like flowers. In 1887, we asked Vilmorin to provide us with a perennial bean that we knew existed in North America. But instead of the Kidney bean, a large perennial bean from Arizona, which the Apaches eat green and dry, they received and gave us seeds of Amphicarpæa monoica. We sowed them on April 25, 1888. They vegetated well, fructified above and below ground, and the underground beans left in the soil produced abundant, vigorous plants in the spring of 1889. Amphicarpæa is as easy to grow as chickling vetch (Lathyrus sativus). The underground fruits should be removed as late as possible. In November, there are still pods forming in the ground, at all stages of development. Apparently, yields in loose, well-smoked and well-worked soil could be as high as one liter per square meter, but we can't guarantee this. Harvesting would be much more difficult than for ordinary beans, but it might have the merit of providing a fresh vegetable throughout the winter. We consulted the authors in vain to find out whether Amphicarpæa seeds were edible. They are silent on this point. They could have been poisonous, and we would have prudently abstained from tasting them; but we were fully reassured in this respect by Mr. V. Havard, surgeon of the United States Army. On October 22, 1889, our kind and learned correspondent sent us the following interesting letter: "I went to consult the botanical library of Columbia College, where are the fine collections of Dr. Torrey and others, about Amphicarpæa monoica, but almost without results. I had some notes on this plant at Fort Bufard, which I will pass on to you on my return, if they seem to me to be of any importance. The aerial seeds are not edible; they may be quite tasty, but they are too small and yield too little for cultivation. The underground seeds are edible. I've seen the Indians dig them up and stock up on them in spring, after the first leaves have sprouted - in other words, as soon as there is any sign of their presence. "These seeds are found 4 or 5 centimetres below the ground and are easily extracted with a pointed stick. They are almost entirely formed by the cotyledons, are rounded, somewhat flattened and 1 to 2 centimetres in diameter. They are eaten like beans. The Indians often just roast them over coals. I don't distinctly remember their taste, except that it's not to be despised. "This plant has never been cultivated by the Indians, much less by the whites, who are unaware of its properties. "Torrey says that, in places where this curious plant abounds, pigs dig into the ground to extract the underground nuts, from which it gets its name of Hog-nut. The plant seemed interesting enough to us that we felt authorized to distribute the product of our first collection of aerial seeds at one of the sessions of the Société nationale d'Acclimatation. JAPANESE EARTH HORN AMPHICARPÆA EDGEWORTHI Benth. var. JAPONICA. Leguminosae family. We have grown A. Edgeworthi Benth. var. japonica Oliver, which we received from Japan, where it bears the names Gin-Mame and Yobu-Mame. It is the Aha of the Ainos. In their note entitled : Ainu Economic plants (Transactions of the Asiatic Society of Japon, vol. XXI, 1893, p. 217), Messrs Batchelor and Miyabe state that the Ainos collect the seeds of this plant in large quantities. They are boiled, alone or with rice, after having been stripped of their husks, roots and young shoots. They are said to be very sweet, with a taste reminiscent of chestnuts. The underground seeds are about 7 to 10 times larger than those produced in the pods, which are born above ground. The former are known as Aha, and the latter as Ahacha. Ahacha are also collected and usually eaten boiled, with rice. From a culinary point of view, this plant does not seem to us to present any notable differences from the American ground bean, with which it is very similar in botanical characteristics. Among the latter, we would highlight the shape, volume and color of the aerial seeds, which in the A. monoica, are kidney-shaped, depressed, averaging 6 mm long by 5 mm wide, light brown in color, mottled with blackish brown, whereas in A. Edgeworthi, var. japonica, they are slightly smaller, oblong-cylindrical, obtuse at both ends, covered with a grayish vestimentum that can be rubbed off and under which the black testa appears. HARICOT MUNGO PHASEOLUS MUNGO L. - Phaseolus radiatus L.; P. minimus Rumph; P. hirtus Retz; P. Max Roxb; P. subvolubilis Ham; P. aureus Roxb; P. viridissimus Ten; P. humilis Hassk. Leguminosae family. Annual plant, with cylindrical, hairy, twiggy, rarely voluble stem; leaves with three oval or oval-lanceolate, acuminate leaflets; geminate, oblong lanceolate stipules; long petioles; axillary, angular peduncles bearing flowers gathered at the head; yellow flowers; pods are slender, cylindrical, horizontally arranged, hairy, 5 to 6 centimeters long and 3 to 4 millimeters in diameter, black when ripe, containing six to fifteen very small seeds, glabrous, green, white, black, yellow or red, depending on variety, oval, truncated at both ends; linear hilum. Germination is interesting: the cotyledons remain underground, and the second internode is very long. Under the name Phaseolus Mungo are grouped a large number of related forms, which various botanists have made into species, while others consider them to be mere varieties. They are simply characterized, some by a subvivaceous root, instead of being annual; others by a more or less climbing stem instead of being straight, a fistulous stem, more or less wide stipules, small, or rare, or long and fairly abundant hairs; the color of the seeds has also been used to distinguish some of them, although the same variety sometimes seems to have seeds of different colors. P. Mungo L. (typica) has green or black seeds, while P. radiatus !.. has brownish-red or greenish-yellow seeds. The Mung Bean is native to Asia and the Indian Archipelago, and is generally grown in India. MUNGO BEANS IN CHINA Dr. E. Bretschneider wrote to us: "I believe that Soja hispida and Phaseolus radiatus have a great future in Europe. "The very small-grained Phaseolus, grown in Peking under the name Lou teou (Green Pea), and from which vermicelli is made, is the true Phaseolus radiatus. It's a very important plant for the Chinese. This vermicelli is very good to eat. I'll get you some samples from Peking, but later. The doctor had previously written to us: "I'm sorry you haven't received the pasta made from the seeds of Ph. radiatus (1). These are long threads, like vermicelli. The dough is made from the flour of Lou teou seeds and hot water, and passed through a sieve. A JAVA On his return from Java, Dr de la Savinière pointed out the importance of growing Ph. Mungo (1) These pastes, sent to the Société d'Acclimatation, did not reach him. - P. B. in the country, and we took interesting notes under his dictation. In Java, the plant is known as Katjang heedjah or hied-joe (pron. Katian idiou). Among other uses, it is sown very tightly on an impermeable bed and covered with a centimetre of water. After two days, the shoots are harvested; they are Om, 07 in length and have the color of Capuchin Beard. These shoots, marketed in considerable quantities, are called Taugee (pron. Taugué). Gado-Gado is made as follows: put a little oil in the pan; throw in the Phaseolus Mungo shoots; after cooking for ten minutes, add black sauce (Ketjap), egg yolk, turmeric, etc. IN JAPAN The mung bean is one of the Japanese plants we've been growing for the longest time. We received seeds from Dr. HETT in June 1878, accompanied by this note: "This bean does very well here (Haute-Savoie) and ripens to the last pod. It should be sown very far apart, at least 50 or 60 centimetres, and only two seeds per clump, which becomes very strong." The following November, the doctor wrote to us: "I enclose with my letter a variety of Adzuki, different from the one I sent you. It was called Ahi Adzuki, i.e. Autumn Adzuki, because it is sown in July and harvested in November; the one I sent you earlier is called Natsu Adzuki, i.e. Summer Adzuki, because it is sown in May and harvested at the end of August. I believe that both varieties need the whole summer to ripen their seeds. September 1879. "I am sending you a sample of the paste or jam made in Japan under the name of Yo-kan with Adzuki, sugar and a kind of gelatin extracted from seaweed. This absolutely tasteless gelatin is known in Japan as Kan-ten. The Japanese use it extensively in cooking, and large quantities are exported to Europe. I'm afraid it will be difficult to sell Adzuki paste cheaply in France; sugar is too expensive. "Here's the recipe for Yo-kan, as practiced in Japan, and as I've often used here: soak the Adzuki for twenty-four hours in non-calcareous water; here, I use rainwater; in Japan, we simply use river water that contains no trace of lime. Cook them with water, then strain them to obtain a slightly thick purée; add half the sugar (by weight), then cook again and add, at the end of cooking, jelly obtained by dissolving Kan-ten (seaweed glue) while hot. I can't tell you what proportion of kan-ten to use, as the strength of kan-ten varies greatly according to quality. A square stick of Kan-ten, Om, 37 long by Om, 03 square, will, when of good quality, make a very consistent jelly from a liter of any liquid; I forgot to tell you that while the Kan-ten dissolution is hot, it must be passed through a cloth to retain any impurities or undissolved parts. "I have grown Adzuki (Natsu Adzuki) only in very small quantities for my own use; they grow quite well 18 here; however, I believe we would be more assured of good maturity in more calcareous and less compact soils. Here, the clumps become enormous and the inner pods often rot... "Although I don't have much Adzuki to harvest this year, if you'd like to try it out on a larger scale, I could send you some for seed, as you don't need much, as the plants have to be sown very far apart." April 1881. "In Japan, Adzuki (P. Mungo) are soaked in water and germinated in warm, dark earthen vases; they are then washed with plenty of water to remove the skins, and the seedlings are eaten under the name Moyashi (eye and legs). Needless to say, they are cooked first in salt water, then in Shoyu. It's quite tasty and an easy way to get a fresh vegetable in winter." IN FRANCE In 1862, Mgr Guillemin, Bishop of Canton, presented the Societé d'Acclimatation with a very large collection of seeds, including Phaseolus Mungo, also known as Lou teou, the green pea from which the Chinese make a fine vermicelli, Lou teou sze, and a highly esteemed wine, Lou teou tsieou. At the same time, Mr. P. Dabry also offered the Society a collection that included two species or varieties of P. Mungo, under the name Tsin teou. In June 1880, Mr. Eugène Simon sent the Muséum a considerable collection which also included Lou teou. At the same time, Mr. C. Ford, Director of the Hong Kong Botanical Garden, sent Mr. Giquel, who had kindly requested it for us, a collection in which the Lou teou was included, with the words: Medicine in fever. Syn. Liu teou. It was also at this time that M. Faivre, from Beaune, was receiving and distributing Lou teou seeds from China, a plant whose importance had been pointed out to him. Since 1878, we've been growing Phaseolus Mungo on garden beds, and every year we've harvested ripe fruit. Ripening is very late, however, and we strongly recommend leaving plenty of space between plants and sowing no more than three seeds in each hole. We have tried cultivating four or five varieties whose pods have not matured, and have only succeeded with Summer Adzuki (Natzu Adzuki), whose seeds are relatively large and dull red. The clumps are strong and the yield equal to that of our common beans. We'd also like to mention the Yaye nari or Bundô, Lou teou of the Chinese, whose extremely small, green gramn is used to make pasta. Whenever we sowed it on its own, its shoots melted as soon as they came out of the ground, and nothing remained of them; but as chance would have it, some seeds mixed with those of Adzuki were sown in the same holes, and we saw with pleasure, and not without surprise, that, under the protection of the latter, Yaye nari grew quite well and produced a small harvest. We consider a result obtained in this way to be a failure and assume that, in a climate a little warmer than that of Crosues, the cultivation of green mung beans will present no difficulties. We have made a purée with early Adzuki that we have found to be very good. Its flavor is close to that of both beans and lentils; it's a good vegetable. We are awaiting with extreme interest the samples of Lou teou vermicelli that Dr. Bretschneider has kindly requested for us in Peking. If the tasting is favorable, we hope that they will try to win the prize offered by the Société d'Acclimatation for the cultivation of Phaseolus Mungo or radiatus: "The prize will be awarded to the person who has successfully cultivated this Bean in a field of at least half a hectare. "If there are several competitors, preference will be given to the one who produces the finest specimens of food preparations obtained from its seeds. "Competition extended. Prize: 300 francs. Phaseolus viridissimus Tenore, a variety of P. Mungo, was sent to us from Baghdad in March 1889 by Mr. C. Metaxas, delegate of the Société nationale d'Acclimatation. This bean is known in the country as Mash. Here is the information accompanying the shipment: "I am sending you seeds of a summer vegetable grown here in the same way as beans. The Arabs call it Mâsh. It is sold at the market as you see it. The natives like it very much, and some prefer it to lentils, although it doesn't fetch the same price. Shortly afterwards, we received the same seeds from Mr. Alexandre Bataline, professor of botany at the Imperial Garden in Saint Petersburg. They had come from China. A little later, M. de Heldreich, professor of botany in Athens, sent us Phaseolus viridissimus, grown, he told us, in Messinia. We had often received it from Japan, under the name Yayenari, and from China, under the name Lou teou. From Pondicherry, we received ten varieties of mung bean, including not only Phaseolus viridissimus, but other varieties with very small white, yellow, red or black seeds. The area of cultivation of this plant extends from Japan to Greece, over China, the whole of India, Asian Turkey, and finally Messinia. We cullivated Mâsh unsuccessfully in the Paris climate, but not without harvesting a few seeds, and we hoped that the plant would have flourished beyond the Loire. There, we thought it might have produced a yield equal to or greater than that of the lentil, and that it could have replaced this vegetable, whose cultivation has been abandoned in France, to such an extent that the trade is obliged to obtain supplies abroad, in Moravia, Bohemia and neighboring countries. Thanks to M. Mélaxas, we now have a sufficient quantity of seeds at our disposal to carry out cultivation trials in various parts of France, beyond the Loire, in Algeria and Tunisia. We have to declare that the results of the experiment were not satisfactory. In France, only in the southernmost parts of the country was the harvest good; on the other hand, it was a complete success in our African possessions. The shipment of seeds we received from Pondicherry included the following Phaseolus species, whose cultivation did not give us any results worth mentioning: Kârâmanie payarou, Phaseolus calcaratus Roxb. Sada payarou, Phaseolus farinosus L. Toulka payarou (Moth or Mothi in Hindustan), Phaseolus aconitifolius L. fil. Caloundou; Mäsh Kolai (Beng.); Urd Mäsh (Hind.), Phaseolus radiatus L. Patché payarou; Mung or Mug (Hind.), Phaseolus Mungo L. There was also a bag of seeds of Cottavaré (Cyamopsis psoraloides DC.), a curious annual legume of the Lotée tribe, cited by Dr. Forbes Watson as supplying green peas to a large part of the population of India every year. The plant does not ripen its seeds in our climate. IGNAME ORIGINS Spontaneous Yam does not appear to exist in India. In Ceylon, Mr. Thwaites lists six native species. In China, Dioscorea Batatas has not been found in the wild. Other species have probably existed in the wild. Dr. Bretschneider lists three Dioscorea species as cultivated in China, and adds: "Dioscorea is indigenous to China, as it is mentioned in the oldest medical textbook, that of Emperor Schen nung. In Japan, Dioscorea japonica Thunb., generally cultivated under the name Naga imo, is probably indigenous, as is D. Batatas, Yama imo, which is not cultivated and is uprooted in the woods. Dioscorea alata, cultivated in the Pacific islands, is probably native to these regions, but has not yet been found in the wild. In America, Dioscorea triloba is probably indigenous. There are a few other species that are little or not at all cultivated. In Africa, there are even fewer spontaneous Dioscorea species than in Asia and America. In short, several wild Dioscorea species in Asia, especially in the Asian Archipelago, and others, less numerous, growing in America and Africa, were introduced into cultivation as food plants at times probably less remote than many other species (1). We are happy to present the reader with the unpublished study that follows, which we owe to the learned professor and tireless worker Dr. Paul Sagot. He could not have given us a gift we could appreciate more, and we are deeply grateful to him. THE DIOSCOREA BOTANICAL GENUS The Dioscorea genus contains over two hundred species, the vast majority of which are found in warm countries. However, a sizeable number of species grow outside the tropics, in Chile, the Cape of Good Hope, Australia, China, Japan and the United States. A small species was discovered on the (1) Vorigine des plantes cultivées, by Alph. de Candolle. southern slopes of the Pyrenees, fifteen or twenty years ago, to the extreme surprise of botanists. Many species are large lianas growing very high on trees; many are of more modest proportions; some (particularly in Chile) are very small plants, 2 or 4 decimeters high, with tubers as large as a Hazelnut or a Pea. Almost all have a tuberous perennial stump, and the stems, though tall, are only annual. The D. villosa from the United States, however, has a woody rhizome, small, elongated and running underground. Botanists have varied on the nature of the tuber: for some, it is a rhizome, i.e. a radiciform underground stem because it emits buds and has a structure reminiscent of a stem; for others, it is a root-like organ, gorging itself with starch and accumulated nutritive juices. In samples of mediocre-sized species whose roots have been harvested, many of the root fibers seem to emerge from the plant collar above the tuber. The tuperculus is sometimes soft and floury, sometimes hard and leathery, sweet-tasting and edible, or acrid and poisonous, simple or lobulated, fasciculate, multiple, concentrated or diffuse with partial pedicellate tubers. It is placed underground, at a lesser or greater depth. The stem is climbing, voluble on the left. It is tender or leathery, the size of a pen or slightly larger, or very spindly, inert or thorny, round or angular, with membranous wings. Leaves are usually cordate, acuminate, attenuated at the apex, polynervate, and never toothed at the edges. They are firm-coriaceous or membranous-thin, usually glabrous, sometimes a little tomentose or pubescent, especially on the underside. They vary from species to species, being large, mediocre or small, narrower or wider, strongly cordate at the base, obtuse or even rounded and sub-attenuated. Some species have lobed leaves or even 3, 5 and 7 leaflets. Leaves are either opposite or alternate; however, on the opposite-foliated species, there are a number of alternate leaves, especially on the small terminal branches. These details, perhaps a little long and meticulous, will make it easy to understand that the Dioscorea genus is still poorly known, difficult to study and to classify in herbariums; that it is very easy to suppose distinct specimens of the same species (main stem or terminal branches, abnormally shaped flowers or fruit...).); that, if botanical sections have been established in the genus in relation to seed, fruit or elamines, many specimens are impossible to classify, if they have only male flowers, female flowers without ripe fruit, too young fruit..., etc.; that, depending on whether the botanist likes to distinguish many species in minute detail, or to unite many forms under a single name, there will be many more or many fewer species admitted. The latest monograph on the Dioscoreaceae is by Kunth (1850): Enumeratio plantarum... It includes one hundred and fifty-nine species of Dioscorea and thirty species of Helmia, a genus that most botanists now group with Dioscorea. Judging by a few cultivated or American species that I've seen alive and with which I'm familiar, I think Kunth has multiplied the species too much. On the other hand, after Kunth's work, herbaria received a number of new species, notably from Brazil, Mexico and Africa... Various local floras have also published new species, or combined several earlier species into a single species. M. de Candolle's chapter on yams (Origine des plantes cultivées) gives a glimpse of the many uncertainties surrounding our knowledge of this very difficult genus. Dioscorea globosa Roxburgh- Excellent species, ranked first in English India; highly esteemed by Europeans. White, rounded roots, often very large; Roxburgh has only seen this species cultivated. D. rubella Roxb. and D. purpurea Roxb. may be compared as varieties. I have not found this D. globosa in the Museum herbarium. Its description bears some resemblance to D. alata. The stem is winged, the petiole also winged; the leaf shape is similar, but there are some differences. The lower part of the stem is aculeate, the leaves are alternate or opposite, the petiole is longer, and the flowers are fragrant. D. triloba Lam. - Excellent American species. Tubers very tender and floury. D. alata L. - A good species, though not as fine in quality as the previous two. Considerably cultivated in Oceania, where it is (along with Taro) the main food plant. Highly productive when carefully cullivated. Grown in Java, Mauritius... Quite numerous varieties. D. Berteroana Kunth (D. cayennensis Lamk.). - West African species, cultivated in America, where it has been imported. Fig. 35 - Winged-stem yam (Dioscorea alata). large, productive, very hardy. Single, rounded, discoid tuber, sometimes a little hard. Leaves somewhat leathery, resistant to sunburn. D. atropurpurea Roxb. - Asian species, widely cultivated in Malacca, Pegu and the eastern islands. Very large tuber with purplish flesh, at least near the bark. Appears to be a very productive species. The description bears some resemblance to D. alata, which has varieties with purplish roots. D. eburnea Loureiro. - Wild and cultivated in Cochinchina; large, long, curved, white roots, vaguely similar in shape to an elephant's tusk. No spines. Leaves alternate, cordate. D. aculeata L. - Wild and cultivated in India, the Malay Archipelago and Oceania. D. pentaphylla L. - Tubers very slender, floury, of mediocre volume, subrounded, multiple. D. sativa L. - Uncertain species, poorly known, previously confused. Leaves cordate, alternate; bulbils axillary; fruit obovate wedge-shaped. Linné received the seed from America and cullivated it in a garden where the tuber persisted in the ground without the stem flowering (Voy. Hortus Cliffortianus). Flora writers Miquel, Flora Indiæ batavæ; Bentham, Flora Honghongensis; Bojer, Hortus mauritianus, mention this species as wild and cultivated in India, Mauritius and the Moluccas. Linné, who bred it from seeds received from America, assures us that it also grows in India. Appears to be very similar to D. bulbifera, differing only in less vigorous vegetation, a tuber without pungency, and a larger fruit. D. Schimperiana Hochst. Collected wild in Abyssinia by Schimper. Cooked root, edible according to Schimper's label. Young shoots puberulent. Similar to D. sativa L. D. bulbifera L. - Appears to grow wild on the old and new continents. The American plant (Guiana and West Indies) has been described as D. lutea Mey. Vigorous vegetation; abundant flowering; large, light-green, cordate leaves; numerous, very large axillary bulbils. Tubers probably large, but pungent, to be destroyed by washing with water. D. japonica Thunb. and D. Batatas Dene. - Species that can vegetate in temperate climates, where however the first cold snatches their leafy stems before they have supplied all their juices to the tubers; also cultivated in warm climates, in southern China, in Java. Numerous axillary bulblets. Tubers fusiform, elongated. I was unable to find out whether, in Algiers, the leafy stems of D. Batatas turned yellow and dried out in autumn before the roots were harvested, but I did learn that it takes several years for a plant raised from bulblets to become a vigorous plant. D. triphylla L., syn. D. dœmona Roxb. - Wild-growing species in Asia, the Malay Archipelago and East Africa; very vigorous vegetation; aculeate, elevated, sublinear stem; gradate, trifoliolate leaves; paniculiform male inflorescence with numerous dense, short, bracteolate flower spikes; ovoid, oblong, trigonal, large fruit. Tuber voluminous, discoid, probably a little hard, often (always?) nauseous (Roxburgh). D. oppositifolia L. (D. nummularia Lamk.). - Wild species, growing abundantly throughout southern Asia, around which are grouped various related species, which are perhaps only varietals; medium vigor of vegetation; young shoots more or less puberulent. Leaves oval, slightly acuminate at the top, not cordate at the base, briefly petiolate. Abundant flowering. Male spikes short, clustered, fasciculated on a short floral axis. Fruit ovoid, rounded, trigonal, flattened, leathery. Roots hard and somewhat woody, according to Roxburgli, of mediocre volume according to other authors, eaten by natives. A. Reynoso says that certain lgnames can multiply by cuttings or marcotte (I assume he means D. triloba), which I have never seen multiply by cuttings in French Guiana. In general, yam tubers that have been cut and washed in water are slippery and glutinous, exuding a peculiar gum. They are dense and sink to the bottom of the water. They are thought to be more nutritious than potatoes. Inerminous species with tender leaves could make good green fodder. Aerial bulblets are abundant or occur at least fairly often: Dioscorea Batatas; D. japonica; D. sativa; D. bulbifera; D. crispata Roxb.; D. toxicaria Bojer, from East Africa; D. alata. Single voluminous tuber or tubers in small numbers, fascicles, united, not growing to a depth that makes extraction inconvenient: D. Berterouna; D. alata; D. eburnea; D. atropurpurea; D. aculeata Roxb. ; D. fasciculata Roxb.; D. globosa Roxb. Tubercules pedicellate, multiple, somewhat diffuse, but easy to extract: D. triloba Lam; D. pentaphylla. Tubers pungent to some degree or even poisonous: D. bulbifera; D. triphylla and its variety D. dæmona; Roxburgh says of the latter that it is "terribly nauseating, even after being put in water"; D. toxicaria Bojer, from the east coast of Africa (Monbaze). Vigneron-Jousserandier says that in Brazil, an Iguame native to Africa, with a thorny stem and large single tuber, which seems to me to be D. Berteroana, has a somewhat bitter tuber. Wild species whose tubers are good, or at least of acceptable use in times of shortage: Good: D. pentaphylla; D. aculeata. Fairly good: D. anguina Roxb., lnde. - - - D. oppositifolia, India. D. Schimperiana, Abyssinia. D. tomentosa, Ceylon (Thwaites, Flor. Ceylon), resembles D. triphylla. D. intermedia Thwaites. - D. spicata Roxb. Notes communicated at various times to Dr. Paul Sagot. In 1854, agricultural journals in the United States recommended the cultivation of lgnames in the Southern States: D. sativa (?) D. alata (?); it was claimed to be very profitable, yielding 17,000 kilograms per hectare (Patent Office Reports). New Zealand yam has been tried in France, but without success. Professor Chatin published a brochure on this species. M. Hardy had obtained some beautiful tubers in Algiers. What botanical species was this New Zealand yam? D. alata, var. (??) It had been received from Calculta, from Mr. Piddington (Report by M. Hardy, Bull. Soc. d'Accl., vol. V, p. 546, 1858). The New Zealand flora does not mention any Dioscorea. M. Hardy, at the Hamma garden in Algiers, made many observations on lgnames. He exhibited a fine collection at the 1867 Universal Exhibition. At the 1878 Exhibition, there were none. In 1877, following multiple cullings, M. Hardy published the following table of comparative yields: kil. 1° lgname ailée blanche........ 12,380 l'hectare. 2° Igname cultivée.. 20,960 3° Purple yam............. 23,700 4° New Zealand yam. 23,700 5° Chinese yam........... 33,000 6° Elephant yam........ 37,040 7° Trifoliate yam............. 46,660 8° Turtle-leg yam..... 64,780 - - - - - - - - Yam, being an irrigated summer crop, is difficult to spread widely in Algeria. Injured tubers are said to rot easily. When propagating by tuber fragments, M. Hardy explained that the fragment had to bear bark for a shoot to emerge. In Algiers, the planting of a bulbous Chinese yam yielded, in the first year, only a root the size of a little finger; this root could be used as a seedling for the following year. The largest roots of Chinese yam, in the first cultivation trials, weighed 675 grams. Later, much larger roots were obtained. I cannot guess which botanical species are represented by Mr. Hardy's vulgar names, other than: I. ailée blanche, D. alata; I. de Chine, D. Batatas; I. trifoliée, D. triphylla. I don't know whether the Chinese yam in Algiers reaches full maturity, i.e. turns yellow and dries out. I don't know whether the Botanical Garden of Orotava, Tenerife (Canary Islands), has cultivated yams. In the Canaries, by misuse of words, we call lgnames Colocases, Aroïdées tuberculeuses. The same vicious name is sometimes used in Brazil, where real lgnames are called Caras (Vigneron-Jousserandier). I'm not aware of any collections being cultivated at the Jardin d'Acclimatation in Antibes, nor at the Jardin d'Acclimatation in Collioure. I believe they were grown most extensively at the Calcutta Botanical Garden. In Calculta, the rains don't start until May or June, so it would be easy to ask for seedlings for France in March or April. There is a large plant of D. alata in the greenhouses of the Muséum. As it is kept in the most humid, warm greenhouse, its stem veges, I believe, continuously. 16 SUMMARY GLOSSOLOGICAL INDICATIONS The word Igname appears to come from the American word Namain (Caribbean dictionary) or from the American word Inicoma. The authors say that they are called Yam in part of the west coast of Africa, and that this word also means "to eat". On the island of Cuba, D. triloba, an American lgname, is called Aje, while D. Berteroana, or yam brought from Africa, is called Name (A. Reynoso, Agricultura de los indigenas de Cuba et Haïti). It can also be found in Brazil, in the yams known as Caras, while the name Inhame is sometimes used for an edible tuber Aroidea, a species brought from Africa, with a large single tuber, spiny stem, sandy soil and good drought resistance: D. Berteroana. In Mauritius and Reunion, yams are called Cambare. In Taïti, Oceania and the Malay Archipelago, they are called Ubi, Oubi, Oebi. In Calcutta, Aloo (alou), Kam aloo, D. alata. In China, Sain-In, Chou-Yu, Tou-tchou, Chan-yu (De Candolle, Origine des plantes cultivées). In general, lgnames are much more cullivated in hot, humid countries than in hot, dry ones. In Oceania, there is a strong desire for regular rainfall immediately after planting. In hot countries, they usually remain in vegetation for six or eight months before being harvested. If the plant is strong and the soil fertile, vegetation lasts longer. If a plant is weak, it may take up to two years before it can be harvested. SUMMARY The study of yam cultivation is complicated by the fact that there are fifteen or twenty good species in cultivation, and that they differ greatly from one another. Intertropical America, Attica, India, the Malay Archipelago and China cultivate their own particular species, and although several of the good species have been carried from one continent to another, it is not possible to compare them on the spot or with equal knowledge of each other. Each species has, moreover, several varieties of unequal merit, and one author may have appreciated a species without knowing its best varieties and its best method of cultivation. No botanical garden in warm countries has yet assembled a collection of all cultivated Iguams, and if one were able to do so, it would still not be possible to establish a complete and rigorous comparison of the relative merits of the species, as the local climate may to some degree favor the vegetation of one species and discourage that of another. Without leaving hot climates, one species may require more abundant and prolonged rainfall, another a longer dry season, and another too much solar radiation. The cooking of lgnames, even in their native country, is delicate and shows great variations in yield. Nothing is more uncertain than a general assessment based on a partial trial of one species, especially a foreign one. Cultivation in European greenhouses, and even at the Jardin Botanique d'Alger, has not provided much in the way of solid documentation. In Algiers, the climate is far too dry in summer, the only season when the heat is sufficient, and Yams have only arrived from a few countries, without any precise bolanic determination, most often with simple vulgar local names. It is even possible that some of the species collected by Mr. Hardy have not been preserved in the garden. In greenhouses, we can't give the lgnames sufficient space, soil or heat, nor can we alternate the six or eight months of humid heat with six or four months of dry heat and rest. For some species, cultivation under glass may be a little more successful than in the greenhouse, but the house will never achieve a general result that can be applied to all species and lead to truly practical results; a mealy root, however good it may be, cannot hope for a fancy price like an exotic fruit. The general conditions for successful yam cultivation in hot climates are as follows: Choice of an excellent species; Planting of robust seedlings. In the best climate and in the best soil, a small, young bud can only produce a small, unproductive plant, which will take at least two, three or five years to develop into a good seedling; Fertile, well-drained soil, wide spacing and a support system to support the climbing stems and expose them to the air and sun; Sufficient, sustained, humid heat for leafy vegetation, which normally lasts six to eight months and which, in insufficient temperature, should be prolonged further to acquire its full development; Dry heat of at least four months for tuber formation, reabsorbing the nutritive juices from the leafy stems, which must turn yellow and dry out while the tuber grows and gorges itself with starch. The study we have just read deals scientifically with the question of lgnames. It does not reveal the rank each variety occupies in the esteem of those who cultivate it, nor the details of cultivation and use, particular to each country, which should not be ignored. We find these various nolions in the writings of botanists, in the publications and correspondence of travelers and Creole and European planters, and we believe it useful to present them to our readers. I. - Yam in China and Japan. Yam seems to occupy only a very secondary place in the cultures of China and Japan, and is represented there, with few exceptions, only by Dioscorea japonica and one or two varieties of the species. Dr. Bretschneider writes: "In Peking, only D. japonica is grown (I don't know whether this species differs from D. Batatas, as the late Mr. Decaisne claimed). Like you, the Chinese complain about the great difficulty involved in pulling up the fragile rhizomes of this plant. They have to break up the ground to pull them out. In Peking, yams are only grown in gardens. The natives think less of it than the potato (Batatas edulis), which is grown on a large scale in the fields". In Le Japon à l'Exposition universelle de 1878, we read: "Tsuku imo (Dioscorea japonica) is eaten cooked. One of its varieties, called Jecheimo (1), has flattened tubers; its seeds, known as Nukago, can be eaten (2). "Jinen jo (Dioscorea japonica, var.) is a wild plant used in much the same way as the previous one; it produces a highly prized starch. "Naga imo, or Dioscorea japonica, is used for the same purposes as the previous one; it includes a variety called Shisen imo. It can also be made into a (1) It should probably be read Jeche imo. (2) Don't we mean bulblets here? a kind of gruel, eaten with a special sauce, if grated and pounded beforehand; this also applies to the two preceding varieties. It can be seen from the above that Dioscorea japonica is the only variety grown in Japan, and that Naga imo appears to be the tvpe, of which there are four varieties. We received Naga imo (1) and another variety with long rhizomes; we found no appreciable difference between these two yams. We also received a variety with rounded rhizomes: it vegetates badly and its tubers do not grow. We received from Dr HE**, under the name Kiri imo, two small tubers of a Yam cultivated in Japan. The doctor wrote to us from Savoie: "This yam does not do well here. I tried it successively in clay soil, then in gravelly soil, both of good quality, and it did not grow well on either side. In Japan, it is grown preferably in greasy sand that retains a little moisture and is very heavily smoked, which is essential for harvesting slightly large yams." Here are some more extracts from the doctor's letters: "Naga imo, long tuberous edible root, as the word imo applies to all starchy edible roots. This is the name given to one of the cultivated varieties of D. Batatas. The same, in its wild state and more highly esteemed as a food, is called Yama imo, mountain root. I don't know either Tsuku imo or Jinen jo. In the part of Japan I lived in, we don't cultivate D. Batatas; we just collect its tubers, which are very common in the woods, but very difficult to pull up. They are more highly prized than cultivated yams. In the case of Kiri imo (D. Decaisneana), for small plantations, we confine ourselves to preserving the snares as and when they are consumed; we plant them in April and uproot them in winter. "For larger plantings, bulblets are collected, sown in spring and pulled out in autumn. The bulbils can vary in size from a walnut to a small egg, and provide planting material for the following year. "Here, it's quite different; this lgname grows very little and gives no bulbils (1); this is a pity, as it is much easier to grow than D. Batatas. DIOSCOREA FARGESHI Franch. Yam from Farges or Mao-Vu-Tsé. M. A. Franchet described in Revue Horticole, December 1 1896, p. 541 (Un nouveau Dioscorea alimentaire de la Chine occidentale), under the name of Dioscorea Fargesii, a new species of Yam, native to Se-Tchuen, where it is found at an average altitude of 1,400 meters, below latitude 32° 5' North. In 1894, the R. P. Farges, missionary at Tchen-KéouTin, sent us, under the name of Maô-yu-tsé, (1) We have collected bulbils, but in small numbers. - P.B. Fig. 36 - Igname de Farges (Dioscorea Fargesii Franch.) (The flowering branch was drawn from a sample kept at the Herbarium of the Muséum.) aerial bulbils of this Dioscorea, to M. Maurice. de Vilmorin. At the same time, he sent the dried plant to the Museum herbarium (Farges, no. 227). Doctor Henry also collected it as a dry plant (Docteur Henry, ner 2666 et 750t). D. Fargesii Franch (fig. 36), has edible tubers; it belongs to the Dioscorea group, which instead of having whole or lobed leaves, has 3-5 distinct leaflets, close together at the top of the petiole like those of the Virginia creeper, and is very reminiscent of D. pentaphylla L., which R. P. Delavay encountered in Yunnan, in the mountains, at Tapintze and on the Ilee-chan-men, at a height of 2,500 meters. The tubers of D. pentaphylla have long been used in food, and the altitude at which the plant was encountered by R. P. Delavay seems to indicate that it could withstand fairly low temperatures. According to M. Franchet, D. Fargesii differs from D. pentaphylla in its leaflets, which remain thin, almost membranous; in the narrow, lanceolate shape of the bracts, which are as long as the flowers during anthesis; in the tomentum, which persists on the fruit; and in the rough aerial bulbils. In D. pentaphylla, the leaflets quickly become leathery; the bulbils are shiny and very smooth; the bracts accompanying the male or female flowers are constantly very wide, almost orbicular, concave and abruptly terminated in a very short mucron. M. Maurice de Vilmorin was kind enough to entrust us with a bulblet of D. Fargesiï. We planted it in Crosnes and, after two years of cultivation, we have just harvested a spherical, slightly depressed tuber, 0.55 in diameter, about the size of a mandarin orange, and yellow-brown in color. Particular attention should be drawn to this new and very interesting Yam, whose tuber, of good quality, develops almost on the surface of the soil and is therefore easy to pull up. It is certain that the lubercule will reach a much more considerable relalive size after three years of cultivation, the time required by Chinese Yam and Decaisne Yam to reach full development, when reproduced with bulbils. The Farges yam seems to us to be superior to the Decaisne yam, its tuber being smooth and the production of bulbils extraordinarily abundant, the only plant we have having enabled us to harvest 120 this year (1897). II. - Yam in the East Indies (1) DIOSCOREA GLOBOSA Roxb. Beng. Choopuree aloo. "1 Rounded, white tubers. I have only found this species in cultivated elat. It holds first place among the tuberous roots on which the Hindus of these regions feed, and is also the one most esteemed by Europeans." (1) W. Roxburgh, Flora indica, vol. IlI, pp. 797-808. Extracts. D. ALATA Willd, IV, 792. Beng. Kam-aloo. "2 This species is cultivated in various parts of India. On the Coromandel coast, it is, I believe, the only one cultivated as a food. "In Bengal, it ranks only second, D. globosa being more highly esteemed. Nevertheless, it is widely cultivated. It flowers at the end of the rains. D. RUBELLA Roxb. Beng. Guranya-aloo. "3 I have only come across this species in cultivated form. The Hindus place it immediately after D. purpurea; consequently, it ranks only fourth among the Dioscorea in their esteem. It is widely cultivated near Calcutta: "oblong tubers with red skin". D. PURPUREA Roxb. Beng. Lal-guranya-aloo. "4. oblong tuber, colored in all its parts more or less dark purple, but always deeply tinted, and farmers and cultivators of this species say that this color is permanent. "I have only come across D. purpurea in the cultivated state, and I don't know where it exists in the wild. Its root is

D. alata, and therefore ranks third among aloos. This species is widely cultivated. D. ATROPURPUREA Roxb. "5. subrounded tubers, purple in all their parts. "This species is widely cultivated in Malacca, Pegu and the eastern islands. Its tubers are very large, irregularly shaped, smooth, almost round and grow so close to the surface of the soil that, in dry weather, they can be seen through the cracks they produce by lifting the earth. This fact and the very dark color of the stems make it easy to recognize the species". D. ACULEATA Roxb. " 6. Native to Bengal. Its roots are oval, generally weigh 2 pounds or more, are pleasantly white, and, during the cold season, are plucked from the woods, as the plant is not cultivated, and carried to the Calcutta market, where they are known to the natives as mou-aloo. Outwardly, this lgname closely resembles the species we have named D. fasciculata, the Soosni-aloo of the Hindus". D. FASCICULATA Roxb. Beng. Soosni-aloo" 7. This species is cultivated on a very large scale in the vicinity of Calculta, not only as a foodstuff, but also to extract starch from its roots. "These roots consist of numerous tubers, the size and shape of a young hen's egg, linked by spindly filaments to the base of the stems.... "The cultivation and use of the roots of the various species mentioned above or below are too well known to require that I expand on the subject here. My aim is to dispel the obscurity with which useful plants of this kind have hitherto been shrouded, not only with a view to satisfying botanists, but with a view to indicating to those who are not botanists the signs by which they can distinguish the species; and the surest of these are generally to be found in the shape and color of the root. These characters are permanent and guide the Hindu cultivator; but I fear that botanists will blame me for departing from Linné's rule, which forbids reliance on color. In that case, they will have to consider D. globosa, rubella and purpurea as varieties of the same species, to which I readily consent, if they find it a better method than the one I have followed." D. PULCHELLA Roxb. " 8. Native to Chiltagong. Tubers almost round, quite small. So exactly similar to the D. crispata which follows, that it can only be distinguished by its perfectly soft, smooth skin and cylindrical stems, which show not the slightest appearance of ribs. "It blooms at the end of the rainy season in the Botanical Garden. 255 " 9. A large, elegant species, native to the interior of Bengal and introduced by Dr. Carey, in 1798, to the Botanical Garden, where it flowers during the rainy season. "Tuberous roots, approximately round and small in relation to the large dimensions of the plant, with numerous, branching, tenuous threads emerging from all parts of the tuber... Like potatoes, the old roots perish when the new ones are formed... Axillary bulbils, both numerous and large, can be used for reproduction." D. ANGUINA Roxb. Beng. Kookoor-aloo. " 10. Cylindrical tubers. I found this species in the wild in the woods near Calcutta. It flowers at the end of the rains. I first mistook this species for D. oppositifolia; but on finding some old woody plants armed with numerous strong thorns, I became convinced that they belonged to a very different species. Their roots are horribly nauseating, even after boiling." D. PENTAPHYLLA Willd., IV, 789. Beng. Kanta-aloo. " 17. I have only found this species in the wild, although its root is large, white, considered very salubrious, of a pleasant flavor and eaten by the natives (1)." 111 - Yam in Fiji (2). "In the whole of Polynesia, man's diet is based, to the exclusion of all grains and vegetables, on the Yam, Taro, Banana, Breadfruit and Coconut: but in the various islands, the largest share is provided by a single one of these plants. In the Hawaiian group, Taro dominates, while the fruit of the Coconut palm is considered a delicacy, of which women were once absolutely deprived. In some of the smaller Coral Islands, the inhabitants feed almost exclusively on the nuts of the Coconut palm. The Samoans place the fruits of the Breadfruit Tree in first place. The Fijians prefer Yam to any other food, although the other plants we have mentioned grow perfectly well on their islands and present an infinite number of varieties. (1) This species also exists in China (see p. 250). (2) Flora vitiensis, by Berthold Seemann (Art. Dioscorea, p. 250). 17 A striking proof of the importance they attach to the yam is the fact that its cultivation and the season in which it ripens form the basis of their calendar, and that the only one of the eleven months between which they divide the year, and which bears no name referring to the yam, is the one in which the harvest requires no particular attention or has been covered up. "A version of this calendar has been published by Wilkes, in The narrative of the United States exploring expedition, and is placed opposite the one dictated to me by an intelligent Bauan chief and the consulate interpreter, Mr. Charles Wise (1). The names I give and the order in which I place them are not entirely consistent with those of Wilkes. This difference is partly due to the fact that Wilkes wrote his list according to Europeans imperfectly versed in Fijian, and adopted an inaccurate spelling. Month names may also vary in different parts of the group. The subject, however, calls for further study." FIDJIAN CALENDAR (according to Seemann). 1. Vula i werewere. JUNE-JULY. Month of balance. When the soil is cleared and cleaned. 2. Vula i cukicuki. AUG. When yam fields are cleared and planted. 3. Vula i vavakadi. SEPTEMBER. When the yams are given reeds on which to climb. (1) Seemann's version seems sufficient, and we do not give Wilkes'. - P. B. 4. Vula i Balolo lailai. OCTOBER. When the Balolo (Palolo 5. Vula i Balolo levu. viridis J.-E. Gray), a remarkable Annelid, makes its first appearance in small numbers. NOVEMBER. When Balolo appear in large numbers. November 25 is generally the day when the most are caught. 6. Vula i Nuga lailai. DECEMBER. A fish, named Nuga, appears in small numbers. 7. Vula i Nuga levu. JANUARY. Nuga arrives in large numbers 8. Vula i Sevu. 9. Vula i Ketikeli. 10. Vula i Gasau. 11. Vula i Doi. number. FEBRUARY. When offerings of the first plucked yams (ai Sevu) are made to the priests. MARCH. When the yams are plucked and placed under shelters. APRIL. When the reeds (Gasau) start to grow again. MAY. The Doi (Alphitonia zizyphoides A. Gray), a tree from the Fiji Islands, produces flowers in profusion. "The main cultivated lgname is Dioscorea alata L., which has a quadrangular, quillless climbing stem. Naturals distinguish many varieties, all of which are known by the collective name Uvi. Some have large roots, while others have small ones, either white or more or less purple in color, and differ both in shape and in the time of ripening. These varieties are called Daunini, Keu, Kasokaso or Kasoni, Voli, Sedre, Lokaloka, Moala, Uvi ni gau, Lava Namula, Rausi, Balebale, etc. At Nuava, in Viti levu, the Kuruduadua chief showed us a batch of 6-foot-long, 100-pound lgnames, which are by no means rare in the group. Skilled growers claim that, to obtain large, abundant roots, plantations must be made in hard, unprepared soil. According to them, the yam must meet resistance, or, as they sometimes say themselves, become irritated before it can display its full strength. I've even heard of a bet won by a woman who had practiced this method of cultivation, and who had wagered that she would obtain a root big enough to feed twenty people, while the man who bet against her had only produced one insufficient to feed a third of that number, even though he had taken great pains to loosen and prepare the land intended for his plantation. "The general signal for planting is the flowering of the Drala (Erythrina indica L.). As soon as the flowers begin to appear, which is around July or early August, all hands are on deck. The ground having already been cleared during the preceding months, mounds about 2 feet high, and 4 or 5 feet apart, are raised; these mounds are known as Buke, from which the highest mountain in Kadavu, which Mr. Pritchard and I first climbed on September 6, 1860, and whose shape resembles that of these mounds, takes its name from Buke levu or great mound in Ignames. "There are no spades or iron tools for ploughing. Everything is done with Mangrove poles and by sheer force of arms. Sections of old yams are planted at the top of the mounds, and after the short space of time it takes the plant to grow, less than a month, it requires reeds to climb on; then it's just a matter of clearing the ground of weeds. "Around February, the first yams begin to ripen and are used as offerings to priests in pagan districts. March and April see the main harvest, which is stored in sheds covered in Coconut leaves. During the season, the contents of these sheds must be visited at least once a month; roots showing the slightest trace of disease are removed, to prevent them infecting healthy ones. Yams are eaten baked, boiled or steamed, and naturals can consume large quantities. Whole cargoes have sometimes been shipped to New South Wales and New Zealand, and whalers and merchant ships never touch Viti without making ample provision. "Another species, Kawai (D. aculeata L.), is also planted on artificial mounds, although not as high as those where the winged yam is planted. Its creeping stems are round and furnished with thorns, but they are not raised on reeds, like those of the species mentioned above. D. aculeata ripens around June; by the 27th of that month, it was leafless. According to naturalists, it never produces flowers or seeds, and I have searched in vain in the fields in the hope of convincing them of this error. It is propagated by planting small tubers which, like the old ones, are oblong, brownish on the outside and pure white on the inside. "When cooked, its skin peels off like birch bark, as Wilkes puts it. Its root is very starchy, like that of a good floury potato, but whiter. Its taste is reminiscent of the South American Arracacha. All in all, Kawai can be considered the best root food in the world, and I strongly recommend its cultivation to all tropical countries that do not yet have it. "Several species of wild yam, such as Tikau, Tivoli and Kaile, hang in graceful festoons from the branches of trees and shrubs in almost every woodland. Tivoli (D. nummularia Lam.) has a spiny stem like that of the cultivated Kawai and climbs very high. Its roots are long, cylindrical and as big as a man's arm. Often, when entering forests, the natives pull out its roots with a stick, roast it and eat it on the spot. They find it excellent. "The Kaile (D. sativa L.) is somewhat similar in appearance to the Tivoli and is often found entwined with it, but its stems and branches are round and ineradicable, and the pungency of its roots means that they must be macerated in water before boiling. The dish prepared with them has the appearance of potatoes, crushed enough to be eaten only with the spoons provided by the leathery leaves of the spoon tree or Tatakia (Acacia laurifolia Willd.), or any other sufficiently sturdy leaves on hand. "Kaile-tokatolu (D. pentaphylla L.) is sometimes cultivated, according to Mr. Storck, and its tuber is a good food. I have not been able to obtain specimens of the wild yam that the natives call Tikau, but it is established in the Fijian Dictionary (p. 323. and 324) that it differs from Tivoli and that its name is used in some dialects of the group instead of the generic name Uvi. Could this be D. pentaphylla, or is it just another species? "The following list gives the key to Viti species: alato. tereti. Caule inermi Caule aculeato D. alata. D. sativa. Foliis oppositis. Foliis alternis integris. ! digitatis. D. nummularia. D. aculeata. D. pentaphylla. IV. - Yam in New Caledonia (1). "Yam is particularly demanding in terms of soil lightness, whether natural or acquired. It is only in light, deep soils that tubers so large that two men are sometimes needed to transport them can be obtained. As for the composition she seems to prefer, it's a mixture of sand and limestone, as found in soils underlain by coral, and strongly mixed with decomposing organic matter. "Another essential quality of the soil used for yams is that it has nothing to (1) Letter from Mr. V. Perret, former director of the Dumbéa agricultural penitentiary, December 20, 1883. fear from humidity. Few plants are as sensitive to the disastrous effects of stagnant water and persistent humidity. "All varieties generally thrive inland, as they do by the sea. Some, however, fear the influence of the sea wind. This is the case of the variety named Kilira in the North, and Cocodyi in the Centre, which does not grow by the sea. In short, although New Caledonia is essentially a clay soil, yams can be found everywhere, provided the land has been sufficiently well prepared. Opposite is a list of the main varieties of yam, with their names in two of the colony's languages, one of which, Falanguito, is widely spoken. Preparing the soil involves loosening and cleaning. "Here's how the natives operate in New Caledonia: when September comes, which is the dry season, they have chosen the site of their plantation and marked it with a stake, at the top of which is a handful of grass; this is a taking of possession. Some time later, when the day is favourable, they set fire to the grasses, and before the arrival of the Europeans, Caledonia was burning completely. After clearing the ground of shrubs and other obstacles the tool may encounter, the native attacks it with a stake 2.50 to 4 meters long, 6, 7 or 8 centimeters in diameter at the base, sharpened and hardened by fire. The natives use only certain special, heavy, hard woods for this purpose. They violently drive this instrument into the ground, repeatedly throwing it up and down the same hole, Names of the main varieties of Yam in New Caledonia.
