Top-Ondou... ===--- -c'-G~~~~o ~~~ ~~-=ec= meileure "" cicicicio until it penetrated to Or, 30 or so. In a few weighing operations, once the first clods have been removed, they topple a block of earth several cubic decimeters in size, sometimes even larger, better than could be done by workers armed with tools, especially when there are three or four of them breaking together. These overturned blocks are passed into the hands of the women, who slowly move forward on their knees, breaking up the clods as they go, first with small sticks, then with their fingers, until they are reduced to dust and all weed roots have been extracted. When this loosening work is complete, the layer of loose soil is increased by piling it up. If the cullivated portion is on the plain, the natives dig two deep furrows on each side and raise the soil up sharply in the shape of a humpback. For a width of 6 meters, I suppose, the difference in level can be as much as 1.50 meters. The colony's main valleys are covered with these furrows. If, on the other hand, cultivation has been carried out on the side of a hill, the soil is brought in from all ends and piled up in the middle in the shape of a plank, or crescent more often than not, unless it is in the thalweg, whose sinuosities it then follows. "They then prepare their seedlings; these are cut pieces of about half a cubic decimeter in volume, preferably the top of the plant. They are divided several days in advance to allow the wounds to dry. Yam is very sensitive to humidity and rots very easily; they put two pieces on each plant, almost flush with the ground, and cover them with a small dome of very fine soil. "When the shoot has emerged, they drive in large poles inclined away from the windrow and planted at Om, 40 or so from the base of the yam, so as not to disturb it. "As soon as the shoot is about Om, 30 long, they stick a small wooden or bamboo rod at its base, which connects the foot of the plant with the neighbouring pole, and the yam is led along this inclined rod to the stake that is to support it. "Three to six months later, depending on the species, the tubers are ripe. Depending on the species and variety, they range from 1 to 15 or 18, and come in all sizes. Some yams, from the New Hebrides in particular, have been seen on Coral soil, reaching over 2 meters in length and exceeding a man's weight. "The natives take great care in extracting them. The preservation of lgname depends on its integrity, and if it has been removed whole and healthy, it can be kept for seven to eight months in a suitable room. "This plant never flowers in New Caledonia; it is certainly imported. There are at least thirty varieties. The entire surface of the lgname skin is budding, despite the absence of visible eyes. V. - Yam in French Guiana (1) "The species cultivated in French Guiana are: "The Indian yam (D. triloba), cultivated throughout 41) Bull. Soc. bot. de France, 1871, t. XVIII, Dr Paul Sagot. antiquity by the natives of America. It is the species whose tubers are the most pleasant to the taste. "Igname pays-nègre or Guinea yam. Spiny yam, D. cayennensis Kth. (D. altissima Lam.). Its tubers are very large, but less delicate. "Igname franche, often misleadingly called Igname française (D. alata), is less widespread than the preceding varieties. "Here is their brief description: "The Indian yam, D. triloba L. (D. affinis Kth., D. truncata Miquel, D. trifida Meyer), has a thornless stem, raised with prominent membranous ridges. Leaves are broad, with the lower ones having 7 or 5 lobes and the upper ones 3, not reaching half their length. Foliage is light yellowish-green. Tubers are numerous, ovoid or rounded, covered with blackish, cracked bark. This American species is grown in Brazil and the West Indies, as well as in French Guiana. It is an excellent species. "Igname pays-nègre, D. cayennensis Klh. (D. altissima, Berteroana Knth.), probably brought from Africa in ancient times, has a spiny stem. Leaves are whole, cordate, dark green, shiny and fairly small. The tuber is generally simple, flattened and more or less ovoid. It is very large, but harder and less delicate to the taste than that of the Indian yam. It is also more productive and less demanding in terms of soil quality. "D. alata L., native to the Malay Archipelago and Oceania, has a thornless stem with prominent membranous ridges, and heart-shaped, entire, yellowish-green leaves. The tuber is ovoid, more or less elongated. This species is less widespread in the colony than the previous two. Its tuber is not as delicate as that of the Indian yam. "D. pubescens Poir is still sometimes grown in the colony, but I have not had the opportunity to observe it. The tubers of the wood yam, D. bulbifera, which grows wild in the forests, are sometimes collected. According to what Mr. Leprieur told me, the Indians at the top of the rivers cultivate, in addition to the Indian yam, a particular species that the colonists do not possess. Vl. - Yam in Venezuela (1) "Among the different species of yam grown in the country, there are three main ones, commonly known as : Name de SantoDomingo, Name liso and Name de espina. "These three species have not yet been classified, as far as I know; however, the first, or the so-called Santo Domingo Yam, would seem to correspond to Dioscorea bulbifera. "It is a plant with a sarmentose stem whose main production consists of the bulbils or tubers which develop on the aerial part of the plant, in the axils of the leaves; the underground root is only an accessory product; so it forms around it a (1) Plantes alimentaires du Venezuela, by M. A. de Tourreil (Bull. Soc. d'Accl., vol. VI, 1859, p. 481). kind of trellis on which it can climb and spread its branches. This yam is the least prized; its starch retains a certain bitter taste that is not very pleasant, but which could undoubtedly be corrected. "Could the smooth yam be Dioscorea sativa? It produces more or less elongated, club-shaped roots, with fleshy, compact, starchy, opaline-white tissue. Its tawny-brown surface is plain, with generative organs around the collar (1). Its roots sometimes swivel to great depths, but in general, the underground swellings that constitute the plant's essential product are almost round and easy to pull out. The stems also bear fruit, and stakes are placed near them to support them. "The spiny yam belongs to the same genus. It differs only from the smooth yam in that its glaze has eyes similar to those of the potato, and spiny rootlets. Its starch is fatty, soft and succulent; its stems are also climbers, reaching heights of several meters, but they are only staked for support, and the bulbils they bear are ignored. These two species are closely related to the Chinese Dioscorea. "There is another mealy root, known in the country by the vulgar name of Mapuey, whose shape and sarmentose stem indicate that it also belongs to the Dioscorea family (2). (1) By these words, organs of generation, we mean buds. - P. B. (2) Dioscorea trifida, Dr. Ernst. - P. B. "It comes in two different species, one purple, the other white. "The purple Mapuey is preferred; its radiciform swellings are large and short; its starch is very crumbly and tasty; cooked in salt water, in the oven or under ashes, it is eaten like bread. It's also a vegetable plant par excellence. "These plants like warm climates, but they also adapt to temperate climates. "Their rhizomes ripen within ten months at an elevation of 600 or 700 meters above sea level; but at higher altitudes, 1,600 or 1,700 meters, they take fourteen or fifteen months to reach maturity. "Here, yams are reproduced using whole tubers or sections of the upper part of the rhizome. This operation is often carried out without destroying the aerial part of the plant; the root is uncovered, cut about an inch below the collar, the excavation is filled in again, and the upper portion of the root is replaced and covered with topsoil. "Yam likes soft soil and usually spreads according to the space prepared for it; a plot dug 1 metre deep and 50 centimetres wide yielded yams in proportion to this size. Yam is capable of acquiring very large dimensions; in Venezuela, we have seen yams weighing up to 100 kilograms. "It often happens that, unable to develop fully underground, either for lack of space or because of some obstacle, the rhizomes of the yam rise above the ground to more than a quarter of a meter; they are then carefully covered with earth, which offers no obstacle to their production. "The yield of lgname is higher than that of the potato; it contains almost as much floury starch as the latter, and contains a nitrogenous principle that makes it nutritious. Its substance is recognized as very nourishing and easily digestible; it is used, if necessary, as bread for country people and, in general, as a vegetable plant for daily use. It also lends itself, in various forms, to the composition of various stews, and can be cooked in no time, either in salt water, in the oven or under hot ashes. "Hot climates are the most favorable for yams: the more intense the heat, the more active and vigorous the vegetation. The average temperature under which it vegetates is around 22 degrees centigrade and the maximum cold 15 to 16 degrees, while it tolerates very well a maximum heat of over 29 degrees. "The hardiness of the yam is such that its cultivation here requires no fertilizer; it even grows wild, and the birds are responsible for spreading its seed. VII. - Yam in France 273 The Chinese yam, introduced in 1848 by M. de Montigny, is the only one that has received serious attention in France. We shall be silent on this Dioscorea, which has been the subject of persevering experimental cultivation and is still grown in amateur gardens. We shall confine ourselves to listing the notes and memoirs that have been published on its subject, which are summarized in the article devoted to it by Messrs Vilmorin-Andrieux et Cie, on pages 283-285 of Plantes potagères. A yam introduced from China to the Muséum in 1862, to which M. Carrière has given the name D. Decaisneana and which is perhaps no more than a variety of the preceding species, would interest us more if it were more productive. It has the advantage of not sinking deep into the soil, and is therefore easy to pull up. It is of good quality and absolutely hardy; unfortunately, the tuber that is planted perishes and is simply replaced by a slightly larger tuber, so there is no multiplication. You have to resort to the aerial bulblets that the plant provides in small numbers and whose volume rarely reaches that of a hazelnut. These bulblets are planted in a nursery and, three years later, tubers of very irregular shape and mediocre size are harvested. M. Doumet-Adanson has worked with D. Decaisneana for many years, with all the success 18 S Fig. 37. -- Chinese yam, leafy branch. He has presented some very fine specimens to the Société d'Acclimatation. This yam is of good quality, takes up little nursery space and deserves the care of amateurs. -L Fig. 38 - Chinese yam, tubers. M. Paillieux, setting an example, was able to provide the Société d'Acclimatation with several thousand tubers, which it distributed to its members. For several years now, Mr. Paul Chappellier has been making laudable efforts to obtain new varieties of Yam, either by sowing seeds of Dioscorea Batatas and D. Decaisneana, or by crossing these two plants (1). For its part, the Museum requested, received and sowed seeds of D. japonica. These seeds have sprouted, and in four or five years we'll know whether the experiment has produced any useful results. It would be a good idea to repeat the sowing every year, and to do it on a large scale so that success is likely. Once a variety shorter than D. Batatas and more productive than D. Decaisneana has been found, it should be fixed by repeated cultivation. Crossing these two varieties is highly desirable and is likely to produce the desired result, but in the Paris climate, we cannot obtain seeds of either variety. We will therefore have to operate in the south of France, or in Algeria, or in one of our other colonies. How is it that no cross-breeding has occurred, either spontaneously or artificially, in Japan or China, where both plants are cultivated and suffer no less than we do from their defects? We can't explain it. In any case, and in the meantime, we propose to grow the flat yam from Japan, which is D. Decaisneana. We received it directly from Japan a few years ago. (1) See Revue Horticole. Les Cultures de M. Chappellier, by M. D. Bois, 1893, p. 15; Culture de l'Igname, by M. P. Chappellier, same collection, same year, p. 274. Revue des sciences naturelles appliquées : Nourelle méthode de culture de l'Igname de Chine, by M. P. Chappellier, 1895, 1st semester, p. 402. This yam is of superior quality; it is hardy and easy to grow. We had to give up the Chinese yam, Dioscorea Batatas, which was impracticable to uproot; the flat yam from Japan is harvested from the ground. Harvesting bulblets in autumn, sowing them the following spring, and obtaining a plant which, when cultivated again, produces tubers of marketable size, are the operations applicable to these two yams. BULB PRODUCTION The largest tubers available are used for this purpose. They provide us with equally large tubers which we use for the same purpose, i.e. each year we replant new tubers from the bulblet-producing bed. They are deformed and unsuitable for sale, but produce strong plants that yield good harvests. You can expect an average yield of 5,000 bulbils per hundred feet of these large yams. We estimate that, in a garden, it will be sufficient to maintain a plantation of around fifty producing plants. We grow them in well-smoked beds, in two rows spaced Or,40 apart. Only these two rows are planted on a 1.30 m bed. As soon as they emerge from the ground, the young plants are given stakes at least 3 m high, on which the stems are directed, observing that they are voluble from left to right. The soil is then cleaned of weeds by a few hoeings; a light ridging, although not necessary, seems useful to us, as the lubercules sometimes appear just above ground level. Harvesting takes place at the beginning of November. IIIIt t 14 Fig. 39 - Chinese yam, bulb. Tubers are preserved either by placing them on boards, without stacking, in a cold, dry place, or by stratifying them in dry sand, or by burying them under a frame in the garden, protecting them from frost. SOWING BULBILLES (1) On garden beds 1.30 m wide, make four furrows in which to sow bulbils like peas. It would certainly be better to space them evenly, but it would be a lot of trouble. You can also thin out the planting after the bulbils have emerged, to obtain larger plants. As soon as the young plants emerge from the ground, they are reamed like peas. The soil is kept clean (1) It would be more correct to say "planted". by hand weeding. In early November, the plant is harvested to produce tubers of marketable size the following year. The resulting roots are stratified in sand in a cold, dry place (1). FIRST-YEAR TUBER PLANTING Plants are planted in holes about Om.20 deep, spaced 0.20 m apart, or at an even shorter distance if the soil is heavily smoked, and arranged in three rows in beds 1.30 m wide. As soon as the plants emerge from the ground, they are given stakes 2m, 50 to three meters high. Keep the soil clean with a trowel. You can leave the stems to coil spontaneously around the stakes, but it's better to tie them to the stakes, directing them from left to right. Harvesting takes place in the first days of November, and is either consumed or delivered to the trade. Only tubers too small for sale are kept for replanting. USES Japanese yam starch is remarkably fine and delicate. It does not have the sweet taste of potatoes, which many people dislike. Flat yam is superior to all others for sweet dishes (sweet from the English, mehl speise from the Germans), cakes and soufflés. It is excellent: as a light purée (1) By this we mean that they should not be kept in a place where humidity and heat could cause them to sprout untimely. for soup, sliced and fried in batter, baked in juice like cardoons, cooked under ashes. OTHER SPECIES We can only speak from personal experience. Some twenty years ago, we attempted to grow two varieties of Dioscorea alata under chassis, with absolutely negative results. Later, and on two occasions, we planted tubers of Mapuey branco (D. trifida, white) and Mapuey morado (D. trifida, purple), from Venezuela. The latter did not form tubers. The former gave us imperfect tubers, but cultivation under a frame may not be impracticable; unfortunately, the white Mapuey is much less esteemed than the purple variety. The latter was particularly recommended to us by Dr Ernst, Professor of Natural History in Caracas. We have already seen the esteem in which Dr. Sagot holds this species (1). We have tasted the Mapuey violet and declare that no better root could be found. It has an excellent flavor and is so floury that it crumbles as soon as you touch it, like the sand pies that children make. (1) This yam is cultivated throughout the West Indies, where it is known as Cousse-Couche, Couche-Couche. According to R. P. Duss, Flore phanérogamique des Antilles françaises, p. 566, it usually produces a large number of elongated or fusiform tubers, attached to a thread like those of the Topinambour, providing a delicate, highly appetizing and sought-after food, which far outweighs other yams in value. consider the cultivation of this yam impossible in the Paris climate. We have planted several New Caledonian species under frames, but we received them too late and the tubers that some of them gave us, not having reached maturity, were not preserved during the winter. We will, however, exclude the wild bulbous yam, which ripened its tubers and bulblets, a serious success in that it gives us hope that other species, chosen from among the earliest, will also be able to give happy results. The Société d'Acclimatation is offering a first prize of 600 francs and a second prize of 400 francs for the introduction and cultivation, for two successive years, of a lgname (Dioscorea) combining superior quality with easy harvesting. Will these prizes be won? We doubt it, if we're talking about a field-grown yam; we don't doubt it if we're talking about a vegetable-grown yam treated using market-gardening methods. These procedures are sufficient to obtain perfectly ripe and well-preserved tubers and bulbils from D. bulbifera, a species native to the hottest regions of the globe, and spontaneous in India, Guyana, etc. This lgname is not particularly early, and the information we have gathered tells us that the vegetation of several other species is of shorter duration. We therefore need to find the latter, and we have every reason to believe that this will not be long in coming (1). (1) In 1894, M. Maurice de Vilmorin gave us a bulblet of D. Fargesü Franch. (see p. 248), a new species from New Caledonia. We have given above a list of 42 species or varieties of Yam cultivated in New Caledonia. We have been informed of a bulbous variety, very productive, very early, which would require only three months of vegetation in the country; another, precocious, of which each plant would give 10 to 12 tubers, short and easy to store; a third, sweet and fragrant, slightly musky, precocious, producing an abundance of short, budded tubers, which keep better in the ground than outside, can be pulled up and planted all year round. It can be estimated that New Caledonia possesses no less than 50 species or varieties of Yam, which need to be cultivated experimentally and compared with each other, excluding those with very large roots. Several of these will be suitable for market gardening. NOTICE BIBLIOGRAPHIQUE Revue horticole. Note sur le Dioscorea Batatas, 1854, p. 242 et 443. Dioscorea Batatas, by M. J. KETMIE ACIDE Guinea sorrel. HIBISCUS SABDARIFFA L. Fam. Malvaceae. Annual plant. Stems 7 to 8 decimeters long, glabrous, reddish, twiggy. Leaves toothed, lower ones oval, upper ones with three wedge-shaped lobes. In June-September, flowers yellow, purple-ground, solitary, axillary, almost sessile. Twelve-toothed calicule. East Indies. We have cultivated Guinea sorrel under a frame, and have obtained flowers and seeds without difficulty; but we cannot conceal from ourselves that this culture will never be profitable in the Paris region. It may find a place in the kitchen garden of the curious, but it won't make it out of there. It's an amateur plant. "After flowering, the calyx lengthens, becoming thick, fleshy, dark red and acidic in flavor. In the West Indies, this acidity has led to it being substituted for sorrel, whose properties it shares. According to the nature of their respective functions, cooks and chefs use it in coulis and butter, and make good use of its calyxes and leaves for glazed stuffings, on which different meats are to appear; confectioners reserve a more distinguished use for the same parts, and make preserves and jams with a pleasant, refreshing acidity. "Acid Ketmia grows naturally in Guinea, the West Indies and all of southern America..." (Descourtilz, Flore des Antilles). Another note from A Manual of Gardening for Bengal and upper India, by Thomas A. C. Firminger: "Roselle-Indian Sorrel. Annual plant, native to the West Indies, but now cultivated in most gardens in India. "The part of the plant that is used is not the fruit itself, but the calyx, whose large, thick, succulent sepals are used to make delicious pies and puddings, as well as a remarkably beautiful jelly, which differs only slightly from red currant jelly, of which it is, in many respects, an excellent substitute. "There are two kinds of Indian sorrel, one red and the other white, which are very similar, except that the white one seems a little less acidic...". The Hindostani name for the plant is Patwa. We bought some Guinea sorrel jam from M. Hédiard, which we tasted and found pleasant. The formula was sent to us by an inhabitant of Pointe-à-Pître: Confitures de Groseille-pays. (Letter from Mr. BrEr.) " Once the fruit has been picked, the red petaloid calyx must first be separated from the green receptacle to which it adheres. Here, where the acidity of the fruit is feared, on the pretext that it is safe, and where, on the other hand, ripe fruit is disliked, saying that it is past its prime, here, I say, we begin by throwing boiling water on these quasi-flowers, to remove their excess sourness, and we don't use this first infusion. In a second operation, the red calyxes are cooked in water and the sugar melted. The proportions of sugar are at least those used to prepare all acid fruit syrups, without taking into account the pectin that the juice may contain; for it sometimes happens that the jams we eat are almost set in jelly, and, at other times, that they remain in syrup... It remains to be seen whether it is appropriate to use the first water, which is rejected here because of its excess acidity. Is there not a risk that this water will carry away all or part of the pectin along with the acid? In either case, one principle is sacrificed for the other. So it's up to you to decide which you prefer to keep. "I think we could perhaps make a first concentrated infusion, intended to prepare the acid jelly, and, with the same fruit, prepare a jam that would still be sufficiently sour. Another species of Hibiscus, H. cannabicus L., is often cultivated in tropical regions. Its acidic leaves are eaten like sorrel. KITESH or KEN HIRUGAWO CALYSTEGIA JAPONICA CHOISY (SYN. CONVOLVULUS JAPONICUS THUNB.). Family Convolvulaceae. This plant has some analogy with our great hedge bindweed (Calystegia sepium): the stems are voluble and can reach several meters in height; the rhizomes are extremely abundant and absolutely comparable to those of its European congeners. Batchelor and Kingo Miyabe: Ainu Economic plants (Transactions of the Asiatic Society of Japan, p. 226), say that the long, fat roots of this plant are dug up in spring by the Ainos, for whom they serve as food. They eat them roasted or boiled, alone or with other foods such as rice or millet. We received the plant from Japan and it proved absolutely hardy in the Crosnes garden, where it quickly invaded the flowerbed dedicated to it. We tasted a few of the many roots harvested: fried, they proved stringy; cooked in water and pureed, we obtained a mild-tasting starch, pleasant enough, but of no particular merit. Among the other food plants sought after by the Ainos and cited by Messrs Batchelor and Miyabe (loc. cit.), but which we have not cultivated, we would point out: 1° Anemone flaccida Fr. Schm. (Pukusa. Kina, Gajo-so or Fukubero, whose leaves and stems are eaten by the Aïnos. The people harvest large quantities in spring, when it is in flower; they dry it for the winter, although they eat a little green. They eat it boiled with fish or sometimes in soup; the 2° Corydalis ambigua Cham. et Schlcht. (Toma, Engosaku). The Ainos eat large quantities of this plant's tubers, especially those from the Ishikari valley of Saghalien and the southern Kuriles. The tuber has a slight bitterness, which is removed by boiling it in water several times. In Etorup, the Ainos boil them with some earth to remove their bitterness. They are eaten either simply boiled or mixed with rice. In Saghalien, they are generally cooked with seal fat. Tubers are often boiled and then dried for later use. 289 AMORPHOPHALLUS RIVIERI Durieu, var. KoNJAC Engler, Monographiæ Phanerogam. vol. Il, p. 213; Conophallus Konjak Schott, ex Miq, Prolusio flor. jap. p. 134; Amorphophallus Konjac C. Koch, in Berl. Allg. Gartenz. 1858, p. 166; Arisæma Conjac Sieb. Fam. des Aroïdées. M. le Dr Vidal has published, in the Bulletin de la Société d'Acclimatation (July 1877 issue), a memoir of the highest interest on the Konniyakou. This memoir occupies fifteen pages, and its length does not allow us to reproduce it in extenso; but we believe we must extract the essential parts and advise growers to have recourse to the memoir itself, when they undertake the cultivation of the plant. Extracts from Dr. J. Vidal's memoir. Dr. Vidal witnessed the great consumption of Conophallus Konjak tubers by the Japanese population. He was able to study its cultivation methods, as well as the type of preparation the tuber undergoes for use as food. "The specific name Konjak, given to the plant by the botanist Schott, is obviously a poor transcription of the native word Konniyakou, because the y is generally replaced by the j in the spelling of Japanese deposits, adopted by the Dutch and Germans. 18 DESCRIPTION "Konniyakou is a perennial aroid with a tuberous root. Its stem is a fairly light-green spike, speckled with brown and blackish spots, which can Fig. 40. - Amorphophallus Rivieri. reach a height of around 1 meter, with a circumference of Om, 70 to 0m, 80 at the base; it ends by giving rise to three large leaves (1) which spread obliquely, forming equal angles with each other and with the axis of the stem; the blade of these leaves is deeply and somewhat irregularly cut, so that (1) What Dr. Vidal refers to as stem and leaves is actually a single leaf whose petiole arises directly from the tuber and whose blade is trisected. They are almost pinnate; the main midrib is accompanied on each side by a band of leaf blade, which continues with that surrounding the secondary veins of the leaflets. At a depth of 3 or 4 inches below ground, the stem is implanted on the middle of the upper surface of a tuber, more or less developed according to the age of the subject; this tuber, which is more or less hemispherical, represents quite exactly the ties égales; and it is in the middle of the slightly concave plane of section that the neck of the plant is formed; as for the convex surface of the tuber, which is the largest, it gives attachment to a few rootlets and is provided with eyes, in the manner of Potatoes." The doctor did not see the flowering and fruiting organs of the Konniyakou, which, he was told, only flowers every two years and probably does not flower in the central provinces of Japan. It is therefore probably native to the southern provinces. According to the information provided to the author of this report, the Konniyakou flower has a spadix with male flowers at the top and female flowers below, surrounded by a large, red, lanceolate, acute spathe which curves at right angles through its middle, so that its upper half, now horizontal, rests on the tip of the spadix. The fruit consists of a large number of small red berries implanted on the corresponding, strongly swollen part of the spadix. CULTIVATION "Konniyakou cultivation is one of the simplest and most economical, and this is how the doctor saw it practiced in one of the most successful districts, some thirty leagues north-west of Yedo, in the province of Jo-chiou. "In this mountainous country, farmers prefer to plant Konniyakou on arid, non-irrigable land, unsuitable for growing cereals, and most often located on a more or less steep mountain slope; Holes 4 to 5 inches deep are then spaded, about 2 feet apart; a tuber is placed in each hole, and simply covered with soil; no fertilizer is ever added, and watering is also unnecessary. The tubers are planted in the spring, at the end of March or beginning of April, taking care to use only medium-sized tubers, i.e. those no more than one or two years old, as large tubers are said to be less successful. "Once the plantation has been established, little more attention is paid to it, and the tubers multiply in the following way: As the stem emerges from the ground, a certain number of underground branches or stolons of varying size emerge from the eyes on the convexity of the tuber, and when they have reached a length of a few inches, they swell into a mass that is the beginning of a young tuber; this develops rapidly, in turn supplying rootlets and producing an aerial stem that soon emerges from the ground shortly after the one that came from the first tubers planted. Each of these tubers gives rise to around fifteen new tubers within a radius of around 1 foot. "Harvesting takes place regularly every year, in autumn, in the last fortnight of October and in November. It simply consists in pulling out the tubers, exactly as if they were potatoes, sweet potatoes, etc., and then levelling them. All that's left to do is to roughly level the land, and use the harvested produce for other purposes. If the tubers are not to be used until some time in the future, it is essential to remove as much earth as possible from them, and to store them in a dry, well-ventilated place, on a floor as far as possible. "One might think that after a first harvest of Konniyakou, it would be necessary, as is usually the case in Europe, to plant a new crop in order to obtain a second one; but this is not the case, and the Japanese don't take this trouble; and, something which, while seeming strange, is no less real, they obtain every year, without any new expense or work, a harvest as large as that of the previous year. The Japanese explain this fact by saying that, firstly, it's very difficult not to leave a few unnoticed tubers in the ground when digging them up; and secondly, a certain number of those that appear too small are deliberately neglected. As a result, there are always enough tubers left in the ground to produce a normal harvest the following year. "During the winter, the Konniyakou stem withers, dries out and dies: it is replaced the following spring by a new stem emerging from the collar, immediately next to the place occupied by the previous one on the same tuber. "This brief description shows that nothing is simpler and less costly than cultivating this Aroidea; and, in fact, it's hardly possible to apply the name of cultivation to such simple procedures. There is no difficulty in choosing the land, since it is precisely the most ungrateful soils that are preferred by the Japanese; nor is there any expense, either in terms of fertilizers, which are never used, or in terms of labor, which is reduced to planting once and for all, and then simply uprooting. "However, given that the Japanese devote only their least fertile and even arid soils to this type of cultivation, we should not conclude that these types of soils are necessary for the success of the operation; This would be a mistake, as I've found that Konniyakou are even more successful when they are grown in better conditions, for example, when they happen to grow on good soil in the vicinity of farms, and can benefit from the fertilizers and watering given to the other crops they grow in. The Japanese have told me that they plant Konniyakou in poor soils only to make use of soil that is unsuitable for any other crop, and this is why it is only grown in mountainous districts; indeed, wherever the soil in the plains and valleys is of better quality or can be irrigated, it is always devoted to growing cereals, especially rice, which is by far the most important crop for the Japanese people. Nevertheless, in the sericulture districts, where plantations are often laid out around the edges of fields, rows of Konniyakou are also planted along the edges, sheltered by these same Mulberry trees. And on this subject, it should be said that, although this plant is very hardy, it should be avoided, if possible, if it is to be exposed too directly to the sun's rays; at least in slightly hot countries; for it should not be forgotten that it belongs to the Aroidae family, and that most plants in this family thrive in damp, shady places; so I have often seen plantations of Konniyakou in the vicinity of either Mulberry trees or Tea bushes; but any tree or shrub could perform the same protective function just as well, with the exception of resinous trees. "It has already been said that each tuber planted gives rise, on average, to about fifteen new tubers in the first year; this is enough to give an idea of the yield of a plantation; moreover, as the plant is perennial, the tubers can be left to grow for several years; but for food use, the Japanese only make their preparations with those from the first or second year, because those that are older lose in quality what they have gained in size. The weight and volume of the tubers vary greatly according to their age; those of the first year weigh, on average, from 75 to 100 grams, also having, on average, a circumference of Om, 18 to 0m, 20. I found that the weight of a third-year tuber was 1 kilogr. 700; with a circumference of Om, 56. These tubers are covered with a thin, brownish skin; their mass is formed of a white, resistant parenchyma, which, when cut, recalls the appearance of Turnip or Rave; they give off a strong, characteristic, unpleasant odor, which they do not lose by cooking in water; when cooked and simply divided with the fingers, they present a gritty, starchy appearance. Their flavor is strong, pungent and acrid, giving the mouth and back of the throat a tingling, burning sensation that can last for hours. "Fortunately, a simple and inexpensive treatment is all that is needed to transform it into a healthy and pleasant food. The secret of the operation consists in subjecting it to the action of a little milk of lime, by means of processes that the author has seen used by the Japanese, who prepare large quantities of it. "The Japanese use the Konniyakou tuber as a foodstuff in three different forms; but the basic preparation is the same, and it is only the handling that differs a little, as well as the form given to the manufactured product. Thus, they prepare a kind of vermicelli which they call Chira tahe, a wafer called simply Konniyahou and finally a kind of dry cake known as Chiro Ro." The author describes the processes used to make Chira take. He even gives two drawings showing the apparatus used. The product has the appearance of a large white vermicelli and is a healthy food with a fairly good taste. The doctor uses it in the same way as Italian pasta is used in Europe. The patties known as Konniyahou are slightly inferior to Chira take. The memoir describes how they are made, as well as Chira ko. Whatever form you give to the dough obtained from the Konniyahou pulp, the basis of the preparation is the same, and it always consists of adding a small quantity of milk of lime to the pulp. One thing is certain, and this has been demonstrated by centuries of Japanese experience: Konniyakou tubers, prepared as described by the author, are perfectly safe and wholesome. The memoir we have just read had been published for several years when we received a leaflet, translated for the first time from Japanese, which its translator, Count de Castillon, kindly sent us. The leaflet includes: 1° a brief history of Konniyakou; 2° its botanical description; 3° the methods used to cultivate it; 4° those employed in the manufacture of the food products it supplies; 5° finally, it describes the plant's uses other than for food. This document is of considerable interest. It confirms the information provided by Dr. Vidal on a number of points, but says nothing about these harvests which, according to him, would continue without new planting or fertilizers. It is likely, therefore, that the simple, economical cultivation methods described by the author of this report are only successful in the province he mentions. We will now read a description of Konniyakou cultivation, extracted from the notice translated by Count de Castillon, whose precision and clarity leave nothing to be desired. "After the spring equinox, the tubers saved for seed are dug up and planted in ridges of the same width as the Sato imo (Colocasia antiguorum). Although the price of seed varies from place to place, in general a weight of 24 kuwan me (90 kilograms) is worth around 2 yen (10 fr. 50). Although the shady, dry soils on the mountain slopes are ideal for growing this plant, it is prone to rot in very shady areas. Places under the cover of trees, where the sun only shines from ten o'clock to noon, are excellent. For fertilizer, we use horse litter, which decomposes and serves as manure. It also serves to intercept the sun's scorching rays during the hot season. At the end of autumn, when the stems are slightly wilted by the first white frosts, a general uprooting is carried out. The small tubers that have formed around the roots are detached. The large ones are converted into Konniyakou, and the small ones, destined to be used as seed the following year, are buried as if they were Sato imo tubers, in warm soil, in a pit covered with planks and then with earth, forming a small mound opposite the north and facing south, where they will be sheltered from the cold, rain and dew. "Although the yield varies according to the year's conditions, in good conditions 1 tan (9 ares 91 centiares) can yield 70 tawara (1) of tubers, just as they come out of the ground, i.e. unwashed. The cultivation rules we have just described (1) Tawara. In the wholesale of Imo, etc., one tawara represents a weight of 16 kuwan me, net, in Tokio and 20 kuwan me in Kioto. 1 kuwan me - 1000 me (3 kilogr. 750). pose vary slightly, depending on whether the soil is cold or hot. In warm soils, we cultivate on ridges in hata, hillside fields, and harvest in the course of the same year. In cold soils, the tubers are dug up and buried in silos. The following spring, they are replanted in hata. Silage is made as we have already mentioned." USES OTHER THAN FOR CULINARY PURPOSES "When rats cause damage, we keep them away by spreading this raw root, cut into slices, as if we wanted to make a plaster. "These tubers, also raw, are used to make a paper glue that sticks so well that it cannot be peeled off, and is also used to make snacks. "It is said that the decoction of this root is good against the winds. KON NIYAKU D'II NUMA "In the village of li Numa, Umi-Gami district, Simosa province, there's a plant called Kon niyaku d'li Numa; but it's not the one we've just been talking about, it's Tsuno mata (Gymnogongrus pinnulatus), which, when boiled, resembles the true Kon niyaku in consistency, hence its name. It is cooked with Shô yu, or eaten after being preserved in miso." After reading Dr. Vidal's memoir, which made a deep impression on us, we asked for Koniaku tubers in Yokohama, which we received and planted. Our personal observations seem to justify the author's assertions regarding the hardiness of this Aroidée, whose importance increased singularly in our eyes when we received the notice from M. le comte de Castillon. Koniaku is not a vegetable plant; it belongs not to horticulture, but to agriculture, whose domain is not ours; but, when we come across a plant of such interest, we cannot resign ourselves to ignoring it. KUDZU (Japan), KO (China). PUERARIA THUNBERGIANA Benth, Journ. of the Linnean Society, vol. IX, p. 122. Pachyrrhizus Thunbergianus Sieb. et Zucc, Fam, nat. Fl. jap, II, 113; P. trilobus DC, Neustanthus chinensis Benth, Flora Hongk, 86; Dolichos hirsutus Thunb, in Linn. Transact, II, p. 339. Leguminosae family. Plant with climbing, tomentose stems. Leaves trifoliolate, with mediocrely pedicellate lateral leaflets, rounded, pale and reticulate beneath, slightly hairy on both sides, 3 inches long, the terminal larger. Flowers purplish, arranged KUDZU Fig. 41 - Pueraria Thunbergiana. in clusters 6 to 7 inches long; peduncles tomentose, I inch long. Pods linear, compressed, 3 inches long, ending in a curved tip, covered with long yellowish hairs. Kudzu (pronounced Koudzou) grows in Japan (Thunb.). Ko in China In his Novus Atlas sinensis (1655), Martini reports that, in the town of Li-ping (prov. Quei-chen): "Pannos conficiunt incolæ ex cruda cannabe, seu herba cannabi prorsus simili. Co Sinæ vocant. Ex his vestes pro tempore æstivo eximiæ plane sunt ac commodissimæ." (P. 102). Pueraria Thunbergiana Benth (Pachyrrhizus Thunbergianus S. et Z.), Ko, in Chinese. P. trilobus DC. has the same Chinese name. Both plants appear to be textiles (1). In his Nouveaux Mémoires sur l'état de la Chine (1696, Paris, 2 vols.), Louis Le Comte reports that, in addition to cotton, the Chinese wear fabrics in summer made from nettle and another species they call Ko pou, which they highly esteem. The latter is supplied in Fokien by a plant called Ko, a creeping shrub that they let grow in the fields and which is often extremely long. It has rounded leaves, larger than those of Ivy. They are soft, green above and covered with white down below. The stem is as thick as a finger. To obtain textile fibers, the stems are immersed in water, as we do for flax, and, after stripping them of their outer bark, the fibers of the second bark are used to make a fabric that is very beautiful, transparent and fresh (I, 242). Pueraria Thunbergiana Benth. Already reported by Martini, Ko, in Chinese (2). (1) Bretschneider, Early European Researches, p. 14, London 1881. (2) Bretschneider, loc. cit,, p. 28. Information on the seeds of a textile plant sent from China by a French missionary (1): "The Ko plant is also called Hoang-kin, Lan-ho and Khithsi. It can be found everywhere, but especially in the provinces of Tche-Kiang and Kiang-nan. It grows just as well in uncultivated areas as in cultivated fields. It starts to grow in spring and extends its purple stem to a length of 1 me 2 tchang (3 to 6 meters). The stems are harvested and, after suitable preparation, textile filaments are obtained. The root is violet on the outside and white on the inside; it grows to the size of an arm and 2 to 3 meters long. Its leaves are three-lobed, like those of the Fong tree, but longer, with a green surface and pale underside. In the seventh month, this plant produces reddish-purple flowers in clusters. After drying in the sun, they can be boiled in water to make stock. "The fruits of this plant resemble small yellow beans. They should be picked in the seventh or eighth month. HARVESTING " The stems of the Ko plant are usually ripe in summer, which is when they are harvested. Those that are still green and short are left. Those about 3 meters long are harvested with the root, which, according to some authors, yields a nourishing starch. They are called Theou-ko, or Ko stems (1) Industries anciennes et modernes de l'Empire chinois, by MM.Stanislas Julien and Paul Champion. Paris, Eugène Lacroix, libraire-éditeur, 1869. of the highest quality. When the stems are extraordinarily long, with white spots near the root, they are good for nothing. As for those without white spots, 2 or 3 meters of each stem are cut off; these are called eulko, i.e. second-quality Ko stems. PREPARATION " Once the stems have been collected, they are boiled in water over a hot fire. The filaments are removed with the fingernail; they are as white as those of Hemp, but are not adherent to the green part of the plant. Once the filaments have been plucked, they are washed in running water, beaten and, after being thoroughly cleaned, dried in the open air. Their whiteness increases if they are exposed to the dew for one or two nights. After that, they should be placed in the shade, as they fear the sun's rays. WASHING KO CLOTHES "If these garments are washed in pure water crushed with Meï leaves (Amygdalus nana, according to M. Abel de Rémusat; Arbutus, according to P. d'Entrecolles), they will remain reeking all summer long. Some people wash them in a porcelain basin with boiling water, in which they have crushed Meï leaves. It's essential not to use a wooden tub, otherwise Ko's clothes will turn black. "According to M. de Jussieu's opinion, the plant in question appears to be a Phaseolae related to Dolichos and particularly Dolichos bulbosus. Kudzu in Japan In the book Le Japon à l'Exposition universelle de 1878, we read: "Kudzu (Pueraria Thunbergiana) is a wild plant whose roots yield starch. Its leaves are used to feed cattle, and its fibers to make cloth. We are indebted to the kindness of Count de Castillon for two notes he has translated from Japanese, which describe in the most instructive and comprehensive detail the use made in Japan of Kudzu roots and fibers. The one we're about to read describes the process used to extract the starch. The other, published in the Mémoires de la Société des Etudes japonaises, 1878-1879, describes the manufacture of Kudzu cloth. It and the foreword that precedes it can be found at the end of this chapter. KUDZU KO (Kudzu starch) (Extract from the Notice sur les pluntes à fécule du Japon) "Kudzu (Pueraria Thunbergiana) is a climbing plant that grows wild in mountains and wastelands. In spring, young shoots emerge from the old stem. Each petiole bears three leaves similar to those of Ingem mame (Phaseolus vul20 garis); leaves and petioles are covered with hairs. In autumn, clusters of wine-purple flowers similar to those of Mame (Leguminosae) emerge from the leaf axils, producing pods. From the ninth and tenth months until the twelfth, when the eyes start to develop, these roots are pulled out with an iron pick or hoe. They are washed to remove the soil, and carefully crushed on a flat stone with a wooden or metal mallet. A tub is filled with water, in which the roots are kneaded to extract the soluble parts. The pulp is filtered and placed in a hemp canvas bag, which is then pressed. The residue is discarded. Immediately afterwards, filter through a cotton cloth bag. Leave to stand for one day. Decant the top water and dry the deposit, which is divided with a cleaver. The black starch underneath is scraped off and discarded. Place a second time in the sumasi-ohe (washing tub) with a large quantity of water, and stir while swirling. As the deposit precipitates, the falsetto is successively removed from the superimposed cinnamons, starting with those at the top, to draw off the water from the upper part. This process is repeated two or three times. After the precipitate has dried, it is removed with a small trowel and placed in the sara-si (flat box), lined with a cloth sprinkled with ash, to dry in the sun. Kaï-Kudzu (Ashen Kudzu, Grey Kudzu) is then obtained, and to make this starch perfectly white, a suitable proportion is placed in a tub and stirred circularly in water as before. Filter through a tightly woven sack; leave to stand for a day, after which it is decanted; change the water a third time, leave the deposit to precipitate, and, by repeating this operation seven or eight times, remove all soluble parts. When the starch is dry, we place it in a sara-si stretched with thick paper, and, by putting it to dry every day in the sun, it forms perfectly white lumps, and we then have Kudzu ko (Kudzu starch)." In 1879, we sowed seeds we had received from Dr. Hénon against a wall facing south. They produced vigorous stems that soon reached the wall coping. They didn't flower, and when winter came, they froze. The stump did not suffer. In 1880, the stems were stronger than those the seedling had produced, and 30 degrees of cold froze only those parts that exceeded 1".50 in height. The lower parts, which had become woody, therefore withstood the severe winter; they had not flowered. In 1881, the full height of the stems survived the winter well. The plant did not flower. Since then, the plant has flowered every year at the end of September. We have therefore observed that the Kudzu is absolutely hardy, with luxuriant vegetation, but at the same time, in the Paris climate, it is completely sterile, making it impossible to multiply the plant by sowing. At the Muséum, where a few plants of Kudzu exist, the plants have, like ours, withstood the winters; it is true that they are placed in the part of the garden known as the layers, i.e. in the best sheltered spot. We tried taking cuttings, but failed. Laying down stems gave better results. Then, the mother plant having acquired extraordinary vigor and development, we were able to lay down some twenty stems, which gave us as many new plants. Multiplication is therefore easy and unlimited. This is a crucial point, as the plant does not produce seeds in the Paris climate. We have seen that the Kudzu stump provides a valuable starch and that its leaves are sought after by livestock; but Pueraria Thunbergiana is first and foremost a textile plant. In Japan, however, it does not seem to be cultivated for its fibers as widely as in China. When asked about this, Dr. Hénon wrote on July 29 1883: "If I have not spoken to you about the uses of Kudzu as a textile plant, it is because I have never seen it put to this use. In the part of Japan I lived in, Kudzu grows everywhere in coppices; it vegetates there with great power, grows stems 8 to 10 meters long, flowers very abundantly and is covered with pods, which, to tell the truth, contain few seeds. It is not cultivated, but in winter, poor people pull out the roots, not without difficulty, and an excellent starch is made. The starch obtained from this starch is much better, more resistant, without being brittle, than that from rice or wheat. This starch is sometimes used as a food starch and in cakes. Kudzu is therefore cultivated for two purposes: firstly, an excellent starch is extracted from its roots, and secondly, it provides material for the manufacture of fabrics, the industrial importance of which was pointed out to us a few years ago by Mr. Eugène Simon, former French consul. The stems of Pueraria Thunbergiana emit such long, numerous and fibrous sprays that one cannot doubt the profit that would be made if the plant were naturalized or cullivated in France. We believe in the accuracy of the information that presents its fabric as remarkably beautiful. It seems to us that Kudzu or Ko seeds should be imported from its native countries. It is so hardy that it can be grown anywhere by letting it crawl along the ground. Indeed, it would be impossible to provide high enough supports for a plant whose stems reach 8 to 10 meters in length. So we have to let it crawl, as the Chinese and Japanese do. In a letter dated August 16 1883, Count de Castillon wrote: "In the San kaï meï San dzu ye, Kudzu is shown as a plant crawling on the ground, and the drawing shows Japanese peasants extracting it. There is therefore no need to climb it. Kudzu or Ko could therefore be grown by letting its stems spread out over the field, and its stumps would yield abundant, inexpensive harvests for an indeterminate period of time. A perennial, textile and extraordinarily productive plant deserves serious and decisive experimentation. As for us, we thought we could take advantage of its nutritional properties to talk about it at length, as we have just done. LA TOILE DU KUDZU Notice translated for the first time from Japanese by M. le comte de Castillon (see Mémoires de la Société des études japonaises, 1878-1879). Kudzu (Pueraria Thunbergiana) is an extremely valuable food and industrial plant. It is also an ornamental plant, and its spirited growth combined with its extensive foliage make it ideal for lining walls and cradles. It also has medicinal properties that merit serious study. Its roots, flowers and pods are used as a remedy in China and Japan. In the latter country, its leaves are used as fodder, and its long, thin, very resistant twigs are used as string. The starch provided in abundance by its long, voluminous roots, which can reach a length of 3 meters and a diameter of Om, 10 to Om, 12, is of an exceptional quality. Its finesse, brilliant whiteness and pleasant flavor make it the best of its kind. Dr. Filhol, the learned chemist from Toulouse (1), has recognized the nutritive power of this root as equal to that of the potato. The only plant of this species that exists, we believe, in Europe, was introduced by us three and a half years ago, in the spring of 1875. (See Annales de la Société d'Horticulture de la Haute-Garonne, 1875.) Everything leads us to believe that this precious plant will adapt perfectly to our climate. Moreover, it requires no cultivation care. Finally, its climbing stem provides a fabric with special properties, but manufactured by processes that our industry will probably find ways to simplify. This is what we will be able to judge after hearing the Japanese author, to whom we leave the floor: "Kudzu (Pueraria Thunbergiana), a perennial plant of the Leguminosae family, closely resembles Fudzi mame (Dolichos cultratus Thunb.) and Ingen mame (Phaseolus vulgaris L.); but its stem and leaves are covered with hairs; its sarmentose stem is extraordinarily vigorous; its seeds are inedible. "This plant grows spontaneously in the various provinces, on mountains or in wasteland, depending on the place. Everywhere, a starch called Kudzu ko is prepared from its roots, and at the Kake gava relay station, in Yen siu (Totoini province), a thread is extracted from its stem, with which a fabric of the highest reputation is woven. This Kudzu comes from the mountains and wastelands of the village of Kura-ma, to the north-east of the aforementioned post house, from the localities of Matsu-ba and Oho zawa, and from the village of Tomi-da, located south of Naka-yama, Koya, etc. It is considered to be of the highest quality. It is especially considered to be of superior quality if it grows on the mountains of the village of Musi-bu, which faces the north side of Mari-matsi, in the Sudzi district, and on those of the village of No-be. "If now we investigate how long ago Kudzu began to be exploited, although this point is not yet elucidated, as we possess a song composed by His Exc. Tameal (1) on the Kudzu cloth of celieu, it is today something like more than 680 years since this fabric must have been made. "We will now give a general description of the manufacture of this fabric. As for Kudzu starch, it will be dealt with separately when we talk about this type of food. "Although Kudzu stalks are found to have reached a length of 2 to 3 zyau (3 to 9 meters) and beyond by the time autumn arrives, they must be cut for a period of about fifty days from the first fortnight of the fifth month (June 5-21) to make thread. Their length, which at this time is only 7 syaku (2m, 10), reaches 9 syaku (2m, 70) in the middle of this period, to reach 1 and 2 zyau (3 to 6 meters) in the last days. But as the 3 or 4 syaku (Om, 90 to 1°, 20) above the roots are of poor quality, they have to be left, which reduces the harvested length from around 3-4 to 6-7 syaku (Om, 90-1m, 20 to 1m, 80-2m, 10). "On the same day as this operation, they must be plunged into a boiler full of boiling water. They are shaken in all directions for a very short time (2 and a half seconds, 5 of our European seconds). After stirring, they are returned to the water for the same number of seconds, then removed. "They are then soaked in running water, where they are left for a day and a night. A doormat is spread on the ground, on which they are piled; another is laid on top as a blanket, and they are left wrapped in this way for two days and two nights, and, when they are uncovered, the fermenting stalks give off vapours; they are watered, which puts an end to this fermentation. This is called naha midzu (middle water, second water). "The mats are replaced as before, and the stems remain in this state for a day and a night. "They are then put to soak in shallow running water, where they are trodden on with the feet; the bark is then removed and the stem core discarded. The strips of bark are equalized in length, and placed in bundles of thirty; then, taking two of these strips at a time, with one hand, they are inserted between the branches of a small fork 2 tan (Om, 06) long, which is held with the other. "When they have turned white, they are hung on a horizontal bamboo pole to dry in the sun. Before drying is complete, they are removed and, by shaking them briskly, the bark fibers separate by rubbing against each other, thus obtaining the filasse, which is then left to dry completely before being converted into Kudzu wo (Kudzu thread). "To make Kudzu wo, we choose the best strands from the tow; with thumb and forefinger, we tear them apart as finely as possible; then we add them end to end, knotting them tightly. Scissors are used to cut off the ends of the threads that extend beyond the knots. The threads whose knots have been sheared off in this way are called tsuguri. They are then rolled up in the wo oke (a round yarn box with a louvered bottom), which is then placed in a basket of the same shape and immersed in water. "Then, by interlacing the threads in the shape of an 8 around the thumb and little finger (literally: as in the game children call aya, cat's cradle), small hanks are formed, and they are ready to be used for the weft. As this is their only use, silk, hemp or cotton threads must necessarily be used for the warp. "The loom does not differ in any way from the ordinary loom; only that the weft thread is wetted with water before being placed in the shuttle, and as it passes the reed, the knots are lifted out, which are trimmed with scissors when the weaving is complete. The fabric is then rinsed with clean water, dried, then re-wetted; it is wound onto the maki ginuta (fulling cylinder) and slowly beaten with small strokes, which gives the fabric a sheen, which is then dyed in various shades, after which it is stretched by means of the sii-si (1), and a coat of nori, a primer composed of 10 momme (37 gr. 50) of Kudzu ko (Kudzu starch) and 400 me (1 kil. 500) of water, then the Kudzu cloth is finished. (1) A dyeing instrument designed to remove wrinkles from a fabric; it consists of several bows of elastic bamboo that hold the fabric taut across its width. This instrument is also known as a mogari (thief). The bamboo generally used for this purpose is ma-daké (B. Quilioi), which has earned it the syuonym mogari take. "This fabric does not soften under the action of water; and, as once wet, it dries quickly, it is used for kappa (1), kakama (2), etc. The pieces being woven in large widths, it is easy to weave them. The pieces, woven in large widths, are also used for fusuma (3) interlinings, and this type of production has developed considerably in recent years. "The first manufacturer of Kudzu yarn at the Kake gava post house is Sudzuki gen pe. The main manufacturers of this fabric in the same place are Naru hira Kiu meï, Sató Kuni yosi, Matsu moto yosi naga, etc. The main house for Kudzu cloth is Sató Kuni yosi. The main manufacturer of Kudzu cloth is Hyo-do, in the village of Taka-mura. "A total of 60,000 tan (a tan is worth 8m, 50) of this cloth (500,000 meters), with a gold value of 49,200 yen (263,809 fr. 65), is manufactured in the places we have mentioned, in equal quantities in the three types, upper, middle and lower."
