KUMARA (1) BATATAS EDULIS Chois. var; Ipomxa Batatas Poir. var; Ipomæa chrysorrhiza Hook. f. Family Convolvulaceae. Closely related to the common potato, Ipomxa Batatas Lamk. of which it is merely a variety; cullivated since ancient times by the Maoris of New Zealand. In 1884, Messrs Vilmorin-Andrieux and Cte received Kumara tubers from Mr. J. Hooker, harvested at Kew. Unfortunately, these tubers arrived in such a state of decomposition that it was impossible to plant them. This is a very unfortunate accident; as the climate of New Zealand is more or less the same as that of central France, the Kumara would have been a great potato for us. There are between twenty and thirty varieties of this Ipomoea. (1) Notes taken almost entirely from a report published by Mr. Schomburgk, Director of the Adelaide Botanical Garden in South Australia.) Its tubers differ from one another like those of our potatoes; some have red skin, others purple, others white. Some are smooth and cylindrical, others deeply furrowed or regularly fluted; others are short and thick, obtuse at the tip; but there is never any mixing between these varieties. All reproduce exactly the types planted. The only sign of degeneration, caused by soil or drought, is in the volume of the tuber. All the varieties are ancient and have been perpetuated by the perfect preservation of the tubers. In the memoirs of the New Zealand Institute, Mr. W. Colenso gives a very interesting report on this favorite vegetable of the Maoris: "They have been using it," he says, "since prehistoric times, as many legends show. "When the seasons and soil are favorable, its yield is considerable. However, it has a powerful insect enemy in the form of a large larva, which devours the leaves of young plants long before the roots or tubers have developed. "Each plant is visited by elderly women, using a planter or pointed instrument, to select and pull out some of the season's most beautiful new tubers. The soil is loosened around the plants, and then mounded again, not unlike the hoeing of potatoes, but with much greater care. "The young tubers from this first uprooting are scraped and half-dried in the sun on clean mats, carefully protected from the dew. When dry, they are either eaten or stored in baskets as a kind of candy or candied tuber. They are held in high esteem by the Maoris and eaten raw, or soaked and crushed in a little hot water. "During the general uprooting, which takes place late in autumn, but always before the frost, great care is taken to collect the roots. They are carefully sorted according to volume. Any that are bruised, broken or slightly injured are set aside for prompt consumption. The rest are collected in large flat baskets, always new, and placed in good time in suitable stores, taking great care not to do so

Colenso says, "In my opinion, one of the most remarkable things about this useful tuber is how the first varieties were obtained. Mr. Colenso further says: "In my opinion, one of the most remarkable things about this useful tuber is still to be noted: how were the first varieties obtained?" On this subject, he could learn nothing to his satisfaction from the Maoris themselves, except that all the varieties came to them from their ancestors. He tried to find out if the old Maoris had ever seen the Kumara in flower. "No, they had never known anything like it," he said, and they never brought in their crops until the leaves had dried out. Mr. Colenso also often wanted to know if any new kind or variety had recently been obtained by the Maoris or their immediate ancestors; to which they also replied: "No." "Is it not possible," says Mr. Colenso, "that in remote times the plant flowered and that ancient cultivators, voluntarily or accidentally, obtained roots from sown seeds? Is it not also possible that the plant, after artificial, persevering, assiduous cultivation, tending to obtain early varieties, and continued for centuries, came to flower definitively, as happened in England with potato varieties, after repeated cultivation? The earliest varieties give neither flowers nor seeds. Professor Kirk of Wellington, New Zealand, has twice sent Kumara tubers to Mr. Schomburgk, and has enclosed the most interesting information: "The Maoris," he writes, "cultivate about twenty varieties, all of which are said to have been brought to them by the mystic 'Hawaiki'. "The one with the most delicate taste is a small pink-fleshed variety. Its cultivation is almost abandoned, but it deserves to be grown as a delicacy. I haven't seen it for twelve or thirteen years, but I'll try to send it to you next spring with other varieties. I intend to visit the north, and there I'll be able to make arrangements. Mr. Kirk continues: "I have written to a Maori friend of mine, asking for information on the vegetation, cultivation and cooking of Kumara, and will send it to you when I receive his reply. For the moment, I'll limit myself to saying that Kumara is planted on small mounds arranged in staggered rows, about 18 inches apart. The soil should be light rather than compact and rich. Once the piece of land has been planted, it is strictly forbidden to women. When ripe, the crop is harvested with great ceremony. It's best baked in the oven, in the Maori way. The planting season begins in October and continues until December. The soil is ploughed or spaded, and small mounds are formed after finely breaking up the clods. The mounds are 8 to 10 inches high, and a tuber is planted in each one, barely covered with soil. The plantation then requires no further care other than careful weeding. Harvesting takes place in March and April, with the crop ripening around mid-March. To preserve the tubers, we use the Maori rua method. The rua is a shallow pit, dug in an easily drained area and in soil of sufficient consistency, the excavated earth being used to raise the sides and cover the tubers to form the roof. The sides of the rua are built without using water, the earth simply being beaten with the hand. A post is placed at each end of the pit, and these posts support a ridge from which fascines and pieces of wood are stretched to the wall, tightly packed together and covered with earth. Tubers are also kept in shallow pits with a good layer of branches at the bottom and a good covering of the same nature at the top, before the soil is thrown on top. If they are exposed to the air for long periods, they are lost. They are removed with sharp-tipped sticks, about a foot long, a job that must be done with care to avoid injuring them, as those damaged will soon perish. If the tubers are left in the ground, they quickly rot during the winter rains. The Maoris prefer to cook them in their ovens, although they often boil them like potatoes. Some types of Kumara are quite watery. These are not good boiled, as this method of cooking makes them even more watery. All are even better when baked or roasted. It will be noted that Mr. Colenso, speaking of an initial uprooting, says that it is done by elderly women, whereas Professor Kirk says that women are absolutely forbidden to enter Kumara plantations; but there is no contradiction here. This is obviously a belief shared by many of our gardeners, who at certain times attribute a pernicious influence to the presence of women, particularly on Melons and Mushrooms. In 1884, the Kumara had not yet been fully acclimatized in South Australia, but its cultivation seemed likely to succeed, and Mr. Schomburgk hoped to distribute tubers in 1885. The plant had recently been introduced to the East Indies. It had produced first-rate harvests there. Mr. Duthie, Director of the Saharanpur Botanical Gardens, said in his last report: "I consider Kumara a very valuable addition to our vegetable varieties. In his work entitled Origine des plantes cultivées, M. Alph. de Candolle, in his search for the potato's native country, states that considerations of roughly equal value would allow either America or the East Indies to be attributed the plant's paternity. However, the Kumara, which is certainly only a variety of the Potato, is found neither in America, where it does not yet appear to exist, nor in the East Indies, which recently received it from New Zealand. According to this hypothesis, this primitive type of Potato should have been carried from America or India to Oceania, where it would have been modified to form a distinct race. Moreover, this introduction would have had to go back a very long way, since the plant seems to have existed among the Maoris since prehistoric times. In addition, Alph. de Candolle states: "The plant is cultivated in Taïli, the neighboring islands and New Zealand under the names : Umarra, Gumarra and Gumalla, described by Forster as Convolvulus chrysorrhizus, is Batate according to Sir Joseph Hooker. Seeman points out that these names resemble the Quichuen name for Batate in America, which, he says, is Cumar." Until now, we were unaware that Kumara was cultivated in Taîti and the neighbouring islands. We believe it would be a useful acquisition. 21 LEWISIA Chita, Spatulum or Spœtlum of the Indians. Canadian bitter root. White Lewisia. Bitter Root. Mountain rose. LEWISIA REDIVIVA Pursh. Fam. Portulaceae. Our attention was drawn to this plant by a report from the Commissioner of Agriculture (1), which states: The California Indians give Lewisia the name "Spatulum". Its root is compact and fusiform, dark on the outside, white and mealy on the inside. It provides an abundant supply of concentrated food, of which a single ounce is enough for a meal. The plant is worth cultivating." We'll say later what we think of the extraordinary nutritional value attributed by the report to Lewisia and affirmed successively by several botanists, who seem to have repeated, without further examination, what their predecessors had said; but this is not the only merit of the plant: it is also very ornamental, and, with its spring flowers, pink and relatively large, is quite worthy of taking its place in our gardens. And that's not all: its vitality is quite astonishing, making it a real plant curiosity. We are going to present the information we (1) Report of the Commissionner of Agriculture for the year 1870. Food products of the North American Indians, Washington; Printing office, 1871. on its habitat, its nutritional properties, its vitality and its hitherto little-known cultivation as a decorative plant. HABITAT "The plant is found on the summit of Mount Diablo, looking north to Colombia, east to Montana, Utah and Arizona (1)." "It is found everywhere in Oregon, near mountains, in arid prairies, along rivers (2), among them the Lewis and Têteplate rivers (3), as well as on the banks of Clark's river (4)." FOOD PROPERTIES "The natives use it extensively as a food. Stripped of its bark, the inner white part of the root is boiled in water to produce a substance similar to boiled Salep or Arrow-root. The dried root is almost converted to starch by maceration in cold water (5). "It is a favorite food among aborigines. With the bark removed, a handful of roots, boiled with meat, forms a considerable quantity of nutritious mucilage. According to Douglas, thanks to their powerfully nutritious quality, Lewisia roots are an admirable resource for camps during (1) Geological survey of California, Botany, vol. I, p. 78. (2) Torrey and Gray, Flora of North America, vol. I, p. 677. (3) Nuttall, Journal of the Academy of natural Sciences of Philadelphia. 4) Hooker, Botanical miscellany, vol. Ier, p. 344. (5) Torrey and Gray, loc. cit. long journeys, two or three ounces a day is enough for a man, even when he has to endure long fatigue (1)." VITALITY "The roots of Lewisia have such vital energy that the specimen in Lewis's herbarium, as Pursh notes, showing some signs of vegetation, was planted in a garden in Philadelphia, where it lived for a year; and Douglas's specimens, in the same conditions, vegetated for some time in the garden of the Horticultural Society in London (2)." "In June 1826, Douglas had collected specimens in flower, but they had fallen from the boat into the water while descending a rapid; in March 1827, those that recovered to live, after having been compressed in the herbarium, were planted as said above (3)." "We have had desiccated specimens, kept for two years, or more, which still gave fresh harvests of leaves. "The specimen on which our figure was taken at Kew, collected in English Columbia, to be preserved in the herbarium, by Mr. Lyall, R. N. of the delimitation expedition, had been plunged into boiling water because of its well-known obstinate vitality. More than a year and a half later, however, it was showing signs of life and producing its beautiful flowers in (1) Nuttall, loc. cit. (2) Torrey and Gray, loc. cit. (3) Drummond. in all their perfection, in May, in the Royal Gardens at Kew (1)." DECORATIVE CULTIVATION "Lewisia rediviva (Spatulum). A very remarkable and beautiful rock garden plant, resembling Purslane. "It is very dwarfed, only about an inch high, and has two small tufts of narrow leaves, from the center of which rise flowering stems. "The flowers are large relative to the dimensions of the plant, measuring from 1 inch to 2 1/2 inches in diameter, and varying from dark pink to white. The roots are succulent and have the property of retaining their vile principle under the most unfavorable conditions. "It sometimes fails to develop its annual leaves, with the result that it is often thought to be dead and difficult to treat, although this is not the case. "When grown in pots, it should be planted in broken stones and its roots plunged into a light, sandy loam (2) with peat. "After flowering, the plant curls up and looks like a heap of dried-out, twisted bits of string; but such is the nature of the plant and hence its name (3)." (1) Botanical Magazine, pl. 5395. Our drawing is taken from the colored figure published in this work. (2) English loam is an excellent quality free soil. (3) Robinson, The English flouer garden, London, 1883. Our kind and learned correspondent, Mr. Havard, United States Army Surgeon, wrote to us on June 14 1888: "I have mailed you a small Fig. 42. - Lewisia rediviva. packet of roots of Lewisia rediviva (Portulaceae), one of the most sought-after native plants of the Oregon Indians. I received these roots from one of my correspondents and it is quite likely that, if you wish, I can send you another package. "The plant was in full bloom at the time of removal, which probably explains the thinness of the roots. They must be much fleshier in spring, when the first leaves sprout, which is when the Indians pull them out. It's a very hardy plant, as its name suggests, and so I hope that, despite the adverse circumstances, the roots will retain their vitality until you can get them into the ground." We planted these roots at the reception, and by autumn, although absolutely deprived of water, they were showing budding leaves. They gave us flowers in May. Another letter from Mr. Havard, dated September 1st: "I will answer as many of your questions about Lewisia rediviva as I can. The Indians do not cultivate this plant; they pull it from uncultivated land in spring (April), as soon as the first rosette of leaves allows them to recognize it, i.e. before it flowers. They eat the root dried or baked in an oven, which simply consists of an earthen hole lined with stones. The result, after removal of the bark, is what you see in the enclosed small package received from Washington territory. These cooked roots will give you the answer to your question about their maximum size. Of all the samples I have received, I have not seen any larger. The Indians eat them as they are, or make flour from them, which they eat either as bread or in soup. "No one has ever ventured to sow Lewisia seeds. Although a perennial plant, it is more than likely that medium-sized roots would be produced in the first year. It remains to be seen whether the starch, which forms the main part of these roots, would be accumulated in sufficient quantities to make them excellent. "Does the plant have divisible stumps? I believe that the many branches of the mother stock could be separated without their vitality suffering. "The whites of Oregon and Washington territory are not fond of it; it is not an ordinary article of their cuisine. "In short, these roots are nourishing, but their mealy flavor has nothing to recommend them in any special way, and they seem far too small to be profitably cultivated, unless cultivation can modify them, which is quite possible. "If Lewisia is a disappointment as a vegetable plant, it deserves to be grown as an ornamental. I expect to receive a parcel of it before long, so that I can send you a few more roots and probably some seeds too." We received this package; it contained seeds which were sown immediately. Wishing to ascertain the nutritional properties of Lewisia, we used the cooked roots we received for this purpose, and gave them to Mr. Arnaud, chemist at the Muséum (1), who very kindly offered to examine them. We didn't put enough roots at his disposal for him to make a quantitative analysis, but enough for him to give us this (1) Chevreul's successor as Professor of Norganic Chemistry. reply: "The root of Lewisia rediviva contains no nitrogenous matter and, consequently, no albuminoids or alkaloids whose presence could explain the nutritional value attributed to it. It can therefore only be considered as a starchy material, analogous to many other roots or tubers. As we have just said, Mr. Arnaud did not have a large enough quantity of roots to make a complete analysis; but we have since received from Mr. Havard the following analysis, published by Mr. H. Trimble, Professor at the College of Pharmacy, Philadelphia: Fatty matter, resin and wax............ 4.98 Mucilage gum.................... 14.80 Albuminoids.......................... 3.58 Starch.............................. 8.57 Water................................... 12.17 Ash............................. 2.53 Woody fiber and indeterminate matter.. 53.37 100.00 and the American chemist accompanies his analysis with the following observation, approved by M. Arnaud: "The quantity of starch found may seem low when one considers the use made of the Lewisia root, but the large quantity of gum and mucilage makes up for what is lacking. We can therefore believe, without taking on the task of resolving the question, that the Indians really feel nourished by the "spatulum" and that the nutritional power they attribute to it is not imaginary. We conclude by recommending the cultivation of Lewisia, made easy by its extraordinary vitality, either with the aim of obtaining larger roots than the wild plant, or to add a very pretty flower to the current riches of our gardens. LIS COMESTIBLES La Flore des serres et des jardins de l'Europe published (vol. VI, 1850-1851, p. 145) a very interesting note by the learned botanist Fischer, as follows: "Sur les Liliacées alimentaires de la Sibérie. The disaster that has struck the potato harvest in recent years has focused the attention of botanists and farmers on plants that are supposed to be able to replace, in some way, this precious plant. Unfortunately, none of the species proposed to growers combines all the valuable qualities of the potato: ease of cultivation, rapid and fertile multiplication, abundance of nutritive matter, pleasant flavour, easy digestion. Yam, Batate douce and Manioc require tropical temperatures, and their cultivation, which alone could be on a par with that of the Solanée, is strictly excluded from northern latitudes. "Tuberous nasturtiums have too strong a taste; Topinambur, whose merit in other respects is indisputable, and Oxalis are too watery; Apios requires a large area for cultivation, and soon becomes hard and inedible; Psoralea esculenta scarcely lives up to the expectations we had of it; Camassia esculenta has perhaps more right to the protection of agriculture than the last two of these plants, if, however, its multiplication is prompt and easy. It is the only one of all the plants mentioned that can be compared with the Liliaceae of Siberia, which have always served as food for the inhabitants and which deserve to be better known in this respect. "Erythronium Dens-canis, the beautiful large-flowered variety of which is widespread in the south of western Siberia, is sought after and harvested there as an excellent food, of which a certain supply used to be sent annually to the court of St. Petersburg. Propagation by seed is very reliable, and the plant produces many of them; sown as soon as they are ripe, these seeds all sprout, but it takes three years for the bulb to reach its normal size. The local name is Kandyk. "In Siberia, lilies are known as Sarana, and Lilium tenuifolium L. and Sarana kamtschatica Fisch (Lilium kamtschaticum, Fritillaria kamtschatica by the authors) are the edible species par excellence. L. spectabile Fisch. is also used as food. These three plants multiply with extraordinary ease: L. tenuifolium L. reproduces almost exclusively by seed, but also, like the other two, by cloves, and it seems that each of the bulb scales, elongated and pointed in L. spectabile, large, short and rounded in the shape of a tuber in the Kamtschatha Sarana, forms a new plant; this mode of propagation is even essential for the Kamtschatka Sarana, as it rarely bears seed, a large number of flowers becoming sterile through abortion of the pistil. In Kamtschatka, there is a lily very similar to L. canadense, which I call L. avenaceum, after the name it goes by in the country and because of the shape of the bulb scales, which resemble large oat seeds; this species has not yet been introduced into our gardens; in its native country, it is eaten like the ordinary Sarana, which is nevertheless preferred. "Nowhere in Siberia are these useful plants cultivated; everywhere, it is the bulb of the wild plant that is harvested, and harvested in abundance. Lilium tenuifolium and Lilium spectabile are found in the eastern part of the Tomsk government and are widespread around Baikal and throughout trans-Baikal Siberia (Daury), as far as the eastern ocean. The Kamtschatka Sarana can be found along the Eastern Ocean coastline, in Kamtschatka itself and on the islands off the west coast of America. "It is not impossible that, when cultivation, and especially careful cultivation, ad hoc cultivation is involved, satisfactory results of improvement will be obtained. "The southern steppes of Russia are covered in spring by a brilliant flowering of Tulips; among these Tulips, there is a species fairly well known on the Don which does not differ essentially from T. suaveolens (1) and which is avidly sought after and eaten by (1) Aitchison, Notes on Products of Western Afghanistan and Northeastern Persia, says that Tulipa montana covers the arid plains of Afghanistan. The bulbs are collected and eaten; stripped of their outer skins, they are said to be sold in Bombay under the name Salep. The same traveler cites Eremurus aurantiacus and Olgæ, other Liliaceae, whose young leaves are cooked and eaten as a vegetable in the Kuran Valley districts of Afghanistan. Dr. Aitchison and his travel companions say that it is a vegetable that the inhabitants know perfectly well how to distinguish from others, which they do not touch. This magnicum lily grows wild in bushes, in rock crevices, in woods and on mountains, up to a fairly high altitude; it is resistant to temperatures of 8 and 10 degrees above zero, and flowers from June to July. It is common throughout most of Nippon Island, especially near the cities of Yokohama and Yokoska. Its edible bulbs are S Fig. 44 - Litium speciosum. used in Japanese cuisine. It is widely cultivated by Japanese horticulturists for its beautiful flowers, which adorn gardens and are often placed in vases inside apartments; it is reproduced on Japanese drawings, paintings and porcelain. "Lilium Thunbergianum Rœmer et Schultes is referred to in the Somoku-Dusets as NatsuSukashi-Yuri, Haru-Suhashi-Yuri and HiratoriYuri, and in the Phonzo-Zoufou as Skachi and Hogaku. It can be found in fields, mainly on Parry Island. It is also found on Nippon Island and is common around the city of Yokohama, where its edible bulbs are used in Japanese food. "Lilium tigrinum Gawler, classified in SomokuDusets and Phonzo-Zoufou as Oni-Yuri. Very common on the islands of Nippon and Yeso. The edible bulbs of the Tiger Lily are eaten by the Japanese, cooked, boiled and candied. "Lilium callosum Sieb. et Zucc. Hime-Yuri or L. pomponium Thunb. According to Kæmpfer and Siebold, this species is common, in the wild, in the sparsely wooded mountainous regions of Japan, at an altitude of 165 to 650 meters. Mr. Maximowicz says it is commonly cultivated on the island of Kiusiu, near the city of Nagasaki. The Japanese use the edible bulbs of L. callosum in their food, and eat them cooked, boiled and candied. They extract a white starch from these bulbs, which was represented at the 1878 World's Fair, class 69 (Cereals, floury products from Iwaté prefecture, Rikuchiu province). This species is also called Yama-Yuri by the Japanese". Dr. Mène also cites the following Japanese Liliaceae: "Erythronium grandiflorum (which is only a variety of E. Dens-canis, as Fischer points out in the note above). This plant appears in the Somoku-Dusets and Phonzo-Zoufou under the names Katahuri and Katako-Yuri. According to Dr. Kramer and Dr. Savatier, it is found in the wooded hills of the northern provinces of Nippon Island. The Japanese botanist Keiske marked it as existing on the island of Yeso. The bulbs contain a starch that is used in Japanese food. Flasks of this starch were on display at the World's Fair. Dried flowers of this species are sometimes used in Japanese food; but it is mainly in China that they are used in food, and they are a favorite dish of the Chinese. In the Note explicative des objets exposés par l'École agricole et forestière de Komaba à l'Exposition univer(1) Kwa-ui (choix de plantes), 1759. sellede 1889 (Ministère de l'Agriculture et du Commerce, Tokio, Japon), we find the following: "The Lily: OuRi (Lilium). It is usually grown as an ornamental plant, but its bulbs are also used in cooking. They have an excellent taste, so some growers cultivate them specially. There are over twenty varieties of the lily, but only two or three whose bulbs are used. Lilies are planted around the farm, and the bulbs are harvested after two or three years. Some bulbs have a circumference of almost 33 centimeters. The lily flowers are sold to flower merchants. As for the bulbs that we like to taste, growers reserve them for themselves, either to eat or to sell". In 1878, Dr. H*** sent us bulbs of Yama-Yuri (Lilium callosum) and Wasei-Yuri, with the following note: "Yama-Yuri is the species of Lily most cultivated as a vegetable in Japan, at least in the center and south. I believe that in the north, L. auratum is grown as a food plant, but I'm not sure, as I haven't travelled there. "The Japanese name for Wasei-Yuri means early lily; it is grown less than the other; in Japan, it is planted in autumn and Yama-Yuri in spring. On several occasions, our kind correspondent has given us interesting information on Japanese edible lilies. We reproduce them below: "Most Japanese lilies are edible, but 22 not all. In the part of Japan I lived in, only Yama-Yuri and WaseiYuri are grown for the table. Both are very good; I ate them all winter, once or twice a week, and they really are a very good vegetable. "The poor people gather from the wild the bulbs of L. japonicum Thunb. (in Japanese SassaYuri, i.e. with a small Bamboo leaf), very common in the mountains; it's a mediocre eater. "They also sometimes eat L. tigrinum (OniYuri, i.e. Devil's Lily), but this is a detestable, extremely bitter food. It is, however, quite abundant in Japanese markets, at least in Yedo and Yokohama. While I was in Japan, I grew L. tigrinum in my garden and, despite the protests of my Japanese cook, who told me it wasn't good for a rich man like me, I wanted to try it. It's very bad and frankly bitter, despite having been blanched twice in boiling water beforehand. I'm not suspected of being too severe about this dish, as I declare L. callosum an excellent floury vegetable, equal to our best. "In the far south, in the Liou Kiou islands, we are said to eat the bulbs of L. longiflorum, which grows wild there; but I can only speak of it by hearsay. "The preparation of lily bulbs for the table is very simple. They are separated scale by scale, then carefully washed and lightly blanched in boiling salted water. They are then served either sautéed in fresh butter, in a red sauce with meat juice, or in a white sauce. Now that's European-Japanese cuisine. "The true Japanese recipe is as follows: after blanching them as above, they are cooked for a few minutes in a sauce composed solely of water, Shoyou and a little grated dry fish. They are quite tasty this way; but at first sight, a European palate finds fat-free sauces strange." In the Bulletin of micellaneous information from the Royal Garden of Kew, 1889, page 116, we find the following additional details on lily flowers and bulbs used as food: "It is well known that, in countries where lilies are indigenous and abundant, they are sometimes used as food plants. For some years, the dried flowers of certain Lilies have been an important article of trade in China, and in the consular report on Chinkiang trade for the year 1886, page 10, it is stated: "The export of Lily flowers has increased from 7,033,000 pounds to 7,677,622 pounds; this is the most considerable export of many years. The harvest was very good, and prices were remunerative for most of the year. No more than two-tenths is consumed here; the rest goes south, where it is used to flavour soup. The cultivation of this plant increases annually in the north of this province." In a letter to Kew from Mr. Pelham L. Warren, Consul in Taiwan, dated August 16, 1883, the following indicalions are given, relating to the provenance of the lily flowers used in China: "The lily flowers mentioned in Mr. Watter's consular report, about which you ask for information, are the dried flowers of Hemerocallis graminea and Lilium bulbiferum. They are used by the Chinese to flavor soup; they also eat them as a vegetable. Lily flowers are also said to be effective for lung ailments, and to have tonic properties. Enclosed is a small sample of those imported here. Hankow is China's leading export city; large quantities also come from Japan." The sample sent by Mr. Warren was evidently a Hemerocallis flower, and Mr. Baker said that it could indeed be H. graminea Andr. (H. minor Mill.), the Asphodete Lily. There were no samples of Lilium bulbiferum flowers. The Japanese make much use of lilies as food, according to Professor Penhallow, who gives the following interesting account of their use in the American Naturalist, volume XVI, page 119: "Various species of Lilium abound in the forests and are used as a source of floury food. At the beginning of autumn, women can be seen returning to their villages, laden with bulbs. They are completely crushed in a large wooden mortar, after which the starch is separated from the cell mass by repeated washing. The starch is then hung up in sacks for winter use, while the cell mass is dried in round, perforated cakes, somewhat resembling miniature millstones, and hung up to dry." Efforts were made by Kew's Museum of Applied Botany to obtain lily cakes from Japan. Here is a copy of a letter addressed to this establishment by Mr. Power Le Poer Trench, at the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, Tokio: "I have made the utmost efforts to obtain, for the Director of the Royal Garden, Kew, samples of lily bulb cakes, said to be used as food by the Japanese; but, in spite of all the researches I have made, I have not been able to find any in Tokio or Yokohama. "However, Mr. Wooley, who was passing through Yokohama last December on his way back to his post, took it upon himself to inquire whether any could be obtained in any village or town in the northern part of Japan, and, the month before last, he wrote that he could not get any in Hokodate, and that the Japanese said they had never seen any. "He was inclined to believe that Professor Penhallow had confused them with Fu cakes, which are made from wheat and hung up in stores to be sold, and that the ones he refers to are found only in the Aïno region. He had, however, written to Mr. Brooks, botanist attached to the Agriculturau College, Sapporo, and asked him to try to obtain the requested samples. "Later, I received another letter from Mr. Wooley, saying that he had at last succeeded in obtaining the requested cakes, and he sent me a small package containing a specimen of the smallest kind, the larger ones being, he said, of an awkward size for transport and running a greater risk of being broken." Here is the description of the samples explaining the economic use of Lilium cordifolium bulbs: Uba-Yuri (Japanese name), Kiu (Aino-name), sent to the Royal Garden Museum, Kew: "1° Fibre from which the starch has been partly extracted. "After the bulbs have been thoroughly washed and soaked in water for two days and two nights, they are pounded in a wooden mortar, then transferred to a vat filled with water. The whole mass is kneaded with the hands, and left to rest overnight to allow the starch to settle, after which the cell mass is removed and dried. "2° Cakes are made from the above fibers, by pounding them in a mortar with a little water, then kneading the mass and shaping it with the hands. The cakes are hung to dry in the Ainos' huts. The coloring is caused by smoke. The cakes are not eaten by the Japanese. The Ainos boil them and eat them, either plain or mixed with rice (the cakes vary in size, the largest being over a foot in diameter). "3° Starch made by the Japanese from UbaYuri bulbs. The starch made by the Ainos is similar, but coarser. "4° Uba-Yuri confectionery made by the Japanese. Of the various species of edible lily reviewed in the course of this study, Lilium tigrinum is the only one that grows vigorously in our gardens, and we might even add that it stands up perfectly to our harshest winters. But, as we have seen from the information supplied by our correspondent Dr. H***, its bulbs are only a mediocre source of food. Even if it were otherwise, its cultivation as a food plant could only be recommended as a curiosity. It takes far too long to obtain Lis bulbs that are sufficiently developed to be used. The same observation applies to Erythronium grandiflorum, Sarana kamtschatica and a large number of other bulbous plants, some of which offer considerable resources in regions where they grow naturally and can be harvested abundantly around the home. We thought it would be interesting to show that, nonetheless, certain Lilies, which we generally only know to be of high value for the decoration of our flowerbeds, could, in a pinch, provide us with a vegetable that would not be to be scorned. MACA (Native name in Quichua language.) LEPIDIUM MEYENH WALPERS. Cruciferae family. In September 1893, Messrs Vilmorin-Andrieux and Cit kindly associated us with the trial they were about to carry out on a new food plant: "One of our customers has come to offer us," they wrote, "seeds of a plant cultivated and indigenous to Upper Peru, called Maca in the old Peruvian dialect (Quichua language), and which would be used for human nutrition. It is said to produce an edible tuberous root, similar in shape to the turnip and in taste to the potato. "Just looking at the seed and tasting it, we think it's a cruciferous plant, and that the seed is shaped like that of the Alen cress; but what it really is, we'll have to find out in a trial. "We think it would be of interest to you to do the same, and to this end we enclose a sample of these seeds. Please keep us informed of the results you obtain. Shortly afterwards, Messrs Vilmorin-Andrieux and Cle received a letter from the same person, providing them with valuable information: "A few days ago, we gave you a packet of Peruvian Maca seeds. "We have just received some information about this plant and how to grow it. "Maca: Quichua name. "This plant belongs to the Cruciferae family. It has many points of resemblance with the Cresson alénois, grown in our gardens. Its leaves are cut and give off a strong odor. "The stem does not rise when flowering, but branches out and spreads over the surface of the soil. "The taproot is fleshy and sweet. It is used as food by the Indians of the high plateaus of the Andes. "The seeds were harvested in the Pampa de Junin, at altitudes ranging from 4,000 to 4,100 metres above sea level, where it freezes like in France. "The Indians grow Maca in blackish, peaty soil, which must not contain limestone. In France, this soil is replaced by a mixture of loam and heather. The plant is grown on humpback lines. It is thought that this procedure is necessary over there, because the soil is peaty and at the same time very marshy. If the roots are raised above the ground, it's probably to protect them from humidity. "We sowed some in Dauphiné, in August 1885, and the harvest would have been satisfactory, had this crop not been neglected at the onset of winter. "The Indians say that the roots should be left in the ground for two years. This was not the simplest of trials. The letter does not say when the Indians sow Maca; the seasons in the Andes are reversed relative to ours; our altitude above sea level is insignificant; the soil around Paris is neither peaty nor humid. We first sowed under glass, cold, in October 1893. The seeds germinated quickly and produced small, spindly plants, most of which resisted the frosts, albeit with little protection. In spring, these plants developed little. spread out on the ground, flowered early and gave fertile seeds, but their roots were so tenuous that they were not usable. In 1894, we sowed seeds from our harvest and the result was the same: some plants had a slightly thicker stump, about the size of a walnut, but too hard to be eaten. In 1895, new seedlings were sown in the open air. The plants stood up perfectly to the rigors of winter; although they were partly eaten by flea beetles, they flowered in spring and disappeared after ripening their seeds, without giving us the results we had hoped for in the letter reproduced above. We attribute our failure to invasions of flea beetles, which make it almost impossible to grow crucifers in the Crosnes garden. Maca seems perfectly suited to our climate. TWO-HORNED MACRA Ling-Kio. TRAPA BICORNIS L. fil. Family Onagrariaceae. Annual aquatic plant. Slender stem; leaves, some submerged, reduced to linear strips, others in rosettes floating on the surface of the water, with fairly long petioles, cylindrical at first, then vesicular towards their middle, at flowering time, with diamond-shaped blades, entire or only slightly toothed. Flowers white, small, briefly pedunculate, set in the axils of the upper leaves; calyx with two accrescent divisions, later becoming the horns of the fruit; corolla with four petals; stamens four; style small, ending in a captive stigma. Fruit woody, with two opposite, thick, obtuse horns, curved at the top. "The great Lake Talo is famous for its Linkio aquatic fruit. This fruit resembles the Tribulus "ad instar triangularis pyramidis undequaque prominens". Its rind is green, thick and reddish at the tips, turning black as it dries. Its subsFig. 45. - Trapa bicornis. inside is very white; its flavor is that of the Chestnut "magnitudine tres quatuorve castaneas xquat". The plant is cultivated in stagnant waters throughout China." (Martini, Novus atlas sinensis, 1655). "It has small leaves that float on the surface of the water, at the end of very long stems. The numerous fruits remain hidden underwater. Cultivated on a large scale in the lakes and rivers of northern China." (Roxburgh, Flora indica). Robert Fortune reports that he saw Ling-Kio fruit being harvested: "Having returned for some time to Shanghal, I resolved to penetrate, if possible, into the district of Kwey-chow-Foo. Going up the river in a south-westerly direction, I arrived, shortly after passing Kea-Hing-Fo, a city of some 270,000 inhabitants, at an immense pond which, I presume, communicates with the famous Taï-Ko. The water was very shallow and covered with Trapa bicornis, which the Chinese call Ling, and whose fruit, of rather bizarre shape, as is well known, resembling an ox's head armed with its two horns, is highly prized in China. I observed three very distinct varieties, one of which produces fruit of a beautiful red color. "A large number of women and children, in small circular boats much like our laundry tubs, were busy fishing the Ling. In fact, nothing could be more suitable for this kind of work than these singular boats, which, large enough to hold the fisherman and all the catch, steer gently through all the plants without breaking them. The sight of this immense quantity of individuals thus navigating this marsh, each in his own vat, formed for me a most entertaining sight." The Trapa bicornis," says another traveller, M. Marchal, from Lunéville (1), "is highly esteemed in China. It forms the food of populations where the rice harvest is insufficient. Harvesting them is reminiscent of the grape harvest in France. Ling is sown at the end of autumn, in those parts of ponds where the water is shallow and clear, and in those places most exposed to the south. (1) See De l'amidon du Marron d'Inde, Ad. Thibierge et Dr Remilly, 1857. The Chinese claim that this crop absorbs the putrid emanations rising from the surface of stagnant water. If the harvest is very abundant, Trapa is fed to barnyard birds, which quickly fatten and acquire an exquisite taste. A hundred years have passed since Abbé Grosier, whom we quote below on the subject of the Pitsi, Heleocharis tuberosa, to which, like Duncan, he gave the name of Water Chestnut, recommended the cultivation of Ling-Kio (Trapa bicornis). We have and we neglect," he said, "in some provinces of France, a species of water chestnut that the Latins have named Tribulus. The missionaries believe that this plant could be the one the Chinese call Ling-Kio, from which they derive great benefit; if this identity were confirmed, it would be easy to extend its cultivation everywhere, which would offer a new resource in times of shortage: "1° This other water chestnut, Ling-Kio, is a refreshing fruit, pleasant in summer. When it is green, it is sold in Peking, on the markets, like hazelnuts in Europe; " 2° Dried and ground into flour, it makes a very good porridge, especially when a little wheat flour is added; you can even mix a third of it into the flour used to make bread; " 3° Baked in the oven, candied with sugar or honey, it becomes a healthy and pleasant food; " 4° It provides a suitable food for geese, ducks and other farmyard birds. "The cultivation of Ling-Kio requires no care. The plant reproduces itself wherever it is found. When you want to throw it into a pond or stream, throw the seed in late autumn into the part of the pond where the water is shallow. Choose a spot where the water is clear and the shore faces south. The more heat the Ling-Kio receives, the healthier and tastier it is, and the more fruit it produces. TWO-PINTED MACKER Singhara. TRAPA BISPINOSA Roxb, Pl. of the Coast of Coromandel, pl. 234. This species differs from the previous one in having a fruit with straight, sharp-pointed horns and toothed leaves. It grows in central and southern Asia, where it is Fig. 46. - Trapa bispinosa. called Singhara, as far as Ceylon and Japan; it is also found in southern Africa, as far as Zambezia. Its fruits are often converted into starch, and can be used to make excellent-tasting cakes and soups, or stored and preserved as food. Production is abundant and economical, as the plant reseeds itself spontaneously. It lives for several years. In some regions, such as Kashmir, Trapa fruit is an important food source for the local population. This species probably includes Trapa cochinchinensis Lour. and Trapa incisa Sieb. et Zucc. M. L. Bouley, director of the gardens of the Maharadja of Kashmir, in Srinagar, published the following interesting note in the Revue horticole (March 16, 1884): "Trapa natans and bicornis (1) are so abundant in the lakes of Kashmir, and grow there with such vigor, that the parts where these plants are found look more like immense meadows than lakes; it is therefore impossible to navigate there, even with the frailest boat. "The products of these two plants are considerable; in autumn, the harvest occupies thousands of people. "Before being delivered for consumption, the chestnuts are crushed to remove their woody, pungent husks, and then ground. The coarse flour obtained is eaten as porridge. "This flour is sold very cheaply, which can be explained by the absence of cultivation costs, and its consumption throughout the winter is enormous. Every day, large ships loaded with Trapa fruit arrive in Srinagar. Mr. Ermens, Mr. Bouley's predecessor in the position of (1) This must be Trapa bispinosa. the Maharadja's Director of Cultivation and a long-time resident of Kashmir, has kindly provided us with the following additional details on Singhara cultivation: "After a great famine, the governor of the district in which the Woorlake is located introduced Trapa cultivation. This lake, which measures at least 2,000 hectares, is so full of it that navigation is impossible. The fruit is harvested by the boatload, and the governor profits greatly from it, as each grower pays him a royalty. It is widely consumed, and in the recent famines, this harvest saved the lives of a large number of inhabitants. "Fresh Trapa fruit is eaten like walnuts, cooked in water; it is a common food. It is very indigestible, and the Hindus, to obviate this inconvenience, place on their stomachs a heater called a kangreiï, which, according to the country's doctors, facilitates digestion; hence it follows that their stomachs are blackened and as if smoked by this process." The preceding notes were published by us, in 1888, in the Bulletin de la Société nationale d'Acclimatation; shortly afterwards, Mr. P.-L. Simmonds, F. L. S., whose name is known to all those concerned with applied botany, sent us a copy of the Journal of the Society of the Arts, issue of August 31, 1888, containing an article signed by him and entitled: Edible aquatic plants. In it, we found some details that we think it would be useful to reproduce: "In the last issue of the Bulletin de la Société d'Acclimatation de Paris, an article appeared on aquatic food plants, which deserves attention for the novelty of the information it provides, and to which, while taking one or two passages, I can add a few additional details that will be of interest. "It is said that Trapa natans formed a large part of the diet of the ancient Thracians, just as Trapa bispinosa does for the inhabitants of Kashmir and T. bicornis for the Chinese. Dr. Royle says that the former produced up to 12,000 pounds a year in revenue to the Runje Singh government, the tax being levied on 96,000 to 128, 000 donkey loads, from the great lake of Oaller..... " In Gugerat, its fruits form an important food item. On feast days, it is made into a red tincture, which is mixed with a yellow color derived from the flowers of the Butea frondosa. "Colonel Sleeman gave the following interesting account of Trapa bispinosa in his Travels in the South-West Provinces: "Here, as in most other parts of India, the reservoirs are spoiled by the Water Chestnut (Singhara), which is everywhere planted and cultivated in water-covered fields of great extent, as regularly as Wheat or Barley is on dry plains. It is cultivated by a class of men called "Dheemurs", who are everywhere fishermen and palanquin bearers. They have boats to plant, weed and harvest Singhara. The tenures of each cultivator are carefully indicated by long Bamboos driven into the water, and they pay so much per acre for the part they cultivate. "The long stems of the plant reach the surface of the water, over which their green leaves float, and their pure white flowers bloom pleasantly in their midst in the latter part of the afternoon. The nuts grow underwater, after the flowers have wilted. They are triangular in shape and covered with a coarse, brown tegument, adhering strongly to the kernel, which is white, edible and of a fine, tight texture. "These nuts are highly prized. They are carried to market on the backs of oxen, often 200 or 300 miles away. They ripen at the end of the September rains and can be eaten until the end of November. "The rent paid for an ordinary tank is about 100 rupees (L. 10) a year. I have known of a very large one for which 200 rupees and even 300 (L. 30) a year were paid; but the silt grows so rapidly by cultivation, that it promptly destroys any tank in which it is allowed; and, where it is thought desirable to conserve the tank for use of the water, it must be expressly prohibited." Necklaces made from Singhara nuts are sold in Benares and considered sacred. "A few years ago, Captain J.-P. Pogson of Simla advocated more extensive cultivation and use of this plant: "Having the example of Kashmir before our eyes," he says, "it is very singular that the much-vaunted reservoirs, the great lakes and the inland sea of fresh water of the Madras Presidency, and also the immense 'Vheels' of the north-west and west provinces, are not regularly employed in a more profitable manner by the cultivation of Singhara. The dried nuts would keep, I believe, for many years, and would still be sold in England as Arrow-root or Singhara starch, for food or industrial uses. Any old Singhara store could be processed and sold in this way. "Like Wheat grain, Singhara kernels can sustain human life for a definite time and are pleasant to the taste, seasoned with salt and pepper, or in soup (porridge) with sugar, "ghoor or jaggery" (1). If the almonds are broken into small pieces, they can be ground into flour using a hand mill, and the product kneaded into a dough for making small "chuppaties" or cakes. "By soaking almonds overnight in cold water, they can be boiled the following morning to make food. Steaming them might be just as good, maybe better, but I've only tried soaked almonds boiled. A few years ago, I ate a "Hulwa" made with Singhara flour, and it was more pleasant than the one made with wheat flour flowers. "The Madras Presidency boasts some ancient and magnificent hydraulic works: the Viranum reservoir (2), 35 square miles in extent, with 12 miles of dikes; the Cauverypank reservoir, whose 4-mile dikes are consolidated with stones throughout their length; the Chembrumhankam reservoir, similar to a picturesque inland sea, a work whose execution dates back to unknown times. The first of these artificial lakes has a surface area of 22,400 acres, and the third, compared to an inland sea, is ten times as large. (1) "Ghoor" is the sugar residue of India; "jaggery" is the sugar residue of China. (2) The English word tank can only be translated by reservoir, which however is not applicable, in French, to ponds, lakes, inland seas, i.e. the vast bodies of water referred to here. However, we know that in Kashmir 30,000 human creatures feed on the Singhara nut for five months of the year, and if the picturesque inland sea we're talking about deserves its name, its bed and water surface could be put to profitable use by Singhara cultivation. The 35-square-mile reservoir could become a first-class nursery to supply Madras with nuts for seed. "The yield of the Water Nut, per acre, is, as yet, unknown; but, assuming the harvest of 4 quarters (480 pounds) each of walnut kernels per acre, the 22,400 acres would yield 43,800,000 pounds of food, and, reckoning 60 pounds per adult per month, the above quantity would suffice to feed 716, 800 people for one month, or 143,360 for five months. "The cost of this abundant and permanent food supply would be insignificant. There are 4,000 square yards in an acre, and by allowing one nut of seed per yard, the price of nuts and rail transport could easily be calculated. The first year's harvest would more than cover the costs, and we would then have a harvest in perpetuity that would only have to cover the costs of supervision, care and harvesting. "As the harvest belongs to the State, a considerable income would be obtained from the sale of the nuts, even if the almonds were only sold at half the price of rice or another grain. "In good years, the farming population would make ample use of this nourishing and economical food, and sell and export their harvests of Rice, etc., and in bad years of famine, the walnut harvest would be a resource; finally, their presence would have a great influence in preventing the artificial production of local famine. "Walnuts, once sown, resow themselves, so that the square yard could soon produce nine plants or more. The inland sea, when covered with these nuts, will be a source of wealth for the state and profit for the public. As the annual, spontaneous or artificial exlension of the crop is a very simple thing, the government of Madras would in a few years find itself the owner of a Singhara plantation of great value." Captain Pogson, writing from Simla, goes on to say: "The Singhara Nut of India and Europe being a perennial aquatic plant, the old roots bear fruit annually. If they grow in water 2 feet deep, we enter it to collect them, going from one to the other, and deposit them in a floating container, such as, for example, a 5-gallon barrel. If the water is deeper, we'll have to use any raft or canoe and pick the nuts." "In this country (India), they are gathered several times during the season, and the last ones to be harvested, i.e. those that are the largest and have hard kernels, are eaten boiled. Others are peeled, dried and ground into flour, while others are buried whole, to be used as seed the following year. For example, the pond or depression in the ground which has provided a crop of walnuts may dry out in hot weather; at the start of the rains, this place will become a pond again; the walnuts for seed will then be dug up and sown 12 inches apart in the still slightly deep water, placing each walnut about 2 inches deep in the submerged soil, and sowing is continued in this way every day, as the waters rise. The nuts will germinate in due course and provide the year's harvest. "Naturally, if the water didn't dry up, the old roots would live and, within the season, give a new shoot of stems that would bear fruit as usual. It follows that, when a perpetual harvest is desired, the nuts should be sown in the bed of a shallow, freshwater pond that never dries out." MACRE VERBANAISE (1) Lagana. TRAPA NATANS L., var. verbanensis Jaggi. Under the name Trapa verbanensis, De Notaris described a plant that grows in Lake Maggiore and whose fruit has only two obtuse, unbarbed horns. Mr. Jaggi, in a note he has published on Macres (2), makes it very clear that this is simply a variety of Trapa natans with aborted median horns. (1) We presented the fruits of this species at one of the meetings of the Société botanique de France (Bull. Soc. Bot., 1888), in comparison with fruits of Trapa natans with only three horns. 6) Die Vassernus (Trana notms L.) und der Tribadues der Allen Zurich, 1883. We can't reproduce here all the reasons he gives in support of his thesis, but one of the most convincing is certainly the fact that in the same lake, fruit with two or three horns can be found mixed with the four-horned Trapa natans. All gradations of abortion are thus to be found. Fig. 47 - Trapa verbanensis. Professor Gibelli of Bologna, who transplanted T. verbanensis to Modena, obtained fruit with two or three horns. Leysser, Mertens and Koch, in their flora of Halle; Boehling, in his flora of Germany, say that Trapa natans sometimes has two-horned fruits. They did not think there was any reason to create a species of this variety. The fact that the horns are obtuse is also of little importance, as we sometimes find Macres with four horns that are more or less rounded at the top. Cultivation trials carried out in Modena prove that the Fig. 48. - Trapa verbanensis. Leaf seen from below, and young fruit, natural size. Trapa verbanensis reproduces at least for some time. Here is the information we have gathered on this variety. We are indebted to Mr. Rovelli, President of the Verban Horticultural Society of Pallanza; to Mr. D. Lampertico, Secretary of the Vicenza Agricultural Show, who was kind enough to consult Professor Saccardo, of the University of Padua, and to Dr. Mattirolo, of the University of Turin. Trapa verbanensis is known as Lagana on Lake Maggiore and Lake Varese, and is also commonly known as Castagna del lago and Castagna d'acqua. It is found in large quantities in the Bay of Angera, Lake Maggiore, where the bottom is visible. It vegetates admirably without any care in botanical jardius; it is a little less productive than Trapa natans. Its fruit ripens at the end of October, a time when it is easy to obtain. They are eaten either green, like hazelnuts, or cooked, requiring a fairly long cooking time. They are not much sought-after, and are rarely seen on the market. The same fruit is used to make rosaries, which are frequently sold in Arona, Varese and other towns. We immersed some rooted Verbanese mackerel plants in a pond, as a gift from Mr. Latour-Marliac of Temple-sur-Lot. They vegetated well and gave us a few green fruits on September 15, the time when M. Latour-Marliac obtained his first green fruit. Latour-Marliac, for his part, obtained a certain quantity of ripe fruit. Trapa verbanensis has larger leaves than T. natans, which is well known in our western regions. Its petioles are red; the variety is more beautiful than the type. There is another variety of Trapa natans that Areschoug has named T. natans, var conocarpa (1). It originates from Lake Immeln, in Shonen, Sweden, and differs from the type in the way the horns are inserted, which are not very coherent, the lateral ones detaching from the fruit at mid-height, the median ones at the lower quarter: the fruit is therefore naked over a large part of its height. MANGARIDE Taye, Tayove, Chou Caraibe. Mangareto (Brazil). - Toya, Ouaheu (Caribbean). - Tayo (Arouage). - Tayoba (Brazilian Indian). - Toloman (West Indies). - Qualusa (Peru). XANTHOSOMA SAGITTIFOLIUM Schott. Bot. Mag. tab. 4989; Engler, Flora brasiliensis, p. 191; Arum sagittifolium L.; Descourtilz, Flore des Antilles, 8, t. 553; Arum xanthorrhizon Jacq, Hort. Vindob, 11, 73, tab. 157; Hortus Schœnbr, 11, 32, tab. 188; Caladium xanthorrhizon Willd; Caladium sagittæfolium Vent; Caladium utile Hort; Yanthosoma utile C. Koch et Bouché; Yanthosoma xanthorrhizon C. Koch (Engler, Monographiæ phanerogamarum, vol. II, Araceæ, p. 469). Aroidae family. At the request of the Société nationale d'Acclimatation, M. D. Albuquerque has kindly sent us (1) Mémoires de l'Académie royale des Sciences de Stoekholm, 1874. tubers of Mangareto branco, with a promise to research the violet variety for our use. Vigneron-Jousserandier cites Mangaride as being cultivated in Brazil and yielding from twenty to thirty small tubers, and V.-L. Baril, comte de Lahure, in a work entitled L'Empire du Brésil, has this to say about it: "The Aroidea called Mangareto branco grows in small clumps. The main tuber is about the size of an apple. It is surrounded by several other small tubers as big as Walnuts, and often smaller. It is these small tubers that are edible. They have a pleasant taste and are floury, though less so than potatoes. Mangareto branco is one of the varieties of Xanthosoma sagittifolium, an aroidea which, in tropical America, plays the same role, but to a much lesser degree, as Taros (Colocasia) in India, Egypt, and mainly in Oceania. In warmer regions, the plant is easy to grow, but not very productive; it has been cultivated by the indigenous Indians from time immemorial. In the Journal de la Société nationale d'Horticulture, 1871, pages 506-514, Dr. P. Sagot published a note on Tayes or Tayoves in French Guiana, from which we extract the following: "The American species (Xanthosoma) are distinguished by their milky juice and by the fact that the petiole is inserted at the edge of the leaf and not inside the leaf blade, from below the leaf, at a certain distance from the lower edge. "In the Asian and Oceanian species (Colocasia antiquorum and C. esculenta), Kouchou (India), Kulcas (Egypt), Alcolcalz (southern Spain), Tallus (Java), Tallo, Taro (Oceania), the juice is limpid and the leaves are pellate, i.e. borne by a petiole that is inserted from below, inside the leaf blade. "The varieties grown in French Guiana are : Taye banana, Taye white, Taye violet, Touca, Grande Tayove. "There may be other races in French Guiana, which I have not seen; but, in general, I think I can say that Tayes are inferior, in abundance and quality, to the products of the Taros. "The tubers, of good quality, boiled and cooked, are healthy and pleasant. They are considered more nutritious than other floury roots. The most delicate are those that are young and have grown well hidden underground: for example, the lateral tubers of the rhizome, which form rounded outgrowths around the main root. "The leaves, chosen young and tender, blanched and cooked in boiling water, are good to eat, but have little taste. A few are often added to various dishes frequently used in the colonies. I confess, however, that this way of using them seems to me only a very incomplete justification for the pretentious name of Caribbean cabbage, which has been given to these plants". In the first days of April, we planted the Mangareto branco tubers sent by Mr. Albuquerque, on a bed and under glass. The plants vegetated vigorously, were abundantly watered throughout the season, which was quite warm, and were only uprooted on October 6, after a heavy white frost. We only found a small number of tubers, as big as peas. Cultivation of the American Aroidea is therefore impossible in the Paris climate, even with the help of layers and frames. We were similarly unsuccessful with all the Taros from New Caledonia and India, of which we had received numerous varieties, differing in quality, tuber shape, fertility and earliness, some requiring flooded soils, others being able to be planted in dry soil. However, it seems that the tubers harvested would have reached their full development under the influence of one more month of heat. MARANTA ARUNDINACÉ (HERBE A LA FLÈCHE) MARANTA ARUNDINACEA L., Redouté, Liliac, I, tab. 57; Bot. Mag., tab. 2307; Maranta indica Tussac, Flore des Antilles, I, tab. 26. Scitaminaceae family. Stump producing underground, fleshy, cylindrical, scaly suckers, about 30 cm long and 2 to 3 cm in diameter. Stems about 1 metre high. Leaves glabrous, oval-lanceolate, sheathing petiole. Loose panicle; white flowers. According to Tussac, this species was introduced from India to the West Indies, where it is widely cultivated as a food plant. The starch known as Arrow-root is extracted from its fleshy suckers. These suckers can also be eaten plain, either boiled or prepared in a variety of ways. Arrow-root starch from Maranta arundinacea is also known as A. de la Jamaïgue, A. des Antilles, A. des Bermudes, A. de Saint-Vincent. This starchy product comes to us mainly from America; it is also widely cultivated in India and in certain parts of Africa, notably on Bourbon Island or Reunion Island. Its name derives from its use by the natives to heal arrow wounds. M. arundinacea is the Toulola of the Caribbean. It is grown in the West Indies in Jamaica, in South America where it is called Anvert, in Guadeloupe, Reunion, Guyana and India. The starch contained in its rhizome is known as West Indian Arrowroot. This starch exists there in the proportion of 22 0/0. There are two varieties of Maranta in Brazil and Jamaica: one called White Anvert and the other Red Anvert. In Brazil, the elongated-root variety is more highly regarded in the province of Para than the rounded-rhizome variety. In French Guiana, the Caribs eat the root after cooking it under ashes. The rhizomes are acrid and rubefacient, but cooking makes them edible. Marantas require deep soil that is always cool but not damp. They can be propagated using their creeping, perennial rhizomes. Sections should be planted on divided, stony ground. They should be spaced 1 m to 1.20 m apart. During their growth, these plants only require hoeing, and their rhizomes are removed at the end of the first or second year. An average hectare yields 500 to 600 kilograms of arrowroot and 1,500 to 2,000 kilograms of couac or pulpy residue. Arrowroot is a light, nutritious food. It is especially suitable for delicate people and children. In Cuba, Maranta starch is used to make excellent creams that keep for a long time in bottles. In England, large quantities of arrowroot are imported every year from Ceylon, Hindustan, the West Indies, Bermuda, Nicaragua and Melanesia. Couac, or coarse flour, is used to feed domestic animals. In Botanical Magazine, loc. cit. Maranta arundinacea is a plant of considerable interest, furnishing for the table a distinguished food, sold under the name of Arrow-root of India, extracted from its roots, not as has been said by drying and grinding them, but by macerating them in water, in the same manner as starch is extracted from Wheat, Potato and other starchy substances. "In recent years, Maranta starch has been imported in large quantities from the West Indies to England, and recommended as a food for young children, and as light nourishment for the sick. "The plant is native to South America and is grown in all the islands of the West Indies. Here, it must be kept in greenhouses, where in July and August it produces its flowers, which fade very quickly." Introduced by Dr. William Houston, before 1732. We have tried unsuccessfully to grow Maranta arundinacea. MARGOSE A PIQUANTS Pandipane, Gatole, Caïqua du Pérou, Momordique à feuilles de Vigue. Momordique Papareh, Papareh Herya, Melon de San-Gaëtano. Karela (Hindostani), Karala (Bengali), Khu qua (China). MOMORDICA CHARANTIA L., Spec. ed. 1, p. 1009. DC, Prodr. III, p. 331; Naud, Ann. se. nat. ser. 4, vol. XII, p. 131; Revue hort. 1859, p. 620, figs. 134 and 135; Flore des serres, X, p. 193, tab. 1047; The Garden, XII, p. 161; Cogniaux, CUCURBIT, Monogr. Phanerogam, vol. III, p. 436. Synonyms: M. Balsamina Descourt (non Linné), Fl. des Antilles, III, p. 62, tab. 164; M. operculata Vell, Fl. flum. X, tab. 980 (non Linné); Momordica jagorana C. Koch, Belgique horticole, XI, p. 92. Family Cucurbitaceae. Plant native to Asia and Africa, naturalized in tropical regions of both worlds; annual, with spindly, climbing stems, up to 2 meters high, twining, hairy, with usually simple tendrils. Leaves alternate, deeply 5-7-lobed, dark green above, pale green below, sometimes almost glabrous except at the veins, more often pubescent on both sides; lobes oval, acute, toothed. Flowers yellowish, monoecious, borne on equally long, deflexed peduncles; male flower peduncles bearing a heart-shaped, entire bract inserted towards their middle or at the base; female flower peduncles hanging after anthesis. Male flowers grouped or solitary, larger than females; calyx with five divisions half as short as those of the corolla. Female flowers solitary, with slightly shorter pedicel, quite large, pale yellow. Fruit pendant, oblong, 10 to 15 cm long, thinned at both ends. Fig. 49 - Spiked margosa (Momordica Charantia). ends, obscurely trigonal, covered with tubers arranged in regular lines or ridges between which 25 are other lines of smaller teeth of unequal size. This fruit, which is green at first, Fig.150. - Fruit of the prickly Margose. becomes a beautiful orange-yellow when ripe. Its thick, fleshy rind is torn into three irregular shreds, to which the seeds adhere, enveloped in a pulpy tunic of a brilliant sanguine hue. The seeds are flat, scalloped at both ends and covered on both sides with a perfectly chinoiserie relief pattern. It is this structure of the seed, which appears to be gnawed and bitten, that has given the genus the name Momordica. PETITE MARGOSE A PIQUANTS MOMORDICA CHARANTIA L., var. abbreviata Ser. in DC, Prodr. III, p. 311; M. zeylanica Miller, Dict. des jardiniers, n° 3; M. senegalensis Lamk, Encyclop. méth. IV, p. 239; M. muricata Willd, Spec. IV, p. 602; Belgique hort, XI, p. 93; M. Roxburghiana Don, Gen. syst. gard. III, p. 35; M. Charantia, var. minor Naudin, Ann. sc. nat.; M. Charantia, var. muricata Griseb, Fl. Brit. W. ind. isl. p. 287. This plant is a simple variety of the preceding one, from which it differs only in that it is a little smaller in all its parts. It is native to the same regions. M. Charantia is a vegetable plant cultivated under various names in China, Cochinchina, Malabar, Peru, Mauritius, Reunion, etc. It is known in Reunion under the name M. Charantia. It is known in Réunion under the name of Margose, and is mainly cultivated there in the small-volume variety named by M. Naudin M. Charuntia, var. minor. It is grown in the same way as the Melon de châssis, except for pruning; however, when a large number of fruits have formed, the stems should be pinched, part of the leaves cut off to aerate the plant, and watering should be moderated to avoid rotting. Margose vegetation is very active, and fruiting, right up to the end of the season, is all the more abundant as the fruit is never left to ripen and is used green. In a pinch, after having raised the seeds in a frame, the plant can be placed in the open air, around June 1st, near a wall facing south; but the fruit obtained is often short and misshapen. We cannot recommend outdoor cultivation in the Paris climate. M. Charantia is sometimes grown in France as a curious plant. Its ripe fruits are a beautiful yellow-orange, open on their own and reveal rows of seeds wrapped in a very appetizing cherry-red pulp. This is undoubtedly a wonderful dessert dish, and the seeds are happily sucked; unfortunately, the pulp that covers them is sparse and tasteless. The use made of Margoses in warmer countries is quite different and much more interesting. We do not know how they are prepared for the table in China, Peru, etc.; but we owe to the kindness of Mr. Édouard Wilmann, member of the Société des Botanistes français, precise information on their use in Réunion: "This fruit, which reaches an average length of Om, 07 to Om, 10, is always picked and used before maturity. Once ripe, it is worthless for culinary purposes. Some like it very tender, others prefer it when it has already reached a certain consistency; it is, in any case, excellent until ripe. "For the table, it is prepared: "As achards: Simply remove the seeds and place the fruit, split in half lengthwise, in the sauce prepared for this purpose: vinegar, olive oil, ginger, garlic, chili pepper, salt, etc. It is usually added: cucumber, garlic, garlic, salt, etc. Cucumbers, cabbages, carrots, mangoes, etc. are usually added; "In rougaille: the fruit is cut into rougaille pieces, always after removing the seeds, and seasoned with lemon juice, chili pepper, ginger, etc.; "In curry: the fruit is cooked, halved, with fresh or salted bacon, fresh or salted fish; with or without curry powder, but with the ordinary condiments of Creole cuisine. In India, the fruit is eaten sliced and fried, but must first be cooked in water to remove all its bitterness. We have to say that we have tried unsuccessfully to introduce the fruit of the little prickly Margose into our Pickles. Their extreme bitterness makes them unsuitable for this use. MARGOSE POMME DE MERVEILLE MOMORDICA BALSAMINA Lin, Spec. ed. 1, p. 1009; Lamk, Encycl. meth. IV, p. 237, tab. 794, fig. 1; Dict, se. nat. tab. 208, 209; Revue hort. 1857, p. 180, fig. 75; Naudin, in Ann. sc. nat. ser. 4, vol. XII, p. 132; ser. 5, vol. V, p. 20; Cogniaux, CUCURB., Monographiæ Phanerog., vol. III, p. 237. M. vulgaris Tournef, Inst. p. 103, tab. 29; Cucumis agrestis Miller, Dict. des jardiniers; Neurosperma cuspidata Raf, Journ. phys. 1819, p. 101; Momordica Charantia Descourt, non L., Fl. des Antilles, VIII, p. 335, tab. 594. Cucurbitaceae family. This species differs from M. Charantia in its leaves, which are glabrous and glossy green, and in the bracts of the male flowers, which are toothed and inserted at the top of the peduncles. The whole plant is smaller in all its parts. The fruit is oval-rounded and never larger than a pigeon's egg. "Momordica fruits have balsamic and vulnerary properties, which earned them a reputation all the greater for coming from India, the land of jugglers and miracles. "They are, strictly speaking, edible like cucumbers". (Dic. sc. nat., t. V, p. 387.). We have no information on the use of Pomme de merveille as a vegetable. It could undoubtedly be used for the same purposes as other Margoses. Momordica dioica Roxb. known in India as Gol Kandra, is a perennial species with a tuberous stem. The young fruit is eaten by Hindus. The root is also edible, but that of the female plant is larger than that of the male. MATAMBALA PLECTRANTHUS TERNATUS Sims; Botanical Magazine, tab. 2460; Bojer, Hort, maurit. p. 253; DC. Prodr. XII, p. 65. Labiatae family. On July 15, 1884, our correspondent in the Transvaal, Mr. Mingard, wrote to us: "With this letter, I am sending you some tubers of the Potato fade or wild, called by the Magwamba: Matambala. It has the same cultivation and uses as the ordinary potato. The natives appreciate it very much and prefer it to any other tuber. They keep the seeds (1) in the sand, or hanging in their huts. They plant it at the same time as corn. On October 20 1884, Mr. Mingard wrote again: "I'm sending a small bag of Matambala to the post office. These will be the last ones I can send you. The natives planted them a fortnight ago. It likes light soils, has the same vegetation period as the Potato, but the stems are noticeably different; they widen as soon as they are born. (1) Read tubers. The flower is small, yellow or blue (1). A tuber planted in its entirety yields from ten to twenty to one. Matambala is prepared like potatoes. No flavor. "I delivered the letter and package to the post office on October 29th. The tubers are starting to sprout. According to your advice, I chose the ripest and hardest. However, the tubers from this last shipment rotted on the way; but, fortunately, those sent to us the previous July preserved well in the sand, and we were able to plant four of them on a bed and under glass, on the following March 15th. Three plants thrived admirably; only one died at the end of June. We pulled it out on July 1, and found, to our great surprise, a dozen small tubers forming a tight cluster immediately below the plant's collar. These tubers appeared to be ripe and preserved well in the sand. The other three plants vegetated vigorously, spreading out their numerous stems on the ground, which marcelled spontaneously, and filled the trunk in which they were planted; but, when we pulled them out in autumn, they gave us only an almost insignificant harvest. The plant recovers very easily from cuttings, which has enabled us to obtain a large number of plants that have been distributed to the Muséum and to various per(1) M. Mingard has certainly confused the flowers of several species. In fact, on the east coast of Africa, there are several Coleus and Plectranthus with edible tubers, some with blue flowers, others with yellow. The one we collected had blue flowers. sonnes. They flowered the following winter in the greenhouses of the Muséum and the Jardin Bolanique of the École Vétérinaire d'Alfort. Dr Sagot, in his Manuel des cultures tropicales, p. 84, says he saw Matambala in Mr Paillieux's garden at Crosnes, but only in leaf. It was after examining a flowering specimen, which we showed him the year after his visit to Crosnes, that he applied the name Solenostemon den siflorus Benth. to it, a name written in Bentham's own hand on the label accompanying a specimen kept in the Muséum herbarium, and which he considered to be comparable in every way to our plant. The Museum specimen appeared to us to be merely a compact inflorescence form of Coleus tuberosus, the name by which we referred to Matambala in the second edition of Potager d'un Curieux, and which we now rectify after comparing our plant with live specimens of Plectranthus ternatus grown in the Museum's glasshouses and the figure of this plant published in Botanical Magazine, plate 2460. Plectranthus ternatus is native to the Comoros Islands and Madagascar, from where it was introduced to Mauritius and probably to other countries, since Mr. Mingard sent it to us from the Transvaal. Mr. Daruty de Grandpré, President of the Mauritius Acclimatization Society, wrote to us from Port-Louis: "I read with great interest your notes in the Potager d'un Curieux. I did not see any mention of a plant in which I am interested because of its edible qualities. I'm referring to Plectranethus madagascariensis, known here as Oumime (1). "Oumime produces a tuber that we eat in exactly the same way as potatoes, although it has a distinctive taste that may not appeal at first to European palates. However, I'm sure that it would be easy, through cultivation, to completely remove this particular taste, at the same time as increasing the volume of the tubers. "I'm mailing you some Oumime tubers, so that you can try growing them in France. In his Histoire de la grande Isle de Madagascar, De Flacourt speaks of Houmimes or Voamitza: "These are," he says, "small roots as big as octopus, which multiply extremely, for from one plant more than two hundred will grow". Matambala requires too much heat for it to be cultivated profitably in our climate. But if our kind correspondent's shipment has not produced any useful results for France proper, it has provided our colony of Gabon-Congo with an excellent and much-appreciated vegetable, which will spread from there to all regions with a similar climate. (1) Mr. N.-E. Brown, author of a note entitled: Tuberous Labiatæ, published in the Kew Bulletin, January 1894 issue, establishes that the Oumime is not the Plectranthus madagascariensis, but the P. ternatus Sims. The name applied by Mr. Daruty de Grandpré refers to the Oumime bá̂tard or wild Oumime, which does not produce tubers. The tubers we had donated to the Muséum were multiplied, and two of them were given by Professor Max Cornu to Mr. Pierre, who was leaving to take up the post of Director of the Colonial Garden in Libreville (Gabon-Congo). Mr. Pierre cultivated Matambala, and was so successful that he actively propagated the plant in the colony. He gave some tubers to Mr. Thollou, attached to the Brazza mission, who transported them to Brazzaville, where they multiplied and spread rapidly. During a trip to Paris, Mr. Thollon gave us the following information on our Labiatae: "Matambala grows extremely vigorously during the rainy season. During the dry season (June to October), the plant vegetates, but needs watering to produce tubers. It is during this period that the flowers appear. "Harvesting takes place from December to January. Each clump produces a dozen tubers the size of walnuts, and many smaller ones. "To date, the plant has not been subjected to rational cultivation.
