We are content to detach twigs which, when placed in the ground, take root with the greatest of ease and which, left to their own devices, without any care, nevertheless give the result indicated above." Mr. Thollon considers the Matambala to be one of the most useful vegetables to propagate in our equatorial African possessions; it would take the place of the potato, whose cultivation is impracticable, the yield being almost nil. He fed it to a large number of travelers, who were extremely pleased, as its flavor is so similar to that of the precious Solanée, that they thought they were back to their favorite vegetable, the absence of which in tropical regions is one of the harshest hardships for Europeans. Finally, here's some new information that Mr. Pierre sent to one of us: "Back from a trip to the interior and south of Loango, I've just found your letter, which has followed me here. "It was in 1887 that I brought to the colony two Matambala tubers, as big as large peas, which Mr. Cornu had entrusted to me. I gave one to Mr. Thollon, who transported it to the interior of the Ogoué, in Brazzaville. There are now some in the Oubanghi at latitude 6° north and longitude 17° east. The plant has spread in these countries with extraordinary rapidity. "The Crampel mission, which is said to have found some around 4° north latitude, has eaten the tubers and may import it as far as Lake Chad. When he left Gabon, I gave Mr. Crampel some Basella alba seeds, a plant that will also be imported into central Africa. "Matambala also exists in Loango, where Mgr Carrie introduced it from Brazzaville. "The plant has improved in the interior. We've obtained tubers as big as Walnuts, and I'm certain that, if cultivation were better done, we'd obtain even better results. Plant in well-prepared, not too dry, moist soil, and above all leave more distance between plants. "The taste of the tuber is very similar to that of the potato. It's a plant of the future, especially in the interior of our colony. In Libreville, where we are 5° further north, I have obtained only mediocre results. "The plant is known throughout the interior and on the coast by the name Pomme de terre de Madagascar, which must have been given to it by M. Thollon. As this name is inappropriate, I am doing my utmost to combat it. The existence of several Labiatae with edible tubers is now known to us, namely : CROSNE (Stachys affinis); Marambala or Oumime (Piectranthus ternatus Sims); COLEUS TUBEROSUS Benth. to which Mr. N.-E. Brown (1) links C. parviflorus Benth. (DC. Prodr. XII, p. 72; Hook. fil. Flora of Brit. India, IV. p. 625), a species whose origin is somewhat uncertain. This plant is cultivated in Java, Amboine, other islands of the Malay Archipelago and Ceylon, where, according to a note by Gardner, of the Kew Herbarium, it is "cultivated by the natives like Potatoes". Thwaites also says that the Sinhalese cultivate it for its tubers, which are eaten as a vegetable. Rumphius speaks of this plant at some length; he says that it has recently been introduced from Java and Baly to Amboine, and that the tubers are eaten (1) N.-E. Brown, Tuberous Labiatæ, Bulletin of miscellaneous information. Royal Gardens Kewo, 1894, p. 10. cules boiled or roasted. He adds that it was probably introduced to the island from the Malay Peninsula. On the other hand, he says that the vulgar name used by the Portuguese for this plant is Gotte Keligam or Gotte Kelim, probably because it is widely cultivated on the Coromandel coast, whose inhabitants are called Kelin or Quellin. Neither Roxburgh, nor Wight and Arnot, however, mention this plant in their works, although there are specimens in the Kew herbarium, collected by Wight and Quilon (number 2105, Wight, Kew distribution), but the labels do not say whether it was wild or cullivorous. Dr. Treub, director of the Botanical Garden at Buitenzorg (Java), was kind enough to send us tubers of this species. Unfortunately, we were unable to obtain the flowering of this interesting plant, which has once again disappeared from our cultivations; COLEUS EDULIS Vatke (Linnæa XXXVII, p. 319), which Richard had designated as C. tuberosus (Flore d'Abyssinie, 11, p. 185) but which is distinct from the one to which Bentham had already given this name. C. edulis is the Daunech of the Abyssinians; according to Richard (1). it grows in mountainous areas, near the village of Kouaieta, in the provinces of Chiré and Godjam. Its fleshy tubers are quite similar to those of the potato, not only in shape but also in flavor. This plant is abundantly cultivated in the many gardens surrounding Kouaieta, situated at an altitude of 6,000 to (1) Paillieux et Bois, Revue des Sciences Naturelles appliqués (Bulletin de la Soc. nat d'Acclimatation, 20 aout 1890): De quelques plantes alimentaires de l'Abyssinie. 7,000 pieds au-dessus du niveau de la mer. Schimper (Vatke, loc. cit.), is said to have collected the plant at an altitude of 8,200 feet. We have requested C. edulis from various sources, and to date our efforts to obtain it have been to no avail. In the present state of relations opening up between Ethiopia and Europe, we may not be long in receiving this interesting Labiatae; COLEUS BARBATUS Benth. (Plectranthus barbatus Andr.), from India, Arabia and the tropical east coast of Africa. According to the Flora of Dalzell and Gibson, and a label by Dr. Ritchie of the Kew Herbarium, this plant is cultivated in the Deccan for its fleshy roots, which the natives marinate; PLECTRANTHUS ESCULENTUS N.-E. Brown, a species with a tuberous root and yellow flowers. Samples of this plant were first sent to Kew by Mr. J.-M. Wood, curator of the Durban Botanical Garden, Natal (number 3633), in November 1886, under the name Umbondive or Kaffir Potato, with a note saying that the natives are very fond of tubers. The plant was cultivated at Kew. Mr. Medley Wood, curator of the Colonial Garden at Natal, was kind enough to send us some tubers of this plant, which rotted in the ground before giving any sign of vegetation; PLECTRANTHUS FLORIBUNDUS N.-E. Brown, a yellow-flowered species closely related to P. esculentus, the type of which is native to Natal, but of which a variety (longipes) grows in various parts of tropical Africa: Manganja Hills, Lake Nyassa, Blantyre, Umzingwani river valley, Angola, etc. Mr. N.-E. Brown believes that the roots of this species are tubercular like those of P. esculentus, but he has no proof of this; Lycopus lucius Turcz. or Inou Sirône of the Japanese, a perennial somewhat reminiscent of our Lycopus europæus, but with a stem giving rise to numerous stolons. This plant was sent from Japan to the Museum by Abbé Faurie, and Mr. Maxime Cornu was kind enough to offer us a seedling. The tuberous rhizomes grow up to 20 centimetres long and 1? to 2 centimetres in diameter, and are quite reminiscent of those of Stachys palustris, whose cultivation Mr. Chappellier has sought to propagate as a food plant; but, although their flavour is better, and they are produced in large numbers, there is no reason to recommend their use, as their fibrous consistency makes them unpalatable. Lycopus lucidus has proven to be absolutely hardy in our climate; it is regrettable that we cannot make better use of it. MELON DUDAIM Queen Anne's melon, Canary Island melon, Sweet melon, Fragrant pumpkin, Brahma apple. CUCUMS MELO L., var. DubAÏM Ndn. (Melo rotundus parvus G. Bauhin; C. Dudaim L.; C. sativus Schemmam Forsk; C. pictus Jacq; C. pedatifidus et C. reginæ Schrad; C. Schraderianus Rœm.; C. odoratissimus Mœnch). Vélins du Muséum, t. LXI, tab. 24, 25, 26, 28; Morr. Belg. hort. t. Il, p. 205. Cucurbitaceae family. "The Dudaïms, if we stick to the type form, constitute one of the best characterized and most stable races in the Melon species; but, as they cross easily with all the others, they soon degenerate and lose their character when cultivated a few years in a row in their vicinity. We

The fruit is spherical, slightly depressed at the front and back, more or less bright orange-yellow in color, with longitudinal macules or bariolures of a reddish hue. "The type of this variety has a spherical fruit, slightly depressed from front to back, more or less bright orange-yellow when ripe, with longitudinal macules or bariolures of a dull red, exhaling an extremely pronounced melon odor. This odor is sometimes so strong that some people find themselves bothered by the presence of one or two fruits of this variety in an apartment. Its size varies from that of a small apricot to that of an orange. It detaches from the stalk at the first signs of 25 ripeness. The flesh is thin, yellowish-white or slightly pink, not very sweet, and always leaves an aftertaste that is enough to make it inedible. "This aftertaste is found in all Dudaïm mixtures with other melons, no matter how excellent the variety. It should therefore be kept away from melons if the qualities that distinguish good varieties are to be preserved. 4 Fig. 51 - Dudaïm melon. "The frank Dudaïm deserves a place in some remote corner of the garden, for the kindness of its fruit, which can be used to decorate desserts; but it can never be for us an object of simple pleasure or curiosity. "Probably because of its highly developed aroma, the Dudaim is highly prized in most Muslim countries. It abounds in Persia, Egypt and throughout North Africa. I've even seen it sold in the markets of Algeria. The Spaniards have long carried it to their colonies in America, where they call it Melonsito de olor, as Gilii and Xuares tell us. Various authors give fairly good illustrations of it". (Naudin, Espèces et variétés du genre Cucumis. Extract from Ann. des sciences naturelles). We have just cultivated for the second time a Melon whose seeds were sent from Peking, without any particular observation, by M. le D' E. Bretschneider. The doctor had previously sent them to M. Decaisne, who considered them to belong to the Cucumis Dudaïm. We harvested fruit of the shape and volume of a large orange, with smooth rind, streaked with ten dark-green stripes against a yellow-orange background, running from the stalk to the umbilicus. Fruit height, approx. Om,8; width of stripes varying from Om,01 and 0m,015 to Om,02; average weight, 310 grams. The small volume of the fruit would make it one of those portable melons to which we have given the name of Pocket Melon or Hunter Melon, if it could have the same use; but it lacks sugar and can only be eaten for dessert. In China, it is tasty, sweet and fragrant. M. Naudin acknowledges that it can be used as a dessert garnish. He tells us that it is highly prized in most Muslim countries, that it abounds in Persia, Egypt and the whole of North Africa; that he has found it on the markets of Algeria; that it has long existed in the Spanish colonies of America. So it's not inedible. Its flesh is white, juicy and fragrant when very ripe. We suggest using it as follows: For a table of twenty place settings, arrange twelve to fifteen pieces of fruit in a pyramid or basket, forming a beautiful middle course that will charm the eyes of the guests. For dessert, the maître d'hôlel will remove the dish, cut the fruit in half, quickly replace the seeds with powdered sugar and serve each person half a fruit in the shape of a cup, to be eaten with a spoon like an ice cream. This dessert will be good and very elegant; we've made sure of that. One of our correspondents confirms our assessment. He writes us from Setubal (Portugal): "Yesterday, I got my hands on a Melon zébré de Chine, which I had been displaying for a fortnight on a shelf; it had turned almost all yellow, even in its green part, which had taken on this hue: it was just right for what I wanted to do with it. I divided it up, tasted it and found that it tasted a little like pineapple. I ate half of it, thinking that it could be used for something else. Continuing the experiment, I emptied the other half of its seeds, and, in this empty space, I put sugar, then a very good liqueur wine, called bastardo here. I left it to infuse for a quarter of an hour, and then, using a teaspoon, I started to make it like ice-cream, and the paste looked very much like it. It was delicious. It occurred to me that these melon halves could be glazed in a variety of ways. "I'll give you my test; it's by these random means that discoveries are made. I'm a convert, and I hasten to confess it. If only to eat this little melon, I'll grow it. The C. Dudaim produces only twelve to fifteen fruits per panel; its price would still be quite high. We recognize that in the Paris area, it will not be fruitfully cultivated, and that it will remain what it has been until now, an amateur plant. JAPANESE WHITE MELON Shiro uri. CUCUMIS MELO L., var. Cucurbitaceae family. By way of description, this melon bears a striking resemblance to the large white Paris cucumber. We've been growing it for several years under glass. We don't prune it, for fear of deforming it by making it bigger. In fact, its shape lends itself perfectly to the use we make of it. We pick them when they are very ripe, when they are pleasantly fragrant. Unfortunately, it lacks sugar, like all the melons we have received from China and Japan; but when cut into thin slices, dipped in doughnut batter, fried by a skilled hand and liberally sprinkled with sugar, it makes an excellent dessert, which retains a light pineapple flavour and is very well received by everyone. This is not the only use for Shiro uri. In 1879, at the Horticultural Exhibition in Brie-Comte-Robert, gardener Veniat presented this melon, which he had been growing for two years under glass, in the same way as the Cantaloupe, except for pruning. In a note on this presentation published in the Journal de la Société nationale d'Horticulture, we said: "Each plant gives an average of four fruits, the shape and color of which are those of white Paris cucumbers. The Shiro uri is slightly larger, however. Its skin is thin, its seeds are small, and it is all flesh. "This flesh lacks sugar, so this Japanese melon should not be eaten after soup, but as a dessert, sliced like a pineapple and accompanied by powdered sugar. "Shiro uri confit is very good. In 1879, confectioner Mamoz made an excellent dessert. Later. M. Robineau-Boissier ordered two hundred fruits from M. Millet, a distinguished horticulturist in Bourg-la-Reine, who delivered them exactly as ordered. The famous confectioner diced the flesh, candied it and graciously offered us a certain quantity of this delicacy, which was tasted with sensitive pleasure by twenty members of the plant section (Société d'Acclimatation). "We consider the Shiro uri to be preferable to most melons from the Far East. It can render services and be fruitfully cultivated in the Midi. "As it requires as much care and expense as Cantaloupes, we do not expect its cultivation to spread to the Paris area." MELON BELLE ANGEVINE CUCUMIS MELO L., var. Cucurbitaceae family. Fruits pear-shaped, green, dotted with yellow, marked with longitudinal stripes of a darker green than the rind. Length, Om, 15; circumference at most developed point, Om. 29. This melon, whose seeds were sent from China to Mr. A. Geoffroy Saint-Hilaire by Mr. E. Bretschneider, is shaped and colored like a large pear. Its rind is very thin; its flesh is green, juicy, melting and fragrant; but it has the defect of all its Chinese and Japanese congeners: it lacks sugar. It should therefore be picked ripe, served for dessert and eaten only when sprinkled generously with sugar. We won't repeat here what we said about the Chayote melon. Like Chayote, Melon Belle Angevine is an amateur plant, inferior to the varieties we grow. MELON CHAYOTE CUCUmHS MELo L., var. Cucurbitaceae family. Fruit small, pear-shaped, with smooth, apple-green rind, divided into ten ribs barely indicated by lines darker green than the rind. Length, Om, 16; circumference on stalk side, Om, 20; on umbilicus side, Om, 27. Weight, 370 grams. Very thin rind; flesh thick, white, fragrant, juicy and very melting. This melon is one of the best we have received from the Far East. The seeds were sent to Mr. A. Geoffroy Saint-Hilaire by Dr. E. Bretschneider, without any particular mention. Cultivated under a frame, they have successively given us a dozen ruits, on which we will make our opinion known. These melons from China and Japan have a special flavor, which can in no way be compared to that of melons grown in Europe. We wouldn't accept them in France, after soup, like Cantaloups, Sucrins, etc. We can't, in our opinion, accept them in the same way. In our opinion, they can only be eaten for dessert. Melon Chayole should be picked very ripe, and only eaten when it has softened a little and yields to finger pressure. Then, for dessert, after having been peeled like a pear and cut into two or four parts, it is eaten with a generous sprinkling of sugar. Presented in this way, it looks like a bouquet to many people, but it should be considered as a fruit in its own right, forgetting that it's called a melon, and not wishing at all costs, as is commonly done, that it resemble what we already have. Oriental melons lend themselves better to confectionery than our own, which are watery. Nevertheless, with the possible exception of the Japanese Shiro uri, they are amateur plants that require as much care as the finest Cantaloupes. They are therefore not generally cultivated. Their singular shape may give rise to hybridization trials. MELON DE NOUMÉA OU DE LA DUMBÉA CUCUMIS PANCHERIANUS Ndn, Ann. des se. nat. ser. 4, vol. XII, p. 112, tab. 8, and ser. 5, vol. XVI, p. 161 Cucurbitaceae family. Found in New Caledonia by the late Pancher. Cultivated, but degenerated, in botanical gardens. Ordinary cultivation of melons. Of all known species, this one has the smallest fruits. On average, they don't exceed the size of a large olive. This plant grows wild in New Caledonia, where its fruit is eaten by the children of the natives. We asked Mr. Naudin for some seeds, and he replied: "I would be delighted to be able to provide you with seeds of the Cucumis Pancherianus, which I cultivated at the Jardin des Plantes many years ago; but this curious species, whose fruits are no larger than olives, and which, despite this, crosses most easily with melons, giving fertile hybrids, has disappeared everywhere. It could only be found in New Caledonia, from where the botanist Pancher had sent the seeds. It might not be difficult to obtain new ones by writing to New Caledonia. This is what we did, and our kind correspondent, Mr. Perret, wrote to us on August 25 1886: "I have sent for Cucumis Pancherianus. A few years ago, there was a lot of it in Dumbéa. Grazing and fires have almost entirely destroyed it in this locality. However, we did find one plant bearing two young fruits. Unfortunately, I have to leave next week for a trip that will last about three months. I'll be touring the colony, inspecting all the prisons. I have recommended that these samples be carefully monitored and the seeds collected. As I'll be back in December, it will be soon enough to send them to you. We did, indeed, receive them and sow them. The crop was not very successful. We obtained a few small fruits that we preserved in vinegar and presented to the Société d'Acclimatation. Although these small melons deserve to be sought after for their attractive shape and extreme smallness, we doubt that confectioners or vinegar-makers will be able to offer them at a price that would allow their cultivation. MELOTHRIA PENDULA L., Pluk, tab. 85, fig. 5. Fam. Cucurbitaceae. Annual, climbing plant, common in parts of North America, reported even in Guiana and Brazil by travelers who have visited these countries. Also found in China. M. Fontanier, French consul in Macao, sent some seeds to the Museum. This plant reaches 3 to 4 feet in height; its leaves are cordate, five-lobed or angular, and scabrous. The fertile (hermaphrodite) flowers are borne on simple, thread-like, pendulous stalks that are longer than the leaves. Male flowers are borne in clusters on peduncles about 6 cm long. The calyx teeth are tubular. Petals are obovate, deeply indented. The fruit is green, hairless, ellipsoidal and olive-shaped. Sloane says that the inhabitants of the regions where this plant grows sometimes pickle its fruits while they are still green. We have cultivated Melothrie pendante with complete success. We sowed it on a bed and under a frame, and put it in place at the end of May. Set up on a coarse, very wide-meshed trellis, it covered itself with abundant foliage and a multitude of pretty white-dotted fruits, the color, shape and volume of an Olive. We have put these fruits in vinegar, like gherkins; but we cannot recommend them for this use. Only more or less crunchy fruits should be used in pickles. Melothrie fruits are hollow and soft. They are pleasing in shape, color and abundance, but are of no real use. Mélothrie is an amateur plant. MIOGA ZINGIBER MIOGA ROSC. - Amonum Mioga Kæmpfer. Family Zingiberaceae. Perennial plant. Scaly, horizontally spreading rhizomes. Annual aerial stems, Om 50 to 0m, 60 high, simple, covered by leaf sheaths. Stem leaves alternate, distichous, simple, about Om, 20 long and Om, 5 to Om, 6 wide, lanceolate acuminate, light green, scarcely petiolate, ligulate; lateral veins extend from the median rib of the leaf blade. Peduncle arising from the stump, rising subterraneously, bearing an ovoid inflorescence composed of overlapping bracts, dotted with reddish-brown, unfolding five or six flowers at ground level; calyx tubular, split on one side and transparent; corolla with short tube, formed of three equal, lanceolate outer lobes, about Om, 3 long; labellum three-lobed, obovate, Om, 2 1/2 long. Stamen single, filleted, bearing a two-located lateral anther enveloping the style. Ovary inferere, three-located, enclosing several ovules, surmounted by a slender style, extended beyond the anther into a long, arched beak; stigma funnel-shaped. Blooms in August-September. Mioga is native to Japan. It was described by Kæmpter in his book Amænitatum exoticarum..... fascicule V, page 826. It was collected by Thunberg, Siebold, Buerger and Mohnike (1), near Nangasaki. It grows spontaneously, but is generally cultivated. (1) Thunberg, Flora japonica, p. 24; Miquel, Prolusio Roræ Japonicæ, p. 304. In the book Le Japon à l'Exposition universelle de 1878, we read: "Mioga is a plant whose young stems and flowers are eaten. The fibers of its stems can also be used to make ropes". Fig. 52 - Zingiber Mioga. In the Note explicative des objets exposés par l'École agricole de Komaba, à l'Exposition universelle de 1889, (Ministère de l'Agriculture et du Commerce, Tokio, Japon) we find the following details: "Le Mioga (Amomum Mioga). Mioga flowers are harvested from the roots on the ground around June or July; they are shaped like spinning pins. The young stems, brewed in spring, are tender and taste just like the flowers". Dr. H*** wrote to us on April 11, 1879: "I am sending you a small root of a species of ginger called Mioga in Japan, and Amonum Mioga by Thunberg. The inflorescences are eaten before the flowers bloom; it's quite good. Although all Zingiberaceae are considered to be greenhouse plants, this plant has spent the winters perfectly at my place for the last three years, in the open ground, planted 0.10 m deep and covered in winter with a few dry leaves. It flowered well last summer. If the piece I'm sending you is a little small, it's because I haven't multiplied it much yet. The doctor wrote again on June 7 of the same year: "My Mioga plants have spent the winter perfectly in the open ground and are growing on all sides. If the too-small plant I sent you did not grow, I could send you as many as you would like next winter." We did not ask our kind correspondent for a second shipment. The section he had sent us had vegetated so well that we could plant it the following spring. As we can see from the date we received it, our Mioga withstood the harsh winter. It was, it must be said, protected by a thick blanket of snow; but in 1891, nothing defended it against the cold. We believe there is no plant hardier than the Mioga, nor one that multiplies more rapidly. We have not seen it affected by any disease, or attacked by any insect. We plant the rhizome sections in a garden bed 1.30 m wide, in two parallel lines, Om.50 apart, leaving a space of Om.40 between the lines and the paths, which does not prevent the plants from bearing their stems and inflorescences under them. There are very few weeds in the bed, and a hoeing or two does justice. Watering optional. Do not hoe after July 31st: you risk cutting off shoots and inflorescences. Weeding can be done by hand. Harvesting begins around August 15. Planting is monitored in the same way as for Asparagus; like Asparagus shoots, we cut close to the rhizome, as soon as the inflorescence shows its sharp tip on the surface of the soil. We've never cut off the shoots, except for a few to taste them, for fear of reducing propagation. They are harvested like the inflorescences, and we have found no appreciable difference between the flavor of one and the other. We assume that you should wait two years before harvesting shoots from a plantation, and only cut them in spring, although they are also produced in autumn. We would therefore regularly harvest inflorescences from August to September, and shoots from late April to late May. It would probably not be wise to extend the cutting beyond this period. We won't insist on Mioga's hardiness and rapid multiplication. We will talk about the uses to which its shoots and inflorescences can be put. Our correspondent wrote to us on August 18, 1880: "If you want to try tasting these Miogas, wash them carefully to remove any dust or earth that may have remained between the bracts, blanch them in boiling water with a little salt, pass them through cold water, then cook them for a moment in a sauce au jus, like onions, leeks or celery. It tastes very different from what we're used to eating; but, in my opinion at least, it's not bad." We tasted Mioga inflorescences prepared au gratin like macaroni, with alternating layers of vegetable and grated Parmesan: it's quite good. We ate them as a salad, after simply blanching them in boiling water. We found them very tasty. A slight resinous taste disappears after the second or third bite, and the slightly spicy flavor of the vegetable blends quite well with that of the oil. Finally, to make pickles, we combined Mioga inflorescences with West Indian Angouries, Catawissa Onion bulbs and Spanish Sweet Peppers. We were delighted with the result, and particularly recommend this use. We hope that people will persevere in trying out various culinary preparations; there's still a lot to do. To those who ask us if our vegetable resembles any of the others we use, we say: No, Mioga doesn't taste like cabbage, cardoon, artichoke or any of our other vegetables... it tastes like Mioga. 401 CRYPTOTÆNIA CANADENSIS DC; Chœrophyllum canadense Pers; Myrrhis canadensis Moris; Sium canadense Lamk; Conopodium canadense Koch. Umbelliferae family. Perennial native to North America, Japan and China (1); erect, Om, 30 to Om, 40 high, glabrous; leaves three-segmented, oval, broad, toothed; flowers small, white, united in paniculate umbels, with few, unequal rays; no involucre; involucellum with few leaflets; calyx entire; petals obovate, almost entire, with a narrow, inflected tongue; fruit laterally compressed, linear oblong, crowned by a short stylopod and two straight styles; carpels with five filiform ribs, We sowed Mitsuba in April, under a frame, and transplanted it into the field as soon as the plant was ready. The following spring, these clumps gave us several successive cuttings of young leaves. The plant is very hardy, but emergence is slow, and it was to speed it up that we sowed it under a frame, a precaution that is in no way necessary. In Japan at the 1878 Universal Exhibition, we read: "Mitsuba or Mitsuba seri is an edible plant which is obtained in the following way: when winter comes, its roots are covered with earth and plant detritus; the shoots that appear are then picked, cooked and eaten. We first tasted the young leaves we had put through the wringer as a salad. We were not satisfied with the result. The salad we obtained was inferior to the Capuchin Beard; but its non-etiolated leaves, chopped and prepared like Spinach, Chicory, etc., gave us a very good vegetable dish, with a special, slightly aromatic flavor, pleasant enough in our opinion, for us to recommend the cultivation of Mitsuba to amateurs. Another umbelliferous plant, Phellopterus littoralis Benth. is sometimes cultivated in Japan. The Muséum received seeds from Abbé Faurie, and this rare species flowers and fruits every year in our great national establishment. It is a perennial plant, Om, 50 in height; it has proved to be very hardy, having withstood the rigorous winter of 1890-1891 perfectly. Phellopterus leaves are prized in Japan as a condiment. They have a very pronounced aromatic flavor, reminiscent of both Angelica and Tarragon. Cannibal nightshade. Borodina. SOLANUM ANTHROPOPHAGORUM Seeman. Solanaceae family. 403 The correspondence relating to the Fiji Islands, presented to both Houses of the English Parliament by order of His Majesty, in May 1862, is accompanied by an interesting appendix, consisting of a report by Doctor Seeman on the vegetable productions and resources of the Viti or Fiji Islands, in which a chapter, on page 58, is devoted to vegetables eaten with human flesh, a report from which we give the following extract: "Since, thanks to the influence of trade, Christian teaching and the presence of a British consul, cannibalism survives in only a small number of localities and is becoming every day, and more and more, a mere historical subject, it may be important to know this: human flesh, Fijians have repeatedly assured me, is extremely difficult to digest, and the healthiest men suffer for two or three days after eating it. No doubt to aid digestion, bokola, the technical name given to the flesh of the dead man, is always eaten with added vegetables. There are three main types of vegetable that Fijians believe should accompany bokola: Malawari leaves (Trophis anthropophagorum Seem.), Tudana (Omalanthus pedicellatus Benth.) and Borodina (Solanum anthropophagorum Seem.). The first two are medium-sized trees, growing spontaneously in many parts of the archipelago; but the Borodina is cultivated, and there are usually several large clumps near each "buré" (or foreigners' house), where the bodies of men killed in battle are always carried. "Borodina is a bushy shrub, rarely more than 6 feet tall, with dark, glossy foliage and berries the shape and color of tomatoes. This fruit has a slight aromatic odor and is accidentally prepared as Tomato sauce. The leaves of these three plants are rolled around the bokola, like those of Taro around pork, and cooked with it on heated stones. Salt is not forgotten. "While all other kinds of vegetables and meat are eaten with the fingers, cannibals' food is only touched with forks, generally made from the wood of the Nokonoko (Casuarina equisetifolia) or the Vesi (Afzelia bijuga A. Gray), bearing curious names and having three or four long tines. The reason given for this departure from the usual way of eating is the widespread belief that fingers that have touched the bokola can cause skin diseases when in contact with the delicate skin of children, and, as Fijians love their offspring dearly, they scrupulously make use of forks on the occasions indicated. "The Borodina mentioned above is the subject of today's plate (1). Our plants were obtained(1) Botanical Magazine, 3rd ser. t. XX, p. 5424. from seeds brought back by Dr. Seeman, and flowered in the greenhouse of the Royal Gardens in July. Except when bearing fruit, this species of Solanum has nothing to encourage cultivation. It is only of historical interest, linked to a practice that is falling further and further into disuse with each passing year". In 1878, we sowed Solanum anthropophagorum on a bed and under a frame. On June 2, we planted it outdoors on an old bed. At the beginning of September, quite a few fruits began to turn red. In October, these fruits not being ripe, we enclosed the plant in a glass cage, and shortly afterwards harvested its berries. They were dry, or at least not juicy enough to make a tomato-like sauce out of them. In our opinion, it would be a waste of time and effort to cultivate S. anthropophagorum in the Paris climate; but Mr. Rantonnet (1), a horticulturist in Hyères, has found that it can withstand winter in the open air, and he harvested ripe fruit in February. In his opinion, this new type of tomato could become widely available, especially in southern France. (1) Revue horticole, 1867, p. 326. BALBIS MORELLE SOLANUM SISYMBRHFOLIUM Lamk, Illustr. no. 2386. S. Balbisii Dunal, Solan. 232, no. 195, t. III, fasc. 2; Bot. Magtab. 2568, 2828 and 3954; S. decurrens Balb, ic. et descript. fasc. 1, p. 16, t. Ier; S. inflatum Horn, hort. Hafn, I, p. 221; S. viscosum DC, ic. pict. facult. se. monsp.; Lag. gen et sp. 10, no. 145; S. brancæfolium Jacq, Eclog. 14, vol. VII; S. formosum Cat. Dorp. p. 145, nec H. B. et Kth; S. mauritianum Vélins du Mus, Paris, t. XXI, t. LXVI; S. viscidum Schweigg, Enum. h. region, 67, Mart. en H. Erlang. 64; S. edule Vellozo, Flor. flum. 2, t. CXXI; S. opulifolium Portensch. mss. in h. Vindob. e Sendtn; S. Thouini Gmel, Cat. Carlsruhe, 254. Family Solanaceae. Annual plant, native to southern America, reaching about 1 meter in height, hairy, glandular, bearing yellowish spines on all parts. Stem woody at base. Leaves pinnatifid, five- or seven-lobed, irregularly toothed, bristling with coarse hairs, very thorny, decurrent. Flowers fairly large, white, lilac or violet depending on variety, in terminal cymes; calyx unequally divided, spiny; corolla closely resembling that of the potato. Stamens with yellow anthers; short style. Fruit (berry) as large as a large cherry, partly covered by calyx divisions, saffron-red in color, with sweet, slightly acidic flesh when ripe. What we have to say about Morelle de Balbis will take up less space than its description and synonyms. The plant is more ornamental than edible and should not detain us for long. It acquires great proportions in rich soil. We obtained plants bearing both blue and white flowers, and it seemed to us that the fruits following the blue flowers were of a more beautiful red than those of the white-flowered plants. We measured the diameter and circumference of one planted on an old bed, and we regret not having kept the figures; the dimensions were enormous. The cultivation of S. Balbisii is the same as for tomatoes. It is sown in trays and under beds, planted at the end of May and ripens in September and October. Harvesting is difficult. The plant is equipped with innumerable thorns, which defend its approaches. We try to pick its pretty red berries, but usually give up. We do remember, however, seeing a Morelle de Balbis plant near the Museum's greenhouses, stripped of its fruit by passers-by as it ripened; but surely this innocent petty theft was not accomplished without spilling blood. The taste of S. Balbisii are quite insignificant. They could be preserved in caramel and fondant, like those of Physalis peruviana, which are much better; what we can say is that the plant is very beautiful and worthy of the attention of connoisseurs. MORELLE DE SALTA Small Salta Tomato. Under the name of Petite Tomate de Salta, Dr. Weber has given us seeds of a variety of Morelle de Balbis (Solanum sisymbriifolium). The description we have given of the latter is in every way applicable to the Petite Tomate de Salta. Its fruits are smaller and less abundant, but its leaves are more finely cut and of a brighter green. It's easy to grow, and its development is such that three plants are enough to form a large basket. It should therefore be grown exclusively as an ornamental plant. In this respect, we would prefer it to Morelle de Balbis; we thank Dr. Weber for introducing us to it. BIRD'S MORELLE SOLANUM AVICULARE Forst. (Syn.: S. laciniatum Ait.) Solanaceae family. This species is native to Australia and New Zealand. It is a shrub that can reach 3 meters in height and is cultivated in our European gardens for its ample, laciniated, highly ornamental foliage, as well as for its rather large, purplish-blue flowers. Dr. Kirk, from Wellington, New Zealand, author of important publications on the flora of New Zealand, was kind enough to send us seeds of this plant, which, he told us, the Maoris cultivate to eat the large berries, yellow, orange or violet in color, depending on the variety. Our kind correspondent also told us that these fruits are eaten either raw or cooked, but mostly in the first state. We have grown the plant in Crosnes, but have only harvested a very small number of fruits, absolutely comparable, incidentally, to those of Solanum laciniatum, which our gardeners use to form baskets in the gardens. The flavor of these fruits is nil. Morelle des Oiseaux deserves to be recommended as an ornamental plant, but does not seem to us to be of any interest as a food plant. MORELLE NODIFLORE SOLANUM NODIFLORUM Jacq. Icones rar. 2, tab. 326; Dunal, in DC. Prodr., XIII, 46; S. nigrum, var. patulum Lin. Solanaceae family. Very widespread in Mauritius and Rodriguez, and cultivated in vegetable gardens. Found throughout the tropics. Brède malgache (Flora of Mauritius and the Seychelles, Baker.) Annual plant, reaching 6 to 7 decimeters in height, with sub-frulescent, stalked, almost cylindrical, glabrous stems, with thickened nodes; leaves alternate, petiolate, ovate, acute, a little decurrent on the petioles, entire, glabrous and green on both sides; peduncle simple at the base, dividing at the top to form a kind of umbel composed of five or six flowers, small, whitish, with obtuse sepals; corolla deeply divided, lanceolate, acute, very open; berry round, small, black, shiny; seeds white. Grows in all tropical parts of the old and new continents. Morelle nodiflore seeds were brought to us from Nouméa by our correspondent, Mr. V. Perret. Sown in trays and planted in the ground in mid-May, they produce the most vigorous vegetation. The tops of the plant are edible, but we believe it prudent not to eat the fruit; several Solanum plants in this section have poisonous fruit. Is Solanum nodiflorum spontaneous or introduced to New Caledonia? We can't say, but it is found in the parts of the island furthest from the points occupied by Europeans. The plant is widely used in tropical regions, where it is used in calalou and vegetable soups. Several species of Solanum, such as S. oleraceum Dunal and S. nigrum L., are used in the same way. We tasted Nodiflora nightshade, chopped and prepared like spinach. We didn't like it; it had no flavor other than a slight bitterness. We're too rich in edible herbs - Spinach, Tetragona, Glacial, etc. - to recommend a plant that, in our opinion, is inferior. We felt, however, that a morello that is universally esteemed in warmer countries and easy to cultivate in our climate deserved a place in a curious gardener's kitchen garden. Our taste is not the law. MORELLE DE WALLIS Guayavo, Pepino, Melon Pear, Melon Shrub. SOLANUM MURICATUM Ait, var; S. Wallisii Carr; S. quatemalense Eisen. Solanaceae family. "Plant of great vigor, bushy, compact and very branched from its base, which becomes subfrutescent or even subligneous, nigrescent or better black in all its branches; branches and twigs numerous, slightly angular or subaligned. Leaves very long lanceolate, entire, very rarely and slightly lobed, about 12 centimeters long, 4 centimeters wide, regularly attenuated at the tip; petioles 5-7 centimeters long, purple-black, as are the veins. Flowers gathered in a kind of scorpioid corymb, solitary on a pedicel of about 15-20 millimeters, black, strongly villeinous. Calyx with short oval divisions, very long-lasting. Corolla spreading, 25-30 millimeters wide, glossy dark violet, divisions abruptly narrowed and acuminate at the tip; stamens included, with threads shorter than the anthers. Fruits elongated, heart-shaped, reaching about 6 cm long by 4 cm, sometimes more, in Fig. 53 - Wallis nightshade. diameter, regularly attenuated at the apex, which is abruptly rounded, slightly narrowed at the base, on which the calyx divisions are applied; skin smooth and very plain, shiny, of a very beautiful mottled violet, dotted, often here and there banded or widely spotted. Flesh yellowish-white, thick, melting, very juicy, slightly pungent flavor, reminiscent of melon, slightly sweet, but with a peppery, pungent aftertaste. Seeds borne on a sort of fleshy central placenta, erect in the center of the fruit". E.-A. Carrière (1). After giving this description, Mr. Carrière continues: "The qualities we have just mentioned are those we have observed in fruit that ripened in our greenhouse, and which consequently could not reach the perfection they should acquire in equatorial climates. In Nice, the fruit already appears to be of superior quality, judging by what Mr. Ch. Huber has told us. In a letter from Nice, dated January 13 1877, he says: "I have just eaten another Solanum Wallisii fruit, and I am extremely satisfied with its good taste; the flesh, very melting, yellow in color, like that of a Prune ReineClaude, is very sweet. For the fruit to acquire this quality, you need to wait until it has reached a tender consistency, leave it to rest and avoid squeezing it by hand, as is often done, which detracts from the good taste. The plants I left in the ground did not suffer from the cold of December, and are growing as well as if they were in a greenhouse. For northern countries, I think a cold greenhouse will suffice to overwinter them... I also made some (] Note on the S. Wallisit, colored figure (Revue horticole, 1877). p. 291). prepare them cooked, and I have found that they make a delicious dish". The Wallis nightshade was introduced to Europe by Mr. Wallis, who bought the fruit at the market in Guayaquil (Ecuador), where it is sold under the name of Guayavos. The fruit is highly prized by the locals, who eat it raw or cooked. Mr. Ch. Huber received the seeds from Mr. Ortgies, sowed them on December 12 1875, and, provided with numerous plants, put the plant on the market. We then tried its cultivation, and one of us, in a letter of which we reproduce a part, informed M. Carrière of the results he had obtained: "Cuttings of Morelle de Wallis are taken outdoors as easily as those of Pelargoniums, and the plant provides them abundantly. I took a good number of them last August; they spent the winter under glass, side by side with the Pelargoniums. Only the mother plant died. "When spring came, I kept eight of them, four of which were kept in pots. The other four were planted in the ground, poorly protected by a frame that was constantly raised 25 or 30 centimetres above the trunk. This allowed air to circulate under the trunk. It had to be lifted to straighten the plant, which doesn't rise much, but whose stems I didn't want to drag along the ground, as they would have immediately taken root, which I wanted to avoid. "The plants vegetated vigorously, and were constantly disbudded. Only three or four branches were retained, and they were continually crowned with charming bouquets of flowers. Cuttings kept in pots, also placed under a well-ventilated frame, produced many flowers, but did not set a single fruit. "Of the four plants planted in the open ground, only one set three fruits. Two of these fruits are already much larger than the one shown in our Revue; the third is still growing and looks set to reach the same size. These fruits are of rare beauty. They have been tied since July 1st (1). "The details I've just gone into make it clear where I went wrong. The season having been colder than usual, I should have given my plants a little more warmth. I didn't, and I was wrong. Now let's look to the future. "Wallis nightshade is extremely hardy, in the sense that its cuttings take root in the open air, that its vegetation is always very active, and that no disease or insect seems to be able to compromise its cultivation. "What does it need? A little more heat than I've been giving it. This is no problem for gardeners or market gardeners. Take cuttings in August, keep them over winter in a frame, bring them forward a little in spring on a warm bed, disbud them carefully, remove half the flowers from each bunch - these are the tips I can give today. We are convinced that Wallis nightshade can be grown without difficulty. However, it (1) One of these fruits, fully ripe on September 2, measured 9 centimetres in height and 27 in circumference. Its shape was reminiscent of an unflattened ox heart; its smooth, shiny skin was marbled with violet pink; it was indeed of rare beauty (Rédaction de la Revue horticole). will always require more care in our climate than it deserves. We tasted the fruit we obtained. They were superb, perfectly ripe, fleshy, juicy and appetizing. We were full of illusions about the result. The curious will understand the bitterness of our disappointment; our fruit tasted like cucumbers and left an unpleasant tingle on the tongue. We put them in compote, and they were no better. So why did we just talk about them at length? It's because these beautiful fruits, certainly very good in Guayaquil, could be a useful acquisition for the Mediterranean coast and for Algeria; it's because, after all, we don't just write for the outskirts of Paris. In a note published in the Revue horticole, 1892, page 155, Mr. Ed. André describes the fruits of a variety of Solanum muricatum sent to him by Mr. Naudin from Antibes, which differ from Morelle de Wallis in their creamy-white color with purple streaks and dots. MOTSIJI BIDENS PILOSA Lin; Ceratocephalus pilosus Rich; Kerneria dubia Coss; Kerneria tetragona Mœnch. Family Compositae. Annual plant with erect stems, 1.80 to 1 m high, quadrangular, branching at the top. Leaves opposite, petiolate, glabrescent, blackish-green, upper ones triseriate, lower ones imparipennate, with acute oval segments, toothed at the margins. Petioles ciliate at base. Flowers in discoid capitula; involucre with scales almost equal to each other, about the same length as the disc flowers. Fruit (achain) linear, angular, ending with two more or less divergent ridges, spreading slightly as it ripens. This plant grows in all warm parts of the globe. Our correspondent in Transvaal, Mr. Mingard, an agronomist attached to the Waldensian mission, sent us the seeds, and we extract the following passage from his letter of dispatch: "From today, as you advise, I shall make it a habit to enclose a few seeds with each letter to complete the postage; I therefore enclose some Motsiji seeds, a plant that I eat young and fresh, boiled like lettuce and chopped like spinach. Naturals usually mix it with peanut flour. We sowed our Motsiji seeds on March 20 (1884) in pots, under a frame; they sprouted well, and we put the plants in place on May 20, in the open air. They developed well and gave us ripe seeds at the beginning of July. On May 26, we sowed Motsiji in the open ground, and by July 12 we were tasting the tops of the stems, chopped like spinach; the result satisfied us. We agree that the plant is easy to grow and makes a good summer vegetable, without any particular recommendation. 27 TUBEROUS MUSTARD SINAPIS JUNCEA L., var, napiformis. Cruciferae family. Biennial plant, glabrous, reaching 1 m to 1m,50 in height. Root swollen, napiform, closely resembling common parsnip, white, about Om.17 long and Om.20 in circumference. Lower leaves oblong, lanceolate, toothed, sometimes lyrate; upper narrow, linear, lanceolate, whole or toothed. Flowers light yellow, in terminal clusters. Sepals divergent. Siliques erect, bumpy; beak fairly long, aspermous, somewhat compressed; valves keeled, trinervate, with sinuous lateral veins. In the Bulletin de la Société d'Aclimatation, 3e série, tome IX, page 579, we read the letter dated Peking, November 20 1881, in which Dr. E. Bretschneider, physician to the Russian Legation, announced to the Society a consignment of seeds and tubers comprising no fewer than 112 plants, shrubs or trees, for utility or ornament. Tuberous mustard was part of this rich collection, and its seeds were given to us for experimentation. We reported on our trials in the Bulletin de la Société, 3rd series, Volume X, page 21. Sown in the spring of 1882, the plant went to seed extremely quickly, and did not form a tuberous root. Sown on August 1, it gave us the roots described above in two and a half months. We informed Dr Bretschneider of the favorable results we had obtained, and he wrote to us from Peking, dated February 28 1883: "The Chinese sow this plant in midsummer and collect the roots in winter. These roots play a very Fig. 54. - Tuberous mustard. in China, and are widely cultivated in the North. The Chinese eat them salted and preserved with the fruits of Zanthoxylon Bungei and Illicium anisatum. I find their taste quite pleasant. Cultivation of the Moularde tubéreuse is in no way different from that of the Navet. Like turnips, it should be classified as a catch crop. Its roots, introduced into stews, give the broth a very good taste; prepared in juice, like celeriac, they are tender and slightly spicy. We consider Dr. Bretschneider's gift of this variety of Sinapis juncea, absolutely new to us, to be invaluable. Among the Cruciferae used as food plants in Japan, there is one, Wasabi (Eutrema Wasabi Maximowicz), that we wanted to know about. We found it mentioned in the Note explicative des objêts exposés par l'École agricole et forestière de Komaba (Japon), à l'Exposition universelle de Paris, en 1889. The brochure reads: "Wasabi. To grow this horseradish, choose a plot of land in a valley blocked by a stream. The roots are harvested. There is a variety also called Wasabi, whose leaves, prepared with vinegar, are exquisitely tasty." We asked for this plant in Japan and received rhizomes in 1896, which unfortunately rotted during the trip. Another species of Wasabi (Ainu-Wasabi, Rises- seri or Nisesseri) (Cardamine yesoensis Maximowicz) is cited by Messrs Batchelor and Miyabe: Ainu Economic plants (Transactions of the Asiatic Society of Japan, 1893, p. 215). At the beginning of spring," say these authors, "the young leaves and new rhizomes are collected as food; the older parts of the roots are discarded. To increase their pungent flavor, the Ainos in some places have learned to put the leaves and rhizomes together in bottles, which they keep well corked for a day or so before using. Risesseri is usually boiled before eating." Tsurigane-ninjin or Nunoba. ADENOPHORA VERTICILLATA Fisch. Family Campanulaceae. 421 This plant (fig. 55) is often cultivated in botanical gardens; it is quite ornamental. In Japan, its roots are eaten either fresh, as soon as they are harvested, or dried, which makes it possible to stock up on them: they are eaten after boiling, sometimes mixed with broad beans. The leaves are often added to soup... (Batchelor and Kingo Miyabe, Ainu Economic plants. Transactions of the Asiatic Society of Japan, p. 224). We sowed Mukekashi seeds in the spring of 1896. The young plants were transplanted into beds, spaced about 40 centimetres apart, and covered with glass trunks at the onset of winter. (In the Paris climate, the plant is absolutely hardy, and we recognized the futility of this precaution). By spring, the clumps had become quite voluminous, and we pulled up a few to see for ourselves just how valuable they were as food. The roots, quite numerous, thick and fleshy, have more or less the same flavor as Rapunzel. Cooked in slightly salted water, the youngest were tender, a little aromatic, but edible, while the thickest had an accentuated aromatic flavor. The cooked leaves were tender, with almost no flavor, but pleasant. 7 Fig. 55. - Adenophora verticillata. The young stems were a little stringy, and had a faint Laituc flavor. Egyptian bean, Egyptian pink lily. Pytagorian Bean. NELUMBIUM SPECIOSUM Willd. ; Nelumbo nucifera Gærtn. Family Nelombonaceae. 423 Perennial aquatic plant, native to southern Asia, once widespread in Egypt, where it was introduced and from which it has long since disappeared. It was one of the Lotus species of the Nile; the Egyptians deified it and depicted its flowers and fruits on most of their monuments. Later, they searched in vain for it in the waters of the Nile, and for a long time it was known only from medals and hieroglyphics. The descriptions given of it were all imaginary and erroneous, and only served to confuse the question of its identification. In 1602, the plant was finally discovered in its native India, and the learned botanist Lécluse (Clusius) was able to recognize that Nelumbo and the famous Egyptian plant belonged to one and the same species. This aquatic plant, one of the most beautiful known, is composed of a tracer rhizome, underground, more or less branched, equipped with swellings or nodosities from which the leaves are born, with cylindrical, needled petioles; some with a floating, peeled, flat blade; others rising up to 75 centimetres above the surface of the water, orbicular, with cup-shaped raised edges, about 25 centimetres wide. The upper surface of the leaves is rough, and water rolls over them like crystal globules. The flower blooms in July-August. It rises on a peduncle that starts on the stump and reaches or even exceeds the highest leaves. It is around 25 centimetres wide, with some twenty petals, whitish at the base and pink at the top. The stamens are numerous and have Fig. 56 - Indian Nelumbo (Nelumbium speciosum). extrinsic anthers, whereas in the Nymphaea they are introrsic. The fruit, shaped like an inverted cone, has a flat upper surface with some twenty cells, each containing a seed the size of a small hazelnut. The fruit resembles a watering can apple with large holes. This is the most famous and beautiful of all aquatic food plants. It could have been the subject of a long and interesting article, if, fortunately for the reader, we hadn't been pre-empted by the Bulletin de la Société nationale d'Acclimatation. In his work entitled Les Productions végétales du Japon, Dr. Mène says it all, without prolixity or omission, about Nelumbo nucifera. On pages 376 et seq. of the separate edition, you'll find all you need to know about the plant's nutritional and medicinal uses. The author reminds us that, among the Egyptians, the Nelumbo flower, under the name Lotus, was a sacred flower, and that today it still occupies the same rank in India, China, Japan, etc. We recommend reading the entire chapter devoted to Nelumbo, borrowing only what the author has to say about its food uses: "Its rhizomes and seeds are used in Japanese cuisine. Mounds of Lotus rhizomes, known as Hasu-none, can be found on the markets of Japan, as well as in China, Cochinchina and the kingdom of Siam. The taste of these rhizomes, when cooked, is reminiscent of that of turnip, cardoon and celery; they are eaten raw, boiled in water and ashes, boiled or fried like salsify; they are also ground to a powder, dried and used especially in soups; they yield a pinkish-white starch, which was featured in the Japanese section of the World's Fair and is used in soups. "As for the seeds, which taste a little like hazelnuts and sweet almonds, they are edible and the Japanese eat them with their meals as sweet dishes; they are used to make cakes and pastries. Dr. E. Bretschneider wrote to us on June 7, 1887: "The Chinese eat the rhizomes of this plant raw or cooked, but a starch is prepared from them which is an excellent food for small children; it makes a good porridge in water and milk. Rhizomes are harvested in autumn, when they are very rich in starch. It is widely consumed in China, and tastes excellent. "Nelumbium seeds are eaten both green and ripe, but taste better when green. They are also eaten toasted, and are widely used in China. "The starch is sold everywhere under the name Ngeonfen. R. P. David, for his part, gives information that shows the usefulness of Nelumbo: "I have seen that, throughout China, the magnificent Nelombonée with its large purpurinous flowers, known as Nelumbium speciosum, is highly esteemed, not only for its beauty, but as a food and medicinal plant. But until now, I've only seen the roots and seeds used as they are, or seasoned with sugar. In Tzitan, and perhaps elsewhere, we know how to extract a white starch from this large root, which is said to be very corroborating. To prepare a bowl, a single spoonful of this flour is placed at the bottom of the vase, and boiling water is poured over it, while a small stick is used to shake it rapidly. "This white material loses its color almost immediately, to resemble gelatin, and all the water in the bowl is transformed into a beautiful, transparent, consistent mass, but on cooling it becomes brown and solid. Mixed with a little sugar, Ngeon-fen is excellent for those who are ill, and also for those who are not." When in bloom, Nelumbium speciosum forms one of the most beautiful adornments of the Srinagar lake (Kashmir); its cheerful green foliage completely covers the waters, and from this green surface emerge myriads of peduncles terminating in enormous pink flowers reminiscent of our most beautiful Peonies. These flowers of a sacred plant are placed in temples and in the private oratories of the Hindus and renewed every morning for the duration of the flowering period. The leaves, which are of a very strong fabric, measure 45 centimetres or more in diameter. In the edge of these leaves, a rush is passed which serves as a lace and is intended to raise them so as to hold liquid food. This type of vegetable crockery, unknown in France, is then placed in the shade to dry. It is on these leaves that the great lords of Kashmir have their food served, and as these improvised vessels are only used once, they are assured that their dishes are always clean. Nelumbium seed is very good when fresh, and its taste is reminiscent of hazelnut. Consumption is low, as the flowers are cut to decorate temples. In autumn, when vegetation ceases, the young, undeveloped leaves forming a kind of turion are harvested by the natives, bundled and sold on the Srinagar market. These shoots have the length and appearance of beautiful asparagus and are called Nadrou. The natives, who are very fond of them, eat them cooked. Europeans make no use of them; however, Mr. L. Bouley, Director of Cultivation for H.H. the Maharadja of Kashmir, has eaten them with kari, and assures us that this dish is not to be despised. We have gathered the above curious information from the dictation of Mr. Ermens, the Maharadja's former Director of Cultivation, and from an article by his successor, Mr. L. Bouley, published in the Revue Horticole, March 16 1884. In August 1835, Raffeneau-Delile, Professor of Botany at the Montpellier Academy of Medicine and Director of the Jardin des Plantes, published a paper in the Bulletin de la Société d'Agriculture du département de l'Hérault, entitled: Acclimatization du Nelumbium speciosum dans le midi de la France. In it, he dwelt on the plant's historical interest and its uses as an architectural ornament and religious attribute, but made light of its nutritional properties: "The Lotus of ancient Egypt," he said, "grew in lakes on which people sailed in boats. Its broad leaves rose out of the water enough to shelter the boats, or perhaps to cover them. They were the size of Thessalian hats and conveniently served as dishes and cups, so that Alexandria's stores were well stocked with them...". He reported on the culling processes he had applied to the Nélumbo and the happy results he had obtained. He concluded by saying: "Our plant, because of its rarity, had the privilege of being much visited and much admired, attracting a number of people who learned with interest that it is useful for its seeds and roots, which are good for food in India; but this quality of food is base, without merit, in comparison with the elegance, color and pleasantly aniseed fragrance of its flowers". At the time, Nélumbo was being cultivated for the first time in the open air, and Raffeneau-Delile took infinite precautions to ensure its success; but today this cultivation is well known and no longer presents any difficulties. M. Planchon, professor of botany and director of the Jardin des plantes de Montpellier, wrote to us: "We didn't need to hybridize Nelumbium to cultivate these beautiful plants in the Midi and even in central France. The rough-leaved form, called by Delile asperifolium, can withstand our harshest winters here. It's probably the same variety that flourished, before 1880, in a large pond in the Parc de Baleine, in the Allier region, then belonging to the uncle of Mr. Doumet-Adanson, the current owner. "All the plants were killed by the exceptional winter of 1880. I suppose you could also grow the plant at Crosnes; but I fear that, for want of light, even more than heat, these splendid plants will not flower as easily as they do under our sun." In 1887, we immersed a few Nelumbium plants in Crosnes. They soon disappeared. M. Planchon's letter shows that Lotus cultivation is easy in the south of France and practical in central France. Farther north, however, it would be pointless to try it with the aim of harvesting rhizomes and seeds; this would be a luxury crop, the procedures for which have been known for a long time. In this respect, Nelumbium cannot be grown in the Paris climate. If necessary, our readers may refer to publications, old or recent, in which the uses or cultivation methods of the Lotus are described, notably in: article by Jesuit Father Cibot, on the cultivation of Nelumbo). - Du Halde, La Chine, vol. "The tuberous roots of this species resemble sweet potatoes and are starchy. The seeds have a particularly pleasant taste. FOOD NYMPHEACEA EURYALE FEROX Salisb. Ti-teou-mi. NUPHAR AUVENUM Ait. Ta-wah-pah. Northern American species. Its roots grow 4 or 5 feet deep in water, and Indian women pull them up and collect large quantities. The roots are 1 to 2 feet long, porous, sweet-tasting and glutinous; they are usually eaten boiled with wild birds, but often roasted. Large quantities of these roots are kept in homes as winter provisions. The plant is called Ta-wah-pah by the Dakota Indians. The seeds are an important foodstuff for the Klamath Indians of California, who pulverize them to make bread; they are also eaten toasted (1). NUPHAR MULTISEPALUM Engelm. Native to western North America. Mueller (Select extra tropical Plants) says it produces nutritious seeds with a taste reminiscent of broom seeds, which are used locally as food, but are particularly useful for feeding water fowl. NYMPHÆA LOTUS L. Castalia mystica Salisb. The ancient Egyptians made bread from its seeds. Its rhizome, sweet-tasting and very starchy, is eaten like a potato. NYMPHÆA EDULIS DC. N. esculenta Roxb. ; Castalia edulis Salisb. Indian species, with edible roots. NYMPHÆA RUBRA Roxb. Castalia magnifica Salisb. Indian species, with edible seeds and roots. In Senegal, the seeds of this species provide healthy food for the natives, especially in times of famine. They eat them raw, or boiled in the same way as millet flour, but after roasting them to give them a pleasant flavor. Floury rhizomes are also eaten after being cooked under ashes. Their flavor is reminiscent of the potato (De Lanessan, Les Plantes utiles des colonies françaises). Fam. des Solanées Perennial plant, with spindly, sarmentose or recumbent stems, the lower ones radicating; leaves small, unequal, petiolate; flowers white, solitary, rarely geminate, pendant. Calyx 2 to 3 millimeters in diameter, cupuliform, with five narrow divisions that persist at the base of the fruit; corolla tubular, white, 2 to 3 1/2 millimeters long by 2 to 3 wide, glabrous, with oblong linear divisions having their tips curved; berry oblong oval, 2 to 3 centimeters long by 1 centimeter wide, first green, then white, finally rust-colored near the stalk. This plant is found in Magella, in the pampas of Buenos-Ayres, Montevideo, southern Brazil. According to Dombey, the fruit is sour and can be eaten. Mr. Charles Barbier harvested the plant in the Buenos-Ayres pampas and was kind enough to give us the seed. Here is the information he gave us: "The fruits of the plant are perfectly edible. In La Plata, children seek them out and Fig. 57. - Rooster eggs (Salpichroa rhomboidea.) are feasted on. They are also made into a popular household jam, with the addition of sugar and herbs to make them more tasty. I enjoyed eating it. This plant is very hardy and productive. It's worth trying to improve it. The plant has long existed at the Muséum, but does not bear fruit there. To obtain fruit, it must be trellised against a wall at midday; it is then covered with thousands of small white berries resembling swallow eggs. Perennial because of its roots, it grows back each spring, and it's quite difficult to clear the soil of it. In August 1877, we presented Oufs de Coq to the Société d'Horticulture, under the name Withania origanifolia (1); in October of the same year, we exhibited (Henri Véniat, gardener) a Salpichroa plant laden with a multitude of its pretty white berries, and jams made from these fruits. Here's the recipe for these jams (Mamoz): "Cook the fruit in as many liters of water as kilograms of fruit, boiling for fifteen or twenty minutes. "Remove, strain through cheesecloth while hot; weigh and add equal weight of sugar, set on fire. "Add 20 to 50 grams of Japanese glue, depending on the consistency you wish to give the jam. "Cook until a drop placed on a plate holds together halfway. (1) This is the name under which the plant was cultivated in the botanical garden of the Muséum at the time. It was recommended as an ornamental plant, see Revue Horticole, 1893, p. 525; 1887, p. 328, then under the name Muguet des pampas (same journal, 1897, p. 504 and 529). "The grating of a Lemon is enough for 5 pounds of fruit; it is obtained by rubbing sugar on the Lemon: the sugar is then crushed, dissolved in a spoonful of water, which is poured over the jam at the last moment. It's important that the lemon doesn't cook. In our opinion, Rooster Eggs are more of a curiosity than a useful plant. This is also the conclusion of the notes published on this plant by Mr. Ed. André (see Revue horticole, 1883, p. 525, and 1887, p. 328). CONGO OLIVE SOLANUM OLIVARE Paill. et Bois. A close relative of S. Zuccagnianum, 1 m to 1r.50 high, non-spiny, with cylindrical, green, slightly rough stems, branched from the base; leaves oval, attenuated at both ends, with petiole about 3 cm long; blade 10 to 12 cm long by 5 to 6 cm wide, with uneven base, sinuous margins, covered over all its parts with star-shaped hairs. Peduncles 1-4 florets; calyx tubular, blade divided into 5 acute lanceolate sepals, externally covered with star-shaped hairs. Petals 5, white, once longer than the sepals. Flower 2 1/2 cm in diameter. Anthers coherent, opening at the apex with 2 pores; stigma slightly exceeding the stamens. Berry piriform, pendulous, slightly depressed at the apex and fitted with a small nipple, mostly smooth, sometimes with longitudinal grooves, coccine red, 6 centimeters long, measuring 2 centimeters at its widest diameter; white seed reminiscent of Piment in shape and color. Fig. 58. - Olivaria del Congo (Solanum olivare). This plant is mainly ornamental; the numerous red fruits it bears give it a strong resemblance to certain chillies grown in flowerbeds. The seeds were given to us by Mr. Pobéguin, attached to the Brazza mission, who told us that the fruits are eaten like peppers in the Congo. For us, the Olivaire is of no interest from a food point of view. OLLUCO Ulluco, Melloco, Papa lisa; Uyuca or Rouba (Venezuela). ULLUCUS TUBEROSUS Lozano. Chenopoda family. Perennial plant. Tubers rounded, hardly exceeding, in our climate, the volume of a large Walnut, pink, very smooth, with yellowish flesh, mucilaginous or starchy when the tuber reaches full maturity, developing on rhizomes which originate at the base of the stems; these, branched and creeping, take root at every point where they touch the ground; the leaves are alternate, thick, spatulate, shiny green, with a fairly long, reddish petiole. Flowers are small, greenish, axillary and do not ripen their seeds in our climate. Native to the Andes. It is with regret that we devote a chapter of this book to a plant whose cultivation has been experimented for three years, with great care, at the Muséum, by M. Louis Vilmorin, in the garden of the Société nationale d'Horticulture, etc., and whose rare tubers have been unanimously declared inedible. But Ulluco belongs to the history of attempts to introduce exotic food plants, and it is for this reason alone that it has a place here. We shall therefore limit ourselves to indicating the sources from which we can, if the desire arises, draw all possible information on the Chilean Chenopodea. Mr. Ed. André has kindly sent us the following note on the plant he observed at the Pasto market (New Granada): "Ollocos (Ullucus tuberosus). This vegetable, generally detestable, is represented here by several varieties: violet, white, yellow, green and salmon pink, among which several are really not bad and can almost equal Ocas." In 1895, Messrs Vilmorin-Andrieux and Cte sent us tubers of a plant they had received from Mr. Bourgoin, of Merida (Venezuela), under the name Uyuca or Rouba, accompanied by the following letter: "I have the pleasure of sending you some tubers of Uyuca or Rouba. These tubers are edible and, when cultivated, quite floury; they taste more or less like potatoes, but the family to which they belong is different; it's an Oxalidae. They are mainly used candied in vinegar with chillies. "I believe that this crop would be perfectly successful in France, as the plant is only found here on the high mountains, in what we call the cold country (average temperature of -10 to 12°)". We planted these tubers in the spring of 1896, and they developed leafy stems which enabled us to recognize them as Olluco. The tubers were so much larger and of such a different shape to those we had grown up until then, that we had not had the idea of attaching them to this plant when they came to us.
