GABON OLOMBE SOLANUM PIERREANUM Paill. et Bois. Solanaceae family. Annual plant, erect, about 1 meter high, completely inert, covered with white starry hairs on all parts: stems, leaves, peduncles, calyx, outer part of corolla. Stem cylindrical, branched, blackish-purple. Leaves alternate, first purple, then green, except for the veins, which retain their primilive color; petiole about 5 centimeters long; blade oval, acuminate, 10 to 15 centimeters long, measuring 6 to 8 centimeters at its widest point, almost entire or sinewy, with very prominent veins on the underside. Peduncles opposite the leaves or most often arising from the internodes, about 2 centimeters long, sometimes uniflorous, usually bifurcated and bearing two flowers, rarely triflores. Calyx campanulate, 7 to 8 millimeters long, with 5 or 6 shallow, triangular divisions, accrescent after anthesis; corolla about 2 centimeters in diameter, with 5 or 6 spreading, slightly purplish-white divisions; stamens with very short threads, linear anthers, opening at the apex with two pores, about two-thirds the length of the corolla; pistil the same length as the stamens, with a straight style ending in an obliquely inserted stigma. Fruits sometimes solitary, more often geminate, rarely grouped in threes, borne on swollen pedicels Fig. 59. - Olombé from Gabon (Solanum Pierreanum) conical, pendulous, ribbed like those of S. Gilo Raddi, spherical, depressed at the bottom and top, 3 to 4 centimeters in diameter and 2 to 2 1/2 centimeters thick, colored greenish-red and flamed purple-brown. Olombé seeds were sent to the Museum by Mr. Pierre, Director of the Colonial Garden in Libreville (Gabon). They have been sent to us by Mr. Maxime Cornu. The fruits of this Solanum are apparently eaten by the Pahouins. In 1888, we first sowed the plants in March, under a frame. The plants vegetated well, flowered abundantly, but did not set fruit. In 1889, we sowed again, under the same conditions, but transplanted most of the plants outdoors. This time we obtained a few bright red fruits, flamed black, which we tasted and which did not seem usable to us; but we must say that the plant, sent by us to Mr. Latour-Marliac, of Temple-sur-Lot, was covered at his place with pretty fruits and showed itself, under the climate of Lot-et-Garonne, decidedly ornamental. Olombé is a new Solanum that will be cultivated beyond the Loire, if not for the table, at least for garden decoration. RUMEX ABYSSINICUS Jacq. ? Fam. Polygonaceae. 445 The seeds of this plant were entrusted to us by Mr. Maxime Cornu, professor-administrator at the Muséum, who had received them from Mr. Blaise, a ship's lieutenant, with the following indication: "Oseille Pahouine. Large size. Excellent for eating. From around Donguilah (Gabon)". M. Cornu presented four plants to the Sociélé nationale d'Horticulture de France on July 2, 1896. We sowed these seeds under a frame in the spring of 1896. The resulting young plants were planted outdoors in the first days of June. They grew vigorously, so much so that by autumn they had reached over 1 metre in height and produced abundant, ample, tender, slightly acidic leaves. The plants were ready to bloom, but the first cold spells came before the flower stems could develop. By this time, the clumps had become very large. We lifted some of them into pots to overwinter under glass; unfortunately, they died before the warm weather returned. In 1897, Mr Grosdemange, head of the nursery and vegetable garden, took cuttings from them, which took root without difficulty. Pahouine sorrel is a plant which, thanks to its abundant production of leaves that can be used like ordinary sorrel, is destined to be of great service in hot climates. It is particularly worthy of our colonists' attention. We cannot guarantee the specific identification of this plant, of which we have only been able to observe the leaves and seed. However, from what we have been able to see, Pahouine sorrel is, if not Rumex abyssinicus, at least a very close relative. R. abyssinicus Jacq. (R. arifolius Ait.) is native to Abyssinia, but exists, naturalized (?) in Réunion; according to M. Cordemoy (Flore de la Réunion, p. 285), it abounds in the Plaine des Palmistes and is common in the middle region. On Reunion Island, the plant is known as Oseille sauvage, and its leaves are used as a vegetable. Canaigre sorrel RUMEX HYMENOSEPALUS Torr. Another species of sorrel, Canaigre (Rumex hymenosepalus Torr.), deserves the attention of growers in the Mediterranean region. Dr. Trabut, Government Botanist, Algiers, has published a note on this plant in the Journal de la Société nationale d'Horticulture de France, 1896, p. 383, in which he highlights its industrial and nutritional merits. Canaigre is a Patience native to southern California, Arizona and northern Texas. In these regions, this large sorrel covers immense areas; it vegetates during the winter, then disappears during the dry season. Its tuberous root is reminiscent of that of the Dahlia; it contains very high proportions of tannin, and factories are already preparing highly esteemed tannin extracts on the spot. Mr. Trabut has continued to study the acclimatization of Canaigre in Algeria, where this Polygonaea seems to have found a suitable climate. Struck by the extent of the plant's foliage and its abundance, he had the leaves prepared in the manner of sorrel and, he says, obtained a very acceptable dish, reminiscent of ordinary Patience (Rumex Patientia), which, in his opinion, is too little used. I don't think," he adds, "that this new vegetable is destined to take a major place in consumption, but it should be mentioned; and, because of its hardiness, it deserves a place in the gardens of growers in southern countries. Canaigre is slightly laxative; in this respect, it can be sought after as a hygienic food. Canaigre is grown at the Muséum d'Histoire Naturelle, where it flowered and fructified in 1897. For more complete information on this plant, please consult the following works: Christy, New commercial plants, n° 5 (1882), p. 29-35; Bulletin of miscellaneous information, Gardens Kew, 1892, p. 63-69; id. 1894, p. 167-168; Botanical Magazine, sept. 1895. upo ARalla coRpaTA Thunb., Fl. Jap., p. 127. A. edulis Sieb. et Zucc, Fl. jap. i, p. 57, tab. 25. Japanese name: Udo; Chinese names: Dotooki, Doku quatz, Dosjen, Udo, (Kæmpf. amænit.). Arahaceae family. Root perennial, long, fleshy, thick; stems numerous, herbaceous, often reaching over I meter in height, adults glabrous, young pubescent; petioles sheathing, with dilated, gutter-shaped base; compound leaves, with oval or heart-shaped, acuminate, saw-toothed, pubescent leaflets; small, greenish flowers in globose umbels with a small oligophyll involucre, arranged in a compound cluster. Calyx adherent to ovary, with very short, 5-toothed blade; corolla with 5 petals; 5 stamens; ovary inferere, with 5-10 lodges; 5 divergent styles; fruit drupaceous, blackish-brown, crowned by calyx and styles, with 5-10 monosperm pits. "Udô flowers in August; its fruit ripens in November. Its herbaceous stems perish with the first frosts of winter. "This plant probably comes from China, where it is used as a sudorific remedy; it is cultivated throughout Japan, in gardens and fields. "It is mainly cultivated for its root, which has a pleasant, aromatic and bitter taste, and is eaten in winter seasoned in the manner of our Scorsonères. The young stems serve as a delicious vegetable. "As the plant thrives throughout Japan, it would acclimatize just as well in our gardens; it would therefore be desirable that, cultivated here, it should increase the number of our vegetable foods by a pleasant, fine and nourishing dish". (Siebold and Zuccarini, Flora Japonica, I, p. 57). In the Note explicative des objets exposés par l'École agricole et forestière de Komaba à l'Exposition universelle de 1889 (Ministry of Agriculture and Trade, Tokio, Japan), we read: "Oudô (Aralia cordata). To cultivate it, we make pits and put its roots in them, after having given the fertilizer of horse dung, spread on the surface of the earth. This operation takes place during the very cold season. The roots sprout white buds. It's a vegetable with a delicate taste and a pleasant smell." Our excellent correspondent, Dr. H***, wrote to us on April 25 1879: "The only vegetable I regret not having brought with me is an Araliaceae called Oudô, Aralia cordata de Thunberg, in Japan. The young shoots are harvested wild in spring and eaten cooked, just as we eat celery and cardoons. It is very strong and very bad, as is unbleached Celery; but Oudô, cultivated and blanched under leaves or litter, is very good, and is eaten in the spring." It will be noted that our correspondent does not mention, as Siebold did, the use of Oudo roots in cooking. 28 As early as 1879, we asked Japan for Aralia cordata seedlings. We received seeds, not plants, in the spring of 1883; but to obtain germination of Aralia seeds, they must be sown immediately after the harvest, so ours did not germinate. Thanks to the kind assistance of MM. Vilmorin-Andrieux et Cie, we received, a few years later, ten perfectly healthy plants of the Japanese variety. The Oudò thrived perfectly at Crosnes, proving just as hardy and easy to grow as the North American Aralia racemosa, from which it hardly differs, grown in all botanical gardens, where it is so invasive that it is difficult to keep clumps to a minimum. We stretched the plant, and the shoots obtained had the appearance of medium-sized asparagus; they were white and very appetizing. Unfortunately, the tasting was not favorable; we found them to have a turpentine flavor, admittedly weak, but too pronounced for this vegetable to be adopted in Europe. This failure is all the more regrettable as the plant is very productive and would come to our gardens almost care-free. Chopped and cooked young leaves have given us an acceptable aromatic vegetable, but of no particular merit. Another Araliaceae, Stilbocarpa polaris Decaisne et Planchon (Aralia polaris Hombron et Jacquinot), deserves experimental cultivation in our climate. This perennial plant is native to New Zealand and the Auckland and Campbell Islands. Its long rhizomes are fleshy, sweet and edible. Information on this plant can be found in Flore de la Nouvelle-Zélande, by Hooker f., vol. I, p. 95, and Mueller, Select extra-tropical Plants, p. 324. OXALIS CRENATA Jacq. Family Oxalidae. Perennial; roots tuberous, the shape and diameter of a large walnut; stems ascending, succulent, twining; leaves trifoliate, with obovate leaflets, a little thick; in May-September, flowers large, Fig. 60 - Crenellated Oxalis (Oxalis crenata). yellow, in umbels longer than the leaves; petals crenate. Oxalis crenata was imported from Lima to England around 1829, from where it soon spread to France. In Peru, it is known as Oca. In 1850, Mr. Boursier, French consul in Quito, sent the Museum the red Oca, a variety considered in Peru to be much preferable to the yellow Oca. We remember growing Oxalis crenata in the Landes department of France, and obtaining very good harvests without difficulty. We punctually followed the instructions provided to us by the Maison rustique and by Le Bon Jardinier. We have borrowed from the latter the cultivation methods that have worked well for us: "When properly cultivated, the plant produces a quantity of small yellow tubers that rarely reach the volume of a hen's egg, but whose number is sometimes enormous. This large-scale multiplication is achieved by ridging, or rather by continuous layering, as we'll explain shortly. The tubers contain 10 to 12% starch, but differ essentially in quality from those of the potato, to which Oxalis has been inappropriately compared; they are easy to cook and provide a healthy, light, fairly pleasant food, with a slightly acid taste, which can be removed by cooking them three-quarters of the way through in water. The leaves and tops of the shoots can be used in place of sorrel, of which they have the same flavor; in Lima, they are also eaten in salads. Cultivation is not difficult; the tubers can be brought forward in layers in March, for planting at the beginning of May, or planted permanently around mid-April, or planted by cuttings, which take root with great ease. Soft, light, well-amended soil is best. The distance between plants should be around 1 metre; a single row in a 1n.30 bed is enough to fill the soil at the end of the season. According to Mr. Guesnet, you should start buttering as soon as the shoots are Om, 08 to Om, 10 in length; you first mound in the center, spreading them apart, to force them into a horizontal direction; then, as they lengthen, you refill them moderately with new soil and continue regularly until September, when the tubers start to form.The tubers are pulled out as late as possible, when the stems have been destroyed by frost; the tops can then be cut off and the clumps covered with dry leaves; the tubers keep and even thrive under this covering, as M. Redouté has found. Torn up, they keep very well through the winter, kept in a healthy place and buried in very dry sand. "The red Oca, received by the Museum in 1850 from Mr. Boursier, French consul in Quito, is a variety of Oxalis crenata, from which it differs in the color of the tuber, whose skin is a bright carmine red, and in its slightly spindlier stems, colored reddish purple and remarkable for their extreme flexibility. Its tubers seem to us to be neither larger nor more abundant than those of the old yellow variety, although, according to M. Boursier, the red Oca is considered in Peru to be far superior to the yellow Oca". We have said that we have grown Oxalis crenata in the Landes department, and that we have done so with great success. We must add that the tasting had not been favorable to it and that we had given up its cultivation; but we had tried it in 1849, and Weddell's Note, which is of such keen interest and which we are going to reproduce, had not yet appeared. We therefore absolutely reserve our opinion. Weddell's note (1). - I hasten to say that, to my taste, this vegetable is almost the equal of the potato when properly prepared. The way to grow Oxalis is well known, so I'll deal without preamble with the question of the treatment the tubers require to become edible: "When Ocas are pulled, they are always more or less acidic; however, their degree of acidity differs with the varieties. As a general rule, white varieties are much less acidic than red varieties. Whatever the variety in question, sufficiently prolonged exposure to the sun constantly leads to the disappearance or more or less complete metamorphosis of its acidic principle. In a word, something quite analogous to the ripening of fruit takes place. A white Oca (Oca bianca) treated in this way no longer retains any trace of acidity, and is in no way inferior to a good potato. "Exposure to the sun, whose sole purpose is to destroy the acidic principle of Ocas, should last from six to ten days, and even a little longer, depending on the variety of Oca. The operation is best performed in large woolen bags. The changes that take place in the tissue of the tubers seem to be facilitated by this means, and they can be turned over more conveniently; but the bag must contain few enough tubers that, when spread out on the ground, they form only a very thin layer under their envelope. (1) Remarks on some edible tubers, by M. Weddell, assistant naturalist at the Muséum (Revue horticole, 1852, p. 148). "When exposure to the sun is prolonged much longer, for several months for example, tuber ripening goes much further. The Oca then loses most of its juices and takes on the consistency of a prune, while acquiring a very pronounced sweet taste; this is what is known as Caui." I don't need to tell you that, in the two preparations just mentioned, you must be careful not to let the tubers freeze. "On the contrary, jelly is the main agent in the preparation known as Caia, which I'd like to talk about. Caia or Chuno de Oca is made in the same way as Chuno blanc des Pommes de terre, with the difference that after the tubers have been frozen, they are not macerated in running water, but in still water. Under these circumstances, the Oca becomes partially corrupted and, when dried out, retains an odor and taste that I will simply compare to those of some cheeses. The Indians consume a great deal of Caia, which they no doubt find very tasty. As for me, I can in no way recommend it. I've already stated my opinion on the other forms of Oca, and I won't go into them again. However, I think it useful to point out that in Bolivia, the tubers of this Oxalis, in addition to their intrinsic qualities, have another which is of no small importance: they are multiplied more abundantly than potatoes themselves in La Paz; they cost at most half as much as potatoes. On the one hand, in the Paris climate, Oralis crenata can only be harvested in October, and its tubers are unpleasantly acidic. On the other hand, the only way we are taught to eliminate this acidity is to expose the crop to the sun for at least six to ten days. How can we count on good weather and sufficient warmth in October? It seems clear to us that the experiment should have been carried out on the Mediterranean coast and not elsewhere, and that time and effort were wasted in carrying it out on the outskirts of Paris. It's true that in 1835 we learned that a skilled English gardener named Pringle had found a cultivation method that enabled Oxalis to be harvested in August, and Poiteau, the eminent gardener, botanist and painter, not concealing the jealousy he felt as a French horticulturist, exclaimed: "As for me, I won't sleep again until I've found Mr. Pringle's secret, or at least obtained a result similar to his". What has become of gardener Pringle's discovery? Do the English accept the acidity of Oxalis? The English sun, whose reputation is well established, certainly can't make it go away. Mr. Ed. André, on his trip to New Granada, collected some interesting information on Oxalis crenata. He kindly shared with us the following lines from his travel notes: "Ocas de Pasto are widely cultivated and consumed in these cold regions, and there are more than ten varieties. They should be cultivated more seriously here than is usually the case; don't forget to dry them in the sun to develop their sweetness. Cooked in water, they are very good; they are peeled by turning them by hand in a servielte. Their starch is very fine and they have a pleasant, tangy taste. Varieties are round or long, pink, white, violet, yellow, wine-red, etc. ". Every year, a few tubers of crenellated Oxalis can be seen on the shelves of exotic produce retailers and a few grocers. The red variety is offered as an excellent vegetable. One of us saw some beautiful red tubers on the market in Pau. In the country, they are eaten simply boiled or fried whole in the pan. We don't expect the sale of Oxalis to expand much in Paris. The price will always be too high for its merit. We have read what Messrs. Weddell and Ed. André have to say about sun-drying Oxalis tubers. In our trials, instead of the sun, we used the oven, as is done for prunes. We ate Oxalide fresh, in compote and cooked salad; then, dry, in the same two ways. We found no appreciable difference. If pruned tubers are soaked in water for a few hours, they regain their original volume. They can then be used in compote with wine and sugar, like Prunes, which they taste a little like; or in salad, with a slightly spicy seasoning. In both cases, they are absolutely tender. OXALIDE DE DEPPE OXALIS TETRAPHYLLA Cav. var. Deppei; O. Deppei Sweet. Family Oxalidae. Perennial plant. Root tuberous, napiform, surmounted by small bulbiform buds; petioles 12 to 15 cm long, pubescent in youth, glabrous thereafter, bearing four wedge-shaped, obcordate leaflets, 3 R Fig. Gl. - Deppe's Oxalid. sessile, ciliate, glabrous and purple-zoned above, pubescent below; from May to September, umbels of eight to ten cherry-red flowers, whereas they are purplish pink in the species type, higher than the leaves; petals rounded at the apex. Deppe's Oxalis is native to Mexico. It was brought to England in 1827 by Mr. Barclay, and six years later, towards the end of 1833, Mr. Jacquin aîné introduced it to France, seeing it flower for the first time in 1834. Growing it is not difficult. According to Le Bon Jardinier, it requires rich, well-tilled soil; it multiplies easily, producing bulblets in large numbers around the root collar, which, when planted in April in light soil, become new individuals. We have cultivated Deppe's Oxali le on the assumption that it could be considered a useful vegetable plant, but from this point of view, it seems worthless. The roots are barely edible. We have prepared the leaves as sorrel, and, if this distinction is possible, we have found them to be rather sour than acidic, and in short not very pleasant; but you will see, from the extracts we are about to read from a letter from Mr. Morren, director of the garden at the University of Liège, dated January 10, 1845, to what extent we can differ in opinion. For eight years now," says Mr. Morren, "Deppe's Oxalis has appeared with honor on our tables in more than one form... We use its leaves in sorrel, its flowers in salads and its roots in asparagus. "The young leaves, taken from the center of the clumps, make excellent sorrel, with a pure taste and no crackling of calcareous salts (lime oxalate); their use in soups and sauces cannot be faulted by the most discerning palate. When mixed with lettuce, the cut flowers, even with the tips of their stems, make a substitute for vinegar, with a more exquisite and frank acidity that is even preferred by a delicate palate; this is for the use of the plant during the summer. "Around mid-October, or even in November, we remove the plant from the ground and find around sixty bulbils at its collar, which, when stored, serve to propagate Oxalis; below these bulbils, we discover two to four large roots, 10 to 20 centimetres long and varying from 2 to 5 in diameter. These roots, fusiform like young carrots, have a certain transparency that brings to mind the Salep of the Turks. I cannot share the opinion that these roots are similar to Topinambours and Scorsonères; but, in these matters, it is very difficult to agree: de gustibus non est disputandum. Oxalis is similar to asparagus and young white carrots, but the taste is more delicate and, if you'll pardon the expression, a little oriental. We cook these roots in water and salt, and eat them Dutch-style, with a sauce of fresh melted butter and egg yolks. Friends of mine have assured me that these roots have had a similar effect on their bodies to that experienced when Salep is taken after a debilitating diet. I can assure you, moreover, that I have always found myself enjoying the use of this excellent plant at all my meals. Faced with these assertions, made with such assurance and conviction, one remains disconcerted and can only repeat the de gustibus that the author of the letter opposes in advance to his contradicts. Enthusiasts will appreciate. We have no hesitation in recommending the cultivation of Deppe's Oxalis as a border plant. The plant will be right at home in the gardens of the curious. Philippi sent us some tubers of this plant, which grows wild in Chile and the Argentine Republic. In Chile, the tubers are eaten under the vernacular name Papilla, a diminutive of Papa (Potato). The plant grew vigorously and its long, trailing rhizomes soon completely invaded the frame in which it had been planted. By midsummer, it had given birth to numerous pretty lilac flowers with a very pleasant fragrance. In autumn, we harvested a good number of small, rounded tubers, as big as small potatoes, but with a strong, unpleasant flavor. CUSPID LEAVES Siebold's Knotweed. Cuspid knotweed. POLYGONUM CUSPIDATUM Sieb. et Zucc, Fam. nat. 731; P. Sieboldi Hort (not Meissn.); P. multiflorum Buerg. Fam. Polygonaceae. Japanese perennial, cheerful green, with a very tracing stump. Stems 1 m to 1.50 m and over high, initially simple and straight, then branched, arching and spreading horizontally towards the tip, streaked and washed with reddish; branchlets distichous, spreading or flexuous, also streaked or washed with reddish, as are the petioles. Leaves alternate, distichous, petiolate, broadly ovate or ovate-oblong, acute, truncated at the base. Flowers white, arranged in axillary clusters, spindly, forming upright, spreading or nodding paniculate fascicles. These are followed by elegant, pinkish-white fruits, usually sterile. Flowers from July to August. Polygonum cuspidatum has tempted several tasters. Mr. J. Weber was the first to recommend this plant for its food uses (1). In spring, when he had the leaves removed, he discovered a dozen beautiful white shoots, about the size of a finger. He found them so appetizing that he had the idea of having them prepared like asparagus, and, he says: "My hope was (1) Revue horticole, 1873, p. 393. in no way disappointed; they were sweet and very pleasant-tasting. However, as they are very tender, they only need to be cooked for a moment". L. Van Houtte was not so satisfied; he writes in La Flore des serres, XVI, page 16: "Let's say it very quickly, for the sake of our conscience, the trial we did was disastrous. We needed God knows what reagents to stun our palates with the detestable taste of this famous Knotweed! We etiolated the young shoots of Cuspid-leaved Loosestrife and obtained many pretty, asparagus-shaped etiolates, full, tender and slightly acidic in flavor. Polygonum sacchalinense gave us a similar result. We have, however, relegated these plants to the third series of our etiolate experiments (1), and we don't think they can make any higher claims. PE-TSAI Chinese cabbage. BRASSICA CHINENSIS L. Cruciferae family. Annual plant. Lower leaves oblong, almost entire, obtuse, glabrous; stems lanceolate, amplexicaul. Calyx larger than petal tabs. (1) Nouveaux légumes d'hiver, by A. Paillieux and D. Bois. Paris, Librairie agricole de la Maison rustique, rue Jacob, 26. In 1840, M. Pépin, head gardener of the botanical school at the Muséum, published a fairly extensive memoir on the cultivation and use of Pé-tsai, in which he reports on the results of his multiplied seedlings grown under various conditions. He reports on the opinion he formed of the plant, considered as a vegetable, through repeated tastings. Fig. 62 - Pe-tsai (Chinese cabbage). This memoir was presented by its author to the Société d'Horticulture. We refer the reader to it, and reproduce only a small part of it: "It was some twenty years ago," says M. Pépin, "that Pé-tsai was known under the name Brassica sinensis in botanical gardens, and was considered a simple collector's plant; but for the last three years that M. l'abbé Voisin, a specialist in the cultivation of Chinese cabbage, has been working on it. Abbé Voisin, Attorney General for Foreign Missions, has informed us that this species of cabbage is highly cultivated and esteemed in China, its 30 reputation as a culinary plant has been rehabilitated in France, and M. Ducros de Sixt, lawyer at the Royal Court of Paris, was the first to give us some details on Pé-tsai, its cultivation and uses in China. We believe this to be an error, and that our missionaries had been publicizing the importance of Pe-tsai cultivation in the Chinese empire for over a hundred years. Fig. 63. - Pak-choi, a variety of Pé-tsai. Mr. Pépin goes on to say: "In the spring of 1838, a large number of seedlings were sown, and I know from many people who were interested in studying this new cruciferous plant that, in general, they were not very satisfied, and that nowhere did Pé-tsai peel, a quality that is said to be constant in China". Here, the error is serious, and the results obtained by the author of the memoir must have made him despair by enlightening him. Indeed, the Pé-tsai does not apple, nor does it show any inclination to do so, either in China or in France. In his work entitled Description de l'empire de la Chine, published in 1735, Father J.-B. du Halde pointed out, as a peculiarity of Chinese cabbage, that it does not form an apple like European cabbage. Mr. Pépin goes on to say: "As spring sowing did not give me any different results from those I had already obtained in previous attempts to grow Pe-tsai, I thought I should try autumn sowing; only, this first attempt had already shown me that Pe-tsai is annual and not biennial, and even less perennial, as it is said to be in China". As we can see, the author's information on Pe-tsai was highly inaccurate, which in no way detracts from the experimenter's merit. Shortly after the publication of the above-mentioned memoir, an unsigned note appeared in the Revue Horticole, volume IV, 1838-41, page 112, which we believe must be attributed to Poiteau, then editor-in-chief of the Revue. This note is so complete, so interesting and so probably forgotten today, that we have no hesitation in reproducing most of it: ".... Mme Bréon, wife of M. Bréon, horticulturist-grain grower, who lived on Bourbon Island, where Pé-tsai is grown, told us that it was a resource during the hot, dry season, which it withstood more easily than other plants of the same use. During the rest of the year, Pé-tsai is used, not because it is preferable to other plants, but simply as a change of food, even when its quality is inferior. In Bourbon, you'll never see it eaten. "On the subject of Chinese cabbage, we have researched and read extensively in works on China; we will report what we have found most explicit on this subject in the writings of missionaries and travelers. "Rice, cabbage and a little garlic or onion instead of meat, with a common tea beverage, are often all that make up the meals of Chinese peasants and workers. Although our Lettuces and Romaines are well known to them, preference is given to Pe-tsai, which holds a distinguished place among Chinese vegetables. "The best Pe-tsai are found in the northern province, where the first frosts make them very tender; their abundance is almost unbelievable. During the months of October and November, in the morning, it is sometimes difficult to pass through the immense number of small carts and wheelbarrows loaded with them, which crowd the gates of Peking and Hang-tchou-fou. The Chinese custom is to preserve them in salt, or to marinate them and cook them with Rice, which is naturally fortinsipid. "There are three types of Pé-tsai (1): 1° Pé-tsai, with white, thin, very tender leaves, which tingle like Roman lettuces when a little help is given; 2° Nisontou, i.e. beef strawberry, because its leaves are crenate, very large, fleshy, full of juice and quite mild; 3° Purplish, whose leaves are very loose, smooth, very tender and of a pleasant taste, but mixed with a hint of bitterness, like some species of Lettuce. Among these three species, we also distinguish those (1) This is a missionary speaking. whose leaves are elongated like a snake's tongue, or rounded, cut or plain, with flat ribs like Beets (Poirées), blond or with rounded ribs like Lettuces, etc. (1). We won't insist on the details of these differences. Our cabbages, chicories and so many other vegetable plants long ago taught us in the West that the same plant appears in a prodigious number of forms, sometimes changing beyond recognition. It is inevitable that Pétsai, being grown in such a vast empire and cultivated so diversely, should change from place to place and take on different forms. "The climate, the season and the nature of the land make a big difference between Pé-tsai and Pé-tsai in terms of taste, quality and size. Some always taste bland and almost insipid, while others have a very pleasant taste and a kind of natural perfume. Some are healthy and wholesome, while others are not. In some places, they cannot be eaten young, or even before the first frost, while in others, they can be eaten at any time, depending on how long you want to wait for them. The most careful cultivation always leaves them mediocre in some cantons, whereas in others they come into their own. They grow visibly and swell to weigh from 10 to 12 pounds, to 18 and 20. (1) Pé means white, tsai means vegetable; thus, Pé-tsai is a white vegetable. Of the three species of Pé-tsai cited in the leaflet, two are not worth accepting. The third differs from our Cardes-Poirées only in that the Chinese variety is more tender and less bland, wider and longer than ours. This species has been successful in France; the other two would be equally successful if they were worth trying to cultivate. (These observations are not ours. - P. B.). "The Pé-tsai most esteemed in Peking are those from the area around the small town of Ngan-sun; they are, in fact, the ones preferred for the table of the emperor and his entire family. European gourmets would like to cook and season cabbage in a way that would flatter their delicacy; Chinese gourmets, on the other hand, either through prejudice or finesse of taste, reason about Pe-tsai as they do about venison and game, claiming that the more seasoning you give them, the more they lose their own flavour. We will candidly confess our profound incapacity and ignorance on this serious subject, and confine ourselves to recounting that, while on duty at the palace, we found Ngan-sun's Pé-tsai very good and very delicate, cooked simply in broth and with no seasoning other than salt. Our testimony will undoubtedly prove nothing, but we give it for what it is worth. "Our Europeans in Canton call Pé-tsai Chinese cabbage. Let them say why. It seems to us that it requires less smoky soil than our cabbages. When you want to grow good Pé-tsai rather than big Pé-tsai, and to grow them in their own time rather than pushing their growth, you need to choose an uncovered soil that's damp rather than dry. Low-lying soils that are not too swampy are very favorable. When it comes to choosing fertilizers, ashes of various grasses and powder are best suited to them. "Pé-tsai trees only set seed the year after they are planted. The Chinese take the same precautions to obtain seeds as we do for cabbages. They keep a few selected plants in a greenhouse or other sheltered spot, and when spring comes, they replant them in a sunny spot, watering them until they bloom and set seed. The seed is usually ripe by June or July. Gardeners here are divided on what to sow. Some want the new seed to produce stronger, more vigorous and easier-to-grow Pétsai; others claim that those sown from the previous year's seed, well preserved in the air, in an open, north-facing spot, produce more tender, more delicate Pé-tsai that are easier to peel (1). "In the southern provinces, we sow Pétsai in all seasons, and they come in well. When you want to have them in late spring, summer and all autumn, you need to choose well-watered soil and, as far as possible, an eastern exposure. In the northern provinces, such as Chan-tong and Pétché-li, where they are incomparably better and more delicate, they are sown on a bed in mid-July or August, in the same way as cabbages. Once the heat wave has passed, they are transplanted with a string into holes made with a large planter, so that a little poudrette can be added. Those who are only interested in profit plant them in a checkerboard pattern, 7 or 8 inches apart, because Pé-Isai can be eaten at any period of growth. They split the rows as they grow, choosing those they want to leave behind and keeping only those they see are well on the way to reaching their best size. Those who only want to have beautiful Pe-tsai plant them, as we do cabbages, 1 1/2 feet apart. Whichever way they are planted, they should be watered as soon as possible to bind the roots and facilitate recovery. (1) This probably involves binding the leaves, a rather delicate operation due to their extreme fragility. - P. B. then plough, weed and boot them until they are in full flood and have reached the dews and cool nights of autumn. Autumn is admirable here (1) and almost as beautiful as in our Béarn region. The fine, mild weather is undoubtedly the main cause of the charming way in which the Pé-tsai grow at this time, as if at a glance; as long as the soil is not too parched, we don't bother watering them, and this does them no harm; however faded and withered they may appear in the high noon, the next morning we find their leaves erect, fresh and full of vigor, but they remain green or at most blond until the first cold. "On the other hand, when the first cold weather arrives, their broad ribs soften, and the rest of the leaf gradually turns a perfect white. "Some people tie their Pé-Isai like our Roman lettuces, to obtain a larger head and softer leaf ribs; but others leave them to their own devices and claim that Pé-tsai thereby lose in flavor, taste and fragrance what they gain in whiteness and tenderness. The truth is that in Ngan-sun and other places where they are so good, so tender and so excellent, they are not bound at all. "When the Pé-tsai are fully grown, they are from 2 to 3 feet high, and weigh, as we said, from 15 to 20 pounds. We wait until the frosts have macerated them a little before harvesting. Their first outer leaves suffer a little, but we don't worry about that, as they must remain in the field to form compost. (1) In Peking, no doubt. "As the Chinese are great herb eaters, Pé-tsai are a real harvest, especially for the northern provinces; for, although more southerly than Provence, the cold is so rigorous that gardening there is absolutely closed from mid-November to mid-February; free to our physicists to explain as they see fit, much more closed than in the most northerly parts of France. "We have found several ways of preserving the Pé-tsai harvest and making it last throughout the winter; some preserve them in salt and vinegar; others cook them halfway in the steam of boiling water or wilt them in the cold northern air, after removing the leaves, then pass them through mustard or ginger water, cutting them into pieces. "However, as these sparing or household inventions, which are perhaps not sufficiently well known in our countryside, always take away something of the good taste of Pé-tsai, a large quantity is kept fresh until spring. "There are two ways of doing this: the first is to expose them to the sun for a while to remove any excess moisture that might cause them to turn yellow, and then pile them up in a greenhouse or in specially dug ditches; the second is to plant them en masse in damp river sand, at the bottom of a greenhouse. Those who don't want to keep them that long lay them flat on soil that's neither too dry nor too damp, and, after throwing a bit of chopped straw over them, cover them with a foot of dry earth; those who have a convenient place for it hang them by the foot, like cabbages, and as close together as possible. Moreover, the industry benefits so well from all these ways of preserving Pe-tsai, that they are available in Peking all winter, and at good prices. Consumption is so prodigious in this immense city that we don't dare say what the total must be. We will now say what we think of Pétsai. Our personal experiments have not given us satisfactory results, but they have served to form our opinion. First of all, we shall rule out any summer cultivation. The rapidity with which the plant goes to seed before it is fully developed, the heat of the sun which prevents its ribs and leaves from becoming tender and white, the abundance of fresh vegetables preferable to Chinese cabbage, these various causes would make it unsuccessful to grow Pé-tsai during the summer. The obstacles are different, but equally serious, if you sow in August, to harvest in winter. From the end of September, vegetation and plant development slow down, and the cold soon comes to a complete halt. However, Pé-tsai should be sown at the end of summer, but it must be able to vegetate for a long time, and frosts must not prevent it from reaching the extraordinary size it acquires in China before winter. Roscoff, Cavaillon and Hyères all seem to lend themselves to winter cultivation of Chinese cabbage. We are inclined to believe that Roscoff and the whole of the region where the Gulf stream draws the temperature down would be particularly favorable. In this privileged, truly temperate region, the grower fears neither sunlight nor the rigors of winter. Pé-tsai would develop slowly and widely, and perhaps take an important place in the Paris consumer market. Our streets are often clogged with the immense quantity of cauliflowers sent to us from Finistère; but these, with their strong flavor and difficult digestion, are not suitable for everyone, and would often give way to the milder and lighter Pé-tsai. It would be desirable for the cultivation of the latter vegetable to be seriously experimented with in Brittany. According to Messrs Sagot and Raoul, Manuel des cultures tropicales, Pé-tsai could be of great service in hot countries, where it produces tender leaves that are a valuable resource. Various varieties of Chinese cabbage are said to have been introduced into the English and Dutch colonies in China and the Malay Archipelago. PHYSALIS PERUVIANA L., var. foliis subintegris; P. edulis Sims, Bot. Mag. tab. 1068; Alkekingi virginianum, fructu luteo. Feuillée, Pér. 3, p. 5, tab. 1. Solanaceae family. Perennial herb, Om, 50 to 0m, 70 high, covered with a dense down composed of simple hairs; stems erect, a little stiff; leaves tomentose, cordate, acuminate, entire; in April-October, yellow flowers, mottled with purple; violet anthers; calyx accrescent, vesicular, pale in color, more or less uniform, enveloping a berry the size of a Cherry, orange-yellow when ripe (1). We consider Physalis peruviana to be very (1) Miller, Dictionnaire des Jardiniers, 2nd ed. V, p. 604, describes, under the name Physalis peruviana, Nicandra physaloides, which is quite distinct from it. It is an annual plant, reaching heights of 1 m or more, glabrous, with campanulate, blue flowers and non-fleshy fruit. Nicandra is grown as a garden ornamental. It is superior to all other species of the genus, and we have pursued its propagation with all possible zeal. Its seeds came to us in 1878 from New Caledonia (1). Our excellent correspondent, Mr. V. Perret, director of the Dumbéa agricultural penitentiary, did not know the name of the plant and simply referred to it as a Solanea with Fig. 64. edible fruits. - Physalis du Pérou. but it seems that it had long been introduced into Oceania. In his interesting 1875 publication, Mémoires de la Société des sciences naturelles de Cherbourg, tome XIX, of which he kindly gave us one of the reprinted copies, Captain Jouan cites Physalis peruviana L. among the medicinal plants. Our Physalis is called Konini in the Marquesas Islands, and, according to Jardin, is used by the natives in compresses against headaches. But it is as an edible fruit, suitable for making pies, syrups, jams and various confectionery items, that the berries of P. peruviana are sought after and that the plant that produces them is cultivated in Peru, Cape Town, India, etc.. In a memoir entitled: Enumeracion de los generos y especes de plantas, etc., M. Martinet, professor of natural history in Lima, says: "We eat the fragrant fruits of Physalis peruviana". In the Manuel de jardinage pour le Bengale, which we sometimes quote, we find a chapter devoted to our plant, which we reproduce in full. Physalis peruviana is known in English as Peruvian cherry, Cape gooseberry. The manual states: "A perennial herbaceous plant native to Peru, naturalized in Cape Town and widely cultivated there. "The fruit, which exactly resembles that of the Alkekenge of English gardens, of which it is undoubtedly the closest relative, is enclosed in an appendix of dry leaves (1). "It is of a light amber color, of the exact volume and shape of the Cherry, and as delicious and useful as any of the products of the countryside. There is perhaps no fruit in the world that provides a more exquisite jam. "The seeds should be sown in May or June, and the plant put in place, in the open ground, in lines (1) The calyx develops at the same time as the fruit, covering it entirely and drying out before harvest. 4 feet apart and 2 feet apart. "The plants can thrive in ordinary garden soil, but preferably in soil that has received a little fertilizer. When they are about 8 inches tall, they should be buttressed at half their height. When they flower, it's a good idea to pinch the tips of the sprays so they don't spread out too much, and also to provide more food for the fruit. "The berries ripen in January and February. Although perennial, Physalis should be grown as an annual, and old plants, after bearing fruit once, should be uprooted and discarded. When the season is ripe, seed should be sown to make a new plantation. "The plant is delicate and doesn't tolerate much cold. I tried several times to grow it at Ferozepore, but without success. It grew vigorously throughout the warm season, but the cold would destroy my abundant harvest before it was ripe." The last paragraph of the English author's book is applicable to Physalis cultivation in the Paris climate, and just recently, a plantation that promised us 80-100,000 fruits gave us only 2,000 perfectly ripe. All the rest were destroyed by a 2-degree frost. It's in the South of France that Physalis cultivation will be most fruitful. If growers on the Mediterranean coast would be so kind, they could, with a few precautions, preserve the fruit throughout the winter, ship it to Paris in unlimited quantities and obtain a handsome price. Berries picked on March 7th in Antibes were sent to a member of the Société d'Acclimatation, who gave them to us. Their ripeness and freshness were impeccable. We'll briefly describe our cultivation methods. It should be noted that they are specific to the climate around Paris, and will have to be modified to the north and south of this region: We sow in March on a bed and under a frame. When the plant is ready, we put it into cups, still on a bed and under a frame, one plant per cup. Towards the end of May, we plant in the ground, 1.10 m apart in all directions. The plantation is hoeed twice during the season. We remove all the lower buds and keep only the upper shoots. We pinch off the shoots as soon as they start to flower. Harvesting begins in September and continues until the frosts. The fruit is not ripe until the calyx enveloping it has completely dried out. In our northern departments, Physalis peruviana cuttings should be taken at the same time as Pelargonium cuttings, overwintered alongside the latter and planted at the same time, when there are no more frosts to worry about. South of the Loire, they can be sown in nurseries in the open ground. On the Mediterranean coast, harvest throughout the winter, protecting the plants from frost. Harvested berries should be stored in a cool, dry place. They keep perfectly well for four months. Harvested in the South of France at the end of October, they will provide the basis for a very lucrative trade until March; we say very lucrative, because a good price will always be obtained for a fruit that keeps fresh throughout the winter. Physalis peruviana is extremely productive; we have seen vines loaded with over a hundred fruits. We are not exaggerating when we estimate that 400,000 berries will be produced per hectare in the Alpes-Maritimes, Var, Bouches-du-Rhône and Hérault departments. These berries travel well and arrive in Paris in perfect condition. Confectioners and confectioners will buy them, and once the competition has lowered prices, the working-class population, always eager for fruit, will take their turn. The ripe berries of P. peruviana, eaten in their natural state, are pleasant, but inferior to strawberries, cherries, etc. They are good in compotes. They are good in compotes, but the confectioner's and confectioner's industry makes them excellent. The confectioner prepares them with fondant and caramel. They make exquisite jams and syrups. The confectioner uses them to make flans and tarts. The T... house has made jams and syrup that are second to none in Paris. Maison B... sold 30,000 berries candied in fondant and caramel in the space of a season. The fruit used by these houses had been delivered to them by a friend of ours, an enlightened horticultural enthusiast, to whom we had supplied seeds. As we finish preparing the third edition of Potager d'un curieux, they continue to be sought after by confectioners, who run out every winter. 31 We believe that Physalis peruviana should be cultivated and propagated, and we are happy to see that our efforts to bring it into consumption have not been in vain. In their raw state, they are slightly acidic, with little or no fragrance of their own. We find them insignificant. In fritters, they are extremely acidic and unpleasant. In green sauce, after prolonged cooking, they are acidic but not excessively so, and as they can easily be kept on the board until January, and even well beyond, they could, during the winter, replace the tomato, which they are far from being worth. The Mexicans are said to make an excellent syrup from them, which they use against diseases of the respiratory tract. M. Balarce gave the formula to M. Annual plant, from Om40 to 0m50 centimeters high, covered on all its parts with a grayish pubescence formed of numerous, simple, short hairs. Stem erect, strongly angled, twiggy. Leaves alternate, sometimes opposite, very variable in shape, sometimes broadly oval, with a truncated or rounded base, sometimes obovate, more or less long attenuated, entire, irregularly denticulate or slightly sinuous at the edges, those of the main branches varying from 15 to 18 centimeters long by 10 to 12 wide, those of the secondary branches smaller; petioles 4 or 5 centimeters long. Hairy peduncle, about 1/2 centimetre long at anthesis, reaching 1 1/2 centimetres when the fruit is developed. Calyx campanulate, hairy, with 5 acute divisions. Corolla very small, yellow, campanulate, twice as long as the calyx, externally hairy, 5 to 7 millimeters in diameter. Stamens equal; anthers linear, purple, same length as the netting. Captive stigma. Fruit formed by an oval fruiting calyx, 5 or 6 cm long by 4 cm wide, 5-angled, slightly pubescent, especially on the veins that criss-cross it, inside which is a rounded, greenish berry the size of that of P. peruviana. We owe this new species to M. de Lafon, retired deputy commissioner of the navy, who brought it back from Costa Rica and kindly gave us some seeds, along with those of several food plants from that country. This Physalis had already been collected in Nicaragua by M. Lévy; it bears no. 244 in his collection and is in the herbarium of the Muséum d'histoire naturelle in Paris. Mr. Hemsley (Biologia Centrali-Americana, Botany, vol. Il, p. 419) has associated it with P. villosa Dunal (Prodromus, vol. XII, pars 1, p. 445); however, in this species, the corolla is rotaceous, yellow, spotted with brown, and much larger than that of our plant, which is campanulate and immaculate. Our Physalis has much more in common with P. lanceifolia Nees (Linnæa, 1831, p. 473), (DC. Prodr., XIII, 1, p. 447); it differs, however, in the shape of its leaves and the pubescence covering all its parts. We have grown P. Lafonii in the same way as P. peruviana, and at first admired its powerful vegetation; but this initial impetus came to a halt, and the plant was covered with fruits so abundant that their number exceeded that of the leaves. These fruits have not yet ripened, and we don't yet know whether it will be possible to harvest them in the open air in the Paris climate. PHYSALIS MINIMA LINNÉ M. Indeed, once you've tasted a berry, you'll never be tempted to come back for more. PHYTOLACCA DECANDRA L. Fam. Phytolaccaceae. Perennial, 2 to 3 meters high, with thick, fleshy roots, cylindrical, glabrous, furrowed stems; broad, oval-lanceolate, acute, barely mucronate, soft leaves. In August-September, flowers are first white, then purple, arranged in fairly long clusters, longer than the leaves; pedicels twice as long as the calyx, which has 5 deep divisions; stamens 10; single ovary, surmounted by 10 styles; fleshy fruit, often with 10 ribs. "The word Phytolacca, given by Linné to this plant, derives from a Greek word meaning plant, and from lacca, lacquer; and, indeed, all species of this genus have a red tint. Their leaves become red in the late season, as do their fruits, which are then full of a lacquer-red juice that dyers can fix to a certain extent with alumina sulfate and liquid ammonia. It is grown in Europe... D. °... It is native to North America, particularly Virginia. It has become naturalized in the southern parts of Europe, and even in France, in the West Indies, in the colonies of Martinique and Guadeloupe, in Santo Domingo (Haiti), and so on. "Spinach is eaten in place of Phytolacca, which Creole ladies seek out for their calalous. Some fraudsters color wine with the juice of its berries, which gives it a sharp, unpleasant taste (1)." Descourtilz is not the only one to present Phytolacca as a common food plant. William Darlington, in his Agricultural Botany (Philadelphia 1847), tells us that the young shoots of Phytolacca are a good substitute for Asparagus; that its ripe berries have even been used by confectioners to make pies of dubious merit. He adds, however, that the plant is treated as a weed by careful farmers. Pépin, in the Revue horticole, 1847, volume IX, page 218, claims that during the months of January and March, several Paris edible merchants sold bleached Phytolacca decandra stems. These stems, quite similar to Asparagus, are sold," he says, "in large quantities in the markets of the United States, where this plant has long been regarded as edible. M. Lakanal, who lived in Kentucky for over twenty years, assured him that it was considered an excellent vegetable (2). (1) Descourtilz, Flore des Antilles, vol. V, p. 32. (2) According to Nuttall, The genera of North America, t. I, p. 293, "the young shoots of the Thytotacca, previously boiled, are edible." "Several people in Paris have experimented with it, and have not found the dish to their taste; but it seems that this opinion is not general, since Phytolacca has become, momentarily at least, a culinary plant among our leading restaurateurs." In the Revue horticole, 1868, page 440, Mr. Jean Sisley published a note translated from the American Agriculturist, same year: "The sight of the long clusters of blackish-purple berries on this beautiful plant, ripening at the moment, reminds us of the excellence of its spring shoots. They are numerous, the size of a finger, with undeveloped leaves. These shoots, cut while still young and cooked like asparagus, are so delicate that anyone who has tasted them wants to eat more. "Since the shoots are good, taken from plants growing on the edges of paths and hedges, without cultivation, it is likely that they will be better when the plant is grown in gardens. We therefore recommend that instead of treating it as a weed, we cultivate it with care, and advise those who would like to try it out to transplant the roots in autumn, divide the large clumps and plant them 1 meter apart in well-tilled, smoked soil. "We are well aware that this plant is reputed for its medicinal qualities; but, in any case, the young shoots lose their medicinal properties when cooked, and we know a good number of people who have been eating them for years without the slightest inconvenience." The editor of the Revue adds: "We remind our readers, as we reported in our Chronique (1868, p. 283), that Phytolacca, thanks to the recommendations of M. Lacalm, is used in certain parts of Aveyron, the leaves as spinach, the stems as asparagus. "We should also point out that, in China, Phytolacca edulis, a dwarf plant which appears to have originated in that country, is used for the same purposes. M. Braconnot, from Nancy, has carried out experiments on P. decandra. He concluded from his experiments: "l° that potash exists in enormous proportions in this plant; 2° that its molten ashes can be used commercially as a fairly rich alkali; 3° that potash is saturated in the plant by an acid analogous to malic acid, but which differs in some respects; 4° that the berries can be fermented and distilled to produce a certain quantity of alcohol; 5° that the coloring matter can be used as a reagent; 6° that the lees can be used as food; 7° that the cultivation of Phytolakia can become a branch of industry for the production of potash. 100 pounds of ashes out provided 66 pounds 10 ounces 5 coarse of dried salt, containing 42 pounds of pure, caustic potash." (Annales de chimie, t. LXII, p. 71 ff.) After reproducing what our predecessors have said about P. decandra, we will briefly discuss our personal experiences. The plant is hardy. It is sown in nurseries and planted out when the young plants are mature. The clumps are divided in autumn, and planted 1 metre apart in all directions. In spring, when vegetation begins, place upside-down pots over the plants, after having blinded the holes, and harvest the shoots as soon as they have reached 15 to 20 centimetres in height. Cooked and served like asparagus, they are fleshy and tender, but bland. We ate them, albeit in small quantities, with no ill effects. The red juice of the Phytolacae berry is a popular purgative in the United States, but used in very small quantities, it is completely harmless. In our village, it has sometimes been used to give wine an artificial color. It is used in the same way in various countries. In Germany, it is used to make a preservable syrup for coloring various dishes. According to Madame Davidis, this syrup is prepared as follows (1): "The bunches of ripe Phytolacus berries, the cultivation of which is described in my Vegetable and Florist Garden (2), in the ornamental shrubs section, contain this admirable red juice which is sold as an expensive commodity in Berlin and other large cities, to color frothy sauces, white jellies and milk dishes, and which is prepared as follows: in late autumn, when the bunches have ripened to a glossy black, they are de-stemmed and pressed. To clarify the juice, it is left to stand for six to twelve hours in a narrow porcelain vase, then drawn off. Then put it in an earthenware pot that has contained no fat, with a third of its weight in crushed sugar, and let it boil a few times, while skimming, since longer cooking would turn its beautiful red color brown. (2) Ouvrage de Mme Davidis. cold, it is poured into properly washed, dried and sulphured medicine bottles, corked, sealed and kept in a cool place. ESCULENTA Maxim, Suppl. ad indicem seminum, anni 1868, quæ hortus botanicus imperialis petropolitanus, 1869, p. 23; P. esculenta Van Houtte, Flore des serres, IV, 1848, p. 398 bis; Picurnia esculenta Moq. in Van Houtte, loc. cit., IX, 1854, p. 236; P. pekinensis Hance, in Seem. Acad. 1859, april, p. 404; Miquel, Prol. fl. jap. p. 125, 301; P. octandra L., Spec. p. 631; Thunb. fl. jap. p. 189. Family Phytolaccacees. Perennial, 1 metre or more high, with erect stems, a little woody at the base, marked with warty lines; leaves briefly petiolate, lanceolate, acute, mucronate, narrowed at both ends, decurrent on the petiole; in June-September, hermaphrodite flowers, greenish-white, pedicellate, arranged in shortened clusters, briefly pedunculated, much shorter or as long as the leaves, with straight rachis, a little rough to the touch; stamens 7-8; carpels 5-7, inferiorly fused together. Convinced that Phytolacca esculenta justified its name, or that its natural pungency would disappear under the influence of weathering, we made a fine plantation of it, which we treated like Maritime Crambe. At the end of February, we milled each plant to a sufficient height and obtained beautiful shoots, pink, full, tender like Asparagus; but it was enough for us to eat two or three of them to have our mouths and throats on fire. Note published by Van Houtte in the Revue horticole (1851, vol. XIII p. 76): "For some years now, people have been striving to find substitutes for the old Spinach of our gardens, and the plants proposed have met with varying degrees of success. Here's one that more than meets our culinary needs, and that will replace the old Spinach. It's a Phytolacca whose seeds were sent to me from India, under the name P. esculenta (1). The plant grows to a height of around 1 meter; it is robust and highly branched. Its leaves are large; cooked and prepared in the usual way, these leaves have the advantage of melting half as much as spinach leaves; their flavor, extremely pleasant to the taste, has something aromatic about it, it is more pronounced and does not need to be enhanced by spices. This is the opinion of all the people who ate them at my house during the summer. "Phytolacca esculenta is perennial and multiplies abundantly. I remove the roots as cold weather approaches and keep them frost-free for replanting in March. I'm convinced that, warmed up, they will produce abundantly in less than a month. It will be, (1) The plant was cultivated in Glascow from 1836. 32 in any case, an excellent vegetable plant, which market gardeners will be able to make good use of." We have a large number of spinach substitutes, and the edible Phytolakia, considered as such, is of little interest to us. It would have been different if its shoots had provided us with a winter vegetable like those of Crambé maritime. We have seen above that there is nothing to be expected from them in this respect. PIPENGAIL LUFFA ACUTANGULA Seringe. Cucurbitaceae family. The plant is usually cultivated in tropical regions; it is native to India, where it bears the names Torui (Hindustan), Jhinga (Bengalese). According to Roxburgh, the fruit, half-developed, boiled and prepared with butter and salt, is not inferior to green peas. It is also considered a foodstuff on the west coast of Africa. Mr. Pierre, director of the Colonial Garden in Libreville, has sent us some seeds under the name Long Okra, which is given to him in Sierra Leone and Liberia. The plant is called M'Counive in Gabon. We received seeds from our colleague Mr. E. Harel, and from Dr. de Cordemoy. We sowed it on March 15 on a bed and under a frame, and kept only one plant in a single-panel box. The plant filled the box well and its stems spread outwards. We then raised them on large poles and we believe they would have easily reached a height of 4 meters, if we had been able to provide them with supports of that size. The first fruit appeared on July 18, four months after sowing, and many more followed until September 10, when a strong wind destroyed everything. We believe that four feet of Pipengaille, properly cared for, would be enough to provide a weekly dish for a family's table. We had some difficulty in getting the vegetable properly prepared, although Dr de Cordemoy had kindly given us the following recipe, which is in use in Réunion: "Peel the fruit. "Leave or remove the seeds, depending on how hard they are. "Brown half an onion, then fry everything together. "If necessary, add a dash of vinegar at the end. Three times, when we followed this recipe, we found that the cook had used too much onion and too much butter. It was only by successively reducing the quantity of these two ingredients that we arrived at a satisfactory result. We harvested the fruit at half size, and cut it into one-inch-thick slices. We omitted the vinegar drizzle. Pipengaille is a highly sought-after vegetable in Réunion, and would be very useful in our Midi and in those of our colonies that do not yet have it. It's good, delicate, but weak in flavor. It would travel unaltered between Provence and Paris. It could even be shipped from our African colony. 6 Fig. 67. - Luffa acutangula. When the fruit is ripe, it contains a central, fibrous mass which is used as a sponge, giving the Fig. 68. - Luffa cylindrica. plant the name "Torchon squash". Luffa acutangula (fig. 67) is often confused with Luffa cylindrica (fig. 68). Both plants are used for the same purposes, either at the table or in the home. M. Naudin has this to say about the Luffa genus: "In two species, L. cylindrica and acutangula, the fruit is edible when it is young and the fibers have not yet had time to harden. In our colonies, L. cylindrica goes by the Indian name of Petole, while L. acutangula is known as Papengaye. "These two species are distinct, though related. The fruit is smooth in L. cylindrica, and raised with very prominent ribs in L. acutangula. PI-T'SI ELEOCHARIS TUBEROSA Schult. Cyperaceae family. A tuberous form of Eleocharis plantaginea Retz. Aquatic plant, perennial, native to the Far East. Fibrous, stoloniferous root produces rounded tubers the size of small walnuts. Stem erect, gnarled, aphyllous, with one or two short sheaths at the base. Flowers in terminal, solitary, cylindrical spikes. Scales oblong, membranous, margined. 3 stamens. Style trifid, rarely bifid, thickened at base, compressed. Achains obcordiform, surrounded by small curved bristles. Around 1655, Martinus Martini, in his Novus Atlas sinensis, wrote: "City of Kia-hing (Prov. Che Kiang). The Chinese call Pe ci a plant with round fruits (1) that grows in stagnant waters throughout this region. The volume of these fruits hardly exceeds that of a chestnut. The kernel is covered with a very thin, brown skin, and the pulp is pure white, full of juice, with a pleasant flavor, harsher than that of ordinary fruit and somewhat acidic. If, at the same time as the fruit, you put a copper coin in your mouth, you will break it with your teeth as easily as the fruit, and you will reduce it to edible pulp by a marvellous force of nature, which I have often experienced myself." In 1696, the R. P. Louis Le Comte, in his Nouveaux mémoires sur l'état de la Chine, speaking of the Pe ci, reported what Martini had said, that, when these fruits are chewed at the same time as a copper coin, the latter is easily crushed with the teeth, a tale also found in Chinese books; but Le Comte refutes this assertion by invoking his own experience (Notes taken from the work entitled: Early European researches in to the Flora of China, by E. Bretschneider, D. M. London, 1881). Dr. Bretschneider wrote to us from Saint-Péters(1) Les misionnaires ont eru voir un fruit dans ce qui m'etait qu'on tubercule. - P. B. bourg, November 20 1885: "The Chinese name for Eleocharis tuberosa is Pi t'si. The plant is cultivated all over China, in Peking as well as in Shang-Hai, Canton, etc. "I have sent some tubers of it to you. "I sent tubers the size of chestnuts to the late Mr. Decaisne, but they did not arrive in good condition. Eleocharis tuberosa would be a good acquisition for our crops, because its tubers are very good to eat. To send them from China to France, we'd have to put them in a pot filled with vase and water. Someone would have to take care of this pot on the boat and renew the water that evaporates. In this way, young plants would be brought to Marseille. "The plant is grown in water, like rice. I'm not in a position to point you in the right direction in China, but I would advise you to go and see my friend Mr. Dévéria, first Chinese interpreter at the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, he will tell you what to do." Our learned correspondent wrote again, dated June 7 1887: "The plant is widely cultivated around Peking, in submerged areas. It never flowers. It is grown for its tubers, which do not form until very late in the autumn. They are harvested in spring and eaten raw or cooked, or candied in vinegar. Europeans don't eat much Pi t'si; however, it has a rather pleasant taste. There is no need, as we have been told, to reject the residue after chewing the tuber." In the same letter, the doctor renewed his recommendations for transporting chi noises aquatic plants. We read in Roxburgh, Flora indica, volume I, page 210: "The plant is cited by Abbé Grosier under the name Pi t'si. Under the names Maatai, Pu-tsai or Pe t'si, or Chinese water chestnut. Mr. Duncan, in compliance with the Governor General's wishes, sent it from Canton to the Company's Botanical Garden in Calcutta, where it flowers towards the end of the rains, in September. "For the economic uses of its tuberous roots, I beg permission," says Roxburgh, "to refer, on the one hand, to Abbé Grosier and other historians who have had occasion to observe how they are employed by the Chinese, and, on the other, to the following extract from Mr. Duncan's letter which accompanied the plants: 'Maa tai, Pu tsai or Pi t'si, according to Abbé Grosier, Châtaigne d'eau. Cullive in tanks that are smoked for planting, towards the end of March. The water in the tank is then drained, and small cavities are made in the bottom, filled with human fertilizer and left exposed to the sun for two weeks. Their contents are soon mixed with the muddy bottom of the reservoir, and the splinters or roots are deposited. The water is then returned, and the new crop reaches perfection on September 1st. This nut is highly esteemed by Chinese of all classes, not only as a root for the pot, but also as a remedy. It is eaten boiled or raw. I wouldn't vouch for the singular virtues attributed to Maatai, but I'll just mention one of the most plausible. Children here play with coins in their mouths, which sometimes slip into their stomachs and cause alarming symptoms. The nut, raw or boiled, is then immediately given to them in quantity; it never fails to relieve them and is always considered a specific. It is claimed to decompose metal." We could, like Roxburgh, refer to the Description générale de la Chine, Paris, 1785, by Abbé Grosier, but this work is not available to everyone, and the reader would be deprived of the information it contains, which is most interesting. We therefore reproduce here the passages relating to aquatic edible plants: "In Europe, people have dared to suggest that the Chinese leave part of their land uncultivated; they were unaware, no doubt, that they cultivate the very bottom of waters and that the soil of lakes, ponds and streams provides them with harvests that are still unknown to us. Their industriousness has led them to find resources in a large number of aquatic plants, many of which, such as Pi t'si and Lien hoa, are the delights of the best Chinese tables. "In order to give the people an example of this culture, the government takes care to have them planted in ponds, in bodies of water and in all waters belonging to the State. The emperor himself has them planted in the ponds that adorn his gardens, and almost all his palace moats are filled with them. The flowers and greenery of these useful plants also almost entirely cover the two immense bodies of water in the center of Peking, which are separated only by a single bridge where everyone passes and from which the magnificent gardens of the emperor's palace can be seen. The Pi t'si or true Water Chestnut (1) (1) For us, the Trapa natans is the true Water Chestnut, and grows only in the southern provinces of China; it withers away in Peking. Its leaves are long like those of the Joncus, but hollow and tubular like those of the Chives. The extraordinary thing about this plant is that its fruit is enclosed in an envelope formed by its roots, like a chestnut in its thorny shell; this shell can be broken and the fruit removed without damaging the plant. This water chestnut is very healthy and has a very delicate taste; it is given to sick people to chew on to refresh their mouths". So far, we have made vain efforts to obtain Pi t'si. We received several tubers of Eleocharis tuberosa from R. P. Heude, of Shang-Haï, which arrived in Paris too tired from the journey for us to be able to vegetate them. Dr Bretschneider tells us what to do, but where can we find a complaisant traveller who will agree to transport the roots, give them the necessary water, and add the precious pot to his travel impedimenta? We're not discouraged, but we are extremely embarrassed. What may console enthusiasts in the Paris area is that the climate there would not allow cultivation of Pi t'si; not because it has anything to fear from the cold under the water with which it would be covered, but because the harvest of its tubers takes place late in autumn, and we don't have, as in Peking, after winters where the Pi t'si is the only one; but the Chinese have the Pi tst and the Ling io, and, for them, the piv'est is the true water Châtrigne. thermometer falls to 20°, six months of tropical heat. Abbé David observed 40° in the shade as early as May. The Chinese water chestnut is therefore confined to a few of our southernmost départements. This is the place to mention two Eleocharis, neighbors of the Pi t'si. To our great regret, we have so far been unable to obtain them, and cannot therefore devote a separate article to them. We would be grateful if anyone receiving tubers of these plants would inform us: ELEOCHARIS ESCULENTA Vieillard (Herb. de la Nouvelle-Calédonie, n° 1456). Herbaceous, bushy, stoloniferous plant; stolons with mealy tubers, closely resembling those of Cyperus esculentus L.; stems erect, aphyllic, 40 to 50 cm high, jonciform, smooth, green, divided internally by numerous diaphragms that are barely visible when fresh; pellucid sheaths, membranous, short, terminated by an acute triangular ligule. Flowers in elongated spikes, greenish, hermaphrodite, the lower ones sterile; scales greenish, membranous, broad, concave, scarious at the edges, striate in the center; perigone silky; bristles 8, unequal, white and scabrous; stamens 3; anthers elongated, mucronate, twice as long as the netting; ovary compressed, surmounted by a persistent style; seed black, shiny. This Cyperaceae is very common in flooded areas; its tubers are edible and quite sought-after (1). In the light of the above description and its accompanying indications, we thought it would be easy to find Eleocharis esculenta. We have written to our correspondent on several occasions. We pointed out to him the marshes around Balade as the plant's definite habitat; we tired him with our repeated requests. He always replied that Eleocharis esculenta could not be found. ELEOCHARIS PLANTAGINEA F. Mueller. Local name, Caya. Scirpus sphacelatus Spreng. The Sphacelia Eleocharis inhabits the lagoons, creeks and ponds of Australia. Each plant produces six to eight small, almost spherical tubers. These tubers, cooked and crushed between two stones, are prepared like almond cakes. In Australia (Queensland), we asked in vain for the Sphacelia Eleocharidae.
