COCHET EARTH PEAR POLYMNIA EDULIS Weddell, Ann. se. nat. 4th ser. vol. VII, p. 111. Family Compositae. Root tuberous; stem stout, branched, fluted, angular, more or less bristly at base; upper part, branches (especially near nodes) and peduncles hairy tomentose; leaves opposite, broad, ovate, weakly acuminate, cuneate-based, unevenly sinuate-dentate, upper surface briefly bristly and slightly rough, pubescent beneath and paler green; involucral leaves oval, subacuminate, with a more or less connate base, ciliated; spangles oblong, the same length as the flowers, denticulate at the apex; corolla with rays shorter than the involucre; ligule oval, with a deeply tridentate apex; base and tube short, very bristly. Notice sur l'Ahipa et l'Aricoma, plantes alimentaires du haut Pérou, by A. Weddell (Ann. des se. nat., loc. cit.): "Few countries offer as great a diversity of climates as Bolivia or high Peru; some points of their territory are even situated in such a special way that the inhabitants, on leaving their temperate skies, can reach in a few hours and at will the zone of perpetual snow or that of tropical vegetation. Such, in particular, is the position of the city of La Paz, built at the bottom of a ravine that connects it, on the one hand, with the tropical valleys of the eastern slopes of the Andes and, on the other, with the glaciers of the great Cordillera that rise majestically above it. It's easy to understand the advantages of such a location in terms of plant foodstuffs, which must almost inevitably be far more varied than in less favourably-placed locations. This is indeed the case; so when, on my last trip to America, I wanted to take stock of the edible plants on sale at the La Paz market, I was not surprised to find, alongside Strawberries, Apples and Peaches, Bananas, Pomegranates and Pineapples; But the products that particularly attracted my attention were those that seemed to have some analogy with the potato, for which a substitute was being sought at the time, and I was all the more interested in this examination as, alongside the tubers of Oxalis tuberosa, Ullucus and Tropxolum tuberosum, which were much talked about in Europe, I noticed two others that were still unknown to me and which had more or less the same appearance as the swollen roots of the Dahlia. However, like the Dahlia and the Potato, they were no longer products of the temperate or cold parts of the Andes, since they both originated from the subtropical zone, and as such, they could be seen in the company of the starchy roots or tubers of Manioc, Arracacha, Canna edulis or Colocasia esculenta. "One of these tubers, 10 to 15 centimeters long, tapering at both ends and yellowish in color, is known in La Paz as Ahipa (or Ajipa); the other, larger, stockier and darker in color, is known there as Yacon or Aricoma. Both are brought in considerable quantities from the warmer parts of the ravine, where they appear to have been cultivated since time immemorial. "At the time of my visit, the season was unfortunately too advanced for me to obtain the materials that would have enabled me to learn about their botanical origins; I therefore regretted leaving the country without having been able to satisfy my curiosity; and, despite numerous requests, it is only recently that I have obtained samples (1) that enable me to shed light on the question. "Now, those I received from the plant that provides the first of these tubers, Ahipa, although devoid of flowers and fruit, nevertheless allow me to affirm that it belongs to the Leguminosae family and the Phaseolae tribe, and I thought at first that it must be a species of Dolichos, perhaps the Dolichos tuberosus Lamk, which is claimed (Lamk.. Encycl, Encycl., Il, 295) to have been brought from South America to the West Indies by the Caribs. However, I soon had to give up this idea, for although I found fairly close relationships between the leaves of Ahipa and those of the (1) I am indebted for these samples, as well as for precious material for my Flore des hautes Cordillères, to the extreme kindness of my friend M. Gilbert Mandon, who has just spent several years in Bolivia. Although the plant was represented by Plumier and cultivated at the Muséum under the name Dolichos tuberosus, I also noted differences between the underground parts that made it impossible to confuse them; In fact, the Bolivian plant is usually equipped with a considerable number of tubers of the type I have described, and each of its main roots often has two or three; whereas, in the West Indian plant, on the other hand, not only is there usually only one tuber, but it sometimes reaches enormous dimensions (1). "In order to compare these two plants, I assumed that they belonged to the same genus, which has not yet been demonstrated; I would even add that Mr. Bentham, to whom I sent some of the leaves I had just received from Bolivia, told me that they could very well be those of a species of Stenolobium. It was also suggested that my plant might be one of the Rhynchosia species, which are currently cultivated in India for their tuberous roots. "But if it is true, as the inhabitants of La Paz claim, that the cultivation of Ahipa dates back to the time of the Incas (2), we could hardly have (1) M. Belanger, director of the Jardin botanique de Saint-Pierre, Martinique, assured me that the tubers of Dolichos tuberosus sometimes acquired the enormous weight of 30 kilograms. Their flavor, he told me, is quite comparable to that of beet. The plant is not cultivated, but is found in the wild in the Trois-Islets commune, where the negroes sometimes seek out its tuber to grate it and mix the raw flour with that of cassava. (2) A place is shown at the foot of the Ellimani, where the ancients had the patience to bring water from three leagues away, by means of a gutter, to water the slopes of the mountain on which this vegetable was cultivated. The plant is only an American species. The searches I have made in books and herbariums, following Mr. Bentham's indications, have not led me to any positive results, and, although it seems probable to me that the species has not yet been described, I will wait for further information to confirm this (1). "Samples of the plant that produces the second tuber, the one known in La Paz as Yacon or Aricoma, are much more complete than those of Ahipa, so I had no difficulty in recognizing a completely new species. As I said, the tubers are generally more voluminous and stockier than those of the preceding plant; like them and like those of Dahlia, they result from a particular development of the roots, and they are all the more comparable to the latter as they are the product of a plant of the same family. This plant is a species of the genus Polymnia, for which I propose the name P. edulis, and which I will describe as follows: "It grows spontaneously near Quitame, on the eastern slopes of the Audes de Bogola, at an altitude of 2,000 meters (Triana). It is cultivated in the subtropical region of Peru and New Granada, where, according to Triana, it goes by the names of Jiquima and Jiguimilla. "The tubers I saw for sale at the La Paz market averaged about the size of a fist, but I was assured that some weighed close to 2 kilograms; each strain produced an average of (1) More complete samples were recently sent by Dr. Ernst and established that Ahipa is nothing other than Dolichos tuberosus Lamk. (Pachyrrhizus tuberosus Spreng.) See note on this plant, under Tuberous Dotic. 33 4 or 5: there are some, however, which apparently provide 15 or 20. To complete what I have to say about these two vegetables, it remains for me to talk about their nutritional qualities and flavor. We'll start by saying that both are eaten raw like apples, and that they are held in the same high esteem as apples by the lower classes of the population. Should we conclude from this that their flavor is just as pleasant? "The Aricoma, which I prefer, seems to me more like a bad pear in this respect. Moreover, it contains only a very small amount of starch, whereas Ahipa contains a fairly significant proportion and could be compared, in terms of its nutritional properties, to the tuber of Ullucus tuberosus, while Aricoma would be the analogue of Topinambour, from which it differs, however, by a much higher proportion of sugar. "To sum up, what I have seen of these two tubers has convinced me that, if there could be any advantage in trying to introduce them into our crops - and I have no doubt that they would thrive in Algeria's climate - it would not be as food plants for man, but rather as industrial plants, intended for use either in the manufacture of 'alcohol, or as cattle feed; In both respects, Polymnia edulis would undoubtedly be the preferred plant, both because of the greater quantity of saccharine matter in its lubercules and because of its high yield. I would add that this plant, considered as a successor to the Topinambour, would have one advantage over the latter, namely that it doesn't stalk and is therefore much easier to eradicate from the land where it is grown. Its green parts are, moreover, even more abundant and, above all, more tender than those of the Topinambour and, for this reason, would undoubtedly be more sought-after by livestock. Cultivation of Polymnia edulis failed to produce any useful results, and the plant joined the list of dismal failures in the quest to eventually replace the potato. However, as the story of the discovery of two new food plants is not without interest, we think it will meet with approval to have reproduced Mr. Weddell's note in extenso. Floraison de la Poire de terre Cochet, by M. Quihou. Bull. Soc. d'Acel., 2e série. vol. Il, 1865, p. 652. BIG-FLOWERED PERPET PORTULACA GRANDIFLORA Hook. Bot. Mag. t. 2885. Family Portulacaceae. "Tuberous root. Stem branched, 20 to 25 cm long, round, smooth, fleshy, reddish; leaves fairly wide apart and 4 to 5 cm long, cylindrical, pointed, sessile, contracted at the base so as to appear petiolate, fleshy and glaucous green; axils alone with numerous, long, tangled hairs. Terminal flowers, sessile, grouped in threes or fours at the tip of the twigs and surrounded by an involucre whose leaflets resemble stem leaves and are interspersed with hairs at the base. Calyx with two elongated, oval, green divisions, scarred at the tip and hairy at the point of insertion. Corolla large, beautiful, much longer than the calyx, orange or bright purple-red. Petals 5, fused at the base and seemingly fused with the base of the calyx at the point of insertion on the ovary. Stamens numerous, united at the base of the calyx and corolla and to a lesser degree between them. Dark purple netting. Anthers round, bilocular, purple. Pollen bright yellow. Ovary supere, conical, plurilocular; seeds numerous. Style as long as stamens, filiform. Stigmas 7 to 9, radiating, tenuous, pubescent, curved at tip." (Hook., loc. cit.) In June 1879, we said: This plant grows spontaneously, unknown or neglected, at the foot of the Cordilleras, and we can do no better than to reproduce what relates to it in the letters of one of our correspondents (January 26, 1878): "I have been obliged to wait for POURPIER A GRANDES FLEURS Fig. 69. - Large-flowered Purslane (Portulaca grandiflora). January 26th to send you a variety of Tuberous Purslane, wild near the Andes mountains. Here's its story: I was struck by the sight of a field covered with a violet flower, very large for a dwarf plant. I collected a few vines in 1875 and planted them as ornamental plants. What a surprise when, in April, I found tubers of elongated shape, Om, 06 in length and about the size of a finger. I cut one of them, whose flesh seemed fatty. I cooked some tubers in ash and found them to have an exquisite taste. I cultivated Tuberous Purslane in 1877 and had the satisfaction of obtaining much larger tubers than the first ones harvested. "This Purslane thrives in dry, hot sand. The worst siliceous soils are suitable. The same correspondent, in a letter dated April 1878, adds the following: "This year I obtained some very fine Purslane tubers. Some have reached a length of Om, 12 by Om, 08 in circumference. So I have a very noticeable improvement on my crops, since I have doubled the volume of tubers in a single year. I cooked them for a quarter of an hour in boiling water; I sautéed them in butter: it's an excellent dish. I cook them in ash, which I eat only with salt; the taste is exquisite. I'm sending you some seeds from my cultivation and some from the spontaneous plant. Here are a few explanations: Purslane grows in dry sand. So I recommend sowing in pure sand or very sandy soil. I think this plant prefers bad soil to potting soil. In potting soil, it vegetates with admirable vigor, but its tubers remain small. I give you these details from my experiments. The best results are obtained in sand, kept slightly cool (1). Exposure to full sun. (1) Our correspondent had originally said: in dry, breezy sand. We sowed the seeds we received in buckets, under a cold frame. We obtained only a small number of plants, which we put in place, in the open air, on a piece of old bedding. We could certainly have planted in sand, but our main aim was to have vigorous plants and to harvest the seeds, even if the tubers were less developed. We didn't stick to sowing, and as soon as we had strong enough plants, we took cuttings, all of which sprouted easily. This means that both sowing and taking cuttings can be practised, but mainly taking cuttings. We had hoped to obtain tubers which we had planted and which we assumed would give us stronger plants and an earlier, more abundant harvest. This was not the case; after six consecutive years of careful cultivation, we were no further ahead than the first day. We have only obtained tubers that are insignificant in number and volume. The harshness of our winters, however, is no obstacle to the cultivation of Purslane, since it is an annual and summer crop; the habitat of the spontaneous plant is not free of frosts, and the thermometer sometimes falls to 6 or 7 degrees below zero. Since we are concerned exclusively with vegetable plants, we have said nothing about the Purslane flower, which is the subject of this chapter. It is very large, with a warm, vivid purplish-pink color, close to violet. Enthusiasts who have seen it agree that it is larger than in any other variety of large-flowered Purslane. PROVATZA STATICE SINUATA L. Fam. Plumbaginea. Statice sinuata is a perennial that grows wild throughout the Mediterranean region, but in France is found only on the Hyères islands. The stem reaches 10 to 30 centimetres in height. The radical, rosette-shaped leaves are cut into rounded lobes and bear sparse hairs. The flowers, in dense, terminal clusters, are large, blue, with pale yellow petals. This species was brought to our attention by Dr. de Heldreich, Director of the Botanical Garden of Athens, on the occasion of the publication of the second edition of Potager d'un Curieux. In a letter received on June 10 1892, he wrote: "If I had known you were preparing a new edition of your interesting work, I would have been able to offer you some more material from this country, in particular Provatza (a young plant of Statice sinuata, excellent in cooked salads)." We cultivated Statice sinuata, sowing it thickly in autumn; the following summer, we obtained abundant, very tender leaves, with an excellent cooked flavor. Raw, in salads, the young leaves are very acceptable. The flavor is mild and pleasant. PSORALEA COMESTIBLE, PICOTIANE Tipsina of the Iowa Indians; Taagu of the Osages, according to M. Trécul. PSORALEA ESCULENTA Pursh. Family Papilionaceae. Perennial, hairy, tuberous-rooted, simple, edible; stem from Om, 30 to Om, 35; palmate leaves, with 5 elliptical oval leaflets, glabrous below; in June-July, blue flowers, in axillary spikes, pedunculated; corolla the length of the calyx. This plant, native to Missouri, was introduced in 1846 by M. Lamare-Picot (1) and was proposed as a replacement for the potato. It is only in this capacity that it appears here, as we have not grown it and experimentation has been absolutely unfavorable. We know that the same was true of all the plants we had imagined replacing the potato, if the latter had succumbed to the disease it was suffering from. It's good to know that, with the exception of the Dioscorea, there is as yet no plant that can take the place of the Andean Solanea, and that if there were, the place would be empty. It is reasonable to believe that, in our Midi departments, several types of lgnames could fill this (4) Gaudichaud, Rapport sur un mémoire de M. Lamare-Picot, relatif à l'introduction d'une nouvelle plante alimentaire (Comptes rendus de l'Académie des sciences, 1846, t. XXVI, p. 326; ibid, 1849, t. XXIX, p. 709; ibid., 1850, t. XXX, p. 393). We are convinced that the Picotiane plant has its place, and we urge friends of horticulture to seek out, among the many existing varieties, those that might thrive in our climate. Returning to the Picotiane, here are the conclusions of an article in Le Bon Jardinier (1873, p. 649), to which we refer the reader: "It can be seen that, in its present state, the Psoralea esculenta is far removed from the conditions one should look for in an agricultural plant. It is not impossible, however, that cultivation could modify it more or less profoundly. Trials of this kind are always interesting, as much for their own sake as for the purpose to which they may lead; but they may have to be followed for a long time before bringing about a result, which itself remains doubtful; so that they should be recommended, in the present state of the question, rather to public educational establishments and experimenters, than to growers themselves." As Picotiane is not a resource for agriculture, and even less so for vegetable growing, we won't talk about it any further. Anserine Quinoa. Small Peruvian rice. CHENOPODIUM QUINOA Willd. Family Salsolaceae. 323 Annual plant. Stem straight, twiggy, 1m, 80 high: leaves alternate, triangular, with two sagittate extensions at the base, cut by very obtuse teeth, blond-green, pulverulent when young; flowers whitish, very small, in tight, compact clusters. Compressed, yellowish-white seeds. There are varieties with green, reddish or reddish stems and leaves. Quinoa has been much talked about in the past, but is now forgotten. We are familiar with all the published notes on this plant, which we have grown ourselves. They all agree on the benefits of growing it in France as a successor to spinach. It is in this capacity that it appears in the 1839 issue of Le Bon Jardinier, where it is the subject of a note by M. Vilmorin, instructive and complete enough that, having reproduced it, we have nothing left to say: "In 1836, trials multiplied; everywhere Quinoa succeeded well. The plant is very vigorous, almost impervious to the cold; it produces, in good soil, an extraordinary abundance of seeds, but the seed has so far not been used in a satisfactory way; either it does not acquire in France the same quality as in America, or, more probably, our palates are not shaped to its strange flavor; few people have found it to their taste. It has the real disadvantage of being quite bitter and pungent, which can only be removed by repeated washing. With repeated care, I can make very good cakes and passable soups from it, but preparation is a tricky business; the seed is very small and requires a long cooking time, so it won't meet with the approval of cooks. As for the mistresses of the house, perhaps some of them will judge it like the ladies of Lima, for whom, it is said, Quinoa is a favorite dish; in this case, it would be classified, in France, among those secondary products of which one wants a little for the variety, but not, as in America, among the economic plants of first utility. This would only happen if we could find a way of preparing the seed that would make it easy to use and pleasant tasting for the greatest number of people. "As a vegetable crop, Quinoa will be more readily adopted, because it is a good summer substitute for spinach. "If you want to harvest it as grain, you can sow it either on a bed in March, or even in a sheltered bed, and plant it in April or early May, or in the open ground, in rows, during April. As the plants become very large and strong, they should be spaced about 50 centimetres apart. They require fertile, light rather than compact soil, and exposure to full sun if they are to be harvested for seed. On a plantation that looks able, you can cut off the secondary shoots, which develop in large numbers, to take the leaves; but if you are only thinking of this last product, you should sow in closer rows and cut the stem at the time of the first harvest, to make it branch out. "A Quinoa bed established in this way will provide a succession of constantly renewed products throughout the summer, if watered regularly. In our opinion, Quinoa is a good substitute for Spinach. Enthusiasts will do well, however, to try the other substitutes for this vegetable, which we lack during the summer, i.e. Tetragona, Claytone, Safflower...".

Baselle, Glaciale, etc. Glaciale and Tetragone are our favorites.
