Muell, a plant introduced under the name of Australian Spinach, about which information can be found in the Bulletin de la Société nationate d'Acclimatation, 2° série, volume III, page 634; and volume IV, pages 34 and 106. Australian Spinach is not superior to Arroche or Belle-Dame in terms of quality, and as it is less productive, there is no reason to recommend its cultivation. Two other Salsolaceae are particularly sought-after in the deserts of Central Asia, for their edible seeds. These are Agriophyllum gobicum and arenarium. Prjevalski mentions them in the relabon of his third voyage (in Russian), and we reproduce below the translation (1) of the passages that the learned Russian traveler devoted to them: "Soulhhir is a Chenopodea (Salsolaceae). frequent in the whole of Central Asia, up to 48° degrees of northern latitude. It grows only on bare sand. There are two species: Agriophyllum gobicum and A. arenarium. "The former dominates the Ala-Chan, Ordos and, in general, the southern and eastern parts of the Gobi Desert; the latter occupies the western region and grows as far as Turkestan and the Caspian Sea. "Soulkhir is also found in the upper KhouanKhe (river) and in the Tzaidamme (I spent the winter there: I was unable to pick any of the plants, and therefore cannot say which of the two species it was). This plant is absent from northern Thibet. "I said earlier that Soulhhir grows on bare sand. Like other local plants, it makes do with rainwater, which filters through the sand and then (. We owe this translation to Tobhigeance by M. Vilbouche- vitch. rises to the upper layers through evaporation. "To take advantage of this moisture, the Soulkhir has incredibly long roots, as do all plants growing in the same conditions; they are able to draw water from a considerable depth. It goes without saying that sand vegetation is all the richer the more abundant the rainfall. "Soulkhir is an annual plant. In the Ala-Chan region, it grows up to 1 metre in height in good conditions, and its stem can be up to 5 centimetres (?) thick; on average, however, these dimensions are half that. "Not only is Soulkhir superb fodder for Mongolian livestock, but its seeds, which are as small as those of the poppy, are an essential food for the indigenous population. "The saying, 'You have to have sown to reap', does not hold true in Ala-Chan; every year, at the end of September and especially in rainy years, they reap an abundant harvest of Soulkhir from their sands. "The exposed areas of loess clay subsoil are used as threshing floors; the seeds are then roasted and ground to flour using hand mills. "Birds from the northern Gobi that winter in the Tingueri sands also feed on them. Prjevalski gives the following details of the Tungueri sands in the Ala-Chan desert, where these plants grow. They give an exact idea of the environment in which they thrive. "These sands present a sea of mounds, reaching from 10 to 20, rarely up to 30 meters high; they are arranged in complete disorder; long sand ribs divide the mounds. "The mounds themselves are strongly compressed by the wind on one of their slopes, so that the foot does not sink deep into them. The opposite slope, on the other hand, is very steep, and the sand has no consistency. "Between the mounds of sand there are funnel-shaped holes or valleys that often expose the very bottom of the plain, which is, as in most of these sandy deserts, salty clay, smooth and hard as a parquet floor. Water is generally lacking. It is rare to find a spring. "There are also few places where bad salt water can be obtained, by digging 3-4 feet into the loamy subsoil of the sands. "As a general rule, the sands are bare and some vegetation only appears on the edges of sandy places and, from time to time, on the bottoms of the above-mentioned valleys and funnels; the desert flora is, moreover, quite varied in these parts; we collected seventeen species in the Tungueri sands, which had not yet appeared in the year's herbarium. "Two plants are particularly remarkable and characteristic of the Ala-Chan. They are Soulkhir and Pugionium. RAPHANUS SATIVUS L., var. Fam. Cruciferae. 528 "Root elongated, cylindrical, swollen at its lower end and terminated abruptly by a fine, loose tail, about Om.11 long by Om.05 in diameter; skin fairly thin, bright red in color, marked with lines, in the form of paler folds in the circumferential direction; flesh firm, with a fairly pungent flavor; leaves fairly ample, with a bright pink petiole; maturity intermediate between that of the Summer Grey Radish and that of the Black Radish." (Vilmorin-Andrieux et Cie). "The pink winter radish from China was introduced by missionaries and spread by Abbé Voisin. This variety is excellent and one of the most advanced that has come to us from China". (Le BoJardinier, 1873.) We have little to add to the above. We sow in the first days of August in rows spaced Om,30 apart, and thin out the sowing so that there is Om,12 to om,15 space between plants. The ordinary use of Chinese Pink Winter Radish is that of all Radishes; but we know a housewife who uses it, in place of Turnips, as a garnish. She finds that this root, with its 34 regularity of shape and volume, lends itself better than any other to this use; our readers will appreciate. MONSTER RED RADISH FROM KASHGAR BAN-TCHOUNG-TSI FROM KASHGAR. In the spring of 1890, Mr. N. Zolotnitski, president of the botanical section of the Imperial Acclimatization Society of Russia, based in Moscow, sent us a large collection of seeds from Kashgaria and Pamir. Included in this collection were seeds of Ban-tchoung-tsi, Kashgar's monstrous red radish. We applied to this species the cultivation required by the extraordinary development of its roots. The seeds were sown in rows, and the plant was thinned out to give each plant fifty centimetres of space in all directions. We'll mention the Radishes we obtained in 1893. They were enormous. The largest weighed 3 kilos 700 grams. Given by our gardener to a Paris delicatessen, it appeared in the display under the name of Russian Radish. After attracting the admiration of passers-by, it was served in slices to the establishment's customers, who were very satisfied. We have given many Kashgar Radishes to friends and neighbors; they have been declared excellent. The flesh is crisp without being hard, and piquant without excess. Its fault is that it's too big to be served whole, which is a pity, because it's a splendid root. It sets seed quickly, and we have found that in the Paris climate, it should not be sown before July. M. Edouard Blanc gave the Muséum the seeds of a voluminous Radish he brought back from a trip to Turkestan, which we consider identical to ours. Professor Maxime Cornu, rightly believing that it could be used by farmers, presented some fine specimens to the Société nationale d'agriculture. From this point of view, we won't deal with it here. We must say, however, that in 1891 and 1892, we received from Moscow, under the name Dong-la-bout (?), a variety of long, red, monstrous Radish from Kashgar, which we feel could be presented to growers. Like the Japanese Daikon, its flesh is almost tasteless, but its skin is excessively pungent and could doubtless be served on its own as an appetizer. RAPHANUS CAUDATUS L. Cruciferae family. Annual herb, cullivated in Java, where it is probably spontaneous and its candied siliques, known as Mougri, are eaten. It is also cultivated in the Punjab and in western and northern India. Fusiform root, from which rises a stem the size of a goose feather, from Om, 33 to Om, 50 high, sparsely branched, initially straight and eventually receding; the leaves, alternate, very spreading, with two short petioles, are divided, up to the middle, into a small number of triangular lobes, acute and saw-toothed, as is the terminal lobe, which is larger and ovalelanceolate; as they rise on the plant, their shape simplifies, but even the tallest of them is sinuous rather than simply toothed; flowers gathered in small numbers in leafy terminal clusters, each emerging (according to the figure given by Linné fils) from the axil of a floral leaf; white petals, four in number, with purple veins and an inverted heart-shaped blade; siliques 4 to 5 feet long (1.33 to 1.65 m), thicker than a thumb at the bottom, gradually narrowing from base to tip, more or less curved, reddish in color, roughly rounded in cross-section and containing numerous, oblong, purple seeds in their single chamber. We find the above description in a Note by M. P. Duchartre (1869), made necessary by the confusion that tended to arise between Linnaeus' Raphahauscer 112 2- "Orenine Fig. 70. - Snake Radish (Raphanus caudatus). nus caudatus and a plant from India, called Madras Radish, which M. Courtois-Gérard had encountered in the Botanical Garden of Edinburgh, and from which he had brought seeds to France (1). Seven years later, M. Ed. André published, with figures, in the Revue Horticole, an extensive and most interesting Note, to which we refer the reader (2). Growing Raphanus caudatus is like growing any other large radish, and requires no description. It is as simple and easy as can be. The plant is distinguished from all the other Radishes we have by the extraordinary size of its siliques. Although degenerated, a few years ago it was still producing siliques in our garden that were 40 to 50 centimetres long, alongside others that were shorter; in other words, some of the siliques were longer than the whole plant, as Linnaeus pointed out, and when they reached the ground they spread out in sinuosities, like a snake. Our cultivation was considerably hampered by the ravages wrought by flea beetles and a large grey aphid. The latter, which would certainly not have resisted a spray of labac juice, devoured our plants in a matter of days. (1) Note sur une Crucifère à siliques comestibles, récemment introduite en France (Journ. de la Soc. impériale et centrale d'Horticulture, 1859, vol. V, p. 57 to 63). (2) Le Radis serpent (Revue horticole, 1866, p. 471). 9380 Fig. 71 - Snake radish, flowers and siliques. 535 We cooked them and ate them, either as a salad or prepared like green beans. For this purpose, they must be picked very young; they are then very acceptable, but are far from being as good as green beans. Pickled in vinegar, they are good and preferable to most of the vegetables usually associated with gherkins. They have the merit of retaining their piquant flavor for a long time, attenuated but still sensitive. It's probably when they've been preserved in vinegar that they're called Mougri in Java. Mr. Ed. André has heard that in England, fifty years before the publication of his Note, and when Raphanus caudatus was being cultivated as a new plant, the juice was squeezed out of it, and that it made an excellent sauce, a very palatable juice. The plant is undoubtedly very interesting, and those who are curious will make room for it in their gardens. ROMAINE GIGOGNE O Soune, from Pamir. LACTUCA SATIVA L., var. On February 22, 1891, we received from Mr. N. Zolotnitski, President of the Botany Section of the Imperial Acclimatization Society of Russia, a collection of vegetable seeds collected in the Pamir and Kashgarie regions. Among these seeds were those of a Romaine that we feel deserves particular attention. The figure shown here is an exact representation of the plant. However, we must not wait until Fig. 72. - Romaine Gigogne. the adventitious seedlings and consume them, until they have acquired all the development shown in our drawing. Sown on May 4, our Romaine did not flower at Crosnes until the end of August. Its main stem rose slowly, slowed by the birth of numerous buds which surrounded it and which, successively harvested as they developed, provided an abundance of excellent cooking vegetables and a perfect salad. We believe that this curious variety, very slow to grow and very productive, is a valuable acquisition for our vegetable gardens. Its name is justified by the large family it produces. It requires more space than the ordinary Romaine, but it makes liberal use of the space it is given. We are very grateful for the gift made to us by the Imperial Society of Acclimatization of Russia, and hope to repay it with seed shipments. The letter of dispatch sent to Mr. Paillieux by Mr. Zolotnitski read: "At the same time, I have the pleasure of informing you that, at the last annual meeting of the Imperial Society of Acclimatization of Russia, you were awarded the great medal of encouragement for your useful attempts in the field of acclimatization of new vegetable species." PAMIR O SOUNE ROMAINE. ROMAINE ASPARAGUS. In the bag of O Soune seeds we received from the Société Impériale d'Acclimatation de Russie (1), we found, mixed with the white seeds of Romaine Gigoyne, brown seeds mo ROMAINE DU PAMIR Fig. 73. - Romaine du Pamir. which gave us a very distinct and interesting plant (fig. 73). We consider it to be (1) See Romaine Gigogne, p. 536. identical to the one listed under the name Romaine Asperge in the book entitled "Les plantes potagères", 2nd ed. p. 361, published by Vilmorin-Andrieux et Cie, but it is not included in their catalog. It is not an early plant. Sown in the first days of March, it only reaches maturity at the end of June. Successive sowings mean that it can be harvested until the end of September. It does not apple. Its stalk reaches a height of 50 centimetres, and the edible part, reduced to around 30 centimetres, stripped of its bark and cooked for half an hour in a light juice, makes an excellent-looking and tasty vegetable dish. In our families, it's customary simply to cook the stems in stock, leaving the vegetable its natural flavor. Nothing could be easier, as you can see, but the cordon bleu cooks of our village don't stop there, and one of their most successful preparations is with cream and cheese. In 1892 and 1893, we unwisely gave O Soune the name Romaine-Asperge, which induced consumers to boil the stalks and eat them with white sauce. They were tasteless and the name almost killed the plant; we've changed it to Romaine du Pamir. Gardeners should be warned that our Romaine does not apple. Many have pulled it out, thinking they'd been laughed at. Romaine stems seem to be widely used in China. M. Maurice de Vilmorin received from ShangHaï seeds of four varieties of this species, and kindly allotted us a share of each. These bags were labelled: Ou Sen, Romaine whose stems are eaten. One of them bore the words: Ou Sen odoriférant. These seeds did not germinate. Another variety, presented as early, seemed negligible to us. We consider two varieties, one red, the other white, to be very interesting. The red one is earlier than Romaine du Pamir. Its stems grow less high. We eat 20 centimeters of them. They are very tender and have a very strong flavor. They are very fragrant in the kitchen. They could probably be blanched before cooking. Cooked in broth, they are good. The white variety is a late bloomer. It has the merit of being very white, of being surrounded by small adventitious Romaines like the R. Gigogne, of consequently providing a pretty salad, and, finally, of giving tender stalks, of fairly strong flavor, which can be used over a length of 20 centimeters. Our gardens are therefore now in possession of three varieties of a hitherto forgotten or unknown Romaine, which all our friends have served on their tables and with which they are very satisfied. Every day, we receive new testimonials. SAFRAN COMESTIBLE CROCUS CANCELLATUS Herb, var. persicus Chappellier. Iridae family. This Crocus was collected in Persia in 1881 and distributed in March 1892, by M. Pissard, former head gardener to the Shah of Persia. Mr. P. Chappellier received bulbs at the same time as we did, and published a note on the plant which we are authorized to reproduce in full. We don't expect anything from the bulbs we have, and we must confess in all humility that we have had no success with bulbous plants. We therefore consider ourselves very fortunate not to be obliged to pass over in silence an interesting plant, and to be able to present it to the reader in the name of the enlightened amateur who, as we know, has made a special study of Crocus. Note from M. Chappellier: "The crate in which these bulbs made the journey from Persia to Paris remained en route during the autumn of 1881 and the winter of 1881-1882; they came into vegetation and flowered there. "As far as the abnormal condition of these specimens allows me to judge, I am inclined to believe that this Crocus belongs to the C. cancellatus group. "Under the type cancellatus, created by Herbert, authors have grouped the following subtypes, considered by some as species, by others as varieties or synonyms: "C. cancellatus Herb; "C. Schimperi Gay; syn. C. Spruneri Boiss. et Held. "C. damascenus Herb; syn. C. edulis Boiss. et Blanche. " C. Kotschyanus Herb; C. cilicicus Kotschy; C. pylarum Gay; C. mazziaricus Herb. "Here are the main characteristics of C. cancellatus that I found in the Persian Crocus: " Autumn flowering; " Yellow pollen; " Multifid style; " Synanthous leaf; " No involucre (at the scape); " Bivalve bract (at the ovary); " Strongly reticulated tunics, with persistent star at the base; perianth varying from lilac-white to violet. "In C. cancellatus, the throat is yellowish-white or yellow; in Crocus persan, it is lilac-white or violet. "In the latter, I've also noticed a disproportionately long scape. I don't think these peculiarities are due to abnormal flowering in closed, dry boxes. "The geographical distribution of the varieties of C. cancellatus known to date extends from the Ionian Islands to Syria, where C. edulis grows, and Armenia. "M. Pissard's Crocus was collected at an altitude of around 1,300 meters, in the plains and hills of a locality called Sultabatt, in the province of IrathFérahan, southeast of Amadan, about 120 kilometers from Teheran. "Until now, only C. speciosus had been found in Persia. "Here is the information Mr. Pissard gives on the use of this plant. "The onion is edible; it tastes like chestnuts; the natives eat a lot of it. "In July 1881, these onions, boiled and grilled, were presented at an official dinner at the home of the Minister of Telegraphs; several Europeans attending the meal found them exquisite, and the Shah of Persia ordered a large supply for his table. "Until a new contribution from the place of origin or a normal flowering of the recently introduced bulbs allows a more mature examination, I think that it is with the C. edulis Boiss. et Blanche, subtype of the C. cancellatus Herb. that the new Persian Crocus has the most analogy, and I propose to annex it to the cancellatus type, under the name of C. posrieus" (Paris, April 2, 1882). Jeetcice According to M. de Heldreich (Plantes utiles de la Grèce), the bulbs of Crocus Aucheri Boiss. and C. Sieberi Gay, often found in the mountains of Greece, are eaten raw by shepherds. They have, he says, about the same taste as hazelnuts. Dserlin-lobin. PUGIONIUM CORNUTUM Gærtn. 545 Our attention was drawn to this plant by four lines in the Manuel de l'Acclimateur, by Messrs. Naudin and Mueller: "Herbaceous plant of the Cruciferae family, from Central Asia, from the Caspian Sea to China (1). It is used as a vegetable by the Mongols, and is perhaps cultivated in a few places. Pugionium is not only rare, it's impossible to find. We don't think it exists in any botanical garden today. At our request, the Société nationale d'Acclimatation approached His Excellency Mr. Regel, Director of the Imperial Garden in St. Petersburg, who was kind enough to send us some seeds. We owe him our warmest thanks for this customary gesture of kindness. These rather large seeds, armed with horns that make them a real curiosity, were first sown in Crosnes, under glass, on May 15. They took around three weeks to germinate. To give the young plants some air, we had to raise the frame a little and the flea beetle devoured them, barely 6 centimetres high. We sowed them again, this time in a (1) This is obviously a mistake; the plant has only been seen in Mongolia to date. 38 wire mesh-lined cage. However, the same enemy killed our plants. The flea beetle was in the soil and hatched after sowing. The insect usually disappears in the first days of September, destroyed by rain or cold. We therefore felt that we could sow again, under a frame, on September 6. Winters being very cold in Mongolia, we thought that the Pugionium, with a little protection, could overwinter in the vicinity of Paris; but we suffered a third setback. We had sent some Pugionium seeds to Mr. Ch. Naudin, who was no happier than we were and wrote to us: "Our dry and perhaps too hot climate does not suit it". On July 17 1889, Mr. Alexandre Bataline of the Imperial Garden, St. Petersburg, wrote to us: "Pugionium cornutum Gærtn. is a perennial plant native to Mongolia, where it is cultivated. "It is fairly widespread on the Mongolian steppes, but does not grow in Siberia. It goes by the name of Sagaï, according to a report by the learned Russian traveller, Gregoire Potanine. What part of this plant is eaten? I can't tell you. Its leaves have an unpleasant odor. Twice we received silicas of this species and the seeds germinated, but the plants only lived for a few months. Now we have neither seeds nor plants. Seeds distributed in Italy by our Botanical Garden gave the same negative results. A detailed description of this little-known plant can be found in Diagnoses plantarum novarum asiaticarum, fasc. III, by C.-J. Maximowicz, mélanges biologiques tirés du Bulletin de l'Académie impériale des sciences de Saint Pétersbourg, tome X, 1880. A related species, Pugionium dolabratum Maxim, is a biennial also native to Mongolia. This species has given us ripe seeds in St. Petersburg. I have studied the mode of fertilization of the flowers of this plant, and have published a short article on the subject, which I am sending you today. In the report on his third voyage, Prjevalski, the learned Russian traveller, gives a figure of the Pugionium cornutum, and we reproduce below the translation (1) of the interesting details that accompany it: "The Pugionium, which the Mongols call Dserlinlobine, which means Wild Radish, is much inferior to the Soulkhir (2), from the point of view of the role it plays in the economy of the nomads. "The Chinese prefer the plant itself, of which they collect the young (first-year) plants to make salted meats, which they eat as a vegetable. "Pugionium was, until recently, a rarity in herbariums, since it was known only from two branches that the naturalist Gmelin had in the last century, probably through the kindness of pilgrims returning to Northern Mongolia from a trip to Thibet. "In 1861, I had the good fortune to meet and (1) We owe this translation to the kindness of M. Vilbouchevitch. (2) Agriophyllum gobicum et arenarium. pick the plant in the Ordos country. Mr. Maximowicz considered the specimens I had brought back as belonging to two different species. One was the one Gmelin had described as Pugionium cornutum; the other, a new species to which Maximowicz gave the name Pugionium dolabratum. "Both species appear to be biennial. They are Cruciferae and their habit is that of a small shrub. "The stem does not exceed 1 foot in height, and it usually lies in the sand. In the second year, a thick, spherical or rather oval head is born at the top of the stem, made up of spindly, fragile twigs that are very close together and tangled; at the end of the twigs, small, insignificant flowers appear; they are white or pinkish. This green head of Pugionium has a maximum diameter of 3-4 feet, a minimum diameter of 2-3 feet and a height of 2 feet. In the Ordos region, the plants were much larger in places. "In the Tingueri, we had only one species, P. dolabratum. It was fairly common and formed isolated bushes on the edges of quicksand, more rarely in the interior of the sands. It was late August; flowering had finished, but the fruits were still green. We only collected ripe seeds from one subject. These were the seeds we delivered to the Botanical Garden in St. Petersburg. In the note on the Soulkhir (Agriophyllum gobicum et arenarium) (1), we have reproduced Prjevalski's des(1) See page 526. cription of the Tingueri sands, in the Ala-Chan desert, where the Pugionium grows. We can see that it is a salt-growing plant, and this explains the difficulty of cultivating it. COMESTIBLE SAGITTARIUS SAGITTARIUS FROM CHINA AND JAPAN Tsz'Au (in China). - Kuwai (in Japan). SAGITTARIA SAGITTÆFOLIA L., var, diversifolia Micheli, Monogr. phanerog., vol. III, p. 67; S. sinensis Sims, Bot. Mag. tab. 1631; S. macrophylla Bunge; S. alpina Willd.; S. hirundacea Blume; S. hastata Don; S. porriana Sweet; S. heterophylla Miller; S. acuminata Smith; S. natans Pallas; S. obtusa Thunb. Family Alismaceae. This plant grows in stagnant waters and on the banks of rivers and lakes in the temperate regions of Europe and Asia. Its shape and leaf volume vary according to whether the water is stagnant or running, or more or less abundant and deep. Authors have made species of the various forms it presents; but it is quite certain that these are merely modifications due to the influence of the environment, as one and the same plant may present wider or narrower, acute or obtuse, sagittate or entire leaves, etc. In the typical form, the leaf is flattened, and in the typical form, the leaf is flattened. In the typical form, the leaf is sagittate, with a variable blade, sometimes narrow, sometimes acute, with divided base lobes, sometimes broader, sometimes with obtuse middle lobes, those at the base almost straight. In running water, stems and petioles are spindly and sinuous; the leaf is oblong, with a rounded, emarginate base, more rarely briefly lobed, with divergent, acute lobes (S. alpina Willd.), or the leaf is limbless, translucent, weakly membranous, up to 2 meters long (S. sagit- tæfolia, var. valisneriïfolia Coss. et Germ.; Vallisneria bulbosa Poir.). In warmer regions, mainly China, where this plant is cultivated as a foodstuff, we find subjects with larger leaves, 20 to 22 centimeters long and 14 to 15 centimeters wide, with a rounded, briefly acuminate median lobe, long acuminate base lobes, very acute, often with a twiggy stem (S. macrophylla Bunge; S. chinensis Sims and S. hirundacea Blume). Kæmpfer (Amœnitatum exotic., fasc. V. p. 827) cites the Sagittaria used as a food in Japan: Siko Omodaha, Sagittaria aquatica minor latifolia, with an edible root called Bossai, whose real name is Siro quai. In the book entitled: Note explicative des objets exposés à l'Exposition universelle de 1889, by the Komaba Agricultural and Forestry School (Ministry of Agriculture and Trade, Tokio, Japan), we read: "Kouwai. The tuberous roots are harvested in autumn and used in cooking; a single plant produces twelve or thirteen tubers, which makes production abundant; the tubers have a particular aromatic smell when eaten in culinary preparations. They are therefore highly prized. One variety (Scirpus articulatus), called Kouwai or ShuitaKouwa, is botanically quite different from the first; but its roots are similar to those of the other, and only slightly smaller. They are eaten fresh. Dr. E. Bretschneider, in his Early European researches, tells us: "We know two species of Chinese Sagittaria, i.e. S. chinensis Sims and S. cordifolia Roxb. I believe it is the former that is widely cultivated in China for its edible roots, under the name Tsz'ku. Sagittaria trifolia L. (China) could be, according to Kunth, S. chinensis. Kunth also believes that S. sagittæfolia L., cited by Loureiro, is S. chinensis. However, the doctor wrote to us from St. Petersburg on June 7, 1887: "The Sagittaria cultivated in Peking for its tubers is S. macrophylla Bunge, distinguished by its enormous leaves. It is probably only a variety of S. chinensis. Sagittaria does not appear to be widely cultivated in China. Its tubers are harvested in autumn, from which a starch is extracted that is used in the same way as Nelumbium rhizomes. "It is said that the Kalmyks of the Volga have recourse to this food when they go hunting in the watery areas inhabited by Sagittarius. They rely so heavily on this resource that they don't bother with any mouth supplies. Its tubers can be eaten raw or cooked. Martius compares the starch they provide to that which we know as Arrowroot, which speaks highly in favor of this nutritious substance." (Mouchon, Bromatotogie végétate, p. 281). In his Histoire des plantes, volume II, Poiret says of our common Sagittaria: "Its leaves are eagerly sought after by goats, horses and especially pigs. The numerous bulbs of its underground stems make it a precious plant, too neglected by us, which the Chinese have long cultivated as a food plant. These bulbs contain a firm, white flesh, similar to that of the Chestnut; they are good to eat, even raw, as I have experienced. If man were to disdain them, he could at least feed them to several of the animals he raises. "You can see how advantageous it would be to multiply this plant on the banks of ponds and rivers, wherever it can grow without harming other crops. The Muséum has given us Sagittaria macrophylla. M. Latour-Marliac has given us a species he received from Leiden, under the name S. chinensis latifolia, and also the charming ornamental plant known as S. chinensis flore pleno. We have not yet obtained tubers from either of these plants. ARROW-HEAD SAGITTARIA SAGITTÆFOLIA L., var. variabilis Micheli Monogr. phan. loc. cit. p. 68. S. gracilis Pursh; S. hastata Pursh; S. latifolia Willd. ; S. latifolia Pursh; S. obtusa Willd.; S. simplex Pursh; S. longiloba Engelm. et Torrey. This species inhabits damp places, marshes, etc., almost everywhere in boreal America, from 4 Fig. 74 - Arrow-Head Sagittaria sagittæfolia, var. variabilis (tubers). Newfoundland and Canada to Florida, Louisiana and Texas. "This Sagittaria is sometimes called swan or swamp potato. Its Chippewa name is Wab-es-i-pinig. It grows on the muddy shores of northwestern lakes and rivers, and its tuberous roots are an important part of the diet. "Waterfowl are greedy for them and, to feast on them, gather in spring in these favorite spots, where the Indians kill them for their own feasts. The roots (fig.74) are generally as large as hen's eggs. They are highly prized when raw, but contain a bitter milky juice that is not to the liking of civilized man. This defect, however, disappears when they are boiled, and cooking makes them sweet and pleasant. They are considered excellent when cooked with fresh or salted meat. "To collect them, the Indians enter the water and detach them with their feet so that they float and can be harvested. "Their shape is oblong; their color is a whitish yellow, striped with four black circles. "The plant is also common in the Atlantic States" (1). Messrs Vilmorin-Andrieux and Cle were kind enough to request Sagittaria variabilis for us in America. The plant arrived in very poor condition; only two plants survived, and both were taken to the infirmary at Reuilly, where they recovered their health. These two plants, placed in tubs at Crosnes, developed vigorously and produced a large number(1) Extract from the report of the Commissioner of Agriculture for the year 1870, Washington. Government printing office, 1870. so that they soon invaded the space we had available. The plant flowered and we were able to collect the seeds. At the onset of winter, we proceeded to pull up the American Sagittaria and had the pleasure of harvesting a good number of tubers as large as small eggs, containing abundant, light starch. Cooked in water, these tubers have given us a vegetable that is, if not first-rate, at least very acceptable, and we have no doubt that they could be put to good use if cultivation were attempted on a larger scale. This interesting plant has proved absolutely hardy in the Paris climate. SCOLYME D'ESPAGNE Commonly Cardillo or Tagarninas. Scolymus hispanicus L. Fam. Compositae. Indigenous. Biennial. White, taproot, fairly fleshy. Radical leaves oblong, usually mottled pale green on a dark green background, very thorny, narrowed at the base to form a stalk; stem very stalky, reaching about 60 to 80 centimetres in height, lined with sessile, decurrent, very thorny leaves; flowers in sessile heads, grouped in twos or threes, with bright yellow florets. Achenes flattened, yellowish, surrounded by a whitish, scaly appendage. 1 gram contains around 200 seeds, and a liter weighs 125 grams. Germination lasts three years. When we come across an article in a little-known publication, almost impossible to find for most of our readers, written in perfect agreement with our opinion on the cultivation and merit Fig. 75. - Scolyme d'Espagne. of one of the plants in our kitchen garden, we have no hesitation in reproducing it in full. This is what we'll do for the Scolyme d'Espagne. In the Bulletin de la Société d'Horticulture de l'Aube, Volume I, page 217: Culture du Scolyme d'Espagne. "If there is one vegetable that deserves to be cullivated with care and which is, on the contrary, neglected and almost abandoned in certain localities, it is certainly the Scolyme. No vegetable, old or new, seems to me to possess as many qualities as this one. Its delicate taste, long shelf life, rapid growth and the time it appears on our tables mean that it can compete without too much disadvantage, not with Scorsonera or Salsify, with which it is most closely related, but with Cardoons and Cauliflowers, the vegetables to which gardeners devote all their care; But, unfortunately, Scolyma doesn't always succeed when left to its own devices, and its seeds, whether poorly chosen or preserved, or sown in unfavorable conditions, don't always grow very well. I will indicate, as best I can, the procedures that I have found suitable up to now, and which have been fairly successful. I do not claim to have brought cultivation to any great perfection, but I am convinced that amateurs who have succeeded and appreciated the value of this vegetable once will not abandon it, despite the occasional setbacks they may experience. "The Scolyma seed is small and enclosed in a membranous or foliaceous envelope. This envelope, which hides it from the eye of the examiner, makes it very difficult for him to appreciate its quality by sight. "It is sown from June 20 to July 15; earlier, the plants rise, flower and produce roots that are perhaps a little larger, but which lack the qualities of those that have not flowered. Sown later, the yield may be low. The seeds are placed in furrows 4 to 5 centimetres deep, about 20 centimetres apart. Don't be afraid of sowing a little too thickly in the furrow. "At this time of year, the soil is usually dry, and when worked, it dries out quickly to the depth where the seeds go. Watering, unless excessively abundant, is not always a remedy. To remedy this dryness of the soil, when the furrows that are to receive the seeds are made, they are watered to the point of becoming almost muddy; we sow on this wet ground and cover with the soil from the edges of the furrow, which has also been wet. "This moisture in the soil is maintained fairly well, and a few sprinklings, if the temperature is too dry, are enough to make the seeds stand up; because with this cultivation method, it will rarely happen that the seeds don't stand up in sufficient quantity. In general, summer sowings are not very successful, because the seeds may be a little dried out by the high temperature of the season, and because the soil contains much less moisture than in spring and autumn. If, to these differences in germination conditions, we add the separation of the soil from the seed by the membranous envelope that surrounds the latter, we will understand perfectly why this seed often lifts very poorly, and it is, I believe, this disadvantage that makes it abandon cultivation. "The leaves of this plant, with the exception of the seminal ones, bear a strong resemblance to a thistle. "When all the seeds have risen and the plants have already gained some strength (four to five leaves, not including the seminals), thin them out, leaving a distance of about 10 centimetres between the plants in the furrow. Plants that have been carefully removed can be permanently replanted. I say carefully uprooted; as far as possible, the root tip, which is not long at this time of year, should not be broken, and care should be taken when replanting not to go around it or crumple it. Once the plant has been transplanted, the soil must be kept wet to help it recover. "The rest of the work, during the year, consists of keeping the soil clean and weeding it once or twice, and above all watering it well if the weather is dry. A little manure water, not too strong so as not to burn the leaves, sprinkled on these vegetables will give them rapid growth and ensure a beautiful, good product. Very often, Scolymes don't reach full growth until the end of September and during October. "Scolymes are good to eat from late October or early November. The root is the only part of this vegetable that has so far been recognized as worthy of appearing on the table. In the middle of each root is a hard, woody part; this center, which its hardness prevents from being good to eat, only separates from the edible part when they have been cooked for preparation. This separation is easy to achieve. As far as I know, cultivation trials have not yet succeeded in making this woody part softer that it does not need to be removed for preparation. However, some people believe that this is the result that market gardening can achieve. "When winters are mild, Scolymes can remain in the garden until spring, when they will grow again; but when we fear that they will start growing again, we pull them out, cut off the upper part of the root that holds the leaves and bury them in sand in the cellar. They keep well until June, but in general, when winter approaches, it's a good idea to pull some of them out, prepare them as I've said, take them down to the cellar and cover the plants left in the garden with straw or straw mats. Although the cold only seems to kill the top of the root, the damage is always greater than it seems, because the frozen part causes the part that touches it to rot when it thaws. When weeding, either in spring or autumn, we choose the prettiest plants to bear seed. Place them in spring, 30 or 40 centimetres apart, in a good, well-ventilated spot away from the garden. "In June and July, the plants come up and give yellow flowers; but these flowers, which are born in the leaf axils, follow one another incessantly, and some have already ripened their seeds when the others bloom. "If the plants were not bristling with leaves more prickly than those of the Holly, one could try to collect the seeds as they ripen; I don't know of any amateur zealous enough to have wanted to do this. You seize the moment when the plants seem to have the greatest quantity of ripe seeds; you cut them, then place them on a cloth, in a basket, in a very dry place. After a few days, they are threshed and the seeds collected, which need to be winnowed in order to separate the bad ones. This operation must be carried out with care; many seeds that seem incapable of germinating do, however, germinate very well, as I was able to ascertain by winnowing them one year, in spring, before sowing them. Seeds which had fallen, as bad, in a position not very favorable to their germination have however risen well and in quantity." The note we have just read teaches us everything we need to know about growing Scolyme d'Espagne. It attributes, probably with good reason, the neglect in which this vegetable has remained to the irregularity of seed germination, and indicates the means to overcome this difficulty. But it may not be the only reason. One of the main obstacles to the success of a vegetable, however good it may be, comes from cooks and their routine habits. They probably reject Scolyma because, after cooking in salt water, they have to remove the central part of the roots, which is almost always woody and inedible. However, all vegetables give them at least as much trouble. Don't you have to remove the hay from artichokes, strain purees, shell broad beans, peas, beans, chop spinach, etc.? What's more laborious than removing the Scolyma cord? We hope that housewives, having recognized the superiority of the Scolyma root over all similar roots, will succeed in overcoming the resistance of their blue cords. Scolyme, once up, is a hardy plant. It occupies the soil for only three and a half months; the flavor of its roots is, in our opinion, infinitely more pleasant than that of Scorsonères and Salsifis, which occupy the soil two and three times longer. We can't recommend its cultivation enough. 36 In Spain, Scolyme is used differently from France, where only its roots are eaten. We didn't know this until we read a note by E. Bourgeau, botanist and traveler. Bourgeau, botanist-traveler, inserted in the Revue horticole, year 1852, page 60, a note which we feel we must reproduce: "Edible plant, vegetable, spontaneous in the southern part of Spain, which means that it is hardly cultivated there; but it is not the same in the vicinity of Madrid. It is the petioles and the middle rib of the leaf that are used in Spanish cooking, either in small packets put into stews, or prepared in different ways and combined with meat, or added to eggs, omelettes, etc. "Around January 1st, the fields are harvested, and the leaves are used for cooking. "Around January 1st, fields are almost universally covered with rosettes of leaves, which farmers harvest in the manner of dandelion, i.e. 1 or 2 inches from the taproot. The leaf blade is removed, leaving only the entire length of the rib, which is joined together and tied like a Romaine lettuce. Everyone prepares this vegetable as they see fit, either retaining a little of the root or eliminating it altogether. In January and February, a dozen are sold at the market for 30 centimes; in March and April, a dozen are only worth 15 centimes. "The markets are covered with it for five months of the year, and because it is so widely consumed in the country, the plant produces a fairly good income for country people every year. Sales end in May, by which time the leaves have become too hard and prickly to eat. "Scolyme's long, fleshy root is used as a vegetable in Provence and Languedoc, where the plant grows abundantly; in Montpellier, it's called Cardouille. It is not cultivated there; it is gathered wild in the fields, and, as the central axis is usually woody, the root is split longitudinally, removed, and it is the cortical part, bound in small bundles, that is sold" (1). In closing, we reiterate our advice to grow Scolyme d'Espagne. SCORZONERA DELICIOSA Gussone. Index seminum horto Boccadifalco, 1825, p. 11; Floræ siculæ synopsis, II, p. 389; DC. Prodr. VII, p. 118. Compositae family. Sicily. Tuberous-rooted perennial, cylindrical, oblong or almost globose, brown on the outside, white on the inside; stem simple, rarely twining at the base; leaves linear lanceolate, acuminate, flat, glabrous, a little tomentose at the base; capitula solitary at the tip of the twigs; involucral scales glabrous, acute, the outer ones lanceolate, ending in an acute point. Purple flowers. Here's what Gussone says about Scorsonera délicieuse: "The roots of this species are used (1) Le Bon Jardinier. in Sicily, candied in sugar, then dissolved in cold water; they are also used to make refreshing sorbets, iced and perfumed with large-flowered Jasmine, an aromatic that makes them very pleasant; finally, they are classified among those that provide refreshing decoctions." This plant does not seem to us to justify the ambitious qualification given to it by the Italian botanist. We received it from Mr. E. Morelli, engineer, and from Mr. Todaro, our colleague, eminent jurisconsult and director of the Palermo Botanical Garden. The latter wrote to us on April 27 1886: "With regard to the four plants requested, the Scorsonère délicieuse is the only one of them that we have in the garden and that grows spontaneously in the Palermo area... This species, which is widely used in Sicily, either in decoctions, juleps or sorbets, cultivated in pots, perishes if watered after flowering." In the course of the same year, we received several letters from Mr. E. Morelli, from which we extract the following: "I have never heard of Scorzonera outside Sicily, and even there it is only used by confectioners, who make a syrup that is consumed either as a drink in the same way as orgeat, lemon or other syrups, or for making ice cream... " ... In Sicily, plants are not sown, but propagated by simply separating the roots, which is done in October and November. As the plant is completely wild, no care is taken. The roots, which are the only part of the plant that is used, can be harvested at any time by pulling them up as soon as they have reached their normal size. "To use them, we crush them as best we can, then throw them into an aqueous sugar syrup, boil until concentrated and strain through a cloth. They can also be candied in the syrup, taking care to boil them first to remove the rind... " ... In Palermo, they are used to make refreshing decoctions, syrup and jam. The syrup is sold by the best confectioner in the area for 1 fr. 10 per bottle of about 400 grams, the jam for 3 fr. 20 per kilogram; both contain enough, even too much sugar, so there's nothing to fear from the journey. "The plant tolerates the cold perfectly. In Sicily, it is even found on mountains, where it overwinters under the snow. "My gardener claims that some of the roots are harvested when the leaves start to wilt, i.e. around the middle of August; some are left to avoid destroying the plants, which is all the more important as, in a new plantation, you can only harvest in the second year, whereas once the plants are well established you can pull out roots every year. "In fact, however, this plant is not cultivated here; it grows spontaneously in the mountains, without any care. It is therefore probable that, in cultivated land, it could produce more abundantly. The same observation applies to the question of whether the roots can be eaten as a vegetable. Here, the fiber is very hard; to use it to prepare herbal teas, it is grated after being scalded to remove the outer shell. It is not unlikely that, in your climate and in better soil, it will be softer. We have little to add to the information provided by the letters from which we have just given extracts. We can hardly expect the roots of Scorzonera deliciosa to become tender enough to be used as a vegetable. That they make refreshing decoctions; that they provide the material for syrups, jams and frozen sorbets, is a given, since, in these various forms, they are in general use in Sicily; but we are obliged to contradict the learned Dr. Baron von Mueller, who, in his Select extra tropical Plants, presents Scorzonera deliciosa as a purple-flowered species, equal, if not superior in its culinary uses, to Salsify, to which it is closely related. We planted Scorzonera deliciosa roots in the ground, and they thrived. We covered them for the winter with frames and leaves. Although they overwinter under the snow in the mountains of Sicily, this fact does not allow us to believe that they can withstand the cold in the vicinity of Paris; precautions are necessary. Mr. E. Morelli sent us syrup and jams from Scorzonera. The latter arrived in the most unfortunate condition. All the liquid had drained out of the crate in which they were stored, and all that remained in the jars was a tasteless paste. The syrup was excellent. 567 MALaBaila SEKAKUL Russell; Alepp. II, p. 249 (Syn.: M. platyptera Boiss; Pastinaca dissecta Vent; Pastinaca Sekakul Rauw; Leiotulus alexandrinus Ehrenb.). Umbelliferae family. Perennial, indigenous and cultivated in Anatolia, Armenia, Svrie, Palestine, etc. (Boissier, Flora orientalis, vol. 2, p. 1057). The root is tuberous and oblong; the leaves are bipinnate, with toothed or incised segments. The whole plant reaches 30 centimetres in height or a little more. In a note published in the Journal de l'Académie d'Horticulture, 2nd edition, tome I (1831), p. 134, De Candolle regrets that the cultivation of this Oriental umbellifer has not been given more attention in Europe. Its root, says Rauwolf (Travels, vol. I, p. 66, ed. Rai, London 1693), is cylindrical, swiveling, grayish on the outside, white on the inside, of delicate consistency and as if medullary, an inch thick and an inch and a half long. Instead of fibrils, it has wart-like nodules. Its flavor is mild and not unlike that of the carrot. Jean Bauhin and Morison gave crude figures of it, and from then on the plant was almost forgotten by European naturalists. Miller mentioned it briefly in his dictionary under the inaccurate name of Tordylium Sekakul. Russell, in the report of his trip to Aleppo, vol. II, p. 249, refers to it as Pastinaca Sekakut, praising it as a food plant. Olivier and Bruguières harvested it near Aleppo and sent seeds to Cels. The resulting plants were described and illustrated by Ventenat, Jardin de Cels, tab. 78, under the name Pastinaca dissecta. Sekakul is reminiscent of parsnip in its yellow flowers and fruit, but is easily distinguished at first glance by its sharply dissected leaves with toothed lobes. In the note we quote, published over sixty years ago, De Candolle said: "The root seems to me to have more in common with the parsnip than with the carrot, and if to the delicacy of the former, it joins the flavor of the latter, it would seem to deserve to be introduced into our vegetable gardens." In our research into experiments to cultivate new food plants in European gardens, we have found no trace of trials with Sekakul. We regret to add that, despite the requests we have made in the East, it has not yet been possible to obtain seeds of this Umbelliferous plant, the value of which we would nevertheless have liked to know. We would also like to mention another Umbellifera that we were keen to cultivate, but were unable to obtain; this is Sium Helenianum Hook. f., Icones plantarum, pl. 1032, a plant native to the island of St. Helena, whose green stems are sold in markets under the name Jellico, and eaten raw (Official guide of the Museums of Economic Botany (Dicotyledons aud Gymnosperms), 2nd ed. 1886, p. 83). SERI Panturasee (Bengalese.) OENANTHE STOLONIFERA DC. Phellandrium stoloniferum Roxb; Dasycoma subpinnatum Miq. Umbelliferae family. Perennial, native to marshy areas of Japan, China and India. "This marsh herb is used as a spinach. (Mueller, Select, etc.). "Seri or Œnanthe stolonifera grows wild in wetlands and marshes; however, that which is sold commercially is the result of cultivation and is eaten cooked." (Le Japon à l'Exposition universelle de 1878, vol. Il, p. 137). "According to the Japanese Commission, Seri is frequently cultivated, used in food and eaten boiled and salted". (Dr Mène, Des productions végétales du Japon). A note kindly sent to us illustrates the cultivation method used in Japan. It is dated Yokohama, October 22 1886. "The plant does not reproduce by seed; it reproduces in the following way: the soil must be muddy and constantly covered with water. The rice fields here are very suitable for growing Seri. First, the soil is stirred well to obtain a liquid, even mud, which is left to stand for about a week. Then take the roots, cut into small pieces of about 2 cm, and sow them in prepared soil. The seedbed should be fairly light. The roots are then pressed lightly into the mud. After four or five days, you'll start to see the leaves grow. It's a good idea then, as far as possible, to increase the quantity of water in proportion to the plant's growth, but always leaving the leaves above the water. "This culture can be tried out in a crate, provided the bottom of the vase is 8 to 10 centimetres deep. The above-mentioned note accompanied a shipment of well-packed Seri plants, which, according to the experienced Japanese shipping agent, were due to arrive safely in Paris; however, the plants had died during the journey, and a new request was sent to Yokohama. OENANTHE SARMENTOSA Nutt. We have cultivated this species, which was sent to us from North America. Its young stems and leaves are eaten by the Oregon Indians. The plant has grown vigorously in our basins, and its long, creeping stems have spread out in all directions. But, as the genus Œnanthe contains a good number of poisonous species, we didn't want to taste the American herb until we were sure it was harmless. To this end, we turned to Dr. Gréhant, director of the physiology laboratory at the Muséum, who injected guinea pigs with a concentrated extract. The experiment having been favorable, we had a dish of Œnanthe sarmentosa prepared; but we must declare that, apart from a slight aromatic flavor, analogous to that of Celery, it seemed to us to have no particular merit. COMESTIBLE SOUCHET Souchet Sultan. Ground almond. CYPERUS ESCULENTUS L. Family Cyperaceae. Creeping plant. Rhizomes producing slender fibers, each ending in an ovoid tuber, marked with circular impressions, yellowish-brown on the outside, white on the inside, with a pleasant, sweet flavor. Stem triqueterous, glabrous, leafy at the bottom; leaves as long as or longer than the stem, flat, canaliculate and keeled, rough towards their tips on the margins and keel. Umbel simple or compound, with 7-10 unequal rays, the longest of which branch at the apex, with an involucre of 4-6 leaves longer than the inflorescence; on each ray attach 10-14 lanceolate or linear spikes, compressed, with 10-18 flowers; the scales are elliptical, almost obovate, keeled-navicular, 7-9-veined, yellowish-brown, with a green midline. Yellow nutsedge grows spontaneously in southern Europe, the Orient and northern Africa. Its rhizomes are lined with tubers the size of hazelnuts, with a pleasant, sweet flavor reminiscent of chestnuts. Fig. 76. - Yellow nutsedge (Cyperus esculentus). Easy to grow. Tubers are planted on May 1st, in clumps spaced 25 to 30 centimetres apart in all directions, and first soaked in water. A forty-eight-hour immersion is necessary. If the season is warm, vegetation is very active. The single culm clumps, produced by just five or six tubers, give rise to a multitude of new shoots, spread out and join together, turning the plantation into a meadow. Weed by hand, if possible, to avoid cutting the young shoots; water abundantly when the weather is hot and refrain from doing so when it's cold. Not watering does not compromise the harvest, but it is much less. In Spain, we irrigate the plantation as soon as it is established, and repeat every eight to ten days. Harvesting takes place in October. If it doesn't freeze, we can wait until the first days of November. Valencian growers cut the stems before flowering to encourage the development of the roots and tubers; but in the Paris climate, you don't get flowers, and we remember growing Souchet, not without success, in the Landes department, without our plantation having produced a single flower. Moreover, Souchet is grown in Spain not by sowing seeds, but by planting tubers. Harvesting is carried out by pulling up the clumps, which are knocked against a reed rack over a wicker sieve to separate the tubers from the roots. The tubers are then carefully washed and, when clean, laid out on mats in the sun to dry and preserve them. In this process, they lose around a third of their weight (1). Dried tubers retain their germanative properties for a full year. Chufa, the Spaniards' favorite refreshment, is made with tubers from the last harvest, not from the current crop. Chufa (1) Bulletin de la Société impériale d'Acclimatation, t. X, n° 12. Note presented by Mr. Ch. Barbier, civil engineer. is an orgeat that lends itself admirably to making ice creams, which are widely consumed in Madrid. To prove its merit, we ourselves served it to a gathering of twenty people, who found it very good. Here's the recipe for Chufa: Soak 250 grams of tubers for 48 hours; grind; add 1 liter of water and 150 grams of sugar; strain. Serve as orgeat, or use to make ice cream. It cannot be preserved. We didn't stick to this recipe, and knowing that the Israelites of Oran make Souchet cakes, we managed to have some made that were not significantly inferior in quality to ordinary almond cakes (1). However, we would like to point out a few precautions to be taken. The skin of ordinary almonds is easily removed with boiling water; but the tuber of the Souchet resists, and it is impossible to remove the film that envelops it. This film is so thin that, after careful grinding, there is absolutely nothing left of it; but there is a sort of callus at the point where the tuber attaches to the rhizome, and, to get rid of it, the ground almonds must be placed in a sieve of suitable fineness. The calluses remain there. There is no need to give a recipe for these cakes here. Proceed as for ordinary almond cakes. Instead of bitter almonds (1) Journal de la Société centrale d'Horticulture, 1877, t. XI, p. 40. you can add a little cinnamon to enhance the taste. Yellow nutsedge makes an excellent oil and a good brandy, but we would be going too far if we were to consider it as an economic plant. In this respect, however, we consider it to be extremely important, and recommend to those of our readers who are concerned with agriculture and industrial plants, the Note we published in the Journal de la Société centrale d'Horticulture, 1878, tome XII, 2nd series, page 341. As a limited-use vegetable plant, Yellow Nutsedge nevertheless has a marked place in amateur gardens. SOYA (1) Mame and Daizu, in Japan; Yeou-teou in China. GLYCINE SOIA Sieb. et Zucc; Dolichos Soja L.; Soja hispida Mœnch; Glycine hispida Miquel. Legume family. Soya is native to Japan and probably also to Cochinchina and Java; it is cultivated not only in Japan, but in India, where it bears the Bengali name of Gari-Kulay and the Hindostani name of Bhat, Bhatwan in the island of Ceylon, in the Malacca peninsula, in the Philippine Islands, in Borneo, in Java, in (1) For further details, see Le Soya, sa composition chimique, sa culture et ses usages, by A. Paillieux; Paris, Librairie agricole, 26, rue lacob. -- in the Kingdom of Siam, Cochinchina, Tonkin and especially in China, Thibet, Mongolia and the provinces of Honan, Shengking, Shangtung and Chan-si. 4 Fig. 77. - Soya (Glycine Soja). Kæmpfer describes the plant in his Amœnitatum exoticarum politico-physico-medicarum, fascicule V, 1792: "Sorte de Haricot à pods velues comme celles du Lupin, à graines arronées et blanches comme celles du Pois; haut d'environ 4 pieds. "It has a hairy, unevenly round stem. "Its leaves resemble those of the common bean, but with coarser hairs on the underside. In August, in the leaf axils, it blooms bluish-white, very small flowers on a common stalk, similar to those of the Lentil, with straight, barely spread out standards and wings, followed by numerous pods, barely 2 inches long, covered with rough, long hair, similar to Lupin pods with yellow flowers, containing two seeds, rarely three, similar in shape and flavor to Garden Pea, a little compressed though, with brown umbilicus. " Kæmpfer seems to have known only one type of Soya, with white seeds and a brown umbilicus; but we know that there are at least thirty varieties with yellow, black, white, grey with white spots, greenish, red or variegated red seeds, some with a white hilum and others with a red hilum. In addition, depending on the variety, the leaflets may be rounded or lanceolate, barely hispid or very hispid; the flowers may be blue, white or purple; and the fruits, round or flattened, may contain 5 or 6 seeds. The quality of these seeds is mainly due to their high nitrogen content and the large quantity of oil they contain. Soya is one of the most widely used plants in China and Japan, for both food and industrial purposes. 37 I. - Soya in Japan. The famous traveller and naturalist Kæmpfer (1) was one of the first, if not the first, to make known the plant we are concerned with. We cannot resist recounting the journey during which he saw Soya and was taught its uses. Kæmpfer arrived in Batavia in September 1689, which he left the following May, and embarked as physician to the embassy which the Dutch Company sent to Japan every year. He obtained permission to go on board the ship that was to reach Siam, and finally, on September 25, he went ashore in the small town of Desima, near Nangasaki. By the services he rendered the Japanese, by his complaisance, by his liberality, he insinuated himself into the friendship and familiarity of the interpreters and officers, and won them over so well that they refused to answer any of his questions and, when he was alone with them, revealed to him even those things about which they are bound to inviolable secrecy. A young man who had been given to him for service, and at the same time to study medicine and surgery under him, having successfully treated Desima's principal officer under his direction, received permission not to leave Kæmpfer's side. Kæmpfer taught Dutch to his pupil, who, out of gratitude, brought him all the books he could wish for. So, despite the jealousy and mistrust of the Japanese government, Kæmpfer was able to (1) Kæmpfer Engelbert, physician and famous traveler, born September 16, 1651, at Lemgo, in the county of Lippe, Westphalia; died November 2, 1716. satisfy his curiosity on most of the points he wished to know. When the director of Dutch trade left for Yedo on February 10, 1691, Kæmpfer accompanied him, and thus had the opportunity to see the interior of the empire. The following year, he made the same trip with another director. He left Nangasaki on October 31. Following these trips, he published his Histoire naturelle du Japon. Miso, according to Kæmpfer. "To obtain Miso, we take a measure of Mame or Daeds beans (Soya), which we cook for a very long time in water, until completely softened, and which we reduce to a soft pulp by crushing them. This method of crushing is continued to mix the pulp with four measures of salt in summer, a number reduced to three in winter, as the product is all the more irreproachable the less salt is added; I would add, however, that it keeps less long. "We then add and mix in a measure (of volume equal to that of the beans) of Koos or husked rice, slightly steamed with fresh water; then, after cooling, we place in a warm cellar for twenty-four and forty-eight hours, until it cools and contracts. "This mixture, which has the consistency of porridge, is introduced into a wooden vase that has contained the beer commonly known as Sacki. Before use, this product is kept for one or two months. "Koos gives the pulp a sweet flavor, and its manufacture requires, like the Italians' polenta, the skill of a master. For this reason, there are those who only prepare it, and then purify the resulting product. SOOJU, according to Kæmpfer. "To make Sooju, we take Daeds beans cooked to the same point and an equal quantity of Muggi, or Wheat, and as much roughly pulverized Barley or Rye; the latter seed gives a blacker product. In short, one measure of each substance is used. The beans are mixed with the crushed wheat, covered with a cloth and left to ferment for twenty-four hours. The mixture is then covered with the prescribed salt and placed in a clay pot, where two measures of ordinary water are mixed with half the dose. The next day, the mixture is stirred with a spatula, covered and stirred several days in a row, at least once and preferably two or three times. "After three months' preparation, the mass is filtered and the liqueur is expressed and stored in wooden vases; the clarity and quality of this liqueur are in keeping with its age. Nothing has changed in the last two hundred years in the way Miso and Shoyu are made, and their consumption has not diminished. In Japan, these products are still an absolute necessity. The information we receive from those of our nationals who visit or live in Japan does not differ appreciably from that collected by Kæmpfer in 1689; but the learned traveler said nothing about To-fu (Daizu), a cheese made from Soya seeds; we have to fill this gap. He paid particular attention to the species used to make Miso and Shoyu, citing only three or four varieties, whereas there are probably some thirty. Among these varieties, some are early, others late. The latter cannot be usefully cultivated in central France. Some are used as vegetables for direct human consumption, such as beans, broad beans, peas, lentils, etc., etc.; others are prepared in more or less complicated ways, making them veritable industrial plants. The choice to be made among these varieties is impracticable by far. You would have to take them all in, grow them all, taste them all, and only reject those that should not be kept after complete experience.
