We have extracted the following passage from a letter sent to us by an Orientalist about one of the Mame listed above. It concerns the Nakate mame (n° 3): "The seed in the middle (of the pod) is of a yellowish-white color. In general, its shape and color are similar to those of the previous Mame. It is, however, larger and looser than this other variety. Although, depending on whether the seed comes early or late, the shape and color differ, it is nothing other than what is shown above (1). Mame is grown extensively in all provinces. It is used to make Miso, which is why it is also called Miso mame. Mame no. 9 on our list is not without merit: "Ordinary Soyas," we write, "at least the yellowish-white variety, most commonly used to make Shoyu, To-fu and Miso, are not usually eaten in the wild. Black and green Soyas are sometimes eaten in the same way as dried beans. A variety with large green beans is eaten quite often in Japan after being roasted, ground and mixed with sugar. Children love this kind of racahout or revalescière and eat it by the handful. As far as I know, no pastry or cake is made from soybeans. Mame tea is also eaten plain. It's likely that several other varieties are eaten the same way, but we don't know. What we can say is that all Mame can be used to make Miso, Shoyu and To-fu. Our kind correspondent, Dr. H***, brought us a large, yellowish-white Mame, the most widely grown for making Miso, Shoyu and To-fu (2), but unfortunately too late for our country, where summer is too short, and he had to stick to growing the small, round-grained, yellowish Chinese Mame. The latter is much less beautiful, but ripens perfectly. This is the variety most cultivated in France, and to which we have given the name Soya d'Étampes. We regret to add that all the varieties that the (1) We said that our list was taken from a work with figures. (2) Probably Nakate mame (no. 3). Dr H**T has seen in Japan seem to him to be too late for France. Mame is grown in Japan in the same way as beans, but the plants need to be spaced much farther apart and are more resistant to autumn frosts. The Japanese don't make Soya oil. They use only sesame oil for cooking and rapeseed oil for lighting. Other oils, extracted from the fruits of different trees, are used for industrial purposes. In the part of Japan where our correspondent lived, Soya straw was fed to animals. In Satsouma, in the far south, fine horses were given rations of Daizu seeds, which were said to be a much better, but more expensive, food than naked barley. Horses eat Daizu very well, both raw and cooked. According to Krasnow (1), early Soyas, sown in early to mid-May, require between 2,300 and 3,000 degrees of heat, but most perish as soon as the thermometer drops to 2 degrees below zero. Thanks to the warmth of Japanese summers, the general cultivation of this plant has spread to the northern part of the island of Yeso. It was Eurotium Orizæ, the fermenting agent in Saké or rice brandy, that determined the fermentation process in the making of Shoyu. Here's what the book Le Japon à l'Exposition universelle de 1878 has to say about Mame: (1) Krasnow, Les régions théières de l'Asie subtropicale (1er vol., Japon), St. Petersburg 1897, (in Russian). "Mame has many uses, as it can not only be eaten cooked and ground into flour, but also used to make Shoyu, Miso and To-fu. Mame, its husk, leaves and stalk are used to feed horses; they have also been used recently, on a trial basis, to feed sheep, and the results obtained have proved that they are the best food that can be given to them. "To-fu is made from two types of boiled mame (1): Shiro mame and Ki mame; once pressed and hardened, it can be kept for a long time. Yuba is a similar paste (2), made with the same ingredients." MISO Our correspondent professes deep contempt for Miso. He does, however, give us a recipe for its preparation, which we reproduce because it is very simple and does not, like Kæmpfer's, prescribe the use of Koos, or husked rice mixed with Daizu. Miso," he says, "is a more or less fermented paste made with Daizu (Soya). It's used to make a kind of soup, which is almost every Japanese person's lunch. It's very bad, and I've eaten some on my travels, not having anything else to hand, but it's by no means a desirable acquisition. As for making Miso, it's very simple. Daizu are boiled, or rather steamed, then pounded, with a little salt added. This (1) Mame milk is boiled. 2) We haven't managed to learn what Fuba is. paste in a barrel, and after two months it's as ready as possible. The only difficulty here (in France) with all Daizu preparations is obtaining non-calcareous water. You have to collect rainwater or use distilled water, whereas in Japan, at least in the part I lived in, the water from streams, wells and rivers contains no trace of lime." THE SHOYU In a letter published in the Bulletin de la Société d'Acclimatation, 1862, page 690, Mr. Eugène Simon describes the processes used today by industry to manufacture Shoyu. We reproduce it below: "Soya is a condiment which is consumed in considerable quantities in Japan, and which, a few years ago, enjoyed marked success in America, England and Holland, as well as in India, where it was first introduced. Today, its popularity is limited to America; exports are low to India, where it is replaced by another product, and almost nil to Europe, which has given up on it because of the difficulty of getting it through the warm latitudes of the tropics without corrupting it. "It is nonetheless an excellent product, which would offer culinary art resources of more than one kind, if it could be obtained as good as it is in the country from which it comes. But nothing could be easier; all you have to do is make it locally. "There are more than ten factories in the city of Nangasaki, each occupying an average area of 700 to 800 square meters, and delivering more than 1,200,000 kilograms for consumption every year. "Two types of grain are needed to make Soya: one is a special bean called Haricot Soja, about fifteen kilograms of which are included under No. 5 in the shipment I have just sent to France; the other is ordinary barley. "We make two equal parts of Beans and Barley; we cook the former in the same volume of water and roast the latter; then we put them together in large tubs, into which we pour them little by little, mixing them as much as possible with large wooden spatulas. "When the whole has the consistency of a fairly thick dough, it is placed in wooden molds 1 1/2 inches high, 18 inches long and 8 inches wide. In arranging the loaf or brick in the mold, the top surface should be slightly concave. "These loaves are then transported to hermetically sealed chambers, where they must ferment on shelves arranged around and in the center of the chamber. All walls and openings, with the exception of two panes of glass placed at man-height, so that fermentation can be monitored from the outside, must be carefully padded with straw, secured with Bamboo or other wood. "Fermentation takes place after very few hours; but, if the temperature of the room is too low to decide, it can be provoked by placing a small brazier in the room. "However, this method should only be used as a last resort, as its usual effect is to brown the bread. Fermentation lasts about seven days, during which time we are allowed to enter the room once or twice, to ensure that it is taking place under the right conditions. "When properly done, the loaves should have a uniform golden-yellow hue. "They are then removed and thrown into large vats 6 feet high and 4 1/2 feet wide. Water saturated with salt is added in the proportion of 2 kilograms to 1 kilogram of bread. Stir and mix as the vats fill. "The vats should then be left to rest for at least a year; but when extra-fine qualities of soybean are required, this rest is extended to three years. Whatever the length of time, the paste is finally removed from the vats, placed in sacks made of hemp or, better still, palm twine, and put under the press. "The Soya that runs off during the first few turns of the press is of the highest quality, but because of the price that would have to be charged for it, it would not be sold in any way. This quality is not used, except in the factories of the two capitals of Yeddo and Miaco, where there are a large number of princes and wealthy people who can pay a decent price for it. "Most generally, only two qualities are made: the first from all the juice extracted by the press, which is then of a good average quality, and the second [which is obtained by making a second mixture of press residue with salted water that is left to stand for six months: this is sold only to the poor. "A jar of soya, weighing 214 kilos, 500 grams, sells in Japan for 16 to 17 francs. "Ordinary first-quality Soya is a syrupy, dark-brown liquor. It is almost the sole sauce for all Japanese dishes, rich or poor. It's especially good with fish. "Europeans in China and Japan, who use it to a greater or lesser extent, add it to beef and beef broth, to which it imparts a most pleasing color and flavor. "Case No. 10 of miscellaneous products, which will be part of my shipment, contains three qualities under Nos. 18, 19, 20. No. 18 comes from Miaco, but it is possible that the journey may alter it a little." The importance of Shoyu is such that we have to mention two more recipes: one, because it is much simpler than that of the special manufacturers, and because our correspondent uses it for his own house; the other, because it prescribes a particular preparation of the salt intended for the manufacture of Shoyu, a prescription not represented by the other recipes. Our correspondent tells us: "I make quite good Shoyu, replacing Daizu with Peas if necessary, as is often done in Japan. The Japanese even often substitute Broad Beans, but they give an inferior product. "Here's the recipe for the sauce, as I learned it in Japan: take, by volume, two parts of naked Barley or Wheat and three parts of Daizu. Macerate for a day and a night in non-calcareous water, then steam until completely cooked. The beans should not fall apart, but should be tender. The two grains are mixed together, then spread out in layers 2 to 3 centimetres thick in crates kept in a warm place, neither too dry nor too damp. The grains take twelve or fifteen days to mature, depending on the season. The best time is spring or autumn. The mold should be greenish-blue, thick and velvet-like. "Large white or black molds are worthless, and should be removed as soon as they appear. When the grains are completely covered with mold and form a single mass, expose them to the sun. When dry, they are rubbed between the hands, then winnowed to remove the dust produced by the mould debris. At this point, two parts salt to three parts moldy grains are taken, still by volume; they are placed in barrels or earthen vases, with a sufficient quantity of water to cover the whole with three or four fingers of liquid. All you have to do is stir the mixture from time to time and wait three to six months, after which the sauce can be drained through a sieve. It can be kept in barrels or bottles for several years and, to my taste, is a good substitute for broth or roasted meat juice in dishes. Our correspondent adds: "I don't know how much Shoyu is exported, nor do I have any figures for domestic consumption, but it's a staple of Japanese cuisine. It replaces butter, oil, fat and gravy. Everything - vegetables, fish, pasta - is usually prepared with Shoyu. There's not a village, however small, that doesn't have its own Shoyu makers, and a lot of them are made in private homes. "During my stay in Japan, the price of Shoyu varied, depending on its quality, from 8 to 12 sen (40 to 50 centimes) a mas (1 liter 80). "Please take my information as relating only to the part of Japan between To-Kio (Yedo) and the south-western tip of the large island that Europeans insist on calling Nippon. It's in this part, and especially in the divisions called San yo do and San yu do, that I've been able to see things for myself. When, by chance, I tell you about the north and the southern end, it's from information and not from sight." Recipe taken from the volume entitled: Le Japon à l'Exposition universelle de 1878: "Shoyu, which is one of the indispensable condiments in Japanese food, is prepared in the following way: first, the hulled wheat is separated from the grains that are unripe or spoiled, and any small stones or other foreign bodies mixed in are removed. The grain is then toasted, coarsely ground and a quantity of boiled and cooled Soya (peas) is added. Left in a warm chamber, the mixture turns to yeast after three or four days, and salt is added. "These three substances enter the mixture in equal proportions. According to the old processes, one to of Barley, three sho of Wheat and one to of Pea were mixed, or one to of Pea, three sho of Barley and seven sho of Wheat. In either case, the mixture was toasted and mixed with two to of water and one to of salt. "The best salt is that from Dako, in the province of Havima. To be suitable for making Shoyu, salt is processed in the following way: the best possible salt is taken and placed in a box, where it is left to stand for five to seven months. A sort of brine is then formed at the bottom of the tin, which is left aside; the top layer of salt is then removed and boiled in a boiler, then the liquid is decanted and left to stand. When all impurities have settled to the bottom, the liquid is decanted and the yeast described above is added. The whole process is stirred two or three times a day, from June to September. "After a while, the mixture becomes pasty, but we continue to stir it, and after fifteen, twenty and sometimes even thirty months, we obtain Shoyu. "The mixture is then poured into cotton bags and pressed, which completes the operation. "Once filtered, the Shoyu is boiled, then cooled; any remaining lees are removed, then stored in barrels. "The residue from pressing first-quality Shoyu is used as follows: take five to of this residue, add one to of water, then shake it, press it, boil it, add two to of salt, let it stand and decant it. This new mixture is then added, in greater or lesser quantities, to the different qualities of Shoyu." TO-FU (DAIZU CHEESE) Daizu are soaked for twenty-four hours in lime-free water. Rainwater or distilled water is the only water available for this purpose. In Japan, river water contains no trace of lime. The softened grains are ground in a small, hardstone hand mill, much like the mill used by our Algerian Arabs. This produces a coarse paste, which is diluted in non-calcareous water, giving it a milky appearance. This mixture is passed through a cloth to retain the insoluble parts. The residue is used to feed domestic animals, all of which, from cattle to rabbits, are very fond of it. The albuminous liquid containing most of the emulsified fat is put on the stove. When it is a little more than attified, a few spoonfuls of the mother liquor draining from the piles of sea salt are added to determine coagulation. This mother water probably acts through the calcium and magnesium chlorides it contains, which make it very bitter. What is certain is that it causes the curds to separate, just as rennet does with milk. It also has the same disadvantages: if too much is added, the curd becomes hard and dry. It is drained into molds, where it takes on the desired shape, and then placed in the basin of running water with which every house is equipped. To-fu is usually eaten fresh, very often cooked with shoyu and dry fish, sometimes fried38 , more often grilled. In winter, it is sometimes frozen and then dried, giving it a spongy consistency. In this state, it keeps for a very long time and can be prepared in a variety of ways. In its fresh state, vegetable cheese has a very delicate consistency, but retains a certain raw bean taste, which is not pleasant. II. - Soya in Cochinchina. The plant is grown in Cochinchina. How important is this crop? What are the preferred varieties? Do Soya seeds have any other use in the country than in Japan or China? We don't know, but in the Bulletin du Comité agricole et industriel de la Cochinchine, 2e série, tome Ier, page 456, we come across an article by M. Corroy, which is most interesting. Corroy's most interesting article, and in keeping with our plan, which is to place before the reader's eyes all the documents relating to the issue at hand, we reproduce it below: "We brought back from Manchuria, at the same time as Sorghum, 3,000 kilos of Black Peas, which are generally used as animal feed in northern China, but can only be given as a supplement to rations; the essential oil they contain is said to stimulate the digestive organs; the restorative action of these grains is said to be sovereign on animals emaciated as a result of hard work and long walks! From November 25th to April 7th, we gave 250 grams of these Peas per day to each animal, and we have to declare that, if at times the animals sought them out greedily, it often happened, on the contrary, that they left them at the bottom of the manger, despite the precaution we took to mix them as exactly as possible with Sorghum and Paddy (1). Nor did we find that they provoked, in tired animals, the kind of resurrection of exhausted strength attributed to them by the Chinese and Manchurians. "What's more, these grains are very bitter when taken dry, so they have to be cooked to rid them of the bitter principle contained in the epidermis. The result is great difficulties in administering rafion, difficulties which do not seem to us to be outweighed by the problematic virtues we were told about. We therefore believe that there is no reason to repeat the trial (2). "Nevertheless, as this plant can be used for human food and industry, we have submitted a sample to the Cochinchine Agricultural and Industrial Committee, which has asked Mr. Pierre to examine it and give his assessment of this legume. "Here are the results of Mr. Pierre's observations: "I sowed the seeds reported by Mr. Corroy. The seeds belong to Glycine Soja Sieb. et Zucc., and I am deliberately establishing a synonymy, with the help of Bentham, to show the various forms this species takes, depending on the climate in which it is cultivated. (1) Unhulled rice. (2) Experiments carried out

in Europe show that Mr. Corroy's conclusions are unfounded. - P. B. "There is a noticeable difference between the black-seeded variety and the white-seeded variety; but when we compare the plant from Mr. Corroy's seeds and the variety cultivated in Cochinchina, India and Java, we understand why Miquel was able to make a distinct species of it, under the name Soja angustifolia. "Indeed, the hispid character of the species is barely accentuated in the plants from my seedlings, the leaflets are oval and, in the majority of cases, there are hardly any acuminate leaflets. The flowers are blue and not reddish, as Baker describes them; they are exactly those of Loureiro's description. The fruits are shorter than in the species cultivated near Saigon, wider, flatter and a little falciform. "All these characteristics have been described by several authors, depending on the variety. So far, the following races have been identified: A. Race with white flowers. B. - Blue. C. - purple. D. - with oval lanceolate leaflets, very hispid. E. - rounded, barely hispid. F. - round, elongated fruits with several seeds (5 or 6). G. - - flattened, with one or two seeds. H. - with black seeds. I. - white (1). "These differences in a plant, one of the oldest cultivated by man, and in a family where the cultivated species have acquired the most variable forms, should not surprise; they are much (1) We have seen above that the seeds present other colors. - P. B. more considerable in Vigna Catiang, for example, by the character of straight stems or that of climbing stems and by the multiple shape of the fruits and seeds. "Glycine Soja is grown in China, Japan, India, from the Himalayas to Ceylon, on the Malacca peninsula, in Tonkin, Lower Cochinchina and Siam. It is also cultivated in the Philippines, Borneo, Java and elsewhere. The seeds, lightly boiled or roasted, are eaten by humans, like those of Vigna, Dolichos and Phaseolus. But they are often used to make a brine that is widely used in Japan, China and Cochinchina, and is mixed with all kinds of dishes to enhance their taste. They are also used to make a tasteless porridge, which is nevertheless considered a very pleasant dish once a few condiments have been added. "Like beans, dolics, peas, Embrevades (Cajanus indicus DC.), lentils and Grahm (chickpeas), Soya seeds are also used to feed draught animals and horned cattle. Its green or dry fodder is also much sought-after by animals. "This plant can be grown in lower Cochinchina, with advantage, from May to October, and is used in many crop rotations. It prepares the soil well for growing tobacco, indigo and cotton, but should be fertilized first or grown before these plants. III. - Soya in China In China, we find the same food plants and culinary practices as in Japan. Soya occupies a considerable place here. Its generic name is Yeou-teou. We have been unable to obtain a nomenclature of the varieties, let alone the names of the earliest ones. They are probably no less numerous than in Japan, where there are around thirty. In China, all leguminous plants with pods and edible seeds are called Teou. Whether they are beans or dolics, peas or broad beans, chickpeas or cajans, lentils or soyas, the name is the same, and the qualifier only indicates their color, shape or use, without allowing us to distinguish the genus. As a result, the seed shipments and notes of Mgr Guillemin, M. l'abbé Perny, MM. de Montigny, Dabry, Eugène Simon, etc., could not provide us with the elements of a sufficient nomenclature. We can therefore only present with certainty a very small number of Yeou-teou: Houang-teou.......... Yellow soy. Houang-ta-teou....... Large yellow Soya. He-teou.............. Black Soya. Pe-teou.............. White soybean. Ho-teou.............. Grey soybean. Thsing-pan-teou....... White spotted soybean. However, at the Vienna Universal Exhibition in 1873, there were 13 varieties: 5 yellow, 3 black, 3 green, 2 brown and, among them, 2 early varieties, one yellow and one brown. We don't know their Chinese names. In China, Soybeans are grown like beans. Large-scale cultivation is virtually non-existent. The land is so fragmented that there are hardly any fields larger than one hectare. This is clear from the following letter sent to us by Mr. Eugène Simon, former French consul in China: "There are no large-scale crops in China, neither for Soya nor for other plants. Everyone makes his own cheese. Very often, too, everyone makes his own oil, if not at home, at least at one of his relatives', all of whom are neighbors and more regularly grouped and distributed around the head of the family's estate. "Oil production only becomes the object of a special industry in cantons where there is a very large number of small fields cultivated annually with Soya. "The fields have hardly more than 4 to 5 meous (about 13 meous per hectare). A field of one hectare can hardly be found anywhere except in the north, and, having re-read my Notes, I find that the largest fields that came into view did not exceed 8 to 9 meous. "There are certainly large landholdings in China, but they are smaller and less numerous than in France, except in Mongolia, in the land of grasses and pastures. "Properties larger than 1,000 hectares are exceedingly rare and virtually non-existent. "In the whole province of Tché-Kiang, which has a population of 20 or 24 million, I know of only three or four owners with 250 to 500 hectares. The most common properties among those already considered large are those of 15 to 20 hectares, and the most common absolutely are 3 hectares and below. "Land and wealth are therefore more divided and more evenly distributed than in France. "In the northern provinces, where the victorious Manchu had tried to establish a territorial aristocracy when they entered China two hundred and sixty years ago, there are more numerous estates of 500 to 1,000 hectares, still in the hands of these so-called nobles, who are, for the most part, miserable, some of whom even beg, while the Chinese farmers are rich and continue to exploit the soil without being able to be expelled. "The law and morals that favor work do not allow them to be evicted and, on the contrary, want to ensure them the profit and added value due to their work. To get rid of them, you'd have to give them compensation, a condition that Mongol lords have never been able to meet. But in any case, whatever the value of the property, it's always small-scale farming. "A 20-hectare farmer is rare; 10 to 12 hectares, rare. 3 to 4 hectares form a normal crop, where all the members of the family find advantageous employment at all times, and one can even have a buffalo to do the heavy work. "There are a number of families who live very well and save with 1 hectare, and even less: and there are many who are forced to live with the small field that surrounds the house of the inheritance that the law and customs make inalienable, non-transferable and unseizable. "Most often, this field does not exceed 8 to 10 meous. Garden cultivation is therefore universally applied to Soya. Like other edible legumes, all varieties of soybean are used for direct human consumption. We would only exclude the black variety, which is rarely used for eating, if we didn't know that in times of famine, a handful of its seeds is enough to sustain the life of an unfortunate starving person. Their chemical composition proves it. Yeou-teou are eaten with fat or pork meat, or simply grilled. Black soy is the main animal food throughout northern China and Manchuria. Horses and mules, of which there are many, receive it whole or crushed, mixed with chopped millet straw and a little water. We never see thin or sick horses. It's the most precious and fortifying of feeds. It's also given to sheep. Green Soya is sometimes used for the same purpose. In the southern provinces, cattle are not given Soya seeds, but are fed the oil cakes produced by oil extraction, some of which are also used as fertilizer. The trade in these cakes is considerable. In several ports, they form the cargo of numerous junks, and give an idea of the importance of the plant in cultivation. Pe-teou or white Soya is more particularly used for oil production. The yield of this variety is remarkable. It is estimated at 40 hectolitres per hectare. We'd like to make an observation here that we may repeat: all Yeou-teou are suitable for all uses. Certainly, the Chinese have not, without reason, chosen certain varieties for the table, others to feed livestock, still others for industrial uses, oil extraction, the manufacture of Teoufou, Tsiang-yeou, the ferment for spirits and fake wines, Teou-che, etc.; but we are justified in believing that the chemical composition of all Soya is more or less identical and that they can be used in all ways. It is undoubtedly regrettable that the black variety is late and requires more heat than our climate can provide; but this consideration should not stop our farmers. In the absence of black Soya, they will grow the yellow and brown varieties, and their livestock will do well. SOYA OIL This oil fuels a huge trade. It ranks first among the fifteen or twenty types of oil that the Chinese possess. Europeans reproach it with an unpleasant raw bean aftertaste. Apart from this point, it is of excellent quality; the Société d'Acclimatation has received some several times. Three samples analyzed by the German chemist Seuff showed an average fat content of 18.71%. M. Pellet's analysis of three samples of Yellow Soya from China, Hungary and Étampes, supplied by us, gave more or less the same results. Already in 1855, M. Frémy, a learned chemist and former director of the Muséum d'Histoire Naturelle, had expressed a most favorable opinion of this product, in a letter inserted in the Bulletin de la Société d'Acclimatation, Volume II, page 388, and which we reproduce below: "Oil from oleaginous Peas presents a great analogy with our edible oils; its odor and flavor are pleasant; it is also suitable for combustion. Exposed to a cold temperature of 0°, it becomes pasty; atmospheric oxygen quickly resinsifies it. It therefore belongs to the class of drying oils and could, in this respect, replace linseed oil in some of its applications. "I knew that the Chinese extract 17 0/0 oil from their oleaginous Peas; it was interesting to assess, by analysis, the exact proportion of oil that exists in these Peas. "My analysis shows that the oil peas brought back from China by Mr. Montigny contain 180/0 oil. "If these peas are identical to those harvested in China, you can see, Mr. Chairman, that the Chinese are skilful industrialists, as they lose only one hundredth of the oil. "To sum up, the oilseed pea, whose importance you have already appreciated, should, through its richness in fats and the quality of the oil it supplies, give consumers a new foodstuff and the industrial arts a useful product." TEOU-FOU (SOYA CHEESE) Only the Tartars, scattered throughout the empire, have retained the use of milk; the Chinese do not consume it; Soya takes its place. Its seed is solid milk. No leguminous plant contains so much legumin (a substance chemically analogous to casein); almost none is so rich in fat. Simply crush the Soya seed, spread with water and pass the liquid through a sieve to obtain a product with the qualities of milk, usable like cow's, goat's or sheep's milk. In the Bulletin de la Société d'Acclimatation, 1855, Volume II, page 16, Baron de Montgaudry published a note from which we extract the following passage, relating to Teou-fou: "In China, oil peas are transformed into a food for the poor and a highly appreciated seasoning for the rich. For the poor, the flour of these peas is used to make a paste similar to the white cheese known in France as fromage a la pie, which is sold in public squares in portions of a few centimes, cut from the mass using archal wire, according to the buyer's request. Most often, the Chinese fry this paste or cheese in the very oil that comes from the Pea; they value this frying highly. "For the rich, the seasoning is prepared with greater care and culinary skill. The pea paste is fermented, after adding salt, pepper, laurel leaf powder, thyme powder and other aromatics. During the fermentation process, the preparer sprinkles Pea oil over the paste. After a few days' fermentation, this preparation has reached the desired point. This paste or cheese becomes a powerful digestive and an aperitif no stomach can resist. In Calfong, Honan, Tsi-Nan, Chang-ton, Thay-Yeun and Chan-nsi, oil and pastes made from oil-peas are produced in huge quantities and consumed in these provinces; but the city of Ning-Po, capital of Che-Kiang, is the place where the various products made from oil-peas are centralized, manufactured and shipped. The port of Ning-Po is difficult for large vessels to access, but they can stop at the island of Tcheou-Chan, where there is a very good harbor. Thousands of Chinese junks set sail from Ning-Po, along the coasts of China, with no cargo other than the products of the Oil Pea, which they carry to all parts of the Celestial Empire, to Japan and to all lands that know them." Here is a description of Teou-fou production, as given by Stanislas Julien and Paul Champion in their work entitled Industries anciennes et modernes de l'Empire chinois, etc., pages 185 et seq. "Making pea cheese is simple, but requires a great deal of care. First, the peas are soaked in water for around twenty-four hours, then left to drain in a wicker basket. They are then ground to a fine powder, mixed with the water used for maceration. The millstone used for this purpose is made up of horizontal hard stone disks. The upper disk is pierced by a conical hole; the device is set in motion by an articulated connecting rod which a worker moves with one hand, while with the other he throws Peas with a spoon into the cavity of the upper disk. "To each addition of Peas, a certain quantity of the maceration water is added. The Peas, crushed by the action of the millstone, are transformed into a liquid slurry which flows between the millstones, into a circular gutter and accumulates in a tub. This slurry is poured onto a filter formed from a cloth attached to a frame, and, when filtration is too slow, the material is agitated; to make this operation easy, the frame is suspended from the ceiling, at man-height. "The filtered liquid, stirred by hand, is collected in a wooden tub and poured into a boiler, where it undergoes a slow cooking process. This boiler consists of a cast-iron basin, surrounded by a sort of wooden tub; the metal surface, with its small surface area, enables the liquid to be heated without fear of developing a sudden rise in temperature that could alter the material. This device is almost always used by the Chinese for cooking organic materials. A second boiler is placed alongside the first in the same parallelepiped-shaped furnace, and receives the direct action of the hearth. The liquid drained from the millstone begins to foam abundantly at around 100° Celsius; it is kept at boiling point for about ten minutes and then transferred to the second boiler, which is subjected to a lower temperature, due to the layout of the furnace. Once the first boiler has been emptied, it is immediately filled with a new quantity of filtered liquor; the pulp drained on the cloth filter is washed with water, and the liquid that runs off is used to moisten the Peas that are being ground; this washing water still carries a significant quantity of useful matter. "When the liquor has been heated for a few moments in the second boiler, it is poured into large tubs, where it cools. The foam that forms gathers in the middle of the surface, and is removed with a copper spoon. After a few minutes' rest, the liquid is covered with a thick film which is removed, without tearing, with a chopstick, and dried by sticking the chopstick into a wall. A second film sometimes forms, which is treated in the same way. The material thus solidified on the surface of the liquid is used in food; it is eaten, either fresh or dry, and its taste is not unpleasant. "The liquid remaining in the vat is used to produce Pois de fromage; first, a small quantity of water mixed with plaster, which has been previously cooked in the furnace used for the operation, is added; finally, a few drops of a concentrated solution of salt from the salt marshes are added (according to our analyses, this salt is none other than magnesium chloride). The liquid is gently stirred to form a homogenous mass, which soon coagulates and solidifies. The added plaster certainly has the effect of coagulating the pea casein. As for magnesium chloride, it's hard to say what role it should play, and it's only used in certain Chinese cities. "The formed pea cheese is poured, still hot, into square wooden frames, Om. 40 square and Om. 05 high. These frames, stacked two on top of the other, are placed next to each other on a large stone table, the longitudinal edges of which are hollowed out into channels; the frames, placed on the table, are closed at the bottom by a thin cloth through which the water contained in the cheese can drain. When the volume of the material has been reduced by half, the frames are removed, and the cheese they contain is sometimes shipped great distances. All that's needed for transport is to close the frames with boards nailed with bamboo pegs. Once at its destination, the pea cheese is cut into small fragments using a large metal knife. "Pea cheese is generally grayish-white and jelly-like; it doesn't keep for more than a day in hot weather, and to prevent it from spoiling so quickly, it's usually mixed with salt or sauces of various kinds. It can then be kept for several years. "A fist-sized piece of Pois cheese sells for two sapèques, or one centime. Pea cheese merchants also deliver for consumption the hot, non-coagulated liquid we mentioned earlier; poor Chinese eat this substance, which has a bland but by no means unpleasant taste. At certain times of the day, the stores where this cheese is sold present a curious aspect; Chinese workers come in large numbers to buy a portion of liquid cheese, which they take away in small cups; others absorb the coagulated cheese on the spot. For many of the poorer classes, the morning meal consists solely of a cup of liquid pea cheese, in which they soak a few cakes fried in oil. Pea cheese is made on a large scale in most of the Chinese ports we visited, from the south to Peking, and in a few Japanese towns we were able to visit. "Pea cheese is quite pleasant to the taste; it could be of great service to the diet of Europeans if we could manage to cultivate the seeds which are the basis of its preparation. Fried in fat, like potatoes, pea cheese makes a very delicate dish. "The seeds used to prepare this cheese often contain 17 0/0 of a clear oil whose flavor is not unpleasant. "In addition to the above information, we would like to add some analytical results that we and our colleague Mr. Lhôte have obtained on oleaginous peas and cheese: CHINESE PEAS (Per 100) Water.................... We have omitted the recipe for preparing the curd, which would need to be corrected: "At the end of this period, a solid mass is obtained, which is cut into small pieces. These are placed in a jar into which salt is thrown. The whole is then sprinkled with San-cho (rice brandy) and the jar hermetically sealed. The mixture is then left to rest for at least three weeks. The cheese is made. The brandy can be replaced by any other equivalent substance. "It's useful to note the condition of the cheeses we're putting in front of you, in order to compare them later with those we might make. "We have two stoneware terrines, covered with a brownish glaze, one containing white cheese, the other red. "Cottage cheese. - This food comes in the form of small, unevenly cut pieces, 3 cm by 2 cm thick. The rind shows a kind of white mold. Inside, the cheese is greasy, yellowish-gray in color, and the paste is coarse, though fairly well melted; note that this terrine had been opened prior to receptionSome people compare the taste of this cheese to slightly advanced Maroilles. "We gave this cheese to a large number of people to taste, without telling them where it came from; most found it good, and don't hesitate to think that it would be accepted by the public. "Red cheese - This terrine was perfectly closed; on opening it, it gives off a special aroma somewhat similar to that of Fraise à l'euu-de-vie. Each piece is 4 centimeters in one direction and 2 in the other. It is covered with a red, slightly carmine stain. "On opening the pieces of cheese, we find them colored red to a thickness of 1 millimeter; the center is yellow; the paste is very fine and quite dissimilar to that of fromage blanc. As for the taste, it's different, saltier and without any known similarities. "Some prefer white to red, which at first glance tastes like brandy. In fact, the quality and taste of both cheeses cannot be compared. "The result of these experiments, carried out by over a hundred people of all ages and conditions, is that these cheeses will become popular in France when they can be made on the spot. TSIANG-YEOU SOY. Under the name of Tsiang-yeou, a sauce of general use is made in China, replacing the Shoyu of the Japanese; but its quality is very inferior. It is listed in China's tariff of export duties under the name Soy, and is probably what is sold in England under the name India Soy. This sauce is as black as shoe polish and is not to our liking. We believe that its recipe is common to China and Java, and we reproduce it below, based on a note published by Isaac Titsing, in the Verhandelingen van het Bataviash genootschap, de Batavia, volume III, 1791. We reproduce verbatim the translation of the Dutch text kindly supplied to us: "The preparation of Soya is simple and is done in the following manner: One takes a gantang of Miso beans (Soya), a gantang of burnt or ground Wheat or Wheat, which one assumes is necessary to give the proper color. These three species are mixed together and locked away in a box or cupboard for a period of eight days. Once the mixture has turned completely green, we take it out of the box or cupboard and leave it to dry for a whole day in the sun. "We then take two and a half gantang of boiling water and one gantang of pure salt, which must be dissolved in the water; after this, we leave it to stand for one etmaal (twelve hours), until the salt dirt has fallen to the bottom and the water has become cold, and we decant. The above-mentioned products are then added and turned with a shovel, for fourteen days, from time to time. "Froment or Gruau is used. The difference is that Soya made from Gruau is more fluid or limpid, and that made from Froment is thicker and more consistent, resembling ink. "Soya, nicknamed Ket jap (1) by the Chinese, is used with roast meat as a very delicate and appetizing condiment, both in Batavia and in the Netherlands. OTHER USES Soya is also used to prepare a condiment called Chi or Teou-che, made from its seeds, ginger and salt. Finally, Yeou-teou is used in the preparation of a (1) There is an error. Dr de la Savinière has informed us that the sauce in question is called Ketjap in Batavia. The Chinese call it Tsiang-yeou. - P. B. Ferment for making spirits and imitation wines, called Kiu-tsee. M. Dabry de Thiersant describes its preparation in Bulletin de la Société d'Acclimatation, 3e série, tome V, 1878, page 90: "In Canton, Kiu-tsee is made in a different way: "Take 75 pounds of good rice from the summer harvest (red rice, Hong-my, is preferred), 27 pounds of Dolichos Soja, 4 ounces of old Kiu-tsee and 14 pounds of pulverized Chan-kiue (Glycosmis trifoliata) leaves. "These Glycosmis leaves are first air-dried, and the drying process is completed using the heat of a vat, taking care to cover them with a sheet so that the essential oil does not evaporate. Glycosmis trifoliata is a tree that grows mainly in Kouang-ton province. Kiu-tsee manufacturers claim that, without its leaves, it is impossible to obtain good ferment. "Soya dolichos are cooked in fresh water for twenty-four hours in a cast-iron pot. "Rice is also cooked in a large cast-iron pot. When the water boils, the grain is thrown in and removed after ten or twelve minutes. The fire must be pushed quickly and the water must not be abundant, so that the grain is better seized. The rice is then spread out on a wooden table. When it has cooled, the Peas, Glycosmis leaf flour and powdered ferment are spread over its surface. All these materials are stirred and kneaded to incorporate them together, and placed in a trough, where they are trodden with the feet for fifteen minutes. When the mass has taken on the consistency of dough, we use molds to form bricks or rectangular loaves 6 inches long and 1 1/2 inches thick, weighing one pound. Once out of the mold, these loaves are arranged vertically on a board covering the floor. On the board is a bed of dried rice grains, on which the loaves are laid out, all touching each other. Both sides of the loaves are placed on the layer of rice grains before being erected. After eight or ten hours in summer and eighteen or twenty hours in winter, the sheet is removed and we see if fermentation has taken place under good conditions, if the color of the dough has become grayish and if blisters appear on the surface of the loaves, we separate them immediately and arrange them again vertically, forming empty squares. When, after four or five days, fermentation is complete, which is easily recognized by the whitish tint each loaf takes on, they are transported on nets, stretched out in the form of shelves, to a well-closed room, where they are left to dry for seven or eight days in summer, and four or five days in winter. They are then exposed to the sun for two or three days, on inclined racks; finally, they are placed for twenty-four hours in an oven, covered with a sheet. Drying is then complete, and they can be kept for two or three years. We believe that Soya is also used in a number of other food or industrial preparations, but we have only the vaguest notions about them, and we must refrain from talking about them. IV. - Soya in Austria-Hungary Following the Vienna Universal Exhibition, Professor F. Haberland published a remarkable study on the subject. Haberland published a remarkable study on Soya, demonstrating its importance and accelerating its introduction in all parts of the Empire. The results of experimental cultivation mentioned in the book are of great interest. This book, published in Vienna in 1878, is entitled: Le Soja. In 1880, Edmond de Blaskovics, of the Royal Hungarian Academy in Altemburg, Hungary, took up the subject again in a brochure entitled: Le Soja; de sa culture, de son emploi et de sa valeur comme fourrage; Vienne, 1880. V. - Soya in France Buffon was put in charge of the Jardin des Plantes in 1739, and shortly afterwards, the missionary Fathers of China sent him the most interesting specimens of that country's vegetation: Soya necessarily had its place in their consignments, and, without being able to prove it, we have no doubt on this subject. In any case, we found at the Muséum the bag that contained Soya seeds in 1779. It bears the harvest dates 1834, 36, 37, 38, 39, 41. 43, 44, 46, 47, 49, 50 to 55 inclusive; 1857, 58, 59; 1862, 65, 66, 67; 1870, 71; 1873 to date. In fact, Soya has been cultivated at the Muséum since 1740 most probably, 1779 certainly, and later, from 1834 to the present day without interruption. The plant has always vegetated and fruited to perfection, grown without special care. It has proved its hardiness and the little influence that atmospheric accidents have on it. Since 1855, the constant distribution of seeds by the Société d'Acclimatation has made it possible to try out the cultivation of Soya in France, but it is difficult, if not impossible, to obtain information on previous trials. M. Blavet has, however, found an interesting document, in a brochure entitled : Séance publique de la Société d'Agriculture d'Étampes, année 1832, page 84. One chapter is entitled: "Compte rendu par M. C. Brun des Beaumes, membre de la Société d'Agriculture d'Étampes, chevalier de Saint-Louis, docteur en la Faculté des sciences de France, de quelques essais de culture faits par lui en 1821, sur diverses espèces de Céréales, dans sa propriété de Champ-Rond, près Etampes". A final note reads: "The heat of the summer of 1821 was so favorable to exotic plants, that this year, I saw abundant fruiting at Champ-Rond, near Etampes, in my open-ground cultivations, Dolichos from China, Dolichos Lablab, Dolichos Soja; Niouelle (?) from Senegal showed its long spikes for the first time, etc.". From 1855 onwards, a large number of participants received seeds from the Société d'Acclimatation and experimented with them. Most did not report on their trials; others did, including Messrs Lachaume (1857), Vilmorin (1859) and Delisse (1863); but their crops were not progressive, and Soya had not yet gained a foothold in France. In 1868, M. Chauvin, vice-president of the Société d'Horticulture de la Côte-d'Or, cultivated several varieties of Soya and continues to do so to this day. In 1874, the Société d'Horticulture d'Etampes received seeds from the Société d'Acclimatation, and experimented with garden cultivation until 1880. These cultivations were directed with extreme zeal by M. Blavet, President of this Society. At the same time, Dr. H "*r brought the best Soya varieties from Japan and cultivated them. He failed in this attempt, which involved varieties that were too late. He confined himself to growing Chinese yellow Soya. He had no further difficulties, and made his own Shoyu for home use. In 1878, we received seeds of two kinds of Soya: one from Japan, with white flowers and very pale, greenish-yellow seeds; the other from China, yellow, part of the Chinese Houang-teou series, which we assimilate to the seeds received from M. de Montigny and other donors, and to those grown at the Muséum, in Etampes, in Marseille, just about everywhere. Seeds from Japan produce beautiful clumps, but the plants do not ripen their fruit. The Chinese species is as successful here as elsewhere. In 1879, Marseille received Houang-teou seeds direct from China, sowed, cultivated and harvested them without accident. In 1880, Messrs Vilmorin-Andrieux and Cte included in their catalog a variety grown in Austria-Hungary, thus making the propagation of Soya very easy. Mr. P. Olivier-Lecq, from Templeuve, receives from Mr. Jules Robert, President of the Société des fabricants de sucre, from Austria, in Séclowitz (Moravia), a batch of Green Hay, composed of Corn, Millet and especially Soya, preserved in an old ice-house in a las of 120,000 kilograms. He donated to the Ecole de Grignon the sample he had presented at the Melun competition, which had attracted the greatest attention from agronomists. Trials were carried out in various regions, and the results led to the conclusion that early yellow Soya can be usefully cultivated in France from Paris to Marseille, and that success is certain wherever Corn ripens its seeds, wherever Vines ripen their clusters. For a long time, the nutritional value of Soya was ignored. If our chemists had been familiar with the plant and had established its richness in analyses that would have been authoritative for everyone, it is certain that this would have greatly helped its propagation. But these analyses would not have been enough. Even though they would have been understood by the general public, even though it would have been proven that a handful of Soya seeds nourishes and restores strength just as well as two handfuls of Peas, Beans, etc., for a long time, quantity would have been preferred to quality. It is regrettable that Soya has been presented simply as a new vegetable, as it is a little more difficult to cook than the other legumes; its flavour is good, without superiority; fresh, it is more difficult to shell; dry, it requires twenty-four hours immersion in non-calcareous water. Ignorance of its nutritional properties has not been a determining factor in its cultivation. We stuck to the old-fashioned vegetables. The Austro-Hungarians were more astute; having acquired proof, indisputable moreover, of Soya's value as livestock feed, they had no other objective. At the very least, they seem to have considered the use of Soya for human consumption as secondary. It follows that, as soon as they had enough seed, they cultivated hectares of land, while we were still just cultivating vegetable patches. Soya VARIETY: it would be interesting to cultivate them comparatively; perhaps we could find among them some early varieties to add to those we already have. We have given above a list of those whose names we have been able to find. In the Paris climate, the yellow Chinese and Mongolian varieties are the only ones that can be grown at present; beyond the Loire, the same varieties can be sown, as well as the reddish-brown Chinese variety. We leave the black variety to Provence, Languedoc and Algeria (1). CULTIVATION The plant is easy to grow and differs little from the Dwarf Bean. We believe that all (1) In 1880, we sowed black Soya seeds, brought from Java by M. le pr de la Sarinière. The soil is suitable for this plant, if not equally, at least to a sufficient degree. Clump spacing is a crucial point. When clumps are too close together, ripening is delayed, and it is precisely here that we can encounter failure. If the soil is rich, more space is needed than if it isn't; but what grower doesn't know how to balance the fertility of the soil and the development of the plants? This is the A BC of the trade. We believe that the seeds must germinate very quickly, otherwise they will rot. The best time to sow seeds is between April 15th and May 1st, when the weather is neither too cold nor too dry. The seed should not be too well covered. Two seeds per hole is enough. We leave the Soya d'Etampes Om, 50 of space in all directions. We believe that sowing can be delayed until May 15, if the weather before then is decidedly unfavorable. We sowed seeds from Etampes on May 3 and seeds from Messrs. Vilmorin on June 10; the latter have joined the former. USE Soya harvest begins in September for Hungarian Soya, and in the first days of October for the other varieties. If the pods are not sufficiently dry by the latter date, they are laid out in the barn to dry out completely, but the seeds are left to ripen on the ground beforehand. A frost of 2 or 3 degrees doesn't harm them at all. Soya's hard, hairy pods cannot be eaten, like those of beans, before the seeds have formed, still less like the bean varieties known as Mange-tout. When the Soya seeds have reached full development, but have not begun to dry out, the pods are picked and shelled, and, in the opinion of many, the result is a vegetable superior to fresh shelled Flageolets. Without going that far, we'd say that it's their equal, that it's easier to digest and doesn't have the well-known disadvantages of beans. Its defect is that it is difficult to shell. When dry, Soya seeds are a good food. Their flavor is mild and very pleasant. We prepared them in the same way as ordinary white beans, but no matter how long and carefully they were cooked, they remained firmer than white beans, not harder. Soya should therefore be soaked for twenty-four hours in distilled water. To make up for the need for distilled water, 3 grams of soda crystals per liter are added to the water in the evening; the water turns white if it is chalky, and the precipitate is removed by decanting the next day. We won't go back over the various uses of Soya discussed in the previous chapters, but to show just how rich in nutrients this leguminous plant is, we'll show you the chemical analysis of its seeds. Pellet and Schou are: "That Soya is a concentrated food which contains not only the nitrogenous and non-nitrogenous organic elements essential for animal feed, but which also contains significant quantities of phosphoric acid, potash, magnesia and lime, necessary for the mineral constitution of flesh and bone". We tried our hand at making Soya cheese, and presented the product to the Société nationale d'Horticulture at its meeting on June 24 1880. M. Adrien Sicard, first vice-president of the Société d'Horti(1) Revue des Industries chimiques et agricoles, 1882. culture de Marseille, has carried out similar tests. The cheese is easy to obtain; the pitfall is the raw pea taste, which cannot be removed. In the Tyrol, Soya is cultivated under the name of Coffee Bean, and is used to make a coffee substitute. At the Société nationale d'Horticulture's meeting of August 26, 1880, we presented a flask of roasted seeds; prepared in water, they give an inferior coffee, but with exactly the same aroma as coffee. Later, Dr Ménudier, president of the Syndicat général des Comices agricoles du département de la Charente-Inférieure, once again drew attention to Soya, recommending it for making bread for diabetics (1). More than fifteen years ago, one of us took out a patent, hoping that this use could be justified. We had to abandon this idea. To sum up, Soya provides a highly nutritious food for humans and animals; the oil extracted from its seeds could have useful applications; it is also good fodder. Oilcake, the residue from oil production, is an excellent fertilizer. The whole plant, buried in the soil, would make an invaluable green manure in regions where manure is in short supply. Indeed, growing legumes is the most economical way of obtaining nitrogen, since these plants draw it from the air. A number of the products derived from this legume in China and Japan, which are used in the daily diet of hundreds of millions of people, will perhaps one day be accepted in Europe. Soya is certainly one of the plants whose cultivation is most deserving of encouragement here and in our colonies. TALINUN ÉTALÉ TALINUM PATENS Willd. Portulaca patens Jacq, Hort. vindob. il, tab. 152; DC, Pl. grasses, 173. Fam. Portulaceae Woody plant, native to South America. Stems angular; leaves alternate, obovate-lanceolate, short-stalked. Terminal panicle, elongated, very loose, composed of multiflorous cymes. Flowers small, bright pink. Stamens shorter than petals. Capsule globose, depressed at both ends, trigonal, shiny, brown. This plant is cultivated in collections of succulents; it flowers all year round. Its leaves are thick and fleshy. "Talins have the same properties as purslane. Their leaves can be used as seasoning. Eaten cooked, they are refreshing". (Lamarck, Encyclopédie.) After tasting, we cannot share the opinion expressed above; Talinum spread out is too bland to be used as a seasoning. Cooked and prepared like spinach, it gives the sensation of a food soaked in oil. M. Cornu was kind enough to give us some seeds of a plant sent to him from Zanzibar by R. P. Sacleux, under the name of Purslane, which is eaten in that country, cooked like Spinach. The plant germinated in Crosnes, grew vigorously and flowered. We recognized it as Talinum crassi folium Willd. which is also cultivated in some parts of tropical America as a food plant. It is known in French Guiana as Porcelaine. Like T. patens, the leaves of this species are very fleshy; cooked or eaten as a salad, they are reminiscent of cultivated Purslane. II, p. 239; BUNIUM INCRASSATUM Battandier et Trabut, Flore d'Algérie, by Battandier and Trabut, p. 346. Umbelliferae family. Plant native to southern Spain and boreal Africa, with tuberous root. Durando, professor of botany in Algiers, which we planted at the beginning of winter and which did not suffer from the cold. We had carefully protected them with a bit of straw, but we believe this precaution is unnecessary. Indeed, Dr Marès wrote to us from Mustapha on June 5 1881: "I'm not at all surprised that Talruda spent the winter well in France. Mountains of 1,500 to 1,600 meters in Algeria often have snow. The ones I have in front of the windows of my property are 1,500 to 1,700 meters. The summit is covered with Cedars. I've seen the snow start in October and stay there continuously until the end of April. Every year, I've seen snow there, and it's very cold." To the questions we put to him, Mr. Durando gave the answers we reproduce here: "The Talruda you received was harvested on the Arba mountains. It can be found on all mountains at least 1,000 meters high, in Blidah, Téniet, etc. "It thrives in cool soil. "It thrives in cool, shady soil with humus, quite commonly under cedar trees. Cedars can only be found above 900 or 1,000 meters, and therefore on mountains of at least 1,400 to 1,500 meters. "The locals eat Talruda tubers raw, boiled or roasted, ad libitum. We reproduce below an interesting article by Mr. J. Dugast, director of the agronomic station in Algiers (I), which gives the chemical composition of the tuber and indicates the uses to which it can be put: "The Arabs know this plant as Talghouda. L11. .. "() Bullet. de la Stat. agron. d'Alger, 1894, p. 48. and mix it with barley. 8 "At the request of the Prefect of Algiers, I examined a sample of Talghouda flour. Here is its composition: a-e .  Water...................... 15.66 Ash...................550 Nitrogenous matter................... 7.00 Fat.... 1.34 Starch and congeners.................. 63.12 Cellulose.............................. 640 Undosed matter..................098 100.00 "Starch grains are ovoid or irregular, sometimes in small numbers, two, three or four, forming compound grains. They vary in size. The most numerous measure from 4 to 8 thousandths of a millimeter in diameter: some, the largest, reach 15 to 20 thousandths of a millimeter. "The immediate composition of this flour is quite similar to that of barley; it is less rich in fat, but contains about the same proportion of starch. "On the other hand, if we compare these results with those of potatoes at the same degree of desiccation, we see that the latter is richer in starch and poorer in nitrogenous matter and fat. "Taghouda flour is therefore a food with a certain nutritional value, intermediate between barley and potato. We tasted these tubers and found them perfectly edible, but of mediocre flavor. The plant did not thrive in our soil, which was too dry. It can only be harvested every two years; the first-year tubers are still too small to be used. In addition, we consider the experiment we have carried out to be very incomplete, and recommend continuing it by sowing Talruda on the edge of the woods. Revue Horticole, pl. color. 1880, p. 150; 1881, p. 470. Fam. des Solanées. Shrub or small tree 3 to 4 meters high, native to Mexico, New Granada and Peru, introduced and cultivated in all the higher parts of tropical America, notably the West Indies, Brazil and the Argentine Republic. In warmer regions, Cyphomandra betacea is grown for its fruit, which has a sweet taste when raw Fig. 79. - Tomato de la Paz. Cyphomandra betacea Sendtn. and pleasantly acidic. Peeled and seedless, it can be used for the same purposes as the tomato, and Mr. Miers (Journal of Botany, 1845, p. 358) says it is sold for this purpose in the markets of Lima. Made into a compote with sugar, it is said to make a pleasant meal, with a slightly acidic, very refreshing taste. Dr. Morris, Deputy Director of the Royal Garden of Kew and former Director of the Colonial Garden of Jamaica, has noted that in Jamaica it is impossible to grow this plant in the lower parts, where the temperature is too high, and that, on the contrary, it gives excellent results in the region between 2,000 and 5,000 feet above sea level. Introduced to various parts of southern Europe, this Solanum has done very well there. Its cultivation presents no difficulty in subtropical regions. Dr Morris has made great efforts to spread it in the English colonies, mainly in the higher regions of India. Reports published in the Bulletin of the Royal Garden of Kew, August 1887, show that the results obtained have been most satisfactory. In the Paris climate, the Tomate de la Paz does not bear fruit until the second year after sowing. These seeds germinate easily, and when planted in February-March, on a bed and under a frame, the resulting plants can reach almost 2 meters in height before the return of the bad season. Their large, highly ornamental leaves mean that this Solanum, like many of its congeners, can be recommended for garden decoration. Before the first cold spells arrive, the plant should be lifted from the soil, repotted and placed in a cold greenhouse, where it should remain until the second half of May. Planted on an old bed, it produces flowers which are followed by fruits that remain green until the end of the summer season, ripening only when the plant is returned to the greenhouse. They ripen from November to January. These egg-shaped fruits, more or less attenuated at both ends, are about the size of a guinea fowl egg; their skin is parchment-like, reddish-green, yellowish or variegated depending on the variety; this skin, which has a particularly unpleasant taste, must be carefully removed when the fruit is eaten or used in any culinary preparation. As we have just seen, Cyphomandra betacea cannot be cultivated in the Paris climate, since the beds and frames are inadequate, and the plants need to be housed in greenhouses to produce fruit. - Nevertheless, we thought it would be useful to report here on the results we have obtained, and to draw the reader's attention to this plant, which would obviously be useful in a number of our colonies (1). We have also tried the cultivation of Solanum quitoense Lamk. (S. quitense H. B. K.), originally from Peru, where it is grown under the name Narangita. (1) For further information, see an article published by one of us in the journal Le Jardin, 1890, p. 54 (black fig), and Note sur la Tomate en arbre, by M. Maxime Cornu, Revue des sciences naturelles appliquées (Journal de la Société nationale d'acclimatation) 1893, p. 314.  M. Maxime Cornu, professor at the Muséum, kindly gave us the seeds of two varieties of this plant: one with sweet, sugary fruit, the other with acidic fruit, sent to him by R. P. Sodiro. These fruits are said to have the shape and volume of a small orange. They are much sought-after. We have obtained some very beautiful plants which, cultivated like the previous species, have given us a few flowers in the late season, but which have never set fruit. The fruit of this plant, egg-sized, greenish-yellow in color, with an apple-like odor and pleasant flavor, is said to be highly prized. It is sold at markets and preserved. It would be interesting to know the exact value of this Solanum in order to spread it in our colonies, if necessary. Amb., V, p. 407, tab. 148; Cucumis anguinus L.; Trichosanthes colubrina Jacq. f.; Involucraria anguina Rœm.; Trichosanthes Turolata Hamilt. Cucurbitaceae family. Annual, climbing plant, up to 2 meters high. Leaves alternate, cordate, orbicular, 3-5-lobed, unevenly sinuate. Long, bifurcated tendrils. Male flowers in clusters or cymes, on peduncles about 1 decimeter long, composed of a calyx with reflexed sepals, a white corolla with 5 divisions long and very elegantly fringed at the edges, and 5 stamens. Female flowers are sessile, stamenless, but have a pistil with a trilocular ovary and trifid style. Fruits cylindrical, 50 cm to 1 m long and 4-5 cm in diameter, straight or slightly curved, giving them the appearance of snakes; ripening in September-October, they are green speckled with white at first, turning yellow at maturity. 639 4 Fig. 81 - Trichosanthe couleuvre (Trichosanthes anquina). mcmerd rity; the seed is elongated and reminiscent in shape of the Gourde (Lagenaria vulgaris). Native to the East Indies. "This species, as reported by M. du Petit-Thouars, is cultivated on the Ile de France for its fruits, which are picked when half-ripe; in this state, when cut into small pieces, cooked and seasoned appropriately, they form a healthy vegetable that is pleasant to the taste. (Lamk. loc. cit.). "This classic species has been well known since the time of Linné, and is cultivated in most botanical gardens; but it has some difficulty coming to the climate of Paris. Its fruits, usually sweet, are edible in India and could be used here, when young, for the same economic purposes as those of the Cucumber." (Naudin, loc. cit.) The seeds of the plant we cultivated were given to us by an inhabitant of Mauritius, under the name Patole, the only one used in the English colony and in Réunion. We gave it the same care as the Melons, except for pruning, and obtained a large number of fruits, only a few of which reached maturity at the end of September and the beginning of October. The others were only slightly developed, but very suitable for the use we wanted to make of them. For the table, pick the fruit while it's still very young, cut it into small pieces smaller than a thimble, avoid peeling it, and season it like Flageolets with butter and fine herbs. Thus prepared, they form a fresh vegetable, tender without being mushy, and bearing no resemblance to cucumbers or squash. Needless to say, they can also be prepared in a variety of other ways, as part of a jus or as a garnish for stews. It is unfortunate that they cannot, without being cut into several pieces, be introduced into Pickles jars. They would lose their curious appearance, which would be a great pity; but, if this sacrifice were made, they would be perfectly at home in vinegar preserves. We believe that the Patole deserves to be cultivated in amateur gardens, in the south of France and in those of our colonies which do not yet have it. The fruits that ripened here were only 70 centimetres long, but they were highly contoured and looked marvellously like green snakes with white marbled coats. TRICHOSANTHES CUCUMERINA L. Franchet presented us with a small tuber of Actinostemma paniculatum Maximowicz (1), which was included with herbarium specimens sent to the Museum from Eastern Se tchuen by M. l'abbé Farges. These samples were accompanied by a label bearing the following suscription: N° 93, Kia pey mou, tuberous-rooted plant cultivated to falsify pey mou. This tuber, cultivated at Crosnes, gave us a plant which flowered the following August and which we have kept ever since. Unfortunately, Kia pey mou is a dioecious Cucurbitaceae of which we found ourselves possessing only one sex (female): we were therefore unable (1) Cogniaux, Mouographiæ phanerogamarum, vol. 3, p. 920. fruiting. The specimens received from China and kept in the Museum herbarium belong to the same sex. The plant is absolutely hardy, and so powerful that it thrives in the most arid soils. Each part of the stem close to the ground takes root, giving rise to a tuber, so that when the plant is uprooted in late autumn, it yields an abundant harvest. The tubers have a very particular shape; they are made up of large scales grouped together in such a way as to resemble to some extent the arrangement of the scales in the bulb of a lily, with the difference that they are few in number, very thick and loosely interwoven. These tubers are smooth and pure white when plucked; they contain abundant starch, but have a bitter taste as pronounced as that of Coloquinte, making them absolutely inedible. It is possible, however, that a special preparation will enable the starch to be used. Kia pey mou would therefore not deserve to appear in this book if we didn't think that its merits as an ornamental plant would prevent it from being accepted in gardens. Its climbing stems, from 1 m. 50 to 2 m. high, are fitted with tendrils and bear heart-shaped leaves with 5 palmate lobes, slightly reminiscent of those of the grapevine, but very hairless, dark green on the upper surface, paler green and glossy on the underside, with a yellowish gland at the base of each lower lobe. The furrowed petiole is 4 centimeters long; the leaf blade is 7 to 8 centimeters long and as wide. The female flowers, the only ones we have observed, are barely 4 to 5 millimeters long; they are green, grouped in small numbers in the leaf axils and borne on slender peduncles of unequal length, some very short, others up to 2 1/2 centimeters long. The ovary is globose. The calyx, swollen above the ovary, has the leaf blade divided into 5 lanceolate lobes, long extended to a point, as are the corolla divisions, also 5 in number and similar to the previous ones; both are closely spaced; they are slightly divergent at the apex. Lophophores are very greedy for its bulbs". "Along the way, one of my men pointed out traces of Lophophora, moss and grasses torn up by the broad beaks of these birds, which live on succulent roots, especially those of the yellow Fritillary, known as Pé mou; this plant gave its name to our Gallinacea, which almost always contains some onion of this precious Liliacea in its stomach (Armand David.
