Valerian family. Spain. Annual plant, entirely glabrous. Stem thick, fistulous, branched, 0m, 25 to Om 30 high. Leaves oval, thick, glossy green: lower ones briefly petiolate, entire or toothed, obtuse; upper ones sessile, deeply incised. Small but numerous flowers in corymbs. Corolla with a small tube, three times longer than the ovary, which in turn is three times longer than the corolla spur. In their book, Les Fleurs de pleine terre, MM. Vilmorin-Andrieux et Cie teach the cultivation method applicable to large-stemmed Valerian, considered as an ornamental plant. We refer the reader to this work. The plant has been recommended as a successor to Lamb's lettuce. It can indeed replace them, but is, in our opinion, far inferior. The tasting we did was not favorable. Such is not the opinion of M. Charton, who, in the Revue horticole, 1872, page 259, under the title of : Une bonne salade trop peu connue, published the following note: "We are not short of salads; we have, thank God, some excellent ones, so much so that we can say that the embarrassment is in the choice of species and varieties; But, as the saying goes, an abundance of good things does no harm, and we have no doubt that, despite the riches we possess in this genre, more than one reader of the Revue horticole will be delighted to know about this one, which differs quite markedly from all the salads cultivated up to now, both in appearance and, one might say, taste. "The plant we're talking about belongs to the Valerian family; it's very closely related to Lamb's lettuce, whose taste it has, a slightly fatty sweetness, with a slight bitterness that makes it a little less bland, a little less insipid than Lamb's lettuce, to which, for these reasons, it will be preferred by some amateurs. "This plant is Valerian macrosiphon, or Centranthus macrosiphon, already well known in gardens, where it is grown for its pretty bouquets of pink flowers, and rightly regarded as one of the prettiest ornamental plants. "To obtain a beautiful leafy development and a successive production from September until the frosts, we recommend sowing the seeds on the spot, absolutely as if they were lamb's lettuce, but covering the seed a little less, which is much finer, and this from June and throughout the month of July. "If readers want to believe us, they won't cut the plant too young; if they let it develop, they'll be rewarded with more abundant production and fuller, fleshier leaves. "The young stalks themselves are very tender and edible, and if, from the first forkful, you're a little surprised by the slight bitterness of this salad, you'll be amazed to find it more pleasant as you eat more of it, and finally you'll be convinced that it's an excellent salad as well as being exceedingly harmless, and you might add one of the healthiest and most hygienic, since it belongs to the Valerianae family." VOANDZOU (VOANZO, in MADAGASCAR) VOANDZEIA SUBTERRANEA du Petit-Thouars; Glycine subterranea Linné fils, Decad. tab. 17. family Leguminosae. Annual herb with creeping stems divided into spreading branches. Leaves composed of 3 oblong, oblate leaflets; common petiole 3 to 4 inches long, peduncles short, axillary, inclined, those of biflorous hermaphrodite flowers. Corolla yellow; wings oblong, spread horizontally; standard oval, striated. Vegetable rounded, fleshy, monosperm by abortion. Peduncles sink into the ground like those of the groundnut. "The earliest travelers to Madagascar (1), had (1) The R. P. Camboué, a missionary in Madagascar, sent, on various occasions, seeds to the Société nationale d'Acclimatation (see Revue des sciences naturelles appliquées, 1890, p. 76), as well as to the Société nationale d'Agriculture (See Bulletin de la Société nationale d'Agriculture de France, 1888). noticed this annual legume, which the inhabitants cultivate to eat the fruit or seeds, like Peas, Beans, etc. It resembles the Peanut. It resembles the peanut, particularly in that the flower support curls up and buries the young fruit or vegetable in the soil. It is widely grown in gardens, especially in tropical Africa, and less commonly in southern Asia. It does not seem to be grown much in America, except in Brazil, where it is called Mandubi d'Angola". (A. de Candolle, Origine des plantes cultivées.) VOANDZOU We received a letter from Elim, Spélouken, dated July 13 1882, from Mr. E. Creux, head of the Waldensian mission in the Transvaal, which contained the following passages: "Our church is sending us a farmer, Mr. Mingard; I hope you will see him when he comes to Paris... Sesame, peanuts and an excellent tuber bean whose native name I don't know are widely cultivated by the natives...". We did not see Mr. Mingard, who was already on his way to Africa when we received word that he would be passing through Paris; but we did receive a letter from him, dated Elim Waterfall, Spélouken, February 1, 1883, saying: "I am sending you 100 grams of our Tindlohu or Tuber Beans, and 100 grams of our Tinyawa, Hedge Beans, with oars. "The first species is good and sweet; it makes a very good vegetable here. It is sown at a rate of one bean per hollow. They must be ridged like potatoes. "The second is more mediocre, similar in taste to small beans. For the table, Tinyawa must be boiled beforehand. They grow here in bushes. We received and sowed the seeds announced. The Tinyawa is a Lablab. It has vegetated vigorously, but without bearing fruit. We belatedly recognized that the Tindlohu was the Voandzou, a very interesting plant, but rebellious to cultivation in the Paris climate. We had sown the seeds in a frame, and they had sprouted; three or four leaves, borne on long petioles, formed a small tuft in the center of which were several weak, very spindly shoots... We waited for the stems to emerge, but they didn't, and by the time we stopped, it was too late. The spindly shoots that had appeared, and which were doubtless not destined to vegetate in the open air, were dried out. We still don't know enough about Voandzou. We have learned neither its cultivation nor its uses. We are told that its roasted seeds are sold to negroes in the streets of Rio, among other coarse sweets; but this usage would in no way explain the general cultivation of this vegetable in a large number of tropical parts of Africa, where there are several varieties of reddish-brown or yellowish-white, single-colored or variegated. The seeds are certainly eaten like beans, peas and broad beans. R. P. Camboué has devoted an interesting note to Voanzo, published in the Revue des Sciences naturelles appliquées, 1889, p. 893, in which he tells us that the plant is cultivated in Madagascar in the clayey or silicoclayey soils of the bare Imérina hills, at around 1,300 meters above sea level. Sowing takes place around the beginning of the rainy season (October-November). Around February, when the plant is flowering, it is lightly ridged. Harvesting takes place around March-April. "Voanzo is then sold on the Tananarive market for A francs per hectoliter. In Imérina, Voanzo is eaten prepared like beans or peas; boiled in water, its flavor is somewhat reminiscent of chickpeas and hazelnuts. It will certainly be impossible to grow Voandzou in France. It could be introduced into our African possessions, Guyana, Pondicherry, Cochinchina, Tonkin, etc. The plant is curious enough to warrant a closer look. The plant is curious enough for growers to try to raise a few plants under glass. It would certainly be of great service in those of our colonies that do not yet have it. YOUNG TS'AI IPOMÆA REPTANS, Poir ; Convolvulus reptans L.; C. repens Willd.; Ipomæa aquatica Forsk. Fam. Convolvulaceae The seeds of this plant were sent to us by Fr. Heude, missionary in Zikawei, China. They were accompanied by the following note: "They are everywhere in southern China. We pick the tender shoots and treat them like spinach, amaranth and alfalfa, i.e. we soften them in water and fry them in oil, lard and so on. "It's best to grow them in beds, in cool, open soil. Sow as soon as you receive them. I doubt you'll be able to get ripe seeds in Paris. It is obviously this plant that Ch. Eckeberg refers to in his book Chinese husbandry. The Chinese," he says, "cultivate a water spinach in ponds, which grows in such abundance that it completely covers the surface of the water. It is one of the most common vegetable herbs. Fortune, in his Voyage agricole et horticole, cites Convolvulvs reptans as one of the vegetable plants traded extensively in the markets of southern China. In a letter dated January 28, 1890, Dr. Bretschneider tells us that the most common name for this vegetable in China is Young ts'aï (pronounced Ang tsoi); this is the name by which the plant is described in Chinese botanical books, in the Canton dialect. Its popular name in Beijing is Koung-tsai. Young and Koung are the names of the plant; Ts'aï means vegetable. Although aquatic, it is not grown in water in Beijing. In other parts of China, Young ts'aï is nevertheless grown in ponds or streams, as shown by a note published in the Revue horticole, 1890, page 52, under the title Jardins flottants chinois. It reads: "An ingenious means of protecting crops from flooding and at the same time preserving them from excessive drought is put into practice by the Chinese. "It consists in building rafts of variable length, with a floor featuring numerous interstices. A layer of straw 2 to 3 centimetres thick is laid on top, covered with mud or earth from the bottom of a river, to receive the seeds of various plants, principally Ipomxa reptans, whose stems and roots the Chinese eat. Under these conditions, the straw, then the soil, soon rots and disappears. The roots then develop in the water. These rafts are attached to the banks of lakes, ponds or streams, on which they float constantly, defying flooding, and it is a curious sight," says Mr. Magowan in the China Review, "to contemplate, in summer, these gardens of a kind that is totally unknown to us in Europe." M. Balansa, botanist traveler, in charge of the exploration of Tonkin, gave us some interesting details on the cultivation of Ipomæa reptans in our colony during his stay in Paris in 1889: "The plant exists everywhere. It is widely cultivated. "The tips of the leafy twigs are harvested and sold in the markets. The vegetable is eaten cooked, but has no particular flavor. "In the interior of Hanoi, there is a pond specially devoted to the cultivation of this plant. It is divided into several parts belonging to different owners, which are bordered by bamboos. "The foot of the plant always plunges into the water; the leafy branches float on the surface. Women go out in small flat boats to harvest them. We sowed Young ts'aï seeds under a frame and transplanted half the plant under glass and half in the open ground. The plant did better in the open air than in the trunk. It flowered quite abundantly in both conditions; however, it was in the trunk that we were able to collect three or four seeds. The lateral branches appear very late; they creep along the ground and cling to it like ivy to the wall. They are thick and soft, with fleshy leaves. The flowers, quite large and pure white, dazzle the carpet formed by the leaves. This Ipomoea might not be without interest as an ornamental plant. As a vegetable, it could be experimented with in a more favorable climate than that of Paris, where in any case it cannot be usefully cultivated. According to Krasnow (1), an aquatic gentian, Limnanthemum peltatum, commonly known as Djinsaï, is cultivated in ponds and provides a sweet, mucilaginous salad, much appreciated in Japan. (1) Krasnow, Les régions théières de l'Asie subtropicale, 1st vol. Japan, St. Petersburg, 1897 (in Russian). IRIS JUNCEA Poir, Desf. Atl. fl.; Iris luritanica Ker. Bot. Mag. tab. 679. Fam. des Iridées. 657 Flower bright yellow; outer divisions orbicular oval-bladed, unicolored, obliquely erect tabbed; inner divisions spatulate-oblong, pointed, notched; stigma liguliform-bladed; lamina bilobed. Bulb pelit, barely the size of a hazelnut, covered with a brownish tunic. Stem 1 to 2 feet tall, spindly, slightly flexuous, leafy at base. Leaves linear-lanceolate, narrow, subulate at the apex, glaucous green. Spathe herbaceous, usually uniflorous. Perianth tulip very short, camja naked. Portugal, boreal Africa. In the Bulletin de la Société d'Acclimatation, volume III, 1856, we read the following letter, page 456, addressed to its president by M. A. de Cès-Caupenne: "Monsieur le Président. Settled in Algeria, where I run a large forestry operation in the province of Constantine, and living in contact with the Arabs, I have noticed that among the dishes that make up their frugal diet, there is one that is much sought-after in all the douars I have visited, and that is provided by a plant that grows wild in the forests and wetlands. Zétoutt is the name the Arabs give to this plant (Iris juncea). "Its stem resembles that of the Narcissus sau42 vage. The food part consists of an onion no larger than a hazelnut. "This Zétoutt flowers in spring, at the same time as the Iris and Jonquilles. As soon as it blooms, Arab women rush to harvest it. To eat it, they strip the oiguon of the film that covers it and cook it in butter or water, turning it into a paste, like potatoes, to make cakes. "This plant is floury and its starch has a very fine taste. During the winter, wild boars are very fond of it, and just as the digging of pigs is used to find truffles, the digging of wild boars guides the Arabs in their search for Zétoutt. "There is every reason to believe that we can increase the volume of the Zétoutt onion by means of weeding, thereby introducing a new product into France's agricultural and market-gardening industry, a product which, as it becomes more widespread, could become a valuable resource. This is a question which perhaps deserves the attention of the Society. One of its members, the honorable Mr. Tastet, to whom I spoke about it a short while ago, saw it in this light, and it is with his encouragement that I take the liberty, Mr. Chairman, of sending you a few samples of Zétoutt that I collected myself a few days ago in the Safia cork oak forest. A friend of ours, a landowner in Algeria and director of a cork oak company, had 1,200 or 1,500 Zétoutt bulbs collected for us in his forests, which he gave us as a gift. We didn't take advantage of this precious gift. Believing, perhaps wrongly, that Zêtoutt would only be as useful as its cultivation was economical, and that we should even try to naturalize it on the edge of the woods, we planted our bulbs in semi-shade and limited ourselves to weeding the soil whenever necessary. The experiment lasted four years. The winters did not, we believe, contribute to the destruction of our plantation, but the soil was too arid, and the end result was that we had almost nothing left of our 1,200 or 1,500 bulbs. We probably wouldn't have been any more successful if we'd done things differently, because we have to admit that the bulbous plants didn't give us any satisfactory results. We lost the Japanese edible lily, the Crocus edulis, just as we lost the Zetoutt. More skilful amateurs with more favourable soil will perhaps succeed better than we did. In any case, this is a plant for which we can repeat what we said about edible lilies. It can be harvested and used wherever it grows spontaneously; but there is no advantage in introducing it into gardens, as it takes several years to obtain mature bulbs from seed, which, as we saw earlier, are always small. We have also experimented with a number of edible Irideae which have not given us better results, including Jahuique (Tigridia Van-Houttei Baker), Flore des serres et des jardins de l'Europe, pl. 2174; Baker, Handbook of the Irideæ, p. 69, from Mexico, of which M. Cornu gave us seeds he had received from M. Dugès. When cooked, the bulbs of this species are said to taste like chestnuts. Two other plants in this family also have edible bulbs: 1° Iris edulis Thunb. (Moræa edulis Ker, Vieusseuxia edulis Link), a species native to the Cape of Good Hope. The Hottentots collect the bulbs and stems, which they make into bundles. They cook them lightly and eat them. According to Thunberg, this food is very tasty and nourishing. Siuges also eat it (Encycl. meth.). 2° Gladiolus edulis Burch. A species that grows on the Cape of Boune-Espérance and whose roasted bulbs have the same flavor as chestnuts (Botanical Register, vol.
